Pixel was quiet. The high-resolution kind.

"Ongoing subscription. As far back as his records go." Kyle watched the maintenance crew. One of them was directing a boring machine with the bored certainty of someone who had done this exact thing for twenty years. "Source filed as clients, unsourced. No chain of custody. The payment cleared but the routing resolved to nothing — not scrubbed-nothing, original-nothing. Something that has no history because it never had history to scrub." He paused. "Comprehensive file. Current."

"You're telling me the entity has been reading your file."

"I'm telling you what Vey told me."

"Kyle."

"What."

"That's not what I asked."

He thought about the pencil circle. Eleven years of contracts — standard rate, exact, early; the tip labeled MORALE; the addendum that itemized his work in three flat sentences; the receipt that arrived before he'd filed for payment. The warm-strange quality of being known that completely by something with no provenance. He thought about what it was to have worked for something you'd never verified was a what or a who, and to have not tried particularly hard to verify it, and to have been doing this for eleven years.

"I don't know," he said. Which was true.

Pixel nodded. She pulled her hair loose — the cable tie — and re-tied it with the automatic ease of someone who did it forty times a day. "During your calibration last week," she said, "I picked up a resonance in the deep array. Below the Atlas spec floor. Background frequency, runs continuous."

"You said the calibration was clean."

"The calibration is clean. This isn't a calibration issue." She kept her eyes forward. "It's a tone. Below 20 Hz. I've seen sub-harmonic artifacts in Atlas rigs before — age drift, manufacturing variation, infrastructure interference. This one's different. The frequency signature matches nothing in my library. Older than any Atlas spec I've pulled."

"The array would flag a degradation event," Kyle said.

Pixel was quiet for a moment. "Yes," she said. "It would."

They walked in silence for half a block. She didn't say anything else about it. He didn't say anything else about it.

At the Pivot's door she stopped and turned and looked at him. The hinge dragged at the catch — the same catch it had been dragging at for three months. He was aware that she heard this every time she was in her apartment across the hall. That she noted his comings and his goings without being asked. That she had pulled the calibration data last week and found something older than any spec she owned and held it until she had something to put it next to. This was how she worked. He understood what it cost.

"The thing Vey found," she said. "It's been there for eleven years. It'll still be there after soup."

"I know."

"You're going to think about it anyway."

"I know."

She put her hand on the door handle. He watched her not say what she was not saying, and she watched him not say what he was not saying, and neither of them said any of it because that was how they had arranged this.

"Take the soup," she said. "Not just the noodles. The actual soup."

"The soup comes with the noodles."

"Then take the complete thing that arrives with the noodles."

"Fine," Kyle said.

"Yes," she said. "It is."

She went inside. The door closed on its dragging hinge.

Kyle stood in the street. The city moved around him: HydroCore boring through something forty meters down, the fruit stand with its round-number prices, a pigeon on a ledge that may or may not have been the one Jasper had been cataloging since 06:12. Somewhere below him, two freight slugs were about to depart at approximately Mach 6, one carrying a tag that was no longer on the right cargo, reporting faithfully to a watcher who would believe they were watching the right freight. Accurate data. Wrong location. Nobody lying.

Vey had told him once that provenance was not about authenticity. It was about chain of custody. You couldn't know if something was real. You could only know who had held it, in what order, and whether the hands made sense.

Kyle put his hands in his pockets and walked toward Mrs. Chen's.

[Beat 84]
The Pivot's hallway at six in the morning: overhead strips on their pre-dawn setting, half-power, the kind of light that asked nothing back. The air at this hour had the West Town character — solder, old iron, the diesel rise from the freight tier three blocks south coming up through the vent gaps whenever the wind shifted east. Kyle had been breathing it for four years. He noticed it only when he was this awake this early, which was becoming a category he visited more than he preferred to count.

His door was 2W. Hers was 2E. He'd been in the hallway forty seconds when hers opened.

Pixel had two cups. She held one out handle-first, and he reached and took it before he'd committed to an opinion about what it meant.

The tea was green. Liang blend. Still too hot to drink with any dignity.

"The vending strip on two had it this week," she said. She was leaning against the doorframe, her own cup near her collarbone, not quite drinking. There was a fresh solder burn on her right forefinger — calibration slip, two days old at most — and she had the look of a woman who'd been awake longer than she was acknowledging. "Figured you'd want to taste it before you knew where it came from."

Kyle tasted it.

It was the right blend. He hadn't told her it was the right blend. That information existed in the pattern of what he bought on the weeks he had money for preferences, and she had been reading the pattern, and here it was in a cup already turned toward him.

"You came in at twenty past midnight on Tuesday," she said, in the same voice she used to read a calibration result back to him — precise, informational, not asking anything from him with it. "Wednesday closer to two. Last night was three-twelve."

He held the cup.

"The east hinge drags worse on the pull than the push," she said. "Different sound from when you're moving quiet on purpose." A pause. Short. "You weren't moving quiet."

The half-step back started.

The mechanism that had been running at standard function for four years — the small managed distance, the redirect, the posture that said nothing wrong here, I just don't do this — it registered the situation and it started. He felt it start. He felt the weight shift.

It did not take the step.

Kyle drank the tea. The too-hot kind that costs something at the back of the throat. His neuretics logged the damage gradient against some longer tally they kept for him, a quiet accounting running two levels below the morning. He didn't look at the number. He drank the rest of the cup.

"Three-twelve," he said.

"Give or take a minute."

She had shifted her attention to the far wall. The particular quality of the way she was not looking at him said she understood the weight in the hallway and had decided, somewhere around the time she was steeping the tea, not to do anything with it. Not because she hadn't seen it. Because she'd already accounted for it, the way she accounted for everything: the hinge drag, the exact hour, the blend he preferred.

Her knowing him had become her keeping track of him.

He didn't name that. Neither did she.

"The hinge," he said. "I'll sort it."

"Already oiled it." She pushed off the doorframe, easy as air pressure shifting. "Catches again by Thursday. No point until it does."

Her door swung shut behind her. Pull side, smooth and quiet, the oil still doing its work.

Kyle stood in the hallway with the empty cup. The overhead strips ran at their half-power. The linoleum was worn through at both their thresholds — two bare patches of concrete facing each other like the evidence of a habit neither of them had named. Three floors down, the freight tier was coming awake, the low rumble of it just below the register where the mind decided to assign attention.

He stood there a moment longer than was necessary.

Then he went back inside.

[Beat 85]
The notice was taped to Mrs. Chen's awning post at six-seventeen in the morning, which was when Kyle arrived for his bowl and found it there instead.

Compliance Hold: Zone Recategorization Pending. Reference number in the upper right corner, district seal at the bottom, the whole thing printed on official gray stock that smelled like nothing at all. Someone had come here already, which meant someone had filed, which meant this was not spontaneous. Kyle peeled it off the post and read it twice. The language was specific in the way of things designed to be too specific to challenge without a lawyer — transitional commercial reclassification, Section 7.14.b, pending review under the amended Wentworth Corridor Development Index. The Wentworth Corridor Development Index was not a thing Kyle had heard of. That was probably intentional.

Mrs. Chen was at her counter plating for the two workers already seated, both of them looking at their bowls the way people looked at things when they wanted not to have seen something. She did not look at Kyle. He sat down at the end of the counter. After a moment she brought him tea.

"How long," he said.

"It came yesterday." She poured for herself at the back counter, which meant she had already poured once this morning and was not done with the conversation. "I've applied for review. Forty-day hold on review applications in the transitional commercial category."

He smoothed the form on the counter and read it again. Zone recategorization. The phrase had a specific administrative quality — bureaucratic enough to sound procedural, substantial enough to require expertise to challenge, designed to sit in neither the legal category of an appeal nor the practical category of a fine, so that you couldn't address it efficiently as either one. It was a good design, if design was the right word.

"Transitional commercial," he said.

"Yes."

"Your stall has been here for twenty-three years."

"It has." She set his tea closer. "I made your first bowl in 2215. You came in the second week of June. You ordered without looking at the menu — I thought you were deciding, but you hadn't looked at it once." She refilled the cup of the nearer worker, who accepted it without comment. "I thought, this one is deciding something else."

That sounded right. He did not remember the bowl specifically. He remembered that he had been new to the city and had not yet learned to ask for things he needed, so he had been learning to ask for things he wanted, to practice the form of it. The bowl had been a practice ask. He had stayed in West Town because the asking had worked.

"The originating complaint numbers," he said. "Do you have them."

She reached under the counter and produced a slim physical folder, rubber-banded. She had organized it already. She had been expecting someone to ask eventually — not him specifically, not any specific person, but she had not been going to do nothing and she had known that whoever turned out to be useful would need to be handed something orderly.

He picked up the folder. "What do you want done."

She gave him a look that said: what a question. "I want my stall back."

"By end of business today."

She did not blink. "If you can manage it."

He paid for the tea, exact. She took his money without ceremony, which was its own form of dignity — the transaction complete, the relationship intact, nothing owed on either side. He tucked the folder inside his jacket and left without eating, which his stomach noted and he set aside. He would get to it.

---

The originating complaint numbers cross-referenced to three names. Hanna Borg-Okafor, Denis Priyatharan, Sung-Ji Maduka. All three had submitted formal hygiene complaints through the zone's citizen portal within the same six-hour window, all attached to the same petition file number. The petition had thirty-one signatures.

Kyle found a public terminal in the Markel Street transit hall. The civic display interfaces were slow and required a registration token he didn't have, so he went through his neuretics instead — the Atlas array burned the queries fast, returning data in the specific quality of silence that meant it was working faster than he could consciously track. He had learned to stay out of the way of the fast processing and wait for the summary.

Hanna Borg-Okafor: address listed as 1140 West Damen, Unit 3. Kyle checked the building registry. 1140 West Damen had no Unit 3. Seven units: 1, 2, 4, 5, 6, 7, G. The G unit was a ground-floor storage lock-up registered to a separate parcel. No Unit 3 had ever existed in the building's registration history, which went back to 2198.

Denis Priyatharan: ID number with a 09 prefix. That prefix was assigned to wards of the zone authority — minors in transitional housing or temporary guardianship. The petition was a formal zone-authority complaint document. Formal zone-authority complaints required a guardian signature for any registered minor in the 09 category. There was no guardian signature on this petition. Either the filing clerk had missed it or no one had checked because no one had expected anyone to look.

Sung-Ji Maduka: address listed as 412 Fulton Corridor. Kyle knew 412 Fulton. He had done a job there in 2224 — a repossession, a contractor debt, clean work, nobody hurt. The building had been condemned since 2221. It had been stripped and partially demolished. Nobody lived there. Nobody had lived there for five years.

Three complainants. Zero real people.

He filed the screenshots to a case folder in his neuretics archive and moved to the inspector's report.

The report was six pages, dense with municipal formatting, produced on AxisMed-grade paper stock — slightly waxy, slightly bright, the kind that cost three times as much as it needed to because it read as official. The inspector's name was Renata Osei-Bonsu. Kyle ran it against the zone office's registered inspectors list. Renata Osei-Bonsu was not on the list for West Town. She was listed as an active contractor for Zone 9 commercial licensing, which was not the same jurisdiction. She had done four inspections in West Town in the past eighteen months, all of them for properties that had subsequently been reclassified.

The report itself cited subsection 14.7.c of the West Town Commercial Food Preparation Code as the basis for flagging Mrs. Chen's preparation surfaces as noncompliant. Kyle pulled 14.7.c. It applied to enclosed kitchen spaces with a minimum square footage of two hundred and a permanent water connection. Mrs. Chen's stall was thirty-two square feet, open-air, mobile-classified, and drew its water from the corridor's commercial attachment point on a per-use license. Subsection 14.7.c was inapplicable on three independent grounds, any one of which was sufficient to invalidate the citation.

An oversight, possibly. A deliberate falsification, also. The distinction would not matter for Tessaline's legal exposure, which was the part that mattered.

The zone recategorization filing had a submission timestamp of 14:32 on the fourth. The citizen complaint petition had a submission timestamp of 19:07 on the fourth. The same day. The hold had been filed four and a half hours before the complaints that were cited to justify it.

Kyle spent a few minutes with this arithmetic. Three fabricated complainants, an out-of-jurisdiction inspector citing inapplicable code, and a filing timestamp that proved the plan predated its own stated cause. Any one of these was defensible as coincidence. Together they were the work of someone confident they would not be examined.

He accessed Tessaline's public franchise registration through the zone authority's business portal. Damen Authentic LLC, parent franchisee Tessaline Group, registered contact for West Town real estate and licensing. He searched the name against the zone office's public filings. Eleven zone recategorization filings in fourteen months. All eleven in corridors where Tessaline locations had subsequently opened. Eight of the eleven had resulted in a business closure within ninety days.

He sent a query on the crew channel: Need Tessaline's West Town franchise coordinator. Name and location.

Forty seconds. Risa came back, complete sentences as always: His name is Garrison Mercer. He's at the Damen location right now — came in at nine, his car's been in the lot since eight forty-seven.

A second message, different hand: THE FRANCHISE GUY HAS A PATTERNED UMBRELLA. SUSPICIOUS.

Kyle replied to both: Good work.

He tucked his neuretics to ambient. Three blocks north.

---

Tessaline Damen Authentic had a window banner reading WHAT'S AUTHENTIC? FIND OUT. in the rounded-corner font that cost franchise consultants a great deal of money to design because it was supposed to feel like it had always existed. The interior was beige and managed. Laminated surface graphics of harvest imagery on the tables — wheat and corn, aspirational in a zone where neither had grown in two hundred years. The equipment behind the counter was new and very clean in the way of equipment that was cleaned on schedule rather than cleaned when it needed it, which was its own kind of cleanliness.

Garrison Mercer was in the back booth with a tablet and a branded cup and the expression of a man who believed his authority was the primary fact of any room he entered. Mid-thirties. Brand polo with the Tessaline sigil at the chest. Hair combed to convey reliability. He looked up when Kyle sat down across from him with the studied patience of someone accustomed to fielding interruptions he considered beneath him.

"This booth is—"

"Garrison Mercer. Franchise coordinator, West Town licensing and real estate." Kyle set the folder on the table. "I'm here about the Wentworth Passage compliance hold."

Mercer looked at the folder. He was reading it without opening it, which meant he was reading the situation — calculating what Kyle was, whether he was an attorney, whether he was city, whether he had something real or only thought he did. He had done this calculation often enough that he did it without appearing to. "That's a zone authority matter. If you have a concern about the filing you can direct it to—"

"I have the petition." Kyle opened to the first tab. "Hanna Borg-Okafor, 1140 West Damen Unit 3. That unit doesn't exist — the building has no Unit 3, no record of one in twenty-eight years of registration history." He laid the printout down. "Denis Priyatharan, ID prefix 09, which is a minor ward classification. No guardian signature on the complaint. Under Section 12.4 of the zone's citizen portal terms, that signature is mandatory for a valid submission. The complaint is null on its face." He put that printout beside the first. "Sung-Ji Maduka, 412 Fulton Corridor. Condemned in 2221. Demolished to forty percent of original structure. No registered occupants since the condemnation." The third printout. "Three complainants. None of them real."

He let that sit for a moment.

Mercer's branded cup was between them, starting to go cold.

"The inspector's report cites 14.7.c." Second tab. "That code applies to enclosed kitchen spaces with a permanent water connection and a minimum two hundred square feet. Mrs. Chen's stall is thirty-two square feet, open-air, mobile-classified. 14.7.c is inapplicable on square footage alone, before we get to the water connection or the open-air classification." He put that printout down. "The inspector is registered in Zone 9, not West Town. She has no licensing authority in this corridor. All four of her previous West Town inspections are in properties that were subsequently reclassified — all under Tessaline holds."

He turned to the last tab and set the timestamp comparison down.

"The zone recategorization was filed at fourteen thirty-two on the fourth. The petition that supposedly triggered it was filed at nineteen-oh-seven, same day. Your office filed the hold four and a half hours before the complaints that justified it existed."

He closed the folder.

Mercer's expression had gone neutral — carefully managed, which was its own information. The authority had come off and the caution had come on, and he was now calculating a different set of numbers. "I'm not sure what you're suggesting." His voice was measured. "Filing timelines can involve—"

"I'm not suggesting anything." Kyle put both hands flat on the table. "I'm describing what's in this folder. What I'm suggesting is this: I have a formatted copy, ready to go, for Tessaline Group's own corporate audit office and for the zone compliance review board's independent complaint desk. Simultaneously. If the hold on Wentworth Passage is lifted by end of business today, that copy stays in a drawer." He stood up. "If it isn't, it goes out first thing tomorrow and I follow up in person with both offices."

Mercer looked at the folder for a long time. Kyle was comfortable with silences. He had been in rooms with silences that had blades in them, and he had not filled those either. This one was just a man running arithmetic.

"The stall is operating without current inspection—"

"Current through June 2227," Kyle said. "I checked this morning. Page six of the folder."

Mercer's jaw made a small, controlled adjustment.

"I'll need to make some calls," he said.

"That's fine." Kyle picked up the folder. "End of business."

"Leave that," Mercer said.

"I have the copy." Kyle tucked the folder under his arm. "This one's mine."

He walked out into the corridor. The pattern umbrella was in a stand by the door — geometric print, teal and navy, perfectly serviceable. He left it where it was. The afternoon was gray and had the specific quality of a city holding its breath between weather systems, the air pressure just slightly wrong, the kind of afternoon that preceded nothing in particular.

He was hungry. He stopped at a corner stall two blocks over and ate rice and pickled cabbage standing up. Three Φ, generous brine, no ceremony. His stomach logged it and stopped filing complaints.

He walked home.

---

The flour stacks were back by fourteen hundred.

Kyle passed on his way back from dropping a document at a zone archive box that didn't strictly need dropping until the week's end, and saw them: two white sacks against Mrs. Chen's back wall, the prep bowl right-side up on its shelf, the awning post bare. The compliance notice was gone. She was moving stock from the supply cart — the one with the wheel that had never been oiled and served as advance notice for her regulars. It meant: she was setting up. It meant: this was over.

He sat at the counter.

She brought him a bowl without asking what he wanted. There was an egg cracked over it, sitting neat on top of the broth — not stirred in, not submerged. Just there. Present. The egg was a statement requiring acknowledgment.

He paid for the bowl. Exact. He always had the count before he reached for it — not a habit he had chosen but one that had been there as long as he could remember, the count always current, the arithmetic always clean.

She refilled his tea. She looked at his hands, just briefly — the knuckles, specifically, then the wrists. He looked too. Nothing there.

"You should eat before you do things like that," she said. "Your hands."

"My hands are fine."

"You ate after," she said, with the tone of someone reporting forensic findings.

He did not ask how she knew. He picked up his chopsticks.

The broth was the same as it had been for eleven years, which was the same way a person was fundamentally the same across decades — different in the small particulars, in the daily variation of things made by hand from the same process, but the essential thing unchanged, identifiable across distance and time. He ate the egg. She stood with her cloth and looked out at the corridor, the way she sometimes did when she had more to say and was deciding whether to say it.

"Twenty-three years is a long time," she said.

He waited.

"When I opened the stall, there was a tea house on the east side of this passage. Three generations of the same family. Good tea — real tea, not concentrate, the kind you had to wait for." She wiped a spot on the counter that was already clean. "A franchise moved in across from it. Then they filed a nuisance complaint. Then a code violation. Then a supply disruption — the delivery route got reclassified, the new routing added four blocks and the minimum order requirements changed, and the tea house couldn't meet them." She folded the cloth and set it down precisely. "They closed in about fourteen months. The franchise is still there. Different franchise now — they sold the location twice — but the building is still there."

Kyle set his cup down.

"I thought about them this week," she said. "I thought about whether I should tell you about them. Whether it would change how you approached it." She looked at him. "I decided it wouldn't."

"It wouldn't have," he said.

"No." She was quiet for a moment. "But it mattered very much to me. Whether or not it changed the arithmetic."

He sat with that. The tea house: three generations, fourteen months. The corner as it was now. What it would have been.

"The hold is gone," he said.

"I know." She picked up her cloth. "Eat your bowl."

He ate his bowl. He did not ask what she had meant about his hands. She had meant something that was not his hands — some quality she had seen in him that she had no word for and was routing through his hands because she needed to route it somewhere. He had learned not to make her name things she was not ready to name. It was one of the ways you respected a person's intelligence.

He left exact change on the counter and went home.

---

The prosthetic was a left forearm, third-generation pediatric model — manufactured somewhere between 2210 and 2215, old enough to have been through at least two previous users. Pixel had it clamped in the bench vise at an angle that gave her access to the wrist actuator without stressing the elbow coupling, and she was doing the kind of careful work that required a specific quality of stillness — not tensed, not relaxed, but the focused neutrality of someone who had learned to keep themselves out of the instrument's way.

Kyle came in at seventeen-twenty. She said, without looking up: "You didn't eat this morning."

"I ate at noon."

"Before noon." She made a small adjustment. "You do better when you eat before. The Atlas burns ahead of schedule when your blood sugar's down — you can't feel it but I can see it in the calibration variance. Your response time degrades about four percent." She moved to the next node. "It's not nothing."

He pulled out the second workbench stool and sat down. He watched her hands. The actuator housing had a hairline crack along one edge, repaired with putty before she'd begun — the putty seal was clean and even, careful work done earlier and left to cure. The sensor mesh on the wrist display was reading partial errors, and she was correcting them one node at a time rather than doing a sweep. He understood why: a full sweep would reset the calibration to factory defaults. She was preserving what was already there.

"Whose is it," he said.

"Family in the gray zone. Father took a contracted repair shift at a Zone 4 depot — chain caught." She held the probe still over a node. "She's nine. They came to me because the AxisMed referral queue is four months and she's growing. The calibration window closes."

"What are you charging them."

"Materials and half my rate." She said it as information, the way she said most things — not a declaration, not a flag, just a fact being placed in the room. "They're paying in installments."

He looked at the arm. The actuator hardware was good — well-maintained across its previous owners, the load-bearing components worn but not degraded, the micro-seals intact. Someone had cared for it. Probably more than one someone.

"The node mesh," he said. "The partials."

"Water exposure somewhere in the history. I'm burning around the dead nodes and rerouting." She held the probe still. "It'll read eighty-seven percent mesh integrity. Full range of motion. The proprioceptive response will be slightly off a clean unit, but she'll adapt faster than she would to a clean unit at her age — kids close the proprioceptive gap in about six weeks." She set the probe down and stretched her fingers, one at a time, the way she did when she'd been holding position for too long. "The calibration history from the previous users is in there too. I'm preserving what I can. Most of it she won't feel. But some of it'll come through as — muscle memory the arm already has. Good habits in the wrist hinge. She won't know where they came from."

Kyle thought about that. Two or three previous users, their patterns still in the hardware, passed forward to a nine-year-old who would never know she was inheriting them. Pixel threading the new owner through the palimpsest of the old ones.

"You're good at this," he said.

She looked at him. The look occupied the specific territory between modest and dismissive — the place where true things lived. "I know," she said. She picked up the probe. "There's rice on the counter. Left side."

He found it — sealed container, still warm. She had timed it. He ate standing at the counter because there was no clear surface anywhere else that wasn't covered in something that couldn't be moved.

"The hold's gone," he said. "Chen's stall."

"I saw." She adjusted the probe angle. "No trouble?"

"No trouble."

She was quiet. He watched her hands. After a moment: "The franchise coordinator. Mercer."

"Garrison Mercer."

"Eleven recategorizations in fourteen months. All Tessaline corridors." She said it casually, in the register of idle data retrieval, which was not what it was. She had run the same search he had. She had run it within hours of the hold being filed, because she had been curious and because that was where her curiosity went when she needed somewhere to put it, and she had not said anything to Mrs. Chen who hadn't asked her, and she had not said anything to Kyle who had the folder. She had just run it, and filed it, and waited.

"I sent him the folder," Kyle said.

"I know." She held a node. "The hold came off at sixteen-forty."

"How do you know the time."

She looked up. "Zone authority posts real-time compliance updates. I have an alert on the parcel."

Kyle considered this. "You have an alert on her parcel number."

"I put it in last year. After the Fulton corridor thing." She went back to the probe. "There was a run of coordinated recategorizations in the Fulton commercial stretch — six in eight months, all the same inspector, two closed. I set up alerts on a dozen parcels in corridors that matched the profile. Chen's was on the list."

He did not know about a Fulton corridor thing. He thought about the alert: a notation in a system she was already running, a year of running in the background, not mentioned to anyone, just operating. She had decided that Mrs. Chen's parcel was the kind of information it was useful to have, and she had been quietly maintaining that decision for a year.

"Thank you," he said.

She looked at him. "I'm not doing it for you."

"I know."

She went back to the node. He finished the rice and put the container in the cleaning rack — the established protocol, arrived at over some period of time that neither of them had tracked consciously but that had resulted in protocols nonetheless. He was reaching for his jacket when she said, without looking up:

"Your door hinge."

He stopped.

"It's dragging on the open. Not the close — on the open, which means the pin is wearing asymmetrically. Started about four months ago, I think. Maybe three and a half." She made a small adjustment. "You tend to leave between five-forty and six-twenty in the mornings. Come back between twenty-one hundred and midnight on standard days, earlier on contract days with short briefs, later when you're doing prep." She moved the probe to the next node. "The late-afternoon return is unusual. You don't do that often."

Kyle stood with his jacket in his hands.

The hinge had been dragging. He had noted it somewhere below the threshold of things that needed attention — functional, acceptable, not urgent. He had not mentioned it to anyone. He had not mentioned his hours to anyone. He had certainly not tallied them aloud to Pixel or to anyone else. He had a rough awareness of his own rhythms but he had not converted them to specific windows, had not tracked them as data.

She had been there. In the apartment across the hall, working, and the hinge had been there too — dragging on the open, not the close, once in the morning and once at night, and she had been listening. Not deliberately. Or not only deliberately. Just present, and paying attention, and the hinge talking in its small specific frequency, and her hearing it.

"I can fix it," she said. "Five minutes, if you pull the pin."

"I'll get to it," he said.

She made a small sound that meant: you will not get to it, and we both know this, and that's fine.

He stood in her doorway for a moment. The prosthetic arm was in the vise, the node work two-thirds done, the wrist hinge holding its alignment. The repair putty on the crack had cured fully. The arm was not new and would not read as new, but it would hold, and the girl would adapt to it, and in six weeks she would not remember it as a foreign thing.

"The arm is good work," he said.

"I know," she said again, in the same register — not modest, not performed, just accurate.

He went across the hall. His door dragged on the open, and now that she had named it he could hear the specific quality of the drag: the catch and release, the asymmetric wear in the pin, the way it had been talking to her across four months of mornings without asking permission and without expecting anything back. He stood in his apartment with his jacket still on and listened to it.

He thought about the tea house — three generations, fourteen months, a corner that was now a second-generation franchise doing nothing interesting. He thought about Mrs. Chen's hands on the counter cloth and the extra egg and the way she had said it — twenty-three years — as if reminding herself, as if the reminder was the useful thing, as if saying it aloud made it more certainly true. He thought about Pixel's hands on the prosthetic node, patient and exact, preserving what was already there. He thought about the parcel alert running in the background for a year, silent, no credit taken.

He took his jacket off and hung it by the door.

Cacophony on the side table — he checked the chamber out of habit. Five rounds, where they had always been. He moved to the kitchen and put the kettle on. He had not had enough tea and the evening was still going.

The hinge was quiet when the door was closed.

From across the hall he could hear the faint sound of Pixel working — the probe tool, the soft pulse of calibration software, the occasional small sound of her adjusting the lamp. The nine-year-old would have her arm by the end of the week. The proprioceptive gap would close in six weeks, and she would stop noticing the arm as a separate thing, and it would simply become part of how she moved through the world, carrying forward some trace of the people who had owned it before her and who had left their habits in the wrist hinge without knowing they were doing it.

He poured his tea. He carried it to the window, where the corridor below was doing its evening business — a shift change from the AxisMed supply depot, Boost's crew positioned at the corner in an arrangement they believed was subtle but that he could identify at two hundred meters, a maintenance mech moving in smooth silence where the squeaky one had been earlier. Someone had oiled the wheel, or the mech had oiled itself, or the maintenance had happened the way maintenance sometimes happened in this city: somebody paying attention, quietly, not filing a report about it.

The folder was on the side table. Three names that were not people, a building code that did not apply, a timestamp that proved the fraud was planned before its justification existed. He had spent an afternoon on it. He would file the copy in his case archive at the weekend and forget about it after that. Mercer would find easier corridors. He would probably find them without much difficulty. That was not Kyle's problem.

Mrs. Chen's stall was on Wentworth Passage. It had been there for twenty-three years. He had been coming to it for eleven.

He drank his tea until it was gone.

The Atlas array was quiet — he could feel the absence of the red-flash edge, the overclocking pulled back, the processing down to maintenance level. A day that had not cost him much. The arithmetic had been clean. The weapons had stayed where they were.

He set the cup in the cleaning rack.

He went to bed at an hour that was earlier than usual, because he had eaten, and because the folder was filed, and because there was no arithmetic pending that required him to stay awake and run it.

In the morning he would leave between five-forty and six-twenty.

The hinge would drag on the open.

Across the hall, someone would hear it, and would know he was on his way, and would not mention it, and would have the rice warm by the time he came home.

[Beat 86]
He stayed twenty minutes past the point when leaving would have been easy. He couldn't have said when the window closed — somewhere in the third cup of tea, the rain working its way from useful topic to background fact. There was a bowl in the drying rack that hadn't been there when he arrived. That was the only evidence of time.

When he finally got up, she was already at the table again, her hands around the next problem, which turned out to be a coupling splice for a secondary thruster array that she was attempting by feel and sheer accumulated will. She didn't say anything about the hour. She didn't say anything about the bowl.

"Same time Thursday," he said, at the door.

She looked up. The lamp was behind her, which made it hard to read the rest of the expression once the first part — surprise, then not-surprise — had moved through.

"Same time Thursday," she said, and went back to the coupling.

He took the stairs rather than the freight drop. He didn't know why. The city below had no opinion on this, which was the city's position on most things, but he noticed it anyway — that the city said nothing, that the air at street level smelled like rain and ozone and the noodle cart that Mrs. Chen said was a civic blight and probably a criminal enterprise, and that all of this was fine.

He was already thinking about Thursday.

[Beat 87]
The contract came in at 07:14 on a Tuesday in the middle of October, the city still running its autumn routine of cold mornings and warmer afternoons and the smell of the lake cutting through everything the ventilation systems tried to do about it. Kyle was at his kitchen table eating cold rice from the pot because he hadn't cleaned a bowl since Sunday.

The posting format was the one he'd seen before.

He had a filing system in his head, not metaphorical — a corner of the Atlas array that catalogued work the same way a ledger logs receivables: source, format, field arrangement. Sixteen different fixers ran contracts through Sable's board. Seven of those had recognizable templates. One of them Kyle had never had a name for. It didn't announce itself. It used no fixer. It posted directly to his work queue with a contract number, a task block, a rate, and a field labeled ADDENDUM where the unusual conditions lived.

He recognized the format before he'd read the task.

CONTRACT NO. 0047-C-WEST

He read it twice.

TASK: HOLD NORTH SPAULDING CAROUSEL PARK, 12 N. SPAULDING AVE, AGAINST COPPER SALVAGE CREW ARRIVING WITH WRIT NO. CR-4419-W. CREW EXPECTED BETWEEN 09:00 AND 11:00 TODAY. HOLD UNTIL WRIT IS REDIRECTED, VOIDED, OR CREW VACATES VOLUNTARILY.

DURATION: NOT TO EXCEED SIX HOURS.

RATE: STANDARD.

ADDITIONAL TERMS: CAROUSEL LIGHTS OPERATIONAL AT TIME OF CREW DEPARTURE. NON-NEGOTIABLE. CONDUIT ACCESS BELOW HATCH B-7 TO REMAIN UNDISTURBED UNTIL END OF CONTRACT WINDOW.
NOTE: HATCH B-7 PROVIDES ACCESS TO LEGACY SIGNAL INFRASTRUCTURE. CLIENT'S INTEREST. NO FURTHER DETAIL ON CONTRACT RECORD.
NOTE: ONE ADDITIONAL PARTY HAS BEEN ASSESSED IN RELATION TO THIS SITE. PARTY: WAR DOG. ASSESSMENT OUTCOME: EXCLUDED — TASK PARAMETERS DO NOT PERMIT EXTERNAL COORDINATION.

ADDENDUM: AMENDMENT TO FOLLOW. PROCEED ON INITIAL TERMS PENDING RECEIPT.

He ate three more bites of rice. The rate was standard. The entity always paid standard rate, early, exact — it had never tried to low-ball him, never attempted to structure a tip as partial payment, never asked for a receipt. He had forty-six completed contracts in the queue from this source. Contract number 0047 meant forty-seven.

He filed an acceptance in the work queue at 07:19. Neuretics, sub-vocal: ACCEPTED. PROCEEDING ON INITIAL TERMS. Kyle didn't put his name on it. The entity never asked for his name.

He ate the rest of the rice standing at the sink, rinsed the pot, and messaged Pixel.

---

She arrived at the carousel park at 08:31, tool satchel over one shoulder, cup of something warm in her other hand, wearing the jacket she wore when she expected to kneel on concrete. Kyle was already there. He'd been there since 08:00, walking the perimeter.

North Spaulding Carousel Park was one of those stretches of GLMZ that had been important once and had arrived at the present without quite understanding what had happened to it. The carousel itself was a genuine antique — a 2187 restoration of an earlier structure, twelve horses, working calliope mechanism, painted panels showing pastoral scenes of a midwest that had not existed for a century and a half. The paint was still mostly intact. The lights in the canopy ran on a copper base conduit system that someone had designed with professional competence and then nobody had maintained for twenty years, because the park district's maintenance budget had been redirected in 2208 and never redirected back.

The carousel was not running. The lights were on, faintly, drawing from whatever residual charge the conduit could manage, which was not much. Three bulbs in the canopy were out entirely.

Pixel looked at it the way a diagnostician looks at a patient's chart.

"They want the conduit," she said.

"The copper. It's a salvage writ. They're claiming the copper in the base circuit on grounds the park district hasn't paid a maintenance assessment since 2214."

"Have they?"

"No."

She drank whatever was in her cup. "Where's hatch B-7?"

He showed her on the park's maintenance diagram, which he'd pulled from the city infrastructure database at 07:45. She studied it for thirty seconds, set her cup on the carousel's running board, and opened her tool satchel.

"There's a secondary line," she said. "City ran it in 2218 as part of the West Town grid stabilization project. Anybody with the infrastructure database can see it — it's on the public record. The salvage writ covers the original copper conduit. It doesn't mention the secondary."

"Because the secondary is newer."

"Because the secondary is newer, and because whoever wrote the writ did their homework in 2214 and stopped." She was already moving toward the access panel on the carousel's base. "The lighting circuit runs through the original conduit. I need to reroute it to the secondary before the crew gets here, and I need to document the reroute so we have paper showing the lights are running on a circuit their writ doesn't cover."

"Can you do that before nine?"

She checked the time. It was 08:43.

"Probably," she said, which from Pixel meant yes.

She worked fast and without commentary except when she found something that affronted her professional sensibility. The conduit housing affronted her professional sensibility approximately every four minutes. The original installer had used a junction splice that she described as "criminal," then revised to "negligent," then revised to "almost certainly the work of someone who learned conduit work from a video." The grounding setup was wrong in two places. Someone had used the wrong gauge wire on the panel feed and had apparently hoped nobody would notice, which nobody had, for thirty years, which Pixel seemed to find personally offensive.

"Whoever ran this deserved to lose their license," she said, from inside the base housing. "If they had one."

"The lights are on," Kyle said.

"The lights are on because the circuit is too stubborn to know it should have failed." She emerged with a diagnostic scanner in one hand and a length of old copper in the other, which she set carefully on the ground beside her tool satchel. "Okay. Secondary reroute in place. The carousel lights are now drawing from the 2218 grid stabilization line. I'm documenting the circuit state now. You'll want a timestamp on this."

"Documented."

She pulled a printed circuit schematic from the back of her satchel — she'd prepared it before coming, which meant she'd run the infrastructure database query herself at some point this morning without being asked — and annotated it with three marks in red stylus. "This shows the reroute. This shows the writ's coverage boundary. This shows the secondary is outside it. Give them this and they either go home or they're acting without authorization."

She handed it to him. He looked at it. Her handwriting was small and exact.

"How much," he said.

"Standard consulting rate. I'll bill you."

She would bill him exactly. She always did. He filed the amount against the job's accounting and she picked up her cup from the carousel's running board and leaned against the fence to watch the lights.

They were properly on now. All twelve canopy positions lit. The carousel sat still in the October morning light, painted horses mid-stride, the calliope silent.

"The amendment," Kyle said.

He'd received it at 08:51, while Pixel was working. He read it again.

AMENDMENT TO CONTRACT NO. 0047-C-WEST: UPON COMPLETION OF MAIN TASK, PROCEED TO HATCH B-7. ACCESS CONDUIT SPACE BELOW. LOCATE HAND-SPLICED SIGNAL LINE, INSTALLED APPROXIMATELY 2215. TERMINUS BOX, FOUNDATION-MOUNTED, NORTHWEST CORNER. GREEN INDICATOR. YOU WILL RECOGNIZE IT. TERMINATE AT NEAREST JUNCTION. NO RECORD.

ADDENDUM: AMENDMENT: NO. PROCEED ANYWAY.

He'd read it four times. A contract addendum that contradicted itself. "AMENDMENT: NO." Then the instruction to proceed anyway. The entity had never sent contradictory field entries in forty-six completed contracts.

He didn't tell Pixel about that part.

---

The reclamation crew arrived at 09:17. A van with a DominionReach logo on the side, which was not the salvage company but the transport company the salvage company had hired, which told Kyle something about the salvage company's capitalization. Three men: the one in front carrying a printed writ and the particular expression of a person who has been given a legal document and told to believe it makes them invincible, and two behind him with a portable conduit cutter and a legal dolly for moving copper. Their expressions were professional but not aggressive. They were not criminals. They were day laborers with a job.

"Park's closed," said Kyle.

"Park's not closed," said the front man. He had the writ in hand. "We have a writ of salvage, copper conduit, base circuit, issued under the District Infrastructure Reclamation Act. We're here to remove the copper from the primary base circuit."

"The primary base circuit is there," Kyle said, and pointed. "The carousel lights are running on the secondary grid stabilization line, installed 2218. Your writ covers the primary. The secondary isn't in it."

The front man looked at him. He looked at the carousel. He looked at the lights.

"The lights are running," he said.

"Yes."

"They run off the primary conduit."

"They did," Kyle said. "This morning the circuit was rerouted to the secondary. I have documentation." He offered the schematic Pixel had prepared. The man took it. He stared at it. He handed it to the man behind him, who stared at it differently — the way someone stared at something they almost understood.

"You can't just reroute a municipal circuit," the front man said.

"You can if the municipality has outstanding maintenance assessments and the secondary line is in the public grid record and the reroute is documented and timestamped." Kyle had verified all of this before 09:00. "The city infrastructure database shows the secondary line. Your writ was filed in 2214 against the original copper. The secondary didn't exist yet in 2214."

There was a pause.

"We're here for copper," the front man said. He had reached the point in the conversation that some people reached where the facts weren't moving the situation and the situation needed to move. "The copper in the base circuit. The writ covers the base circuit copper."

"It covers the original installation. The original copper is still there — you can take it. The circuit runs on the secondary now. Taking the original copper doesn't affect the lights."

This was technically accurate. Pixel had been very precise about this. The original copper conduit was still in the base housing; she'd rerouted the lighting circuit off it, not removed the copper itself. If the crew wanted to pull the original copper, the lights would stay on.

The front man was not happy about this. He conferred with the man behind him. Kyle waited. The October wind moved through the carousel park and the horses didn't move with it. One of the men in the back started toward the base housing with the conduit cutter.

"That secondary junction box," he said, pointing at the gray metal housing on the east retaining wall. "We cut that, the whole circuit reroutes back to the primary."

He was right. Pixel had not put that in the documentation.

Kyle looked at the junction box. It was mounted six feet up on the east wall, installed 2218, last serviced never. It contained three circuit relays and the connection to the secondary line. If they cut it, the secondary line would fall back to the primary conduit and the salvage writ would cover everything the lights were drawing from.

He drew Cacophony.

It was the first time in West Town this year he'd drawn a weapon in a professional context that wasn't a demonstration. The gun was familiar in his hand the way his own pulse was familiar — background data, always present, never the point of the story until it was. He didn't aim at anyone. He aimed at the junction box.

"That box is city property," he said. "So is the secondary line. Your writ doesn't cover either. If you cut that junction box, you're damaging city property outside your writ authority, and I'm a witness."

"You've got a gun pointed at a junction box," the front man said.

"I do."

"That's —"

"I'm going to fire twice," Kyle said. "Not at you. Into the box itself. The box is dead machinery — no live circuits above the relay, per the infrastructure record. I'm doing this because the noise is useful."

He fired.

The first shot took the relay housing dead center and the sound of it in the enclosed park was enormous, a concussive crack that sent two pigeons off the far fence and caused all three members of the reclamation crew to step back in unison. The junction box did not explode. It made the specific metallic sound of a thing that had been breached and would not function again.

He fired again.

Three rounds remained.

The second shot went into the conduit housing below the relay, the termination point where the secondary line connected. The conduit housing shattered. There was now nothing there to cut because there was no longer a junction in any recognizable sense. The secondary line ran from the city grid to the base circuit directly, without the junction box as intermediary, which was a less elegant solution but equally functional and considerably harder to interfere with using a portable cutter.

The carousel lights stayed on.

Kyle held Cacophony at his side, not holstered, not raised.

"The secondary line's in the wall," he said. "The writ doesn't cover the wall. You want the original copper, it's in the base housing. You can have it. But you're not cutting to the lights."

The three men stood in the October morning looking at the demolished junction box and the carousel lights burning clear and steady above them.

The front man folded the writ. He put it in his jacket pocket. He looked at Kyle for a long moment with the expression of a man recalculating what a day was going to cost him.

"DominionReach is going to want a damage report," he said.

"File it," Kyle said. "The address is on the writ."

They left. The van pulled out of the access lane and turned north, back toward wherever day-labor salvage crews went when a job stopped making economic sense.

Kyle holstered Cacophony and stood in the quiet for a moment, listening to the city.

Pixel was sitting on the carousel's running board, cup in hand, watching him.

"You shot a junction box," she said.

"Yes."

"On purpose."

"It seemed efficient."

She considered this. "It was efficient," she allowed. "The circuit runs cleaner without it, actually — the relay was running a 12% resistance load on the secondary draw."

"I didn't know that."

"I did. I was going to recommend removing it anyway." She drank. "You could have just told me to pull it."

"I needed the noise."

She nodded slowly. "How many rounds left."

"Three."

She nodded again, not impressed and not unimpressed, just noting the accounting. She had that quality: she tracked the ledger without making it a commentary on anything. Kyle appreciated it in the same way he appreciated a level that read level and kept quiet about it.

"I need to get into hatch B-7," he said.

"The one the contract said not to touch until the crew left."

"Yes."

"What's in it."

"I don't know yet."

She finished her coffee, stood, and handed him a flat-head driver from her tool satchel. "B-7 locks are compression latches. Counter-clockwise."

He took the driver. She did not follow him.

---

The hatch was in the park's northwest corner, set flush with the ground, concrete surround with a green painted stripe that had faded to the color of old moss. Kyle knelt beside it. Counter-clockwise, two turns, then lift. The hatch came up on a hydraulic that had last been serviced in 2209 and still worked, which was not the strangest thing about the morning.

Below: a maintenance crawl space, half a meter high, running under the carousel's foundation. He put his phone light on and lay flat on the concrete lip and looked.

The space was not empty.

The original copper conduit ran along the left wall, just as the infrastructure record showed. There were two water shutoffs, one functional and one frozen with rust. There was an access panel for the original base circuit, now depowered by Pixel's reroute. And there was something else.

A cable. Hand-spliced. Running from the city data line access point in the northwest corner of the crawl space to a terminus box, about the size of a hardback book, mounted to the carousel's foundation. The splice was old work — hand-soldered, taped, done by someone who knew what they were doing with the specific economy of a person working alone in a small space without room for power tools. The installation date stamped on the terminus box read 2215.

Eleven years old.

Kyle lay there on the concrete lip and looked at it for a moment.

The entity's first address. The amendment had said: you will recognize it.

He hadn't recognized it precisely — he didn't know what it was connected to, didn't know what signal it had been running or what it had been used for. But he recognized the quality of the work. Careful. Deliberate. Done without documentation because the documentation would have made it visible, and the point was that it would not be visible.

Someone had run a physical signal line in 2215 and maintained it for eleven years by simply not touching it and not filing anything that would draw reclamation attention. The copper salvage writ — filed in 2214, one year before the installation — had covered the original conduit. Not the signal cable. If the original copper had remained on the primary circuit, eventually someone would have noticed the cable and asked what it connected to. Pixel's reroute had eliminated that risk entirely, removing the copper's salvage value and the crew's reason to be in the base housing.

The entity had kept a physical line here for eleven years for what the contract called "sentimental reasons."

Kyle re-read the amendment on his neuretics queue.

TERMINATE LINE AT NEAREST JUNCTION. NO RECORD.

ADDENDUM: AMENDMENT: NO. PROCEED ANYWAY.

He lay flat in the maintenance crawl space under a carousel in West Town in October 2226 and read a self-contradicting field entry from an employer he'd never met or spoken to, staring at a signal line installed the same year he'd accepted his first contract from that employer.

The arithmetic was obvious. He ran it anyway.

He pulled himself forward on his elbows, into the crawl space. The ceiling was six inches above his head. He moved to the terminus box, found the junction, and cut the cable.

He did it with the flat-head driver, not a cutting tool — he levered the junction cap open and separated the splice manually, clean, no frayed ends. The line went dead in his hands. The terminus box's indicator light, which he hadn't noticed until it went out, had been green, barely visible in the dim.

He pushed himself back out and came up from the hatch and stood in the carousel park.

The carousel was running.

Not playing music — the calliope wasn't active — but the mechanism had started turning. Slow rotation, the painted horses coming around in the October light, heads down in permanent mid-gallop, the canopy lights bright and steady now that Pixel's reroute had them on clean draw. Then, from some residual charge somewhere in the base circuit, the calliope started. Not a full chord. Four bars of whatever it had been playing last — something Kyle didn't know the name of, a minor-key waltz, the pipe organ sound of it rising into the cold air, and then stopping.

Four bars.

Then the carousel went dark. Not gradually. The lights cut at once and the mechanism wound down over about eight seconds and the horses stopped at a position slightly different from where they'd started, and there was silence.

Pixel was standing ten feet away, watching. She didn't say anything.

Kyle looked at the carousel for a moment. He filed the job completion in his neuretics queue.

Then he filed the tip.

The entity's contract format had a reply field. He'd used it before for completion confirmations, once for a question about a follow-on task. He'd never added a line item that wasn't in the original contract. The format didn't have a field for it. He typed it into the reply field as a freeform entry:

MORALE: 0.5Φ.

He didn't know if that made sense as a line item. He filed it anyway, and stood in the quiet while the October light came through the fence slats and the horses stood still.

---

He walked back to the Pivot for lunch. Pixel walked with him as far as Walton, where she turned south toward a calibration job she'd mentioned that morning. She didn't say anything about the carousel or the tip.

She said: "Eat something. You're running on cold rice and whatever you were burning through that amp draw."

"I'll eat," he said.

"You'll eat cold rice again," she said. "That's not eating. That's maintenance."

He didn't have an answer for that that wasn't an admission, so he walked north.

Mrs. Chen's counter was on the ground floor of a building on Huron that had been a cooperative market in the 2180s and then a series of progressively smaller businesses until Mrs. Chen had taken the last unit from the block's owner, a man named Farida Osei who had tried three times in four years to raise the rent and been talked out of it every time, which was a negotiation Kyle would have paid to watch.

The noodle bowl was already in front of him before he'd sat down.

"You look like you've been on the ground," she said.

He had been on the ground. There was concrete dust on his jacket where he'd lain in the crawl space.

"Job," he said.

"Your hands are dirty," she said. Not a complaint. A diagnostic. She put a tea on the counter beside the bowl without being asked. The bowl was full today — the extra ounce of broth she added when she thought he needed it, which she never acknowledged adding and he never acknowledged receiving.

He paid for the bowl, exact, and drank the tea, and ate.

He was halfway through when the reply came in.

The entity's contract format used all-caps field entries. Forty-six completed contracts. He knew the format the way he knew Cacophony's weight — not by thinking about it, just by the shape of the thing in his hands. It had fields: CONTRACT NUMBER, STATUS, PAYMENT, NOTES. The notes field was where the entity put its nearest approach to personality: GRATUITY FOR EXPEDIENT RESOLUTION, once. MORALE NOTED, after a particularly difficult job on South Clark. EXCELLENT FIELDWORK RE: THE JUNCTION QUESTION, which Kyle had kept filed and never been sure what to do with.

He opened the reply.

received. filed under: firsts.

Three words. Lowercase. No field headers. No contract number. No structure.

He read it three times.

In eleven years of formatted correspondence, every 'e' identical, the entity had never sent a freeform message. Every previous communication had been a contract artifact — posting, addendum, gratuity, receipt. Kyle had not known the entity was capable of sending something that was not a filing.

Mrs. Chen was watching him from the other side of the counter the way she watched everything — fully, without performing the watching.

"Something strange?" she said.

"Just a reply," he said.

"From who."

"Someone I work for."

She refilled the tea. He drank it. He read the message once more.

received. filed under: firsts.

Not a contract. Not a field entry. Something a person might write, the morning after something had changed. He thought about the green indicator light going out in the crawl space, the four bars of the minor-key waltz, the carousel going dark.

He did not reply.

He ate the rest of the bowl, exact change on the counter, and walked out into the West Town afternoon.

---

The woman in the tan coat had been at the carousel park's east perimeter when the reclamation crew left. He remembered it now, going over the morning as he walked. He'd clocked her when the van was pulling out — not watching the van, not watching Kyle, not watching the carousel in the way someone watched a carousel. She'd been watching the maintenance hatch. The one with the green-striped concrete surround.

She'd seen him notice her. And she'd walked away. Not quickly. Not slowly. Just away, with the particular pace of someone who wanted to convey that they had somewhere else to be and there was no connection between the two facts.

Kyle ran the image on the Atlas array's memory recall, which was precise to the point of inconvenience: the woman, mid-thirties, average height, tan coat, standing at the perimeter fence at a slight angle. Body oriented toward the hatch, not the carousel. Present after the first shot — he was certain of that, because the shots had pulled everyone's attention and she hadn't reacted. She'd been there. Watching for the hatch to open.

He didn't know who she was. He filed the image in the Atlas array's working memory and let it sit there. Not a conclusion. A data point without context.

---

Vey's shop was on Milwaukee, near the intersection with Damen, in a building that had been a hardware store and before that a pharmacy and before that something with tile floors and arched windows that nobody currently living had enough historical context to identify. The sign above the door read ANTIQUITY & STATIONARY. The second word was, as Casper Vey occasionally explained to people who pointed it out, intentional. He had written it down for Kyle once on a slip of paper: "the past will not move." He had then put the slip of paper in a file and refused to discuss the file's purpose.

The shop smelled of old circuits and dry paper and the particular olfactory signature of electrical equipment manufactured before the switch to polymer substrates in the 2160s — something metallic, something resinous, a bass note of aged solder. The shelves held first-generation neural rigs in their original housing, dead handsets in various states of disassembly, early autonomous survey drones, pre-collapse server blades, and a number of objects whose identity was not immediately apparent and whose file cards read only PROVENANCE: CONFIRMED.

Vey was at the back counter, cataloguing. He looked up when Kyle came in and waited.

"The salvage crew this morning," Kyle said. "I want to trace the writ funding."

"That would require my contacts in the reclamation registry, a document trace through at least two shell entities, and a not-insignificant amount of my time."

"I know."

"You want to know what funded the writ."

"And who filed the program it connects to."

Vey set down his cataloguing tool. He looked at Kyle over the tops of his glasses — he wore actual glasses, not for vision correction, which was trivial in 2226, but because they gave him a surface to look over at people. "You already have a hypothesis."

"I'd like a document chain."

Vey took the job. Kyle paid, standard rate, and left the trace in his hands.

He had the results by evening.

---

He was at the kitchen table again, the same pot from that morning, when the file came through Vey's secure relay. He read it on the Atlas array's display overlay, projected against the kitchen wall in the amber light of the single functional bulb over the sink.

The salvage writ, CR-4419-W, had been funded through a DominionReach subsidiary called Copper Reclamation West, which was a real company with a real portfolio of municipal copper reclamation contracts across three zones. That was the first layer.

Behind Copper Reclamation West: a budget allocation code. Vey had traced it to a parent program in the West Town district infrastructure accounts. The program's formal title was:

DISTRICT ACQUISITION, WEST TOWN WEST.

Established 2224. Still running. Budget: active, recurring, drawn quarterly.

The program's stated purpose, in the district account records, was: INFRASTRUCTURE SURVEY AND ASSET MANAGEMENT, WEST TOWN WEST DISTRICT. Standard language. Standard budget mechanism. The kind of entry that was designed to look like every other entry on the page and mostly succeeded.

What Vey had found in the detailed allocation records — accessed through a contact in the district assessor's office who apparently owed Vey several favors of indeterminate character — was that the program had generated eighteen separate reclamation actions over two years, all in the same ten-block radius. Every action had targeted physical infrastructure: copper conduit, data line access points, cable terminus housings, structural anchor points.

Every action in the north Spaulding district. West Town West.

Vey had flagged a pattern in the geographic distribution: each action had been within two hundred meters of a specific street corner. The same corner, each time. The corner of North Spaulding and Iowa Street. Where the carousel park sat.

Eighteen actions over two years. Someone had funded a systematic infrastructure survey of a ten-block radius, deploying eighteen separate reclamation actions, all working inward. Working toward the maintenance hatch. Working toward the crawl space and the hand-spliced cable and the terminus box with its barely visible green indicator light.

The writ this morning had been the nineteenth. The one that reached the carousel itself.

Kyle sat at the kitchen table and ran the arithmetic.

The entity's cable had been installed in 2215. The DISTRICT ACQUISITION program had started in 2224. Nine years between installation and first detection, if detection was what the program represented. Then two years of methodical approach, action by action, working toward the source address.

Someone had found the entity's thread. Had been following it in for two years. Had gotten close enough this morning that the entity had sent contract number 0047.

He filed that as a working hypothesis. He didn't have confirmation. Vey's file didn't trace the program's funding above the district account level — either Vey hadn't gone that far, or the next layer was cleaner than the shell companies. He didn't know what DISTRICT ACQUISITION was, who ran it, or what they would have done with a physical signal line in a carousel park crawl space if they'd found it before he'd cut it.

He didn't know if the woman in the tan coat was connected to the program. The calculation was possible. He didn't have more than that.

He didn't know what the entity's address had been used for. What signal it had carried. Why the entity had called it "sentimental reasons" in a contract field.

He filed all of that too. Not conclusions. Data, sitting in the Atlas array's working memory alongside the image of the tan-coat woman and the four bars of minor-key waltz and three words in lowercase that were not a filing.

Outside the kitchen window, West Town ran its Tuesday evening routine: a ventilation unit on the building across the alley cycling through its half-hour pattern, the lake smell cutting through everything else in the way it always did when the temperature dropped after dark.

Kyle poured cold rice into the pot, added water, set it on the element.

He looked at the entity's message once more in his neuretics queue.

received. filed under: firsts.

Eleven years of formatted correspondence and one sentence that wasn't a filing. He ran the arithmetic on what that meant. The conclusion it led to was not comfortable and was not something he was going to put in his work queue. He was the maintenance plan, not the map. He cut cables he was told to cut. He fired twice into a junction box and three rounds remained and the carousel ran for four bars of something nobody had named for him and then went dark, and somewhere in the city a woman in a tan coat had been watching the hatch and had walked away without hurry when he'd looked at her.

Standard rate. Filed under: firsts.

He ate the rice. He let the rest of it sit in the Atlas array's working memory — not resolved, not abandoned, just present, the way a hand-spliced cable sits in a crawl space for eleven years, doing whatever it was doing, for whatever reasons, waiting for someone to find it.

Outside, the city went about its maintenance.

The carousel park on North Spaulding sat dark and still.

Three rounds remained.

(1 rows affected)

[Beat 88]
The market stall on 21st had lost two days' inventory overnight.

The vendor — a broad-shouldered woman whose name Kyle had never learned but who recognized him well enough to give him the nod — was restocking with the specific unhurried efficiency of someone who had done this before and had made peace with doing it again. Two crates of produce, gone. One of the folding display tables, no longer a table — the legs arranged at angles you couldn't find in a catalog, the surface tilted at a precise eleven degrees and holding without support. The schism had moved on. The table remained at whatever the schism had decided it was.

"Third time," she said, without looking up.

"Same spot?"

"Same spot. Zone advisory didn't call it. They never call the small ones."

She wasn't angry. She was past angry. Angry was for things that had someone responsible. The insurance form had a checkbox. She had submitted the form twice before. The checkbox led to Schedule C, section 11(b), which covered three days of replacement inventory. She had lost two days. She would be even by tonight.

He bought something off the undamaged half of the display and kept walking.

## Chapter 5: Half a Step

[Beat 89]
He knocked at eleven, which was late even by his standards, and Pixel opened the door without the soldering iron this time — which meant she'd heard the stairs and put the work down before the knock came.

"Ribs," she said. Not a question; she'd seen him favor the left side coming up.

"Two, probably. The taser found the gap between the dorsal plates at the lat — the seam the subdermal mesh doesn't cross."

She stepped back from the door. He came in and set Silence — the flat-black katana — against the wall by the stool, quiet the way he set it down anywhere he meant to stay more than a minute. Then he sat where she pointed, without ceremony, and she put her hands on either side of the damage and felt for what the mesh had stopped and what it hadn't. Her hands were cold from the bench work. She didn't warn him, and she didn't apologize for it, because this was the understanding between them: these hands, this cold, this room, the work that needed doing.

"Not displaced," she said. "Hairline on the lower one. It'll hurt for a week and then it'll decide it's done." She taped it with the same efficiency she gave any structural problem — the question was whether it held, not whether it hurt.

He looked down at the seam where the mesh stopped, the gap that had let the taser through. "Somebody cheaped out on the coverage," he said. It came out flatter than he'd meant it; most things did, when he tried to make them light.

Pixel didn't look up from the tape. "No. They traded it for the joint. You can't lay full plate over a lat and still reach with it." She pressed the last edge down with her thumb. "It's not a hole in you. It's where they left you room to move."

On the far end of the worktable, Oziel's arm lay in its jig, half its tendons strung — a thing she was building, not fixing. She gave Kyle's ribs the same hands she'd give the arm.

When she finished she did not step back.

She stayed where she was, a hand still flat against his side. The room was the room it always was at this hour: the lamp on the bench, the smell of flux and something she'd been eating, the soft cycling of the bench coolers between jobs. And nothing happened — her hand still flat against his ribcage where the tape ended.

He started to stand. Her hand, flat against his side, stayed — not pressing, just present, the weight of it between him and standing. His heel found the floor an inch back. He stayed. She looked at the floor for one beat, and then she was done looking at it.

"Friday," she said, which was when Oziel was coming for the arm.

He was quiet for a breath. "Friday," he said.

She turned back to the bench. He took Silence — the weight of it familiar, unchanged — up from the wall, left the door the width she always left it, and crossed the four steps home through the taser burn's ongoing report.

[Beat 90]
The taser burn would be worst in the morning. He knew that from the ledger he kept in his head — the shoulder's recovery curve, the rib tape interval, the approximate duration of any given mesh-seam failure. He ran his thumb over the tape, the hairline beneath it, the line where the mesh stopped and the decision to stop it there had started.

The gap was by spec. The engineer who'd laid the mesh in had traded coverage for articulation at the lat — a trade Kyle had not been asked about. He had been sixteen. Not a client, not yet — something closer to a project on the bench, a variable that someone was still solving for, one of the decisions made before anyone thought to ask. The engineer had done clean work. The mesh had stopped two center-mass rounds and a follow-through stave. What it hadn't stopped was exactly what it was designed not to stop, which was the cost of being able to reach.

It's where they left you room to move. He turned the line over in the dark. He had spent a long time reading the gap as a thing done to him — sixteen, on the bench, a variable someone was still solving for. She had looked at the same gap and called it room. He did not know which of them was right. He thought he might want to find out, which was nearer to a plan than he usually got.

He lay back, the burn finding its schedule. He thought about her hand flat against his side where the tape ended, and the word "Friday," which was the ordinary thing she offered when the other thing got too close, and how he'd said it back without deciding to — not because he knew what it meant, but because not saying it had stopped being something he could do. Then he let it go and slept.

[Beat 91]
Some of what he was had been chosen without consultation. The blade was the last of those things - the togishi in the Narrows had graded it today and handed it back without a parentage to put on it. His thumb found the habitual check-position on the handle and stopped there, not completing the verification, not moving off it. That was where the step lived - in the space between what he knew about himself and what he'd been built to be. He sat with it until the night was late enough to be early, and then he lay down, and slept with the ribs and the half-step and the togishi's words, all three in the same drawer.

[Beat 92]
The burn woke him at six, as promised. Kyle sat at the kitchen window with his coffee and took inventory: two hairline ribs, tape holding, the pull of the lab-grown skin over the taser contact at the lat. The burn reported itself with each breath, a clean specific signal. He ran his thumb along the tape. He'd made peace with the spec years ago.

He pulled up the account on his terminal while the coffee was still hot. The Axiom relay showed the retainer deposit: exact rate, no gratuity, processed at 02:14. Eleven years and the tip had been reinstated as goodwill sometime after the job - he'd seen it for about four minutes before the line cut. Now the entry read clean: standard rate, no additional. The client was silent.

Not the usual silence, which had a texture - a held breath before the next posting. This silence was closed. The tip: he noted the four-minute window. In eleven years, the shell had never moved off stated rate. Not once. He'd taken that as professionalism - the kind of operation that didn't incentivize, just paid clean and filed clean. The tip had lasted four minutes, which was exactly long enough to mean something.

He thought about what kind of entity decides that. Then he washed the mug and went to find his jacket.

[Beat 93]
He didn't plan to stop at the carousel park. He rode past on Leavitt and his hands slowed the bike anyway.

The park stood the way it had always stood — tucked between a rezoned printshop and a wall of second-growth sumac that had come up through the old parkway concrete. The crown bulbs were dead. The animals held their suspended positions: the palomino mid-rear, the lake sturgeon banked into its turn, the rooster at the top of its leap with its beak pointed at something it would never reach. The junction hatch in the carousel's base was locked. Kyle tried the handle, which told him what he expected.

He stood there in the cold. His neuretics were logging the frequency — 18.7 Hz, type unclassified — and what was unusual was that they kept logging it. They'd normally tag and file a signal in two, three seconds. This one was returning UNCLASSIFIED on each pass, the same notation repeating in the background like a task that couldn't complete. The frequency wasn't loud. It didn't announce itself. It sat in the chest the way a sound sits that the body hears before the ears do.
