He looked at the palomino for a moment. Mid-arrival — front hooves lifted, still in the step it had been making for however long the park had been running. He tried the hatch one more time and came up with the same answer.

He got back on the bike and rode north. The notation was still running when he got home. He had to close it manually.

[Beat 94]
The relay logs went back eleven years, which was longer than Kyle had lived anywhere. He'd been reading them for two days. Not auditing - reading the shape. Every posting had arrived on the same Axiom feed. Every scope was exactly what he would have taken.

He'd sent half a ? back up the line after the Leavitt job - standard habit, the way he'd close a tab. The response had come back wrong. Not the format—the format was gone. Three words, lowercase, no fields, no contract number: received. filed under: firsts.

He read it four or five times. Not a typo. Not a system category. Something had put that word there with intent, which meant something had a folder called firsts, which meant something had been waiting to fill it. Eleven years of clean pay and not one word between them - and the first word was a filing note on his half-? closing a tab.

He put the kettle on. He opened the relay and moved the message into a new folder. He labeled the folder: *Open.* He hadn't replied. He had done something else instead.

[Beat 95]
He told Pixel about the frequency the way he told her things — adjacent, while she was busy with something else. Not the carousel specifically. Just: there was something at the park on Leavitt, and his neuretics were logging it at 18.7 Hz and couldn't classify it.

Pixel was at her bench with a prosthetic shoulder in the truing jig, making minute corrections to the rotation lock. She did not look up.

"Something's emitting in that range," she said. "Your hardware's logging it as unclassified because it doesn't know what to do with it. Seventeen to nineteen Hz — below conscious hearing, above infrasound." She set the probe down. "The body reads it as something alive. Something nearby."

She looked at him for the first time.

"Where exactly is the park?" she asked.

He gave her the cross-streets. She wrote them in the margin of her work notes, in the small mechanical handwriting she used for things she intended to find again, and went back to the shoulder.

He left when she went back to work. In the hall, the four steps between his door and hers, he thought about the filing note — *received. filed under: firsts* — and about the small mechanical handwriting in the margin, two notes in two hands, neither of them his to answer. He went inside.

[Beat 96]
The blade had no maker's mark.

Kyle had looked, early on — running a thumb along the ricasso, checking the tang, going over every millimeter of steel with the methodical attention he gave to anything he'd staked his life on. Nothing. No proof of origin. No file history. He'd acquired it at sixteen from a man who said he'd acquired it from someone else, and that man was gone now in the way that people in this city went, which was to say completely and without paperwork. The blade had come to Kyle at sixteen and had been waiting for him to be worth it ever since.

He ran the cleaning cloth along the flat in one long stroke. The oil smelled of nothing — proprietary blend, no corporate fragrance, nothing that could identify him in a room. Silence, he called it. The name fit more than he'd meant it to when he first said it out loud.

His forearm had stopped complaining two days ago, which meant Pixel had done her work right. She always did her work right. There had been a crease in the skin where the biotape sealed over the arterial nick from the carousel — not the job itself, just a ricochet edge from the machinery housing, bad luck and bad geometry — and she had put four strips down with the same precision she used for everything, each strip overlapping the last by three millimeters, and she had not said a word about the scar tissue underneath from the last time. Or the time before that. This was professionalism. He respected it.

The apartment was quiet. The Pivot's fourth floor ran quiet in the late afternoon — the transit corridor noise peaked at 07:00 and again at 18:00 and it was 16:43 now, the window between, when the building held its breath. Kyle sat cross-legged on the floor with Silence laid across his thighs and the cleaning kit open to his left and he worked, and the city outside did its city things, and none of them required his attention.

He checked the edge. The angle was 10.5 degrees, which was what he'd ground it to, which was what it needed to be. He had a leather-backed gauge he used for this — old tool, pre-collapse manufacture, the kind of thing that cost more than it looked like it should because everything made to last eventually did. He set it to the blade. The light caught the steel.

There was no flaw in the edge.

He had not expected one. He checked anyway.

---

The knock was Pixel's knock, which he recognized the way he recognized the sound of Cacophony's cylinder rotating — not loudly, just correctly. Two-two-one. The interval was the same every time. He had not analyzed it consciously. He knew it anyway.

"It's open," he said.

It wasn't open. He got up, set Silence carefully on the workbench — always horizontal, always edge-in — and crossed the apartment in four steps and opened the door. Pixel was standing in the hallway with a container in one hand and her calibration kit on her shoulder and the expression she wore when she had a reason for being somewhere and wasn't going to lead with it.

"I had extra," she said.

The container was warm. He stepped back.

She came in, set the food on the counter, looked at the workbench. "You were cleaning it."

"Yes."

"You're not hurt."

"No."

"Okay." She set the calibration kit on the kitchen table. "Eat first. Then I want to run the calibration — I've been thinking about the 18.7 reading."

He looked at the container. It was congee, or something close to it — he could smell the ginger. The container was from the place on Meridian, three blocks south, which was not on the way to anywhere Pixel regularly went.

"Thank you," he said.

"I said I had extra."

"I know."

He ate standing at the counter. The congee was good — ginger and a soft-boiled egg and something smoky he couldn't identify, and she had gotten the version with the extra broth, which meant she had either guessed correctly or had listened to him order once and filed it without comment. He didn't ask which. He finished the bowl and rinsed it and set it in the rack and turned around and she was already setting up.

---

The calibration kit was custom — Pixel had built most of it herself, sourced the rest from an AxisMed surplus broker she'd been cultivating for four years, and two components she'd designed from scratch because nothing commercially available did what she needed. She laid them out with the precision of a surgeon who worked alone: sensor array, resonance probe, the interface cable with the proprietary end she'd machined to fit the Atlas NeoCortex's external port, the tablet with the readings software she'd written herself.

Kyle sat in the kitchen chair across from her and tilted his head left, which was the position she'd established at some point and that he maintained without being asked. She plugged in.

"Still warm from outside," she said, not about anything. The sensor was reading baseline.

"It was warm earlier."

"Hold still."

He held still. The neuretics calibration felt like very little — a faint pressure at the base of the skull, the sense of something being assessed without his participation, which was mostly accurate. The Atlas NeoCortex did not report pain. It reported states. He was in a state of being read, which registered as neutral.

On her tablet, numbers moved. She watched them the way she watched everything she was responsible for: with the attention of someone who understood that the difference between fine and not-fine was usually a very small number, and that you had to be looking at the right time to catch it.

"Your delta rhythms are elevated," she said.

"I know."

"You've been not sleeping again."

"I've been sleeping."

"How long."

He thought about it. "Four hours. Approximately."

She made a sound that was not quite a sigh. "Atlas is compensating. Look at this." She turned the tablet toward him. He looked at the readout — the firing pattern in the supplementary motor cortex was asymmetric, the left side running three to four percent hotter than the right. He knew what this meant. She knew he knew.

"Compensating for what," he said, which was not really a question.

"Probably the forearm. The fine-motor processing — the blade work you were just doing — it's been rerouting around the healing tissue. The NeoCortex learned the workaround and it hasn't un-learned it yet." She tapped the display. "This is nothing. I can correct for it. What I want to look at is the 18.7 node."

She found it in three keystrokes — she'd bookmarked it from the last session, he realized. The 18.7 Hz reading had its own line in her software now, labeled LURE in her file system, the word in caps the way you'd flag something you didn't have a category for yet.

"I called it the Lure," she said. "It needs a name. Don't argue about it."

"I wasn't going to argue."

"You had a face."

He didn't. Or if he did, it was the usual face, which she'd apparently learned to read the way she read everything else — by instrument. "What does it do," he said.

"That's what I don't know." She was adjusting the probe, narrowing the frequency band. "It's not interference. It's not a calibration artifact — I checked it against three other units, it doesn't show up. It's inside the mesh, specifically in the deep nuclei, and it's been there every session I've run on you." A pause. "As far back as I have records."

"How far is that."

"Four years." She glanced up. "I started keeping formal records when I upgraded my logging software. Before that I was just running the usual checks." She looked back at the display. "I ran the 18.7 signature against every spec I have. AxisMed. NeurCore. Atlas's own published documentation. Nothing predates the earliest Atlas D-series, which puts the design origin at 2219 at the earliest." She stopped. "Your signature predates that."

Kyle said nothing.

"I don't know by how much," she said. "The harmonic decay profile puts the origin at — I don't know. Pre-commercial neuretics, at minimum." She set the probe down, which was not a thing she usually did mid-calibration. She was looking at him. "It's in the range of the theoretical designs. The research papers. The stuff they did before they built anything."

"That's a long time before sixteen," Kyle said.

"Yes," she said. "It is."

She picked the probe back up. The calibration continued.

---

At sixteen minutes into the session, the readout spiked.

Not a gradual rise — a single upward movement, clean and abrupt, the 18.7 Hz node going from its baseline of 0.003 amplitude to 0.019 in the space of a heartbeat, holding there for three seconds, and then dropping back as smoothly as it had risen. No cause on the sensor log. No external RF event in the building's ambient spectrum. The tablet made no sound. The room made no sound.

Kyle felt it.

Not as sensation — his body didn't register 18 Hz as sensation, not consciously. It was more like standing at the edge of something, the knowledge that depth was present even before looking down. A quality of the air. The light in the apartment doing exactly what light did, and feeling briefly like it shouldn't have to.

He said nothing.

Pixel said nothing.

They stayed in the quiet for a moment — her watching the readout come back to baseline, him watching a point approximately eighteen inches to the left of anything, which was a thing he did when he was processing something he didn't have language for yet. The probe was still at the base of his skull. Her hand had not moved.

"Three seconds," she said finally.

"Yes."

"That's new." She looked at the spike on the log — she was already annotating it, he could tell from the angle of her thumb on the tablet. LURE SPIKE 16:58. "The amplitude before this was never above 0.005. This is almost four times that." She was not frightened. This was one of the things about her — she did not process the unexpected as threat unless it actually was one. She processed it as data that required more data. "I don't know what caused it."

"No."

"Do you feel different."

"No."

She looked at him with the expression that meant she'd heard him and was assigning it a confidence interval. Then she looked back at the tablet and finished her annotations and resumed the calibration without saying anything else about it.

She did not push. This was also a thing about her.

The session ran another twenty-two minutes. She corrected the motor-cortex compensation pattern, adjusted the threshold governor on his sensory processing — the Atlas NeoCortex had been running the pain filter at seventy-three percent since the forearm injury, which was appropriate then and not necessary now, and she brought it back to eighty-two percent where it lived when he was between jobs — and she logged everything, and she unplugged the interface cable and coiled it with the same two-loop wrap she always used and laid it in the case.

"Your processing speed is fine," she said. "The array is running clean. The delta elevation will self-correct when you sleep, which means you should sleep."

"Four hours is sleep."

"Four hours is maintenance mode." She closed the case. "Sleep is when the NeoCortex defragments the processing load. Four hours doesn't complete the cycle. You know this." She looked at him. "Eat, sleep, drink water. You're not a Behemoth."

"No," he said.

She stood.

---

She stood with the calibration kit on her shoulder and her hand on the door handle, and she paused, which was unusual. Pixel's departures were generally as efficient as her arrivals — task complete, goodbye delivered as information rather than ceremony, gone. The pause was deliberate. He waited.

"You always do that," she said. "Half a step."

He looked at her.

"Every time someone gets close. Not away — just back. Exactly half a step." She was watching him with the same attention she'd given the readout spike. Precise. Noting. Not accusing. "You do it with me. You do it at Mrs. Chen's when she reaches over the counter. I've seen you do it with Boost when the kid gets into your radius by accident."

The room was very quiet. Outside, the transit corridor was beginning its 18:00 swell — the city coming home, filling its veins with itself.

"I've been watching it for two years," she said. "I'm not trying to hold you. I just want you to know I see it."

She did not ask him to explain it. She did not offer him a reason for it. She did not tell him it was fine or not fine. She opened the door and she went, and the latch — the left hinge had been dragging since January, a four-millimeter drop in the mounting plate that he'd noted and not repaired and that made a specific sound when it settled, the sound that meant the apartment was sealed again — made its sound.

Kyle stood in the kitchen. The calibration kit was gone. The bowl was on the drying rack. The 18.7 spike was in Pixel's software, annotated, timestamped, waiting for a category that didn't exist yet.

He was aware he had not moved.

He had taken a half-step back from the door when she opened it. He'd registered it at the time — not consciously, more the way the NeoCortex registered motor events, filing them without flagging them for attention because they cost nothing and changed nothing and had always happened. Always happened. He didn't know how long "always" was for him specifically. The particular him, with his particular history of arriving in the world with a blade that had no parents.

He went back to the workbench.

Silence was where he'd left it — horizontal, edge-in, waiting with the patience of a thing that had never been in a hurry. He picked up the cleaning cloth and set it down again. He did not need to clean the blade. He had cleaned the blade. He picked it up and held it in both hands and stood at the workbench in the last of the afternoon light coming through the window at a low angle, striking the steel in a way that showed him, briefly, his own reflection.

The face in the steel was his face.

He looked at it the way he looked at the edge — checking, confirming, finding no flaw, moving on.

---

The half-step cost nothing. That was the fact he'd lived inside without naming it. It was as automatic as the motor routines the NeoCortex ran without consulting him, as free as breathing, as present as the 18.7 Hz hum that predated every spec anyone could source. He had been taking that step his whole life, or whatever word applied to however long he'd been himself, in whatever form that was, and it had never cost him anything because he had always taken it in time, before the geometry of a moment could rearrange itself into something he'd have to account for.

What cost was the other thing. The staying. The not-half-stepping. The moment when someone — Pixel, with her instruments and her annotated files and her habit of arriving with food she had extra of — came close enough to read his scar tissue and the hum spiked for three seconds and didn't explain itself, and everything she'd said about the thing that predated everything she could source was still hanging in the air of the apartment with nowhere to go, and the question that lived underneath everything got a little harder to put down.

That had a cost. He could feel the line item opening the way he could feel a seam in body armor before he found it with his hands.

He set Silence on the rack. He turned off the workbench light. The room went to ambient gray and he stood in it and he knew: she sees it, and she is still here, and those two facts require no response, and the half-step is not fear, it is arithmetic.

He knew what it would cost anyone who tried to hold on.

He had always known that figure. He just hadn't had a name for the step.

[Beat 97]
She knocked twice, a long gap, once. Kyle's pattern for the door, which she had never asked him about and used without comment.

He opened it. She was holding a sealed container with both hands because it was still hot.

"Extra," she said.

He stepped back — not far, just the half-step that was always there, automatic, old enough that he'd stopped tracking it. She came in and set the container on the counter he kept clear for tools. She pulled her jacket off and put it over the back of the chair he used for equipment runs, which apparently was also now the jacket chair, and neither of them had said anything about that.

The food was real. Not a vend brick, not hydration paste — something she'd made, he could tell from the weight of the container and the smell that came off it when she cracked the seal. Broth. Grain. Something charred at the edges that meant she'd used actual heat. Steam rose between them and went flat.

"Didn't know I needed feeding," Kyle said.

"I had extra."

She was already eating. She had her own portion in a smaller container and was standing at his counter with it, using the near edge of the surface the way she always did — half on the counter, half in her hand, her back to the far wall. He hadn't noticed when that had become her spot.

Kyle looked at the container she'd left him.

He didn't eat. Not yet. He didn't move it either.

She fished a blister strip from her jacket pocket — the jacket on the equipment chair — and pressed out a small white tab. Split it in half with her thumbnail. Set one piece on the counter beside his container without announcing it and took the other one herself.

"Medithadol," she said, though he hadn't asked.

"I know what medithadol looks like."

"Just checking."

She ate. He stood. The steam went flat.

He picked up the container eventually — not the tab, the container — and ate standing beside her. It was good. The grain had absorbed the broth all the way through, which took time. He didn't say so.

"You're running a job."

"Day after tomorrow."

"You're thinking about it tomorrow."

He ate.

"Lotus territory," she said. "Corridor work."

He hadn't told her that. He hadn't not told her either. She had her own clients and her own ways of knowing things, and he had found that pointing out either fact produced a kind of patient silence that felt worse than not pointing it out.

"The draw angle on Cacophony," she said. "You've got the ride too low."

He looked at the revolver on the counter — clipped to the edge, where it lived when he was home.

"You clip your elbow on a tight inside draw. I noticed it last month during calibration. High-quarter pull against a close wall — you'll swing wide before the barrel clears."

"I know how I draw."

"I know you know how you draw." She scraped the last of her broth. "I'm telling you what I saw. Raise the ride height two centimeters, or tilt the whole rig butt-forward five degrees. Corridor walls being what they are."

He looked at the holster. She was right about the clip — he'd felt it once or twice on tight doorframes and written it off as clearance. He didn't say she was right.

"You can fix it before the job," she said. "It's not a thing."

She capped her container and dropped it in his recycler, which was close enough to her own that she apparently regarded them as interchangeable. She put her jacket back on.

Kyle reached over and picked up the half-tab from the counter. Took it.

"Eat the rest," she said, nodding at his container. "The grain gets stiff."

She left. He heard 2E's lock engage across the hall.

The holster sat on the counter where it always sat. He looked at it.

He ate the rest of the grain, which had gotten stiff. It was still good. He put the container in the recycler and didn't touch the holster.

[Beat 98]
Three minutes.

The waterworks crew at Elston and the canal had stopped work and were sitting on the curb in the specific posture of people who had made this calculation before: equipment down, no one near the tools, a measured distance back, and the kind of collective patience that wasn't passive. They were watching the micro-schism with the same attention they would give a traffic signal. One was eating. Two were watching the gutter, where the pebbles and grit had organized themselves into a slow drift.

Kyle stopped at the corner and checked the advisory on his relay board. Zone 7, two actives listed. This one wasn't on the board. The crew had found it themselves and made their own call.

"How long?" he asked the one eating.

"Eight minutes so far. Usually three to five. Give it another two."

He waited. At the three-minute mark the pebbles in the gutter settled. The drift decelerated, the organized pattern loosened, the pieces arranged themselves back into ordinary debris that had no opinion about gravity. The waterworks crew stood up, collected their tools, and went back to work without debriefing. No one said anything.

Kyle crossed Elston and continued toward the dock.

[Beat 99]
The fifth came up the block the way ordinary people came up blocks, which was to say Kyle didn't clock her until she was already inside the loose triangle the three of them made, and that bothered him more than if she'd dropped out of a window. She was medium. Medium height, a coat the color of a coat, hair pulled back the way a person pulls it back when they've stopped thinking about it. The kind of face a room would round down to nothing. You could share an elevator with her and a week later swear you'd ridden up alone.

"Sift," she said, which was the codename and the whole of the introduction, and she said it the way you'd give a barista a name you didn't expect to hear called back.

Kyle read her: quiet, polite, forgettable on purpose, which was a skill and not an accident, and skills that took that much work to look like nothing were usually pointed at something. He had no idea what she did. The brief hadn't said. On a clean crew the brief never said more than your own piece — Shroud knew the building, Stash knew the gear, Kyle knew his piece was to stand between everyone else and whatever came through a door — and the fact that he didn't know Sift's piece meant her piece was the one the client cared about.

"You're the muscle," she said to him. Not a question. She had a read of her own. Then, tilting her head the way someone does when they've already decided to like the answer: "What do I call you?"

Kyle opened his mouth. He got one syllable in — maybe less, the K of his name — and Shroud's voice came into the channel, flat: *No real names.*

He closed his mouth. He looked around. The corner. The rain. The shuttered pharmacy with Stash's weight still denting the doorway. And down near the base of the step, where the gutter had carried it from somewhere else, a glass soda bottle with a faded orange label.

"Soda Pop," he said.

Sift snorted. It was a real snort, unguarded, the first thing she'd done that wasn't careful, and it did something to her ordinary face he hadn't expected.

Kyle picked up the bottle and threw it against the pharmacy wall. The glass went everywhere — sharp, sudden, the small violence of it carrying a weight out of proportion to the sound it made.

"Broken Glass," he said.

She made the same sound again. The snort had company now; she was working to keep it contained. She looked at the pieces on the wet concrete. She looked at him. Something moved in her face — amusement, maybe, or a reappraisal, or both — and when it settled she said:

"I'm just going to call you K."

He went back to watching the street, and did not examine why it mattered to him whether she could.

[Beat 100]
The transmission completed. Sift said, "Clean." One word. The job's word for done.

The thing came up the ramp before the echo died — not running, just arriving, the way an appointment arrives. Four arms. Three of them were shotguns, the barrels socketed direct into the wrist housings where hands would have been on a man who had not spent considerable money disagreeing with the fact of being one. The fourth arm had something belt-fed that ran a chain back through the shoulder housing.

It did not announce itself. It did not give her a chance.

It had the angle on her before Kyle was moving, and Kyle was moving before the decision reached him. He moved anyway. He was already moving when it didn't matter.

[Beat 101]
She was dead before the last echo. He was already past her.

War Dog's grenades went wide before they cleared the deck — not thrown at targets, used to clear a kill zone, and the fragmentation radius was generous. Shroud peeled before the first one hit ground. Kyle did not call her back. Stash was already gone — the way people go when they have been in this kind of room before and know exactly what a fragmentation pattern means for their gear.

Kyle dived left as the first grenade went off and came up behind the loading bay's structural column with his ears ringing and the ceiling still raining concrete dust.

## Three Barrels

[Beat 102]
The first shotgun spoke and the loading dock answered in dust. Kyle was not where the dust was. He had read the lead arm's settle a quarter-second before the wrist committed — the way the shoulder loaded, the way a man with three barrels still aims with his eyes — and he'd gone low and wide, off the line, behind the concrete pillar that would hold up against buckshot, which a freelancer would not. The blast took a bite out of the bay door behind where he'd been and the noise of it was a flat hand against both ears.

His neuretics ran the arithmetic and it came back simple: Cacophony held three rounds, the belt-fed left arm held more than three, and nobody wins a trade against a wall of fire. He kept Silence in hand. Silence asked for distance closed, and the only way through a wall was to be past it before the wall knew you'd moved.

One of War Dog's grenades had bounced wrong in the clearing pattern. It had come to rest six feet from the column, arming indicator cycling through the end of its count.

Kyle picked it up.

The Atlas-grade NeoCortex had no governor. That was the thing slowly killing him and the only reason the next three seconds were possible. He neuretically connected to the detonator port — found the encryption on the countdown, the rolling cipher designed to make field disarm impossible — and he broke it. Not with finesse. With processing power, the way you use a sledgehammer when you cannot find the key, cycling through the cipher space faster than the hardware was rated to run. The heat of it came up behind his eyes like a filament burning at both ends.

He paused the countdown with one second on the clock. He set it near the mouth of the ramp exit and left it there.

Then he used the dock. The bay was a stacked geometry of crates and lift platform and the steel stairwell climbing to the loading office, and Kyle made War Dog spend his fire on plywood — not running from the guns, running the room around them, so that every shot was a shot at where Kyle had been and every half-second the three arms cycled their breeches was a step closer. The torso turned with him on a ring independent of the legs, and Kyle felt the lead arm track him through a stack of cartons and threw himself off the angle a breath before the boards became air.

"Pillar's chewed," Shroud said, fast, in his ear — she'd gone clear but she still had eyes on the structure from outside, her thermal scope reading through the dock's steel skin. "You can't trade. He's got a belt-feed on the left arm, he doesn't run dry —"

"I know what he doesn't do," Kyle said, and went up the stairwell.

It was the wrong direction. That was the point. A man fighting three shotguns ran for the exit; Kyle ran for the high steel, because a stair was a corridor and a corridor made three barrels into one.

[Beat 103]
War Dog tracked by heat. The IR dampeners Kyle wore cut his signature but did not erase it. He bought himself seconds and he spent them carefully — moving inside the sight lines War Dog expected to find empty, keeping the structural columns between himself and the belt-fed arm, staying low.

The grenade at the ramp mouth sat dormant. Waiting.

[Beat 104]
When War Dog crossed toward the misdirect fire — toward the ramp-mouth grenade, away from Kyle's position — it put his back to the open center of the deck and his spine between the shoulder housings: the junction where the four arms connected, where the routing ran, where something behind the chassis wall was keeping the whole architecture coherent.

Kyle ran.

He drove Silence in two-handed between the shoulder housings, edge-first, deep, angled up toward whatever was routing behind the plating. He felt it catch — felt it find something that was neither flesh nor bone — something that seized and locked and sent a tremor up the blade and through his wrists that his wrists were still feeling thirty seconds later.

War Dog turned. The left leg did not track with the rest of him. The actuator had frozen mid-stride, and he pivoted on a foot that would not move. One arm — the right-front shotgun housing — had gone slack at the socket and hung at an angle its joint had not been designed to reach.

## Who Vouches for You

[Beat 105]
Stash had pulled the van back from the bay and was standing in the gap between the rear bumper and the dock's concrete wall. He'd laid his coat over her. The shape under it was small and final, and the coat's hem was dark with something that would not wash out. His hands were still at the hem where he'd smoothed it down — the hands of a man who had run out of work to do and hadn't moved them yet.

Kyle stopped. He put Silence away.

"She was already —" Stash started.

"I know," Kyle said.

Ledger stood on the far side of the van. He was looking at nothing in particular, which was the look Ledger had when he was paying the most attention. He had been at his position. The vehicle had come from below. He would put it in the report, and the report would not make it make sense, because there was no sense to be made.

"We need to move," Stash said. "Shroud's already clear."

"I know," Kyle said again. The dock smelled like old concrete and Lake Michigan cold and the hydraulic fluid still bleeding out of the left arm three stories down. He owed her one beat, unhurried, the way he'd owe anyone who'd stood their ground in the last moment they had.

Then he picked her up — coat and all, the way Stash had laid it — and set her in the back of the van himself. Stash's van. His work vehicle, the one he drove for the Vultures jobs, the one that had seen this particular weight before.

The body was evidence. That was a true thing. A Read's neural architecture did not go dark at death the way a normal person's did — not immediately, not with the right equipment. Put the brain in a tank of conducting fluid, run electrodes through the right structures, and what she knew was still in there: the client's name, the data she'd pulled, the job, the faces on the team. Someone with the resources and the inclination could reverse-engineer the last hours of a dead psionic's mind and use what they found to end every person in this van. That was also a true thing.

Kyle knew both true things.

He carried her anyway. You did not leave a comrade behind. That was the rule, and it was not an operational rule — it was a human one, and it did not require justification, and Kyle did not offer any.

He got in.

[Beat 106]
He fired blind. The remaining arms swept backward in wide arcs — not aimed, not targeted, suppression work from something that had stopped trying to find Kyle and started trying to make the air between them unusable. Kyle took cover behind the nearest support column and let the fire pass and counted the pattern.

War Dog backed toward the edge of the deck. One step. Two.

Kyle neuretically reactivated the countdown.

War Dog did not stop at the rail. He did not look back. He went over — three stories of open air and then pavement, a decision made in the half-second between the grenade's arming tone and the choice of what it would do to him if he stayed — and the frozen leg and the hanging arm hit ground together in a sound Kyle felt in his back teeth.

Then War Dog moved. Faster than a frozen actuator had any business moving. Into the city, into the dark, into whatever network had sent him here and was pulling him back now that the job was done.

The parking structure went quiet. Kyle stood in it with Silence in his hand.

Three blocks out, he said, "Stop the van."

Stash stopped the van. Kyle got out and walked back the way they'd come, and he stood on the corner where the light was good, and he checked Silence for damage the way you check a tool after you've done something to it the tool was not designed for. The blade was intact. The edge had held.

He walked back to the van and got in.

Nobody asked.

[Beat 107]
At the second light north, the channel chirped once.

A fresh posting. Pushed wide to every freelancer band in the van's radius — not targeted, not narrow, the kind of push the relay gave anything it flagged as time-sensitive. Clean scope. Listed retainer. A client field that resolved to a shell, and the shell to nothing.

Stash went quiet in the specific way of a man who has just run a number and doesn't like where it lands. He kept his eyes on the road. He did not check the mirror.

Ledger read the posting. Then he looked at the back of the van. Then he read it again.

Kyle sat with the glass through the lining of his coat pocket and didn't open it. Twelve minutes since the dock. The relay didn't know that — or it didn't care — or it had known before they left, and the posting had been ready, and the second light north was the exact second light north, which was the timing of a thing that had been waiting for the shot to land.

He didn't think about that too directly. He watched the rain on the windshield and didn't think about it.

He would open it in the morning. He would take the job. He had a funeral to price.

## Chapter 6: The Quiet Hour

[Beat 108]
Nobody talked on the way back.

Stash drove. Ledger had the cases on his lap, which he didn't need to do — the floor of the van was fine, the locks were fine — but he did it anyway, and nobody said anything about it. The elevated passed overhead in sections, the sodium lights throwing orange bars across the windshield at intervals.

Kyle thought about the reading. Not the woman — the reading itself. The vault-pull Sift had taken from the operator's head, the file she'd carried behind her eyes on the dock, the thing War Dog had come to collect. He would not know what was in it. That was the part of the job you didn't keep. The client had paid for extraction. The extraction had been performed. What happened afterward was not in the scope.

He thought about the way she'd moved on the dock. The way she'd counted the geometry the same second he did, without needing to ask. There was a word for that in the trade. He did not use it in the van.

The relay stayed quiet for the whole drive. That was fine. That was correct.

[Beat 109]
Pixel's light was on across the hall.

The particular rhythm of it — two steps and back, two steps and back — the way she moved between her bench and her terminal when she was in the middle of something. He stood in his doorway for a moment. He did not knock. He went to bed.

He woke at 04:00 and lay in the dark until the gray at the window was enough to call it morning. He made coffee. He drank it standing. He did not look at the relay.

By 07:30 he looked at the relay. No new jobs. The previous client had paid in advance — clean rate, clean scope, full deposit before entry. That transaction was closed. That name was done.

He had Mrs. Chen's number. He called it.

"Thursday," she said, before he finished the question. Her voice was already carrying the weight of someone who had done this before. "Six o'clock. Tell whoever she kept."

He did not ask how she knew. She had been running that counter for sixty years. She knew.

[Beat 110]
The rain arrived first - it always does on Halsted, running down the south face of the elevated infrastructure overhead and making a second street-level weather system out of the drip. Kyle came on foot. He had the address right. He checked it twice anyway, not because he doubted it but because standing on the sidewalk was still standing on the sidewalk, and once he went inside the number would mean something different.

The block had been the same block for forty years. The grate pattern on the streetlights gave it away - the older municipal spec, wider spacing, no sensor housing at the center. The buildings were functional and worn in the way things get worn when they are used correctly for a long time. A laundry line between two third-floor windows held three shirts and a child's jacket, all the same off-color from the synthesis-corridor air that drifted east on certain winds. A sundial dog crossed the far end of the block on whatever circuit it kept, indifferent to the rain.

Kyle stood at the vestibule.

There was a handwritten sign taped to the inside of the glass - visible through it, the adhesive tape gone amber with age though the paper itself was new. Ṭu's full name. Kyle had never seen it written out before.

He stood at the vestibule longer than he meant to.

The rain separated into its registers as he stood there, the pitched thrum off the overhead structure and the softer percussion on the vestibule's aluminum overhang. He didn't need either sound named.

He went inside.

[Beat 111]
Forty people Kyle did not know.

Mrs. Okafor had a clipboard. She was moving through the front room the way a good coordinator moves through a site — not hurrying, not pausing, handing a plate here and redirecting foot traffic there. She caught Kyle's eye once across the room — a coordinator's inventory, checking a new arrival against her list. She found what she needed and moved on. He noticed that the hand she kept at her side was not entirely still.

His neuretics caught the frequency before he finished reading the room.

The Psyk was in the corner. Jaw set, both hands flat on their thighs, seated with their back to the wall in the chair closest to the window. The Read was running open — nineteen hertz, felt in the back teeth, the ambient fracture of something spilling at the edges of containment. He clocked it. He moved on.


On a corkboard near the far window there were photographs. Most were printed on paper feeds — the standard quick-format, slick-edged, from a public output terminal. One was analog. Paper, not plastfilm. A little smaller than the others, the corners soft from handling. Ñu at maybe nineteen, laughing at something outside the frame. The laugh was genuine — full, unguarded, body in it. Kyle looked at it for a while. He reached to take it from the board — and Mrs. Okafor's hand closed gently around his wrist from behind, barely a weight.

"That one stays," she said, not looking at it.

She released his wrist and moved on. He let his hand come back. He did not recognize the laugh. He had known Ñu for six years and he did not recognize it — which meant it had lived somewhere he had not been. A record of a life that had existed before the work made her into what he knew. He had not made room for that version of her. Now he never would.

Mrs. Okafor materialized at his left shoulder and handed him a plate without asking. Jollof rice, two pieces of chicken, a fold of flatbread. She did not wait to see if he took it; she was already turning to the next thing.

He took it with both hands. The plate was warm. He stood there with it.

He did not eat.

[Beat 112]
They came in ones and twos through the rain. Not the kind of crowd that advertises.

Ledger came first, because Ledger was always early and never explained it. He took the far end stool, ordered once, and didn't speak for an hour, which for Ledger was grief. Stash came next, with the careful walk of someone whose load was invisible tonight — she sat beside Kyle without asking and ate in silence with the same precision she applied to everything else. A woman Kyle knew as Casing arrived with someone he didn't know, introduced neither of them, and ate two bowls with the steady efficiency of a person taking care of necessities.

Pixel came from across the hall. Kyle did not ask how she'd known about Ñu — Pixel and Ñu had crossed paths on the job two years back, the Mirrorwell run. That was how Pixel had ended up with the analog barometer that still sat at the far end of her bench, hadn't moved in three years, and probably never would. She'd never explained how she got it. Ñu had never mentioned giving it. Some things between women in the work ran on channels Kyle didn't have clearance for.

She sat at his shoulder and said nothing for a long time. Then: "She was good."

"Yeah," Kyle said.

"I know three people who are that good. Now I know two."

He didn't answer. There wasn't an answer that was the right size.

The others came after — faces from jobs Kyle had worked adjacent to, not with; people from Ñu's side of the ledger, names he didn't have, which was correct, which was how it worked. Forty people in a noodle counter on Halsted, eating broth in a woman's honor whose real name nobody in the room had been given, and all of them understanding exactly what that meant.

[Beat 113]
Four seconds.

The Psyk lost containment and the hum spiked — not louder, deeper, the nineteen hertz thickening into something that sat in the chest. Three people near the window flinched without knowing why, the involuntary micro-flinch of a body responding to something it had no name for. Kyle's neuretics spiked with the frequency, a brief static bloom at the array periphery. He met the Psyk's eyes across the room. The Psyk's jaw was still set, their hands still flat, but their eyes had gone wide and slightly unfocused in the way eyes go when something is running through a person that cannot entirely be contained. Kyle held the look. The Psyk pulled it back — a visible effort, the way a thing is pulled back when it does not want to be pulled. The frequency dropped. The three people near the window settled without knowing they had moved. Four seconds.



Mrs. Okafor was at the far side, refilling a tray. Someone laughed quietly at something someone else had said about Ñu — a laugh that was not the one in the photograph. The hum ran through it all, low and steady. Four beats of it, keeping time, before it dropped back to the baseline and stayed there.

[Beat 114]
Kyle found the Psyk outside on the curb, both hands pressed over their ears. Not in distress, exactly — in management. Their elbows were on their knees and their posture was careful, the posture of someone who has been here before and knows the protocol.

The hum was audible without augmentation now.

He sat down next to the Psyk on the curb. He still had the plate.

The Psyk didn't look at him. They kept their hands over their ears and stared at the wet concrete of the street, where the rain was coming down clean and the runoff was collecting in the gutter at a steady rate.

"It's not one grief," the Psyk said. Their voice was controlled but close to the surface. "Forty griefs running at once. None of them the same frequency."

Kyle set the plate down on the curb beside him. The jollof rice had gone cold. The chicken was still on it, untouched.

He sat with the rain and the cold plate and the forty griefs running at their irregular frequencies, and he did not try to make any of it smaller. He didn't have words that were the right size for it. He sat until the hum leveled and, slowly, the Psyk's hands came down from their ears.

[Beat 115]
Mrs. Chen found him at the counter's end when the second round came around. She set a folded envelope beside his bowl without comment — heavy paper, cream, the kind with a watermark pressed into it. The letterhead read *Vey's Antiquity & Stationary*, and below it a Dearborn address — the private entrance, not the counter-face number he'd filed the first time he'd stood at the shop.

Two words inside, no signature, in a hand that was not Mrs. Chen's — small, formal script, the handwriting of someone who had decided long ago what kind of person they were going to be on paper and had never deviated.

*Meet me.*

He read the two words and let them sit. Whoever had sent this had put a pen to paper and handed it to Mrs. Chen in person, which meant they had been here — to this counter, on a Thursday, at the wake. That was not information that traveled on relay channels. Someone had known where Kyle went when he had nowhere else to be. That knowledge, and the heavy paper, and the address on Dearborn, and the two words that asked for nothing except his presence — all of that had been assembled before he arrived, by someone patient enough to wait for the right room to hand it in.

He folded the envelope once and put it in his coat. He finished his broth. The wok came back up like a bell, and the counter filled again with the sound of something ongoing, and he sat in it for a while before he stood and said goodbye to the ones he knew the names of.

He did not know all of them.

[Beat 116]
He had the address from the building directory at the wake venue — Mrs. Chen had a registration card for the community that had gathered that night, organized the way Mrs. Chen organized everything, by district and block. He'd read it coming in, which was habit, and Null's entry had been at the bottom: *Null/Mimi T.*, which was how she'd filed with the community, which was how she filed with everything.

It was six blocks from the carousel park.

He hadn't made that connection until he was standing at the building door, in the rain, and his neuretics — still running their baseline log since the wake — produced the notation: AMBIENT INTERFERENCE - 18.9 HZ - TYPE UNCLASSIFIED. Different from the carousel by 0.2 Hz. Same classification. Same range.

He stood at the door for a moment. Then he went in.

[Beat 117]
The vestibule was the specific kind of quiet that costs something. Ferrocement construction, old-spec, the flooring composite and cold and worn to the particular smoothness of things that had been stood on for a very long time without anyone choosing to replace them.

The directory in the vestibule had thirty-four names. The third floor had one gap - a slot where the name card had been removed cleanly, the slot itself empty.

He took the stairs.

[Beat 118]
The corner table at Davorka's had a hand-written sign on it.

Not a standard Davorka's menu card — a piece of cardstock in handwriting he didn't recognize, in a plastic sleeve that had yellowed at the edges. It read:

*RESERVED*

*for whoever comes back*

Davorka's had eighteen tables. Seventeen of them turned. He had been here fifty times, maybe more. The corner table had never had anyone sitting at it. He had asked once, early on, and Davorka had explained, with the specific economy of someone who had explained it many times, that a micro-schism had come through the previous winter and done something to the corner's geometry. Nothing structural, nothing visible. The kind of thing you noticed if you sat there long enough: the candle guttered wrong, the ambient sound from the bar didn't arrive in the same sequence it should have, the space between the wall and the chair felt like it had an opinion.

The previous tenants — a couple who had been regulars — had been sitting at that table when the schism passed through. They'd finished their drinks and left and hadn't come back.

They'd left because of the geometry, Davorka said. Not because of anything the schism had done to them.

The staff set a glass at the corner table every shift. They didn't reset it between seatings.

Kyle sat at one of the other tables and ordered what he always ordered.

[Beat 119]
Mrs. Okafor had been there already — the candle in the window, the plate of food on the counter, still warm enough to mean she'd been there within the last hour. She didn't cook for the dead. She cooked for the people the dead left behind.

The room was sparse in the way that said something about how Null had lived — not poor, just without accumulation. Work surface under the window, tools organized in the specific way that practitioners of the Read organized their materials: retrievable, not decorative, kept for function and for history. The window faced east. The first light, when it came, would come through that window at the right angle. She would have known that when she took the room.

Kyle stood in the center of it without touching anything. It was still her room. It had the particular quality of spaces that hadn't yet been told what they were now — held in the moment before they became absence.

[Beat 120]
The photograph was on the shelf above the work surface, in a cheap composite frame. Three people on a rooftop in summer — the elevated visible in the background, the light specific to July or August. Sift was on the right, hair loose, holding a bottle, not looking at the camera. The other two were younger, or she was older; the photograph didn't make it clear. They were laughing at something outside the frame.

He had known her handle for six years. He had known her work. He had not known she had people she stood on rooftops with in summer. He looked at the photograph for a moment and committed neither name to anything. She was who she was. The handle was the working name and the working name was the whole of what she'd offered him. He had not asked for more and she had not given it. That had been, he thought, a fair arrangement — the kind she would have preferred.

[Beat 121]
They were in two stacks on the work surface, under a clear weight — half-size card stock, the kind that came from the practitioners' supply shops in the old zone markets. Printed in the compressed hand she'd used for everything: date codes at the top, classification annotations she would have developed herself. The notation was private — the personal shorthand of someone who ran the Read for hire and kept records the way professionals in dangerous work kept records. Not for other people. For herself.

He didn't read them. Not because he couldn't piece together the grammar — he'd known enough practitioners to have the general key — but because they were hers. Her absence didn't change her claim to them.

He put his hand on the stack. Not picking it up. Just the weight of it under his palm, her handwriting visible in the margin of the top slip: a date from three weeks before she died, and a notation that was two words in her code. He didn't need to translate it. He understood the register.

She had known.

There was a folded note flat under the weight — his handle on the outside in her compressed hand. He opened it.

*Before Broken Glass. Meant to tell you. The name I use — you do the same thing, smaller. Third try. It held.*

He put it in his coat.

[Beat 122]
There was a Psyk in the stairwell on his way down — young, in work clothes, with a read-kit bag, sitting two steps from the second-floor landing, running the controlled breath that brought the sensitivity below ambient.

"It's strong here," the Psyk said. Not to Kyle specifically. To the fact of it.

Kyle paused on the landing.

"Residual?"

"Running." The Psyk looked at their kit, then at the floor. "It knows the geometry. Been running for a while. Before tonight." They made a small practitioner's notation with their fingers — a calibration gesture. "It's not malicious. It's just present. The way a signal is present when it's looking for something."

Kyle looked at the stairwell door. He said nothing else. He went out.

[Beat 123]
He didn't go back inside.

He put one hand on Silence through the coat. Not drawing it. Just locating it — the weight, the geometry, the matte-black edge that kept no light and carried the history the togishi couldn't read. He held it there for a moment. Then he took his hand away.

The hum was gone.

He hadn't noticed when it stopped. It had been in the room all evening — the nineteen hertz, the Psyk's grief frequency, the city's baseline, all of them braided into something that didn't have a clean name — and now it was gone. The rain was just rain. Halsted was just Halsted. The block was a block that would still be here after everyone inside had finished their accounting.

He walked north into the rain that carried synthesis-corridor cold and something underneath it that was simply the city being itself, the way it had been itself for two hundred years and would go on being after everyone who was grieving tonight had settled their remaining tabs.

The relay was at home. He had a job to file under. He walked.

[Beat 124]
The hum had stopped before he left the vestibule. By the time he cleared the elevated infrastructure and crossed onto Fullerton, his neuretics had settled to baseline and the cold was doing its ordinary work.

He was four blocks north when the notation appeared:

AMBIENT INTERFERENCE — 18.9 HZ — TYPE UNCLASSIFIED

The same classification as the carousel.

He read it the way he'd read the carousel notation — without stopping, without altering his pace. The frequency was below hearing; it did not announce itself; the body registered something present and nearby, a low animal alertness without an object. He noted the cross-streets and kept walking. The rain was clean. The drainage was working.

The notation held for six blocks, then decayed. His neuretics filed it under the date. The file was two entries now, three weeks apart, different neighborhoods, same classification. He had not made a folder for them.

He got home. The relay had a new posting. Medical transport, short-haul, loading dock in the south Loop — the kind of logistics cover that moved things that were not quite medical. The client field resolved to the same shell it always resolved to. The rate was inside his range. He accepted it, confirmed the 0600 contact window, and filed the parameters.

He went to bed. He did not think about the frequency.

[Beat 125]
Her field kit was smaller than usual, which meant she'd done the triage before she knocked — she'd assessed what he'd need and packed for it. Not the full kit. The calibrated kit. The kit for a night when someone shows up carrying something they'll need to set down before they'll let anyone near the wounds.

"Sit," she said.

He sat. It occurred to him that he didn't argue — that the argument he would have made twelve months ago, about the nature of field medicine and the independence of licensed professionals, didn't come. He opened his jacket without being asked.

She didn't say anything about that.

She worked across the damage in order: the cut above his ear from the corner of a rack casing, the deep bruise across his ribs that she found through the shirt before she saw it, the palm of his right hand, which had taken a rail edge in a way it wouldn't recover from in anything less than a week. She cleaned each one and dressed each one with the specific economy of someone who had been thinking about this since she heard the outcome.

Sift was dead. The operational truth and the human truth both existed simultaneously; he'd stated them both in the van, in the necessary order, and then done what you do when both truths are stated, which is nothing. There is nothing else to do with the weight of them. The weight is the weight.

Pixel set the second dressing and smoothed it flat with two fingers, carefully, not asking.

That was the thing. The not asking.

He sat in the chair in her workshop with the lamp going and the rain outside and the coupling splice still on the table from whenever she'd left it, and she worked through the damage without offering him anything except the medicine, and it was the most useful thing anyone had done for him in a week, and he did not say that, and she did not require him to.

"Done," she said, which was the word for *I know.*

"Done," he said, which was the word for *I know you do.*

[Beat 126]
She made tea after that. He didn't ask for it and didn't not ask for it. It arrived, which was the relevant fact.

She sat across from him at the workbench with her own cup and put a small oval pill on the table between them, which he recognized as the kind of thing you used after a bad job when your nervous system was still running the scenario on a loop and sleep wasn't going to come for a while. She snapped it in half with her thumbnail, which she did neatly and without drama, and slid one half toward him.

He picked it up.

She put the other half on her tongue, chased it with tea, and looked out the window at the rain.

He did the same. Tea and rain and the coupling splice still waiting on the table and the lamp buzzing very quietly above and the city outside not asking anything of anyone. They sat there while the edge came off and the edges in general got softer and the afternoon — it was already afternoon, somehow, he'd lost the morning somewhere in the docks — moved through them at the speed things move when you aren't watching.

She said, at one point: "I made a self-adhesive gasket seal that holds at fifteen atmospheres. I want you to know that's more impressive than it sounds."

"I know it is," he said.

"I just like saying it," she said.

They sat there until the light changed. The noodle cart below made its evening announcement, which it did with a sound that Mrs. Chen had been describing as criminal since the cart arrived three years ago and which he had privately decided was comforting. Pixel fell asleep sitting up at some point, her chin on her fist, the empty cup still in her other hand.

He didn't move. The afternoon was fine.

## Chapter 8: Before Something Changes

[Beat 127]
The Northpoint job had ended at 21:47 by the overlay clock. Kyle had ridden back on the same route he always took. He'd been in a drawer at Northpoint for just under an hour — concealment work, waiting for a courier to pass a checkpoint who hadn't known Kyle was there — and his hands had found the bag seam in the dark before his eyes had caught up.

At 20:54, the concealment point door had opened four seconds early. Not by much — four seconds was within the window of casual variance, the kind of gap that didn't make a report. His neuretics had made a different call: TIMING VARIANCE — EXTERNAL — SOURCE UNCLASSIFIED. The courier had arrived 53 seconds ahead of the window. The neuretics had logged no mechanism for the early opening. No personnel account. No environmental trigger on record. The door had simply been open when it needed to be open.

He had been in the work long enough to know the difference between a door that opens because someone opened it and a door that opens because something else decided it should. He had started the counter at the moment the notation landed in the job log. He had not decided what to do with it yet.

He did not remember deciding to come here.

The door was unlocked. It was generally unlocked when she was working, which was most of the time, so he pushed it and went in and the door whispered shut on its pneumatic seal behind him, a small finality, and Pixel was at the bench under the ceiling light with her hair pulled into an updo that had been precise three hours ago and was loosening now, and she didn't look up from the sensor array she was calibrating, and she said: "Calibration's running."

Not an invitation. Not a refusal. Information, offered flat, the way she offered everything else.

Kyle stood in the door for another beat. The room was warm and the bench light threw a clean cone down over her hands and the array and the tools laid out in the order she always laid them, and through the cracked window the wet street eleven floors down smelled like factory rain drying off ferrocement, pear drops and machine oil, and somewhere behind that the faint lake-cold that lived in the air in this sector regardless of the month. On the secondary bench, the arm she was building for Oziel lay half-assembled in its jig — a full prosthetic, articulated, the motor tendons still loose and waiting. Friday work.

He took off the jacket and set Silence against the wall in the usual spot, the near corner by the secondary bench where the blade wouldn't be in the way of anything. She had known it was there the first time he came in and set it down without asking, and she hadn't said anything then either.

He sat on the stool. The stool was not his. She had not told him to use it. He had been using it for four years.

In the lower-left of his overlay, the counter ran: 73:41. 73:42. He let his right hand rest on his knee, the chemical-burn scar on the palm turned up toward the bench light.

Pixel's hands moved over the sensor array with the unhurried precision she brought to everything she calibrated. The gyrostabilizer in her right wrist kept them steady in a way that went beyond pharmacology or training, a mechanical certainty she'd installed in herself at nineteen without asking anyone's permission. The array had a rhythm — a tool lifted, set, adjusted; a reading taken, noted, filed.

Kyle's boots sat on the floor.

He did not have a reason to be here. He stayed.

[Beat 128]
The calibration moved through its intervals. Kyle sat on the stool and watched the bench without watching Pixel and after a while he wasn't watching either of them, just the light, and the tools, and the quality of the quiet, which was not the quiet of an empty room.

She set a probe down and made a note on the small slate she kept for calibration records, and picked the probe back up. She worked through the next interval.

Kyle got off the stool.

He didn't have a reason for that either, but the stool was at the secondary bench and the sensor array was at the primary bench and his feet moved him across the floor with the same economy they moved him through everything, and he stopped. Pixel's arm was to his right. The sensor array was between them. Her elbow was at the edge of the work surface, angled in toward the component she was checking, and the space between his arm and hers was not much.

She didn't move. She kept working, the probe tracing the tolerance path it needed to trace, and she was not pretending he wasn't there — that was the thing, she was not pretending — she was simply continuing, and the calibration was not affected by his presence.

Kyle looked at the sensor array.

It was a three-axis unit, civilian grade, the kind used for environmental monitoring in enclosed spaces — infrasound range, low end of the spectrum, the frequencies that buildings and machines put out without knowing they're doing it. Seventeen to nineteen Hz, if you were in the right place. She had written the carousel cross-streets in the margin of her calibration notes three weeks ago, in the small mechanical hand she used for things she intended to find again. She had been building this since then — an instrument tuned to the specific range his neuretics kept logging as AMBIENT INTERFERENCE, TYPE UNCLASSIFIED. He had not asked what she was calibrating it for. He knew anyway. His own hardware couldn't name the thing at the carousel; she was building something that could. He had said one piece of it out loud, once, and she had started making a way to see the rest.

He reached toward the running-close marker — the same reflex that had found the bag seam in the dark at Northpoint. Pixel's probe came down, horizontal across his line of reach — not touching him, just in the way. Her hand was still. He withdrew.

"That one running hot?" he asked.

Pixel glanced at it. "It's within spec. The ambient humidity is skewing the read."

She did not look up at him.

The quiet came back. It was not hostile. That was the harder thing.

[Beat 129]
The calibration reached the parts-drawer stage. This happened in every calibration she ran - a point where the components needed swapping or replacing, and the drawer at the far end of the bench held the spares. Kyle had watched it happen enough times to know the sequence: probe down, drawer open, component selected, drawer shut, probe up.

Pixel reached past him.

Her arm crossed in front of his. The drawer was at the far end of the bench, past Kyle's position, and to reach it she had to extend past him, and she did - steady and unhurried, not pulling back from the proximity of it, not leaning in either, just reaching the way you reached across a bench for something you needed, and her arm passed within an inch of his. He felt the warmth of it. Not contact. The awareness of nearly.

She found the component she wanted and drew back, and then she reached forward again and pushed the drawer shut.

The latch caught.

It was a small sound - the specific mechanical click of a well-maintained drawer finding its seat, a sound Kyle had heard a hundred times in this room, clean and final, the kind of sound that meant a thing was where it was supposed to be.

Neither of them moved after.

Kyle opened his mouth.

One sound - not a word, not yet, a syllable that hadn't decided what it was going to be, that started somewhere in the throat and stopped before it got out, before it found the consonant that would have made it into something nameable.

Pixel's hands went still on the bench.

She had heard it. She was not looking at him. Her hands were flat on the bench surface, the probe horizontal between her fingers, and she was still, and the stillness was not the stillness of someone who hadn't noticed. It was the stillness of someone who had noticed and was holding herself quiet on purpose, and the space between that stillness and a word was the distance of a single breath, and Kyle was looking at the wall behind her, and his overlay read 59:38, 59:39 -

- 1:00:00.

He watched the counter roll.

Kyle closed it. The lower-left of his overlay went blank.

On the secondary bench, Oziel's arm sat in its jig, the motor tendons loose and waiting.

[Beat 130]
Kyle said her name.

She looked up from the array.

"I'll be in the armory if you need a second set of hands."

Pixel's hands lifted off the bench. She picked up the probe.

"I won't need one," she said.

Kyle moved toward the door. He didn't reach for his jacket yet — he picked up Silence first, the way he always did, the weight of it settling into the same grip it always settled into, and then he took the jacket off the hook he'd draped it over and put it on, and the whole sequence took eight seconds and he didn't hurry any part of it.

At the door he stopped. His hand found the frame — not the latch, not the handle, just the edge of the frame where the door sat in the wall. He stood there for a moment with the pneumatic seal a few inches from his palm, and the bench light was behind him and the hallway was in front of him, and he did not turn around.

He left.

The door closed on its seal — a small exhale of pressurized air, the mechanism doing exactly what it was built to do, no ceremony, no particular weight. It was the same sound the door made every time.

In 2E, Pixel picked up the tool she'd set down.

The calibration was where she'd left it, the tolerance markers still running, the probe still warm. She looked at the array. The 17-19 Hz band showed clean — baseline, no source, the same reading it had shown every night since she'd built the instrument. Whatever was at 18.7 Hz at the carousel, it wasn't here tonight. Which was either reassuring or the wrong question to be asking.

She moved back into the work.

On one side of her, the bench had six inches of empty space where Kyle had been standing.

She did not move to fill it.

[Beat 131]
He did not go to the armory.

He told himself he would go to the armory — the cool low-lit room off the stairwell where the gear lived and where the act of moving through a maintenance check had the useful quality of being mechanical, requiring just enough attention to occupy the part of his mind that would otherwise sit in 2E all night waiting for something that wasn't coming.

He went to his apartment instead. He sat at the table with the overlay open.

Three data points.

The first: an AMBIENT INTERFERENCE notation at the carousel park, 18.7 Hz, TYPE UNCLASSIFIED. Eight weeks ago. He had told Pixel about it. She had written the cross-streets in the margin of her calibration notes.

The second: a TIMING VARIANCE notation at Northpoint, EXTERNAL, SOURCE UNCLASSIFIED. The door of the concealment point, four seconds early. Tonight.

The third: Pixel's sensor array, 17-19 Hz band, reading clean.

He mapped them. The carousel park was in Pilsen, a mile and a half south. Northpoint was three miles north. His apartment was between them, offset east by a block. Pixel's lab was thirty feet across the hall.

The source-unclassified tag appeared on both the carousel notation and the Northpoint notation. Different neighborhoods. Different job types. Eight weeks apart. Both logged by his neuretics as external, neither logged with a mechanism or origin the system could identify.

Pixel's array, which she had tuned specifically to the 17-19 Hz band after the carousel conversation, had read clean tonight. The source was not in 2E. Or the source was not broadcasting tonight. Or the source was something that the array, calibrated for fixed-point environmental monitoring, would not catch if it was mobile.

He looked at those three data points for a long time.

He opened the relay. The routing shell had thirty-two characters. He had been staring at that string long enough that it had stopped looking like anything. He did not type a message. He did not ask the question. He was not sure what the question was yet, which was different from not being ready to ask it, but only barely.

He closed the relay.

He went to the armory.

[Beat 132]
﻿He pulled the shell's registration history.

It was public record - any freelancer could look up the operating certificate for a routing intermediary. Most didn't. The certificate listed an address in the West Loop, a management entity called Clearlight Operations LLC, and a registration date in 2209. Three years before Kyle had started working with the shell.

He searched for Clearlight Operations. The company had filed five years of annual compliance reports and then stopped filing. The address resolved to a commercial mail service that had closed in 2210. The shell, meanwhile, had kept operating - routing contracts, processing payments, maintaining active certification with the Chicago Freelancer Registry - without a listed human officer.

He got up from the terminal. He went to the kitchen and got a glass of water and stood at the window for a moment. The synthesis corridor was doing its 0100 thing: infrastructure maintenance crew on the south side, the pedestrian count low, the kind of quiet that belonged to people who kept this kind of hour. He finished the water. He went back to the terminal.

The CFR required a human officer of record. The shell had one: a name Kyle didn't recognize, a credential number that was valid, and an address that was a different mail service than the first one, also now closed.

He searched for the name. A valid CFR credential, a freelance operations license, and no other trace in any public system he could access. No social address. No ? account. No transaction record. A credential that existed, and a person who - as far as the public record was concerned - was not attached to it in any way that could be verified.

He went further back. The shell had been routing contracts since 2209. He had started working with it in 2215, through a contact who had since retired and whose forwarding address no longer resolved. He did not remember the referral specifically.

He ran the referral contact's name through the registry. The operating license had lapsed in 2218. The address on file was a service that had itself closed in 2220. He tried the social address - a placeholder, inactive since 2217. He checked the Freelancer Registry dispute log for any activity. There was none. The contact had, five years ago, stopped existing in any system that Kyle could access. That was possible. It happened. People left the work. But it usually left more trace than a lapsed license and an inactive account.

He sat with that.

He had not, in eleven years, asked how he had come to be working with a routing intermediary whose listed operator was a non-person and whose registered owner was a defunct LLC with a forwarding address that did not exist ? through a referral he could not trace because the person who made it had, themselves, become untraceable.

He had not asked because the shell had performed flawlessly, and in the work, performance was the answer to most questions.

[Beat 133]
He checked the distribution, the clustering, the geography, the timing. Sixty-three percent of his volume over eleven years, from one routing shell. The spread across job categories, districts, and intervals all fell within expected variance — no detectable cadence, no geographic center, no category clustering.

Nothing statistically anomalous.

Which was, he was aware, exactly what a careful selective operation would produce. A pattern designed to be invisible would look indistinguishable from no pattern. A curatorial intelligence operating below the threshold of detection would not cluster its choices — it would space them correctly, type them broadly, time them irregularly. It would look, at every level of analysis, like ordinary market behavior.

He sat with that for a while: something that didn't cluster, that spaced itself correctly, that looked from any angle like the thing it was pretending to be. He recognized it. He had been doing the same thing for twenty-two years — keeping his contacts distributed, his job types varied, his pattern below threshold. He had been careful for so long that it had stopped feeling like care and started feeling like the way things were.

He did not know when the shell had learned to do the same thing. He did not know if there had been a time when it hadn't.

He saved the file. He did not name it. He sat with the screen dark and the city running its 0130 shift and the armory two floors down.

He looked at the relay.

[Beat 134]
He opened a new relay message to the routing shell.

He typed: *I have a question about the Northpoint job.*

He read it. He deleted it. If the shell was what he was beginning to think it might be, that message was not a question - it was a notification. An indication that he was looking, sent directly to the thing he was looking at.

He typed: *What is Clearlight Operations LLC?*

He deleted it. The shell would either have no answer, in which case it confirmed nothing, or it would have an answer that he had no framework to evaluate.

He typed: *Who are you?*

He read those three words for a while. He deleted them.

He sat with the blank message field for a moment - the cursor running its patient interval in the empty text area - and then he typed one word.

*Received.*

He sent it.

He sat with the sent confirmation for a moment. It wasn't a question. It wasn't a probe. It was the word the shell had sent him — on his closing half-Φ, on the first thing it had ever said to him in eleven years — sent back. Returning the filing note. Acknowledging the folder. He didn't know if it would do anything, if anything was on the other end to receive it, if an entity that had been routing his contracts for eleven years needed his acknowledgment of its acknowledgment. But the message had gone out. He couldn't unsend it.

He closed the relay. He went to the armory.

[Beat 135]
He was in the armory for two and a half hours.

The maintenance check was thorough. He found nothing wrong with anything. The mesh held; Cacophony's cylinder was clean and the tolerances were where he kept them. Everything in the armory was in the condition it was supposed to be in, performing the function it had been designed for, traceable in its origin and its history. He knew what it was, where it had come from, and what he used it for. The armory did not have a thirty-two-character identifier and a shell company with a defunct address.

He came back upstairs at 0230. He made coffee. He stood at the window.

At 0247, the relay pinged.

New job. Same shell. Medical transport, short-haul, loading dock off South Michigan, 0600 contact window. The kind of logistics cover he had worked a dozen times.

Standard rate. Clean scope.

He stood there with the coffee going cold and the relay open and the job waiting, and he was aware — with the particular clarity that arrives at 0247 after two and a half hours of finding nothing wrong with anything — that eleven years of working with something and never asking what it was had arrived at this moment.

He opened the acceptance field. He confirmed the 0600 window. Then, in the note field — a field he had never in eleven years put anything in — he typed:

*Query re: Clearlight Operations LLC. Who holds the officer-of-record credential?*

He sent the acceptance.

He sat with the sent-confirmation for a moment. The note would route wherever the acceptance routed. Something at the other end would receive it. He didn't know if there was a human at the other end to read it, or something else, or nothing. But it had gone out. Eleven years, and the first question.

Across the hall, there was a bench with six inches of empty space. He had put it there.

He went to bed. He did not think about the frequency.

[Beat 136]
