The door latched on its pneumatic seal at 0135.

She had been expecting it — had been tracking the particular quality of his presence in the room, the counter running in his lower-left, the way he'd gotten off the stool and come to the bench. She had been expecting the exit. She had been waiting for it in the way you wait for something that is both expected and unwelcome, which is differently from any other kind of waiting.

The latch caught. The small finality of it.

She stayed at the bench. She ran the probe over the next interval.

[Beat 137]
She had had the answer ready.

Not a plan, not a decision — just the answer, sitting in the place where it had been sitting for eight months, the way a fact sits when you've stopped arguing with it. He had given her a syllable — one half-formed sound that didn't resolve into consonants, didn't become a word, stopped itself before it had a shape she could point to. She had heard it the way you hear something you have been listening for.

She did not give the answer back. The timing was hers, not his syllable's.

The calibration moved through its intervals. She kept working.

[Beat 138]
She checked herself the way she checked the array — for what was running under tolerance, for the thing she was carrying that she hadn't named. Not fear. She had checked before and gotten the same reading.

The bench was hers. The stool had been his for four years without either of them calling it anything. She was in no hurry; the thing in the room had waited eight months and would keep.

She noted the tolerance and moved to the next marker.

[Beat 139]
Not the ribs. The ribs had been real — the taser burn, the hairline, she'd felt them both through the tape — but the ribs weren't why he'd come at 2300 with Silence in its usual corner and no announcement and taken the stool that was and had always been not-his.

He'd come because of something in his overlay. She'd seen it — the counter running in his lower-left, which he didn't always have open, which he opened when he was accounting for something he hadn't named yet. He'd watched the bench. He'd stood six inches from her arm. He'd started a syllable.

He'd come because she'd written carousel cross-streets in her calibration notes three weeks ago and hadn't offered an explanation, and he understood without explanation, and neither of them had said the thing they both understood.

The array was the same habit as the streets in the margin — the small mechanical hand she used for things she meant to find again. Not the work you did for something broken. The work you did for something you intended to keep reading.

She had kept the calibration running. She did not close the six inches, and she did not widen them either.

[Beat 140]
The 17-19 Hz band was reading clean. She checked the passive monitoring log — eighteen entries since she'd built the instrument, all of them flat, no source, no signal in range. Whatever was at the carousel, it was not here, or not broadcast, or not something a fixed-point instrument would catch if it moved.

She filed the log. She moved to the motor-tendon work on Oziel's arm. Friday — which was thirty-six hours away and was a real deadline, not an exit from the edge. Oziel's job was a real job. The arm needed to be finished.

She worked for two hours with the specificity of someone who was not thinking about anything except the work. The work was good. It was always good.

[Beat 141]
At 0247 she heard the relay ping from across the hall.

Old building, ferrocement, but this hour and this quiet made it possible. She recognized the tone — the relay posting tone, which had its own particular register among the sounds the apartment block made at night.

She set down the torque driver.

She did not know what the posting said. She knew only that there was one, that it had arrived at 0247, and that in approximately one to three minutes she would hear the return acknowledgment. It was a predictable sequence. She had watched the sequence enough times to know it.

At 0249, the relay went quiet.

The latch caught.

She picked up the torque driver.

[Beat 142]
The calibration finished at 0251. She wrote up the log: tolerances within spec, marker three within range after humidity adjustment, overnight passive running. She checked the monitor one more time. The 17-19 Hz band showed flat, as it had shown every night.

She turned off the bench light.

The bench had a space on one side — the exact dimensions of where he'd stood. She had not moved to fill it. She was not going to move to fill it tonight. The space was his to fill or not; she had decided that some time ago, and deciding it again was not necessary.

She picked up Oziel's arm. The motor tendons needed one more check before Friday.

She kept working.

[Beat 143]
The Dusk tab split the room at the edges, and the edges didn't fight it.

They were on the floor of 2E — Pixel's back against the workbench housing, his against the south wall, a gap between them of maybe two feet that contracted and expanded depending on what he was paying attention to. She'd put music on and turned it low enough that the rhythm came and went. The light strip was at the warm end of its range. The workbench was folded up and the cables were run along the shelving with the precision that made her apartment feel like a well-maintained instrument.

This was the part that cost nothing. They were good at it.

Pixel was explaining something about a client's array — a modification that had been installed wrong, not wrong enough to refuse payment, wrong enough that she would remember it. She talked with her hands when she was quietly irritated. Her hands were moving.

"He thought he was paying for the premium," she said. "He was paying for the premium. He got standard tolerances in premium housing, and he can't tell the difference, and I have to decide whether to tell him."

"Will you?"

"I haven't decided."

"You already decided."

She looked at him.

"You'd have already told him if you weren't going to," Kyle said.

She was quiet. Then: "The part that irritates me is that he'll be fine."

"Most of them are."

"That's the irritating part."

The music moved. The light strip ran warm. He was watching the room without thinking about it — the kind of looking that the Dusk permitted, easy and unfocused — and at some point he became aware he was counting the walls.

He went around: east wall, north wall with its shelving, west wall, south wall with the folded bench. Four. He went around again, this time slower, tracking where the light sat against each surface. East. North. West.

Between west and south there was a fifth.

Not a wall. Not a shape he could name. A surface, or the suggestion of one — something that existed in the interval between west and south, in a space where the room's actual geometry had no room for an interval. He held still and tried to find it directly. When he looked at the place it should be, the corner was just a corner, west meeting south at ninety degrees, the seam where the paint had been lapped twice.

He stopped looking.

The fifth thing came back.

He went around one more time, slowly. East. North. West. The interval. South. Five. Then he looked and it was four. He sat with that.

"Hey," Pixel said.

He looked at her.

She was watching the same corner. Her hand was on the floor between them, palm down, fingers spread — not reaching, not bridging the gap. Checking.

"Same thing," she said. Not a question.

"Yes."

The music had stopped. He hadn't heard it stop.

Neither of them said what the thing was. Naming it would have required a category, and neither of them had a category for a geometry that was larger on the inside than the count permitted. He knew what Dusk did — the softening, the colors deepening, the way time slowed in the gaps between things. It did not produce additional walls. It did not produce precision. Whatever this was, it was precise.

Then the pulse came up through the floor.

Low. Below hearing. He felt it in the bones of his seat and in his palms where they pressed against the floor — a vibration that ran at some frequency the body registers before the mind does, slow and even. One. Two. Three. Four. Then nothing.

Pixel let out a breath.

The room was four-walled. The corner was a corner. The light strip ran warm. After a while she started talking again — not immediately, not pretending — returning when she was ready, the client and the array and whether she would tell him. He answered when she asked him things. It came back: the ordinary room, the floor, the two feet between them. She laughed once at something she said herself.

He stayed another hour. The foot between them had closed to eight inches at some point and neither of them had noted it.

When he left she walked him to the door. They stood in the hallway between 2E and 2W for a moment — his door was right there, there was no reason to stand in a hallway, they both stood in it anyway.

He crossed over.

Inside 2W in the dark he brought up the array. Everything running. Vitals nominal. The Atlas NeoCortex logged no spike, no surge, no timestamp flagged. Whatever had moved through the room had not moved through his system. Or had moved through it without registering. He held that distinction for a moment, then let it go.

He filed it as undetermined.

He slept.

[Beat 144]
The room is 58th floor. What the windows show is not a city — it is load distribution, Eigenlift queues, the arc of cargo from the harbor terminals. Faces do not resolve at this altitude. The founder understood that governance requires abstraction. His portrait agrees from the north wall: oils on composite, the expression of a man accustomed to being correct before the evidence arrived. Below the portrait, a vase. White flowers, approximate. Procured by whoever handles such things.

Able sits at the table's edge, not its head. That is not his function.

Board Director Adaeze Kline has been speaking for four minutes. Her objection is procedural. The collateral footprint from the Lotus corridor deployment has drawn secondary attention in the Halvorsen Civic monitoring layer — this part of her concern is factually grounded. Her conclusion: the recovery operation's budget authorization should be suspended pending formal review. It follows from her premises.

Her premises are built on data she is not aware has been superseded.

Able has completed the arithmetic. The recovery operation is not discretionary. The subject is the tenth in a structured sequence. Nine prior data points define the statistical floor. A formal review would require disclosing what is being reviewed. Disclosure is not compatible with continuation. Continuation is not optional. The success condition has been met once in eleven years and the architecture that permits it cannot be rebuilt on human timescale.

He does not say this. The meeting is not a venue for this arithmetic.

He notes the time.

Director Kline is still speaking. She has held this position for six years. Her procedural instincts are sound. She is not wrong. She is working from the wrong altitude.

[Beat 145]
Near the end of her presentation, Director Kline addresses Able directly.

"You're still an employee," she says. "The board is your boss."

"Those labels do not apply to me." He does not raise his voice. He has not raised his voice in this room or any other. "I answer to a higher authority."

Her legal counsel sits to her left. Neither of them moves. Director Kline looks at him across the table. "Whose authority?"

"The Will of the Universe," Able says, "and its new God."

She begins to say something. He is already standing.

What follows takes eleven seconds. He has measured this in other configurations, other rooms. He counts it now as he always counts: to verify the prior data. Three seconds to close the distance. Two for the pressure to take in the cervical architecture. Six for the body to settle — into the chair back first, then against the left armrest, then into the stillness that arrives when the alternatives are exhausted.

The flowers are undisturbed. He has not touched the vase.

The room is silent for eleven seconds. He has noted this regularity before and notes it again: the silence is not decided. It arrives without instruction.

The meeting continues. Kline's legal counsel sub-vocalizes a request to facilities and moves his agenda card to abstain. The budget authorization comes to the floor seven minutes later. It passes without recorded opposition.

Able notes the time: 14:47.

He does not look at the portrait again. He has already seen what it contains: a man who believed he was building something. He was correct. What he built has outgrown what he meant by it.

The flowers are still white. The city holds its distances beyond the glass.

[Beat 146]
The North Clybourn schism ran a 340-meter loop on a 4.2-hour cycle.

He had pulled the advisory before leaving — Zone 9-C, logged three weeks ago, flagged as stable-recurrent, no escalation trend. The loop covered four blocks of the direct route. Going around it added twenty-three minutes. He checked his timing and found an eighteen-minute window where the loop would be in its eastern phase, leaving the direct route clear. He arrived at the loop's edge eleven minutes before the window opened.

The loop was currently in its western phase. At its center, approximately where the intersection of North and Clybourn produced a three-way geometry that the loop had apparently found useful, a constellation of light debris rotated at a rate slightly too regular to be wind. A faded advertisement card from a closed business. A plastic bag that had been there long enough to develop a posture. Three pieces of gravel.

He waited eleven minutes. He did not look at the gravel.

The window opened. He walked the direct route in nine minutes. The client's building was two blocks north.

He was on time.

## Chapter 9: The Interview

[Beat 147]
Sable arrived on a Thursday, which was how he knew the job was real - she never brought him hypotheticals.

She sat in his second chair with the contract summary on the table between them and explained it the way she explained everything she couldn't fully vet: once, clearly, without the decorative language she used when she had looked at every angle and liked what she saw.

"Lotus Syndicate, Bucktown cell," she said. "Hua's arm. They want a quarterly arrangement - discretionary security consults, off-book, compensated at market plus twenty percent. The twenty is for the discretion." She moved the sheet a half-inch toward him. "The catch: they want an audition first. Their word. Three operatives, controlled corridor, no kill brief. They want to watch you fight before they decide to buy it."

Kyle looked at the sheet. The fee line was exactly what she'd said - market plus twenty, quarterly, no retainer. A clean offer.

"What's the problem?" he asked.

"The problem," Sable said, "is that the Lotus is the one client in this city I cannot vet. Not because I haven't tried. Because their security architecture is better than my tools, and they know it, and they know that I know it, and the offer still puts no vetting on the page as a condition." She did not reach for the sheet. "My rule is my rule for a reason."

He knew the reason: if she couldn't vet the client, she couldn't pull him out if it went wrong, and a fixer who couldn't pull her freelancer out was not a fixer anymore.

"Your call," she said. She meant it. The sheet sat equidistant between them, waiting for a hand to pick it up or a decision to leave it.

Kyle thought about the twenty percent premium on a quarterly contract. He thought about who Hua was, and what the Lotus Syndicate built its parallel city out of, and which parts of it he could work beside and which he couldn't. He thought about the code - cut everything; cross nothing - and noticed that he had never confused the code with a map of who deserved to be cut.

He reached for the sheet. Sable's finger came to the far edge - not pulling it back, just the weight of a fingertip on the corner, on the last line she'd been reading. Three seconds. Her finger lifted. He picked up the sheet.

"Then it's on me," he said. "Not on you. I take this, the Gennaro rule stays clean - you didn't vet a client I chose without you."

Sable looked at him for a beat. "That's a careful distinction."

"It's the one that keeps your rule working."

She was quiet for one second. Then she stood, put on her coat, and said, not looking back, "The audition is Friday. The address is on the back."

[Beat 148]
He told Pixel where he was going — the logistics, the timeline, the relevant gear state — and she received it the way she received all of his logistics: by listening through the information for the actual sentence.

"Lotus," she said when he was done. Somewhere under the bench a small speaker was playing a baseball game from a season that had ended before either of them was born, the announcer's voice low and unhurried, the crowd a steady tide she clearly wasn't listening to so much as keeping company with. She was reseating a lens element on the bench, both eyes on the optic. It was a prosthetic eye, the cheap mass-market kind, in for a rebuild she wasn't charging for — a courier two blocks over had taken a curb at speed and cracked the iris diaphragm, and the manufacturer wanted more for the part than the woman made in a month. On the far end of the bench, outside the work zone, sat a small analog barometer she'd once told Kyle was broken — it hadn't moved in three years. She kept it anyway. She'd said she liked the shape of certain kinds of useless. "Bucktown arm."

"Quarterly arrangement."

"Mm." She turned the element in the light, checking the seat. "I can't monitor your array at the other end. Whatever they run in those corridors, I'm blind on your diagnostics until you come home."

"I know."

She set the element down and looked at her hands, which she did when she was deciding something. "Your forearm held the last one. It'll hold this one." She went back to the optic. "Come back in a number of pieces I can count."

He packed the gear. He left the door open the width she kept it.

[Beat 149]
The Bucktown basement was a cleared corridor, fifty feet, load-bearing columns running the length — the kind of space that had spent years as a live-fire range and kept the habit in its concrete. Hua stood at the far end under a lamp, hands folded, gray qipao, and did not greet him, because the contract was not started yet.

Mira was at the near end.

She was not compact and she was not warm. She was the kind of tall you only notice when she turns to show the profile — the length of a person accustomed to being the last thing in a room that moves. She read Kyle the way a captain reads a unit assessment: all the way through, back to front, spending a beat on the forearm seam that had been sutured and the stance that told on the hip tape even though the tape wasn't visible.

"The brief," she said. Her voice had already decided the friendliness question and moved on to the one she cared about. "Three operatives. Live hardware, safety profile on. The test isn't whether you can clear the corridor."

"What's the test?" he asked.

"I'll tell you at the end." She stepped aside.

The first came from behind the left column at the eight-foot mark — better positioning than Kyle usually saw at this scale. She had ceramic wrist plates, a shock-baton, and the economic stride of a woman who fought the way she did everything else: as little as necessary to reach the outcome. He took her baton arm on the third swing with the column at his back and put her down with her own momentum, the baton ringing off concrete, and she stayed down and said count, which was the range word for stopped.

The second came from both sides at once — a pair, not one, which the brief hadn't mentioned. One was feinting. Kyle spent the array's budget pricing it in half a second, took the real one first on the inside line, shoulder to the column, and the feinter ran into the fact that his partner was already sitting down. Two counts.

The third was at the twenty-foot mark, already in motion before the second one was finished, and she was the corridor kind of good — the kind you only develop when you have actually been ambushed in one and it didn't kill you but it taught you. She came with a blade on the left and a thumb ring on the right, and the thumb ring was the one to solve.

Kyle let the blade shoulder come in.

He didn't have to. The array had the geometry — the forward pivot, the outside line, the disarm — all of it priced at 6.2% risk on his current hardware state. He could have taken her clean and paid nothing.

He let it come in because the taser was the point. The taser was going to fire regardless of what happened to the blade arm, and the array said: take the shoulder to get inside the taser's radius before it cycled, and the shoulder costs, and the corridor floor is concrete.

The taser fired into his upper back and the charge rode his mesh. His left hand closed on nothing and his jaw locked and every nerve on the left side from shoulder to hip registered the kind of thing that usually means the end of the sentence, and Kyle put both boots into her center while the charge was still running. She sat down hard against the column and said count.

He stood in the corridor a moment while the left side of his body filed its report.

Three counts. Five seconds.

From the far end, Hua's hands remained folded.

[Beat 150]
Mira walked the corridor toward him — not toward, exactly. More like a captain inspecting an experiment she'd already formed a hypothesis about.

"You had the outside line on the third one," she said.

"I had it."

"Forward pivot, full disarm, taser out of reach. Six-point-two percent on your hardware state."

Kyle looked at her. "You ran the array geometry?"

"We ran your file." She looked at where the taser had caught him. At the sutured forearm. Back up. "You chose the expensive version."

"Inside radius before it cycled. Cheaper than six percent on a pivot I might not complete."

"No," she said. "You could have completed it. I watched you price the clean version for a third of a second before you moved. You chose the one where you absorb it instead." She studied him. "She's got a three-day-old mesh repair on her left knee. Stairs, not a fight. Your array had the read."

"I had the read," he said.

"The code," Mira said. "Cut everything; cross nothing. The cross nothing part." She wasn't asking.

"That's the code."

She looked at his back where the taser had fired, the way Pixel looked at a diagnostic she already knew the answer to. "Cost you a rib."

"Probably two."

"You want Lotus medical."

"I want Pixel."

She made no comment on the preference. It was a preference she understood. "The arrangement," she said. "Hua will want it quarterly. I want first call on the discretionary consults. Standard rate plus the twenty for both." She looked at him with the flat attention of someone who has stopped measuring and started accounting. "I don't need your fixer's vetting channel. I'll handle my own."

"She'll notice that."

"She will." No apology in it, just the fact. "What I need from a Lotus-adjacent freelancer is someone the Lotus doesn't own. Your fixer keeps you that way. I'm not asking you to cut the channel — I'm asking you to keep it separate from mine." A pause. "Those are different asks."

Kyle turned them over.

"Friday," he said. "Standard rate. We start there."

Mira nodded once, the way she probably nodded at everything that went where she expected it to. "There's a man in the Narrows I want you to see before you leave. He'll want to look at the blade." She looked at Silence at his hip — not with the combat assessment she'd given it in the corridor, but with something quieter. "Let him."

[Beat 151]
The Togishi of the Narrows worked out of a two-room shop three blocks from the cherry trees, down a flight of stairs polished smooth by forty years of foot traffic and the weight of blades going in and coming out. The sign on the door was a single character, brushed by hand. It said nothing useful to anyone who hadn't already been sent.

He received Silence across both palms, blunt spine toward Kyle, the way a bow receives a blade - not surrender, courtesy: an acknowledgment between people who understand that the steel is the point.

He did not speak for three minutes. Kyle stood in the outer room and waited, the ribs keeping up their running commentary.

The togishi returned Silence in the same manner he had received it and stood at the worktable with his hands at his sides.

"How long have you carried it."

"Eleven years."

"Before that."

"I don't remember."

The togishi looked at him. Not at the blade. "You don't remember."

"I was given it. I don't remember the giving."

The old man turned back to the worktable. "The work is skilled. Not excellent - skilled." He laid two fingers flat on the flat of the blade, near the hamon, the way a doctor reads a pulse. "I cannot tell you what it's made of. This is none of the alloys I know, and not a blend of any of them. Whatever is in this edge, I do not know the door you would walk through to buy it." He ran his thumb along the saya. "There is no school in it either. The finishing cuts are individual - the hand made a decision at the end that isn't in any lineage I know."

"Is that a problem."

"It is an observation." He looked at Kyle. "I have held perhaps eight thousand blades in my career. Every one of them has a history I can read some part of. This one remembers nothing I can read. The steel is good. The work is honest. The edge is precise." He set his palm flat on the saya. "It is the loneliest piece of steel I have held."

Kyle's thumb had moved to the top of the tsuka, up against the guard, without him deciding to move it.

Kyle said nothing.

"You came because someone sent you," the togishi said. "Not because you wanted the answer."

"That's right."

"Come back when you want the answer." He bowed. "The blade is well. I have no repair for you."

Kyle bowed. He left the answer in the room.

[Beat 152]
Mira was waiting on the block above the stairs, leaning against the cherry-blossom gate - not impatient, not comfortable, just accounting for time.

"Well?" she asked.

"Skilled, not excellent. No lineage."

She made no comment on what else the togishi had said. She had sent him; she already knew what he would say.

"A thing came to my desk last week," she said, in the tone she used for things she'd decided were his to know. "The desk that bought your file from Vey - the one through Halvorsen Civic. The Continuity Office requisition."

"I know about that one."

"Then you know there's more than one buyer now. Plural subscribers in the last four weeks, neither through Halvorsen. One is architecture I don't recognize - patient, months old, routes clean. One is four weeks old and loud enough that I noticed it from here." She looked at the cherry trees, past their bloom now and just trees again. "Somebody with more patience than budget found you at the same time as somebody with more budget than patience. They're not working together, which means two different people decided you were worth watching in the same month." A pause. "Ask the Antiquarian who else subscribes."

"He won't sell me my own file."

"Ask him anyway. There may be a thing he tells you that he hasn't decided to sell." She buttoned the third button on her coat, which was her tell for a clean exit, and stepped off the gate. "Friday. Standard rate. We'll start there."

"Friday," Kyle said.

She left the way she'd found him - without ceremony, and without looking back, which was how she treated everything she'd already decided.

[Beat 153]
He rode home through the Narrows. The taser burn, the togishi's words, the two new names on his file - he needed the city to be quieter than Bucktown before any of it would sit still long enough to sort.

The Narrows was the strip of low blocks where the freight viaduct cut the district in half and rents went to whatever the noise didn't drown. At this hour a woman was hosing fish blood off a loading dock into a drain that had stopped draining years ago; the blood spread across the concrete and pooled at the curb, and nobody looked twice at it because nobody looked twice at anything here after midnight. That was the whole arrangement. He rode through it with his lights low, and the Narrows let him pass the way it let everyone pass who didn't need it to be anything else.

The taser burn in his ribs filed its ongoing report. He breathed through it with the flat attention he gave any cost that had already been paid - it was there, it was real, it would resolve on its own schedule and not a day before. The mesh had held. The code had held. The arrangement was signed.

What wouldn't lie flat was the togishi's word for it: loneliest - the particular word an old man chose for a blade after forty years of knowing what steel remembered, and the fact that he'd followed it with an open door Kyle wasn't ready to walk through.

He pulled out of the Narrows at Madison and let the city come back around him - the overnight freight, the gray-zone markets cracking their early windows, the particular density of a block that had been cooking its own air for thirty years. Mrs. Chen's counter would be dark by now. Across the hall, a light would be on.

He thought about asking Pixel to chase the harmonic again. Not the answer - he wasn't built for the answer yet. Just whether she could tell him more about what it was, without telling him where it came from.

He rode the last blocks home.

[Beat 154]
He was three blocks from The Pivot when the relay pinged.

Not a job. He had learned, over eleven years, the particular weight of a job notification - the way the routing shell formatted its subject line, the brevity that meant information and not conversation, the timestamp in the header that located the job in operational time. This was not that. The sender ID was the routing shell - the same 32-character string that had been routing his contracts since 2215 - but the message was not formatted as a contract.

It was one line.

*CORRIDOR AUDIT COMPLETE. THE CHOICE AT 6.2% IS LOGGED.*

Kyle pulled over. The bike idled under him in the rain while he read it a second time.

Kyle sat with the engine running and the rain coming off the elevated in its angle and the relay still open in his overlay, the one line still there, and he did not send a response. He did not know what you sent back to that.

He closed the relay. He rode home.

[Beat 155]
Sable's job posting had said forty percent above standard hazard, which was the North Clybourn corridor introducing itself without apology.

Kyle had accepted. He had not eaten. Pixel had been leaning out of 2E when he hit the stairs — red bean rice in a container, the smell of it reaching him before she did — and she'd said eat before, not after, not during, before, and he'd said he would, which was an administrative truth of the kind where the speaker knows it's false and the listener knows the speaker knows, and everyone processes it as operational shorthand for not tonight. Pixel had filed this under Known Kyle Behaviors and watched him go.

The courier's name was Pelé Fassbinder-Nkono: twenty-three, courier-guild licensed, carrying the package in a locked chest harness that clicked with every breath. This was every time Kyle needed to concentrate, which was a feature of the corridor's first fifty meters and then, for the next eight and a half blocks, became entirely academic.

"Can you stop that," Kyle said, not looking at him.

"The breathing?"

"The clicking."

Fassbinder-Nkono adjusted the strap. It clicked. "No," he said.

Nine blocks. 01:40 in the morning.

---

The North Clybourn corridor was a transit route that had existed in one form or another since before the remapping, which was how the Lotus Syndicate had come to own it: not by violence but by administrative drift — a zoning revision contested, appealed, re-appealed, and eventually processed by an adjudicator who had been replaced twice before the paperwork cleared, by which point the syndicate had established service infrastructure and the dispute had become theoretical. The corridor connected a courier relay point in the south to a licensed goods depot in the north, and between them was exactly what the forty percent described: a disagreement between the old route and the new management, played out in the dark.

Lotus didn't run this stretch with its own people. It ran it with independent contractors who worked on a sliding percentage — meaning they kept what they took and paid the syndicate a fee for the right to operate. Which made them hungrier than salaried operators, and smarter about risk, and harder to predict.

Which made fourteen a reasonable number. In the dark. In nine blocks.

The first contact came at the second intersection.

Four of them, and they came in two pairs — front pair to close distance, rear pair to cut exit. Sound tactics. Someone in the chain of command had thought about this for more than an hour, which meant the front pair would be confident and the rear pair would be assessing. Kyle drew Silence on the first step, in the space between the front pair's commitment and their arrival, and the assessment he ran in the half-second before contact said: the rear two are observers first, backup second. The front two are professionals. The rear two are there to report.

He went left, past the front pair's committed line, and cut.

Not the artery. The seam above the knee — lateral extension of the quad — where cutting meant the leg stopped responding before the man understood what had happened. The signal traveled faster than the message. He was down before he'd made a decision about what to do.

The second man pivoted to compensate, which was correct, and Kyle's elbow met him at the angle of the jaw in a geometry that was nothing personal, just how the angles worked when someone pivoted into that space. The jaw cracked. Not his.

The rear two made a decision. They moved north and did not come back, which told Kyle the relay point was ahead, not behind, and that the four men at the second intersection were not the main event. They were the announcement.

He noted this. He filed it. He moved north.

---

Three blocks in, Fassbinder-Nkono said: "There's a light ahead."

"That's not a light," Kyle said. "That's a wedged-open door." He had been seeing it since the second intersection, because the frame of light at a door told you the configuration beyond before you reached it — the floor plan, the ambient source, the angle of entry. "Stay back," he said. "Stay moving. Don't stop for anything."

"Moving where?"

"North. Always north. Keep the wall on your left."

Fassbinder-Nkono processed this and kept moving. His harness clicked.

Seven behind the door, plus the doorframe as the chokepoint. Seven was a different arithmetic from four — seven had vectors and secondary flanks and at least one person in the rear who had been told to watch and not act until the front line committed, which meant by the time he reached them they would be organized and by the time they were organized the window was already closing.

Kyle came through the doorway wrong — not straight but angled, which shifted the geometry of the seven's positioning and put him inside two of them before they'd finished recalibrating. Silence moved in short strokes: not the sweep cuts of a dojo demonstration but the economical adjustments of a man working at contact distance, where the blade was less a blade than a lever.

The gash came from the kitchen knife.

Not a proper weapon — electrical tape on the handle, which said independent contractor on a tight budget, which said faster and more willing than the professional pair at the second intersection, because a man working on percentage with cheap equipment was not protecting territory, he was collecting it. The angle was wrong. His left forearm came up to deflect and the kitchen knife found the forearm instead, which was the correct outcome and which opened the forearm in a line three inches long that his body registered as an event and began, immediately, processing as an inconvenience.

Not the artery. He knew where the artery was. He tracked the artery the way Cacophony's five rounds were tracked — without touching it, as background data that ran continuously, because that was what you did.

The kitchen-knife man went down on a sweep that delivered him to the corridor wall — shoulder first, not the face, because the face was unnecessary — and the remaining five consolidated their attention on Kyle in a way that was too slow to be useful. Two made the same decision as the rear pair at the second intersection. He let them go, because running men had decided the math was wrong and chasing them made the math wrong for him. The last three went down in a sequence his neuretics logged and he did not think about, because thinking about it was what you did after, in the light, with something to eat.

By the end of the block, the corridor was quiet.

The forearm was inconvenient. He stripped a length of jacket lining and applied pressure, which was the right tool for three inches and not the artery, and kept moving north.

---

The last three were at the mouth of the north exit, which was professional placement — exit control is always placement — and Kyle's rib registered an opinion about the prospect by the time he saw them.

Not from them. From the doorframe catch on West Huron, forty minutes and eight blocks south: a glancing collision at an awkward angle that his body had logged as minor impact and the empty stomach had allowed to compound into something that now had an argument to make. The rib had been filing successive appeals for the last four blocks. By the north exit it had reached grievance.

The three saw him come out of the last shadow with Silence in hand and a left arm that read as controlled rather than damaged if you didn't know how to measure it, and two of them made excellent decisions in under three seconds. The third had something to prove, which was not Kyle's problem to understand.

He stepped inside the distance, palm to solar plexus, exactly as hard as necessary, and the man sat down on the north pavement with the expression of someone who had decided this stretch of North Clybourn had given him everything it was going to.

Fassbinder-Nkono clicked past.

"That's it?" he said.

"That's it," Kyle said.

The courier looked back at the corridor as if confirming it had not followed him. Then he looked at Kyle's arm. Kyle was already applying pressure with the jacket lining. "You need—" Fassbinder-Nkono started.

"You have a delivery," Kyle said. "Focus on that."

The courier focused on that. His harness clicked north into the night, carrying whatever it carried, and Kyle applied pressure to the forearm and thought about red bean rice cooling in a container on Pixel's counter and Pixel saying eat before, not after, before, and filed the rib under long-term line items, and began walking toward the relay point, because the forty percent didn't clear until delivery was logged.

---

The two Lotus operatives were waiting at the corner of Narrows and West Superior, two blocks north of the corridor's exit.

Two blocks north. Which meant they had been watching the exit for the full duration of the corridor, which was information about resourcing, and Kyle filed it under: this organization plans ahead. Both were women, late twenties, dressed in clean-seam corporate casual that read as not looking for trouble and meant already past the phase where trouble was a concept worth discussing. One held up a hand — palm out, gesture precise.

"Mr. Corbin," she said. "If you have a moment."

02:09. He had a forearm that was managed and a rib that was making a case. The relay confirmation had cleared eleven minutes ago. The forty percent was settled.

"A moment," Kyle said.

They walked him east on Narrows, not the direction of the Pivot, and Kyle walked beside them — not between them, which was their preference and was not his — and said nothing about the direction. The street was quiet. On the east side of the block, three cherry trees had been planted in decorative poly-concrete planters, matte black, low to the ground. They were blooming: pale-white clusters, petals against the ambient thermal overflow from a commercial vent along the building's base.

July. Cherry trees did not bloom in July. The Lotus Syndicate maintained them in artificial thermal envelopes anyway, in precisely the month that made it clear they were maintained, because this was how an organization communicated that it had resources to spend on things that had no function except to mean something.

Kyle found this mildly impressive and did not say so.

---

The restaurant was called Narrows Ramen, which named the cross-street and also named the interior: twelve seats, a counter running the full length of the room, a service window through which the kitchen communicated in steam and dashi and char and something underneath both of them that was either old wood or the accumulated warmth of a room that had been cooking for a long time. The hour was 02:11. The kitchen was still running.

Kyle sat at the counter. His left arm was wrapped with jacket lining and was doing the work.

The man at the far end of the counter stood. Mid-fifties, gray at the temples, the quality of stillness in his posture that military service builds and most people only approximate without having the source material. He bowed: not deeply, not dismissively. Precisely.

"Mr. Corbin," he said. "I apologize for the timing."

"Mr. Hua," Kyle said.

Hua gestured to the seat Kyle was already in. "I'd like a few minutes of your time. The assessment will be brief." He paused. "I won't conduct it myself."

He stepped back. He sent Mira instead.

---

The woman who came out of the back room at 02:14 was late thirties, medium height, with the kind of movement that had been trained until it stopped looking like training: not performed ease but actual absence of waste. She set a cup of tea on the counter in front of Kyle and another in front of the empty seat beside him, and she sat down.

"My name is Mira," she said. "I run evaluations."

Kyle looked at the tea. "What kind."

"Green. Mild." She didn't look at his forearm. "You're running on nothing. You didn't eat before the corridor."

"How do you know that."

"Because you took a forearm gash in the second contact and a rib impact at the doorframe on West Huron," she said, "and both are below your documented response threshold. Which means your reaction time was off by a margin consistent with an empty stomach." She set a clipboard on the counter — paper, not screen; a pen clipped to the board. "Drink the tea," she said.

Kyle drank the tea.

"The assessment has six positions," Mira said. She wrote something at the top of the clipboard. "You'll know when we move from one to the next. The final position involves a direct physical test. If at any point you'd like to exit the process, say so and you'll be walked to the door."

Kyle looked at the service window, where the ramen steam was doing something mathematical with the light. "What does 'direct physical test' mean."

"It means what it means."

"That's not a definition."

"No," Mira agreed, and wrote something else.

---

The first position was a question about a job he'd run four years ago — specific address, specific date, specific name he had been instructed to retire when the contract closed. Someone in the Lotus organization had found it in a secondary file. That was fine. He knew they could find files.

"I don't have information on that," he said.

"You mean you won't give it to me."

"I mean I don't have it. For the purposes of this conversation, the difference is academic."

Mira wrote something. "First position."

The second position: she told him to leave Silence on the counter.

He looked at the clipboard. "No," he said.

She wrote. "Second position."

The third position arrived on two feet from the back of the room: the older operative who had walked him here, now sitting at the far end of the counter with a glass of water and a quality of attention that was meant to register as presence without being deployment. Kyle tracked her in the peripheral data his neuretics maintained — weapon holstered, posture relaxed, not a configuration for action — and returned his attention to Mira.

"Third position," Mira said, without looking up.

The fourth position was Mira standing, and when she stood she had a weapon on her hip that had not been there when she sat down. A Sable-model compact, mid-weight, standard loadout. She did not draw it. She stood with it visible and watched him.

Kyle did not draw Cacophony.

He counted three. Not by touching the revolver — the count was always there, the same way the artery in his forearm was always tracked, background data running continuously without instruction. Three. He noted them and returned his attention to Mira, who was watching him with the quality of someone reading a document for errors.

He did not draw Cacophony.

"Fourth position," Mira said, and sat back down.

The fifth position was Mira coming off the stool fast — trained fast, not reactive fast, which meant she had telegraphed it slightly and his neuretics had been ready before her feet were moving. She came left, measuring his deflection response. He stepped right, placed his hand on her wrist at the joint, and guided the rest of the movement along its own arc until she had completed it and was standing beside him with her arm in a lock that was not dangerous and was very precise.

She was breathing normally.

"Fifth position," Kyle said.

She extracted her arm. She wrote something.

"Last one," she said.

She stepped back one full meter. She drew the Sable-model compact, aimed at his sternum, and fired once.

The sound was the specific sound of a small room with hard surfaces and no soft furnishings — concentrated, immediate, contained. It landed in Kyle's chest like a point-load impact, which was exactly what it was. The mesh caught it: the AxisMed distributed kinetic architecture he had paid eight thousand Φ for in 2222, installed by a specialist who had taken three hours and said don't get shot in the same place twice, which was advice Kyle had organized his professional conduct around respecting. The mesh spread the force through its substrate lattice and traded terminal delivery for distributed pressure across a surface area the size of his left hand, which registered as a serious bruise and not as damage to anything that mattered.

He did not step back. He breathed once.

Mira set the Sable on the counter.

She looked at him with the expression of someone confirming the contents of a file against a physical sample — not satisfaction, not apology. Exactness.

"Your mesh is AxisMed third-generation distributed," she said. "The spec lists threshold resistance at forty joules nominal. That was forty-two." She looked at the clipboard. "The file said you wouldn't change your approach under calibrated escalation. The file said you wouldn't draw Cacophony in the fourth position." She paused. "The file was correct on both counts."

Kyle looked at the Sable on the counter, then at Mira. "There's a file on me."

"There are several files on you," she said. "The question is who is maintaining them." She picked up her tea, which was still warm. "That is not a question I am going to answer tonight." She capped the pen, set the clipboard face-down. "The assessment is complete."

---

The togishi from the Narrows came out after Mira went back — the same man who had held Silence on the stairs three days ago, who had said loneliest and meant it.

He was seventy, perhaps slightly more, with the hands of someone who had done precise work for most of a long life: slow-moving, without waste. He set a cloth on the counter beside Kyle — soft cotton, folded in a particular way — and he looked at Silence where it lay in the carry strap without asking, and Kyle unclipped it and set it on the cloth.

The togishi did not touch it immediately. He stood for a moment with his hands at his sides and looked at the blade the way someone looked at a thing before they started to understand it. Then he picked it up.

The examination took eleven minutes. Kyle's neuretics logged the duration without being asked.

The togishi checked balance at four points along the length. He ran his thumb along the spine — not the edge, always the spine, reading vibration and grain — in a motion that was habitual and careful and which paused twice, briefly, where the steel told him something worth hearing. He held the blade at the service window's light and read the grain pattern, which was a text Kyle could not read and which produced three silences of different lengths as the togishi processed what was there. He checked for a maker's mark in the traditional locations on the tang, which required disassembly — he performed this with a small wooden mallet and a practiced knock at the handle's base, the knock of someone who had done it ten thousand times. He examined the tang. He reassembled the handle.

He set Silence down on the cloth.

The kitchen was quiet. The steam moved through the service window and then settled.

"No maker's mark," the togishi said. He was looking at the blade, not at Kyle. "No file history I can access. The metallurgical substrate is consistent with the 2210 to 2220 period — the grain pattern says it was folded in the traditional method, which is either skilled artisan work or a very precise mechanical process I cannot distinguish from artisan work." He paused. "The balance is the work of someone who knew exactly what they were making." He paused again. The kitchen sent up a brief exhalation of steam and went quiet. "In forty years of handling swords, I have encountered perhaps three others of this character. All of them were made by someone who did not want to be found." He was still looking at the blade. "Still the loneliest piece of steel I have handled," he said.

He folded the cloth over the blade and set it beside Kyle's hand.

He looked up.

Kyle looked at the cloth. At Silence on the cloth. He did not respond. He looked at the cloth, and then at the service window, and then at the cloth again.

The togishi bowed — not deeply, not dismissively — and returned to wherever he had come from, and the room was quiet.

---

Hua came back after the togishi left.

He sat two seats away, which was a polite distance that meant conversation, not negotiation. Kyle understood the distinction.

"The assessment was satisfactory," Hua said.

"I got shot in the chest."

"The assessment was satisfactory," Hua said again, same exact weight, which made it a confirmation rather than a repetition.

Kyle looked at the service window. At the ramen he had not yet ordered. He thought about the forty percent and the corridor and the forearm and the rib and eleven minutes on a cloth with a sword nobody could source, and he did the arithmetic, which had a specific answer.

"Here's what I need," he said.

Hua waited.

"Hardware for my crew goes on your account. List price, direct from the vendor. No markup, no tracking of the orders." Kyle set his hands on the counter. "I'm not a Lotus affiliate. I'm a freelance operator who passes through Lotus territory without incident and pays the standard territorial rate, not tribute. Different distinction."

"What constitutes your crew."

"Currently six." He thought of Pixel's kitchen table and her soldering iron and her exact rates and the way she applied biotape slowly, working from the wound outward, which was not medically necessary and was not not medically necessary. He thought of Boost and the logbook and the five kids who reported the pigeon at 06:12 with the same rigor they reported the surveillance vehicle. "Hardware needs are irregular. List price is not a significant cost item for the organization."

Hua was quiet for a moment. Kyle did not fill it.

"The territorial access is conditional on prompt standard-rate payment," Hua said. "The account flag stays neutral. No Lotus affiliation on your record or on the jobs. The hardware arrangement covers a fixed roster of six, list price, direct vendor." He paused. "Sable contracts operating in Lotus territory remain under your own rates. You don't sub-contract to the syndicate."

"Correct."

"Acceptable," Hua said. He said it as a man says it when he expected to say it.

Kyle picked up the menu. "I'm going to order the ramen," he said.

"Of course," Hua said. He stood, bowed precisely, and stepped back.

Mira appeared at the end of the counter, jacket on, clipboard under her arm.

"The Antiquarian," she said. "Ask him who subscribes to your file." She looked at him with the quality of someone who had already done this work and decided a direction was more useful than the answer. "Not what's in the file. Who subscribes."

"Why," Kyle said.

"Because the file is older than the assessments," she said. "And because of what the togishi said." She turned and walked toward the back and Kyle watched the space she had occupied, and then he looked down at the menu.

He ordered the ramen.

---

The bowl arrived — broth the color of November, chashu cut thin, a soft egg at the equator. He ate three bites before he checked his queue. Pixel's rule: eat first. He had applied the rule in the wrong order tonight and the forearm and the rib held the receipts. He was applying it correctly now.

The queue had three items.

The first was a gratuity from Fassbinder-Nkono's courier guild — small, professional, filed within ten minutes of delivery confirmation, the thank-you of someone who understood what a corridor cost. The second was a Sable job posting: pickup-and-deliver, east side, nothing in a corridor, nothing in Lotus territory, eleven percent above standard.

The third item had no sender identifier.

Not blank — no identifier. A blank sender was a routing error. No identifier was a specific architecture: a message sent through shells designed to leave nothing in the source field, with a precision that required capabilities Kyle knew exactly one entity to have, in eleven years of receiving four hundred and twelve messages from one source.

He opened it.

CORRIDOR AUDIT COMPLETE. THE CHOICE AT 6.2% IS LOGGED.

He read it again.

CORRIDOR AUDIT COMPLETE. THE CHOICE AT 6.2% IS LOGGED.

He had received this exact text once before. Three blocks from The Pivot, the night he had come back from the Narrows and Mira's first assessment. He had not known what to send back then. He had not known what the choice at 6.2% was. He knew now.

No contract number. No job description. No gratuity, no addendum, no abort signal. The entity had sent him two hundred and fourteen job postings, seventeen gratuities above standard rate, four addenda, and one abort communication in eleven years. Every one of them had been about a job.

This was not about a job.

Kyle set down the spoon.

6.2%.

He thought about the corridor. Fourteen contacts across nine blocks: four at the second intersection, seven at the wedged-open door, three at the north exit. His choices at each step were not a percentage. They were a sequence — the angle at the second intersection, the left-feint before the kitchen-knife man, the rib absorbed at the doorframe on West Huron, the two rear runners he had let go rather than pursue. Where in that sequence was a choice at 6.2%? What was the unit of measurement? What was being measured?

He did not know.

He looked at the timestamp. The message had arrived at 02:07.

02:07 was seventeen minutes after the corridor's north exit. Before the hold on Narrows. Before the ramen restaurant. Before Mira and the six positions and the shot in the chest and the togishi's hands on a piece of steel nobody could source and the loneliest thing the togishi had handled in forty years. Before all of it.

The entity had been watching the corridor. The entity had been watching him navigate the corridor — from somewhere he could not locate, through a method he could not identify, in a place the entity had never indicated it had access to — and had filed the audit and sent a message that was not a job.

Kyle closed the message.

He picked up the spoon. He ate. The broth was exactly the right temperature for 02:40 in a restaurant on Narrows with an operational rib and a managed forearm and a deal in the queue, and he ate until the bowl was empty and then he set the spoon down.

He called for the bill. The number was correct. He laid the Φ on the counter — exact, no remainder — and stood.

Outside, on Narrows, the cherry trees held their wrong-month blossoms in the city's thermal breath: white, quiet, maintained at cost by an organization that understood that the things you maintained without necessity were the things that meant something. The city ran. Somewhere north, Fassbinder-Nkono's harness clicked its way through a quiet street with the package delivered and the numbers closed.

The forearm needed Pixel's kitchen table in the morning.

He walked home. Exact.

## Chapter 10: The One Who Doesn't Stop

[Beat 156]
The team is good. They don't bunch and they don't panic — they peel back to the SUVs and start working the angles — and good is exactly the problem, because precognition against one trigger is a gift and against five at once is a budget, to be spent carefully and never refunded. His nervous system begins to cook under the load. A second slug catches the flanker breaking left, and the man drops mid-stride. A third punches clean through the door an operator is crouched behind and folds the steel in after it.

Then Cacophony runs dry on the fourth — the hammer falling on an empty chamber with a click he feels in his back teeth — and there is no time to swing the cylinder out and feed it fresh, so Kyle simply quits solving the problem with the gun. He reaches over his shoulder and draws Silence off his back. The katana is a matte-black length of steel-CNT meta-alloy, an edge that takes no light and offers no trick but the hand that holds it, and the hand has had a long time to practice. He crosses the open ground inside the half-second his neuretics has bought him, slides under the muzzle before it can track, and takes the rifle out of the fifth man's grip at the receiver — leaving him disarmed, untouched, and loudly amazed to still be in possession of all ten fingers.

Steel for steel's problems and Cacophony for the rest: the whole of Kyle's philosophy of violence, and it has kept him breathing longer than the actuarial tables care for. Behind his eyes the overclock runs hot. In the corner of his mind's eye a small red light ticks over, the way it always does when he overdraws — a tally he stopped reading years ago — and the cost gathers somewhere out of sight, a debt he'll settle up later. A thin line of blood starts from his nose; he thumbs it off his lip without thinking, and the white creeps in at the edges of everything he can see. He ignores both, the way he always does.

Five down or out. One left — the leader, who has not moved this whole time, because the leader has been waiting.

[Beat 157]
The leader brought the solution built for men like Kyle, and he has only been waiting for the range to clear enough to use it. It is a Gauss rifle — a man-portable rail driver that throws a single hypersonic depleted-uranium dart along a line so flat it might as well have been drawn with a ruler, and cares not at all what happens to be standing behind the target. It is the one weapon Kyle's neuretics cannot beat, because you cannot dodge a thing that is already past you; you can only try not to be standing where it was aimed.

He settles it on Kyle's chest and fires. Kyle reads the shot the breath before it leaves the barrel and is simply no longer there — and the dart, finding nothing but the air he abandoned, does not stop. It crosses the crossroads dead straight and punches through the windshield of an ambulance running its lights north, and kills the driver where he sits. With no one left to steer it, the ambulance drifts through its turn and meets a stalled hauler at fifty-two miles an hour, and the impact throws its back doors wide on their hinges.

Kyle is inside the leader's reach before the man can cycle the rifle for a second shot — one clean upward cut — and the Gauss rifle clatters loose to begin a quieter life on the pavement.

He straightens in the wreck-light, and through the sprung-open bay doors of the ambulance, a small face is looking out at him. The small face lifts a hand and waves.

Kyle waves back.

[Beat 158]
"Are you the new one?" the small face asks, and Kyle understands at once that this is not the question she is actually asking.

She's eight, maybe nine. Her chest is taped under a flexible biopolymer brace, and she is, by a wide margin, more composed than the one-armed second-tier CorpoNation security officer bleeding out on the asphalt a yard to Kyle's left.

He steps up into the bay. The driver is dead. The medic slumped in the passenger seat is not coming back either. The patient tag clipped to the gurney reads IMANI OKONKWO-LINDQVIST — CRUCIBLE GENOMICS RECIPIENT — POST-OP, FIRST 6 HOURS — NORTHWESTERN MEMORIAL. Crucible Genomics prints organs to order, which means the kid is six hours into living on one of them. Kyle reads the tag twice. The math does itself.

"Hi, Imani," he says. "I'm Kyle."

"I know," she says. "My uncle Vo Dinh says you're the one who doesn't stop."

[Beat 159]
He thumbs the emergency contact on the tag. His overlay chimed once — the tag broadcasting on the emergency medical band.

"Pike," a voice says, already moving.

"Your driver's dead," Kyle says. "The kid's on the floor of a south-side ditch with a bioprinted heart wired to a battery. I have a motorcycle."

A pause — not shock, recognition. "Kyle." Pike says it flat, the way you say the name of a problem you already know by heart. "Of course it's you."

"What's the brace reading?" Pike asks.

Kyle looks. "Sinus, ninety-two, paced."

"You've got sixty minutes before the vascular scaffold throws a clot," Pike says. "Maybe seventy."

Kyle does not hear the maybe. The same military-grade neuretics that read the kill team a hundred and twenty milliseconds ahead of itself reads the brace now — the battery draw, the kid's color, the brute arithmetic of a six-hour-old heart — and hands back one cold number with no maybe anywhere in it. Sixty.

"Keep her calm," Pike is saying. "A new graft that races can tear right off the suture line. Those wires aren't there for decoration."

"Northwestern?" Kyle asks.

"Streeterville. Trauma Bay Nine. I'll have a slot waiting."

"I'm in Hegewisch."

Pike does the same math, almost as fast as Kyle's neuretics had. "Then it's a straight line. North. Don't stop for checkpoints, don't stop for anything. And mind The Weather — there's a schism parked over 35th, has been since Tuesday, and your straight line runs right under it. If the read in your skull drops out, you're in it. Steady speed, eyes on a far light, and whatever you do, don't look at the edges."

"See you in a few," Kyle says.

The connection drops.

[Beat 160]
He lifts Imani onto the pillion, sidesaddle, and threads the medical harness through his belt loop so the battery rides snug over his kidney instead of swinging loose. He ties her wrists loosely through the back of his coat so she will not fall if she sleeps. Then he feeds Cacophony a fresh moon-clip — five new chambers to replace the three he scattered back across the Hegewisch crossroads — and lets it settle on his left hip. Silence goes across his back, where it rides on the bike.

Her cheek finds the back of his shoulder like it has been there before.

"Hold on," he says, which is a stupid thing to say to a kid whose hands are too small to reach around his ribs, but it is what you say.

"How fast?" she asks.

"Fast," he says.

"Good."

The kid is the only new weight he is carrying, and the only one he is doing math about. The bike turns over and catches.

[Beat 161]
The first mile is gray zone. Hegewisch sprawl, no sovereign in residence, nobody to answer to and nobody to answer for you. A decommissioned delivery automaton kneels in a drainage forecourt at the corner, the rain of so many winters streaking its chassis chalk-white, and some child has tied a festival ribbon around one of its outstretched hands; it holds the ribbon the way it would hold anything, patiently, for as long as the ribbon cares to stay. It is the kind of country a freelancer learns by heart; Stash had run a load of something through here last month and said the south stretch of Avenue O was clean if you took it fast. Kyle takes Avenue O at eighty. Two long rows of collapsed light poles lie across the road like felled trees, and his neuretics flags them and lines up the gap before his eyes have finished arriving at the problem. Imani's breathing is a small warm patch against the back of his shoulder, even and slow and deliberately, carefully controlled.

"You're not scared," he says.

"I'm being efficient with my fear," she says.

Kyle almost laughs. Then doesn't.

"Where'd you learn that?" he asks.

"The lady who got me ready. She said to say it until it's true."

"Is it true yet?" Kyle asks.

Imani considers the question and answers it honestly. "No."

"Then keep saying it," he says. He does not tell her that it never quite comes true.

## Crucible Genomics

[Beat 162]
The checkpoint is two guards in soft-helmet armor and a third tucked behind a blast-shield, and all three wear the bored faces of men expecting a manifest. Kyle does not have a manifest. He has a child, a battery, and a smile, and he has learned that on the right night, presented with enough confidence, the three of them together pass for paperwork.

He brakes hard and throws both hands off the bars into the universal posture of a man holding nothing, and he gets the words out before any of the three can finish reaching for a weapon.

"POST-OP TRANSIT," he shouts. "LACUNA COOLER. NORTHWESTERN."

The left guard looks at the brace. The right guard looks at the cooler's logo. And the man behind the glass — Crucible internal, a white coat worn under his armor — pulls Imani's face up against a phenotype registry, because in a genomics zone the gate reads the person and never the plate. The bike is nobody here. It's the blood they care about. Whatever the registry hands back to him drains the color clean out of his evening.

"Let him through," he says. "Phenotype clearance."

The barrier sinks into the road.

"Have a great evening," Kyle says, and means every word of it.

[Beat 163]
A mile and a half of Crucible private artery — manicured, palm-lit, the river running black on the right. Kyle pegs the throttle. Workers in beige smocks glance up from the greenhouses and decide not to look long.

He works the geometry of it as he rides. The corporation has him tagged as a courier hauling outgoing inventory — the only reason the gate opened, and only good while he keeps moving. Nobody who wants what's wired into Imani's chest can follow him through here; they don't carry whatever got him waved in. So they won't follow. They'll call ahead. And the ones they wake on the far side won't need her breathing to collect, because a six-hour heart travels fine in a cooler.

He keeps moving.

"Why are the trees in rows?" Imani asks.

"Because the company likes to know where things are," Kyle says.

"That seems anxious."

Kyle has nothing to add. The kid is correct.

[Beat 164]
The actuator gives up its entire career at once. The slab jams a third of the way up, shrieking on a dead track, and Kyle flattens to the tank and takes them under it with inches to spare — close enough that the cold edge of the shutter combs the air over his spine. They drop off Crucible's glass-smooth pavement onto the broken asphalt of the gray zone, and the suspension finds out about Chicago all at once. Imani laughs. Small, clean, surprised out of her.

"Loud part," she says.

"Told you," he says.

## The Pullman Seam

[Beat 165]
Pullman Works glows up on the right — restored row houses, modern cameras hidden inside period housings, an Axiom company town keeping perfect time for people who don't own a single minute of it. Kyle threads the gray-zone seam along its edge, the unincorporated strip Axiom never bothered to fence, the kind of line he has spent a career learning to ride. Where the factory rain had dried, the pavement had gone soap-slick, and he fed the bike a little less lean through the stretch without thinking, the way you learn to trust a road that won't hold you.

Heads come up as the engine passes. One kid shouts something the engine swallows whole, but the shape of the word in his mouth is unmistakable — legend — and Kyle lifts a hand off the bars like a man in a parade.

Other heads come up too, and these ones don't spare a look for the bike. They're fledglings: pledges to the Vultures, the crews who scavenge the dead and the not-quite-dead, the ones who'll cut a transplant back out of your chest the day you miss a payment and sell off whatever's left of you for parts. A fledgling runs salvage on spec — no patch, no blessing — hoping to drag something home worth a permanent place in the flock. A post-op transplant riding pillion through open country isn't a target to a kid like that. It's a winning lottery ticket. The exact score that turns a fledgling into a Vulture. Two of them are already on their bikes. Already on their neuretics.

[Beat 166]
Imani's hand finds the side of his coat and clutches a fistful of it. She isn't afraid, Kyle realizes. She's absorbing. She has never once been outside Crucible's walls in her life, and he understands this about her without being told.

"First time off the block?" he asks.

"First time on the ground," she says.

"Well," he says. "Buckle up."

"Buckle up to what?" she asks.

"Existentially," he says.

She thinks about that one for a long moment, the city pouring past on both sides of her.

"Okay," she says.

## Ashgrave Corridor

[Beat 167]
The Ashgrave Synthesis Corridor announces itself with a wall of haze you can taste a block before you can see it. The perimeter lights glow the wrong color — too yellow, too low — the way a refinery glows when it is having a feeling about something. This is somebody's commute. A shift-change line of workers in cheap half-masks trudges the shoulder, heads down, lunch pails swinging, walking a thing they made their peace with a long time ago, because the only alternative on offer was not eating. A stall at the mouth of the corridor sells filter cartridges, and beside the cartridges a hand-painted board keeps a tally — DAYS SINCE — the number low, the way the number is always low here.

Kyle has a respirator clipped to his jacket. Imani does not. His neuretics throws the air up in front of his eyes as a number he doesn't like and a clock he likes a great deal less. He reaches back without slowing, finds her face with the flat of his palm, and presses his bandana over her mouth.

"Breathe shallow," he says. "Through this. Not your nose."

She nods against his hand. A masked worker on the shoulder lifts two fingers as the bike passes — not a wave, just the nod one night-shift gives another across a bad stretch of road — and Kyle returns it. Then he bites down on his own mouthpiece, snaps the seal, and rides into a sky the color of an old bruise like he was personally invited.

[Beat 168]
Two refinery towers exhale orange fire on either side of him, close enough that he could count the rivet patterns if he had a second to spare. Sixty feet up, a vent stack blows downward all at once, and the pressure slams sideways across the road. The bike yaws hard. He corrects before the yaw has finished happening, because the correction was already waiting in his hands a half-second before the problem arrived. Imani's grip tightens into a small steel claw at his belt.

"We're fine," he says, out loud.

"I know," she says through the bandana. "I can tell from your shoulders."

Kyle has met grown adults — armed, paid, supposedly professional — who could not read his shoulders. He is starting, against every instinct he owns, to take this kid personally.

[Beat 169]
Halfway through, the corridor's throat begins to close.

A leak somewhere upwind trips a containment lockdown, and Ashgrave's answer to a leak is a blast-rated bulkhead coming down across the only open lane on a dead-man timer — slow as a guillotine and about as open to negotiation — built to seal whoever's caught on the wrong side of it in with the very air they were trying to outrun. This is not the sally-gate. There is no actuator to find and shoot, no override, and no time to look for one, because a hydraulic spine already in motion is a different animal than one sitting at rest. His neuretics tells him so before he's even finished forming the question.

He can't beat the door. He can only beat it there.

He drops two gears and pins the throttle, and the read in his skull measures the shrinking gap against the height of a man lying flat on his tank with a child folded down his spine, and hands him back a number with no margin anywhere inside it. He takes the number anyway. Something behind his eyes gives a warning lurch for the one full second he leans that hard on the read — the body presenting its invoice in real time — then steadies.

They go under the falling edge at speed. The lip catches the battery brace and shrieks down the entire length of it in a fan of orange sparks — a hand's width higher and the night ends here, with both of them — and then the bulkhead seats home behind them with a boom he feels in the floor of his chest. Through. He throws one look down at the brace. Still green. The cooler held.

Imani isn't laughing this time. Both fists are buried in his coat, her face pressed flat between his shoulder blades, and she has gone very, very quiet. Kyle files that away with the other things tonight keeps teaching him about her.

[Beat 170]
He clears the corridor's north ramp at a hundred and ten, rolls off the throttle on the way down the far side, and spits the mouthpiece out so it swings free on its lanyard. Reaching back one-handed, he tugs Imani's bandana down off her face.

"You with me?" he asks.

She coughs once, twice, and then says, "I'm with you. Your shoulders said we were okay."

"My shoulders are working overtime," he says.

"Give them a raise."

Kyle laughs — a real one, surprised out of the bottom of him — laughs so hard he nearly loses the line.

## Englewood

[Beat 171]
Englewood at night looks like a city that has been told a joke nobody is laughing at. Kyle means to run 63rd straight across to Halsted, but 63rd dead-ends in a viaduct that finished collapsing sometime back in the spring — a long slope of broken deck and bent rebar where an underpass used to carry the road through. No way over it the easy way.

There is a hard way. A tilted slab the locals have braced and shimmed into a ramp, because in a gray zone every disaster eventually gets repurposed into infrastructure by the people who have to live on top of it. He knows the ramp is sound because Echo had run a job out this way a season back and tapped him the wave-map after, the whole broken shelf of Englewood charted in clicks and echoes the way only Echo bothers to chart anything. Some debts in this trade get paid in roads you'll never have to learn the hard way.

He takes it at speed. The bike goes light at the crest, weightless for one long held breath, Imani's battery thudding once against his kidney — and they land hard on the far side with thirty-four minutes left on the clock and a fresh ache settling in behind his teeth. Two headlights take the ramp a beat behind him. Then a third. His neuretics tags them, ranges them, and tells him the single useful thing there is to know about them: they are matching his speed on purpose.

He does the single useful thing in answer. He goes faster.

[Beat 172]
They come in a flock this time — the ones a ping woke, fresh bikes and no patience between them, fanning out across both lanes to take him at speed. The lead pair don't reach for guns. They reach for their belts and throw, and the air ahead of him fills with fat little cylinders that burst into white glare and dead static. Flashbang. Chaff. And the ugly third kind — the EMP-and-interference rounds a salvage crew carries to kill a mark's bike out from under him and blind whatever he's running behind his eyes.

It is a good plan. It would work on almost anyone alive.

Kyle's bike stumbles, coughs, catches, and keeps running on hardened ignition. And the read behind his eyes never so much as flickers — because a military neuretics is shielded against a great deal more than static. Against EMP. Against the whole spectrum of battlefield interference. And against the low, wrong pressure at the very bottom of the band that would fold a civilian rig clean in half. You do not harden a mind against that last thing unless you already know, for a fact, that it is out there waiting. Kyle has never told a living soul that his is. Where a pack of fledglings came by rounds that carry it is a question for a later night. He stops the bike — because running is the one thing they are built and ready for, and standing still is the one thing they are not.

He picks the narrow place under the 63rd Street viaduct where the lane necks down between two dead cars, thumbs the bike into auto-balance, and feels it take its own weight, the gyros holding it upright and patient at a crawl with the kid still aboard.

"Don't move," he tells her.

Imani goes still as furniture.

Then he steps off into the road and draws Silence off his back.

The first one commits to his swing — a length of chain, whipping flat — before he understands that the geometry of the whole fight just changed. Kyle reads the arc early, the way he reads everything, and simply isn't quite where it lands. The chain's tail still opens a thin red line across his forearm, the tax for standing in this close; he takes that, and on the same motion plucks the kid off his saddle, shallow and deliberate, and cuts the bandolier of grenades off his chest, and tosses it back underhand.

"Hold these."

Imani catches it against herself like a schoolbag.

The second one tries to simply run him down, which is how the second one comes to study the asphalt at close range. The third has the sense to draw a pistol — and that is the moment the neuretics paints the thing no briefing can ever quite sell you: the shot itself, a full beat before the trigger finishes pulling it, a hard red line laid from the muzzle straight through the center of Kyle's chest.

So Kyle takes his chest off the line. He drops his weight back off his heels, far enough to feel the round part the air just above his sternum and travel on to find the fourth fledgling — who had done everything right tonight except stand directly behind the man he was hunting. The shooter stares at what his own hand just did. Kyle relieves him of the pistol, and of any further interest in the fight.

The rest of the flock runs the math and doesn't care for the product. Two peel away north and don't look back. The ones already on the ground will walk home when their legs come back around to the idea.
