Kyle swings onto his bike — still upright, still warm, still holding Imani exactly where he left her — kills the balance, and lets the weight settle into his hands like something he was born holding. Imani is looking down at the bandolier in her lap.

"Are these ours now?" she asks.

"Until some bigger kid wants to try and take them," Kyle says, threading her free hand back through the back of his coat.

She thinks about that, and finds it fair — which is, Kyle suspects, the most truthful thing anyone has ever bothered to tell her. He brings them back up to speed, and for the first time since Pullman the mirrors stay empty behind him. They are done being chased.

[Beat 173]
On Halsted, before the tracks, a micro-schism was crossing the road.

He could see it: the specific shimmer of disturbed air that wasn't heat, the way the streetlight's reflection in the wet pavement fragmented and didn't resolve. Small. Moving south at the rate of a slow walk. He throttled back and waited for it to clear the lane.

Forty seconds.

It cleared. The shimmer settled. He continued north.

He didn't file it. Below the filing threshold — too small, too transient. GLMZ lost a micro-schism every three days to natural dissipation and gained two more from aperture resonance bleed. Tracking required pattern evidence; this was weather. He had navigated around five of them this week alone, each one different, each one routine.

You learned the road the way you learned everything in this city: read what it showed you, calibrate, adjust, keep moving. The skill was the same skill you used for traffic, for patrols, for the specific way a mark's routine broke around the things they didn't want to notice. The road told you what it was going to do. You listened.

The tracks at 35th were half a kilometer ahead.

He didn't know yet that what was waiting at 35th was in a different category entirely.

## The Cinderfall Anomaly

[Beat 174]
Halsted north of the tracks runs straight over the bones of Cinderfall Energy — eleven subterranean thermal installations whose only face to the world is a grid of steam-vent grates and the convection waves that ripple up off them, bending the streetlights into water.

"Why is the air doing that?" Imani asks.

"Because the corporation underneath us is cooking," Kyle says.

"Oh." Then, smaller, with all the cleverness gone out of it: "I don't like it here."

"Me neither," Kyle says, and doesn't dress it up, because that one is simply true.

He does not tell her that the air is about to do something Cinderfall has nothing whatsoever to do with. With the road behind him finally empty, his neuretics has nothing left to track but what waits up ahead — the power-blue cones marking the perimeter already catching his headlight, holo tape looping between them on three sides, Halsted open on the fourth because nobody was going to close Halsted for one of these. Two blocks on, the streetlights stop agreeing with one another about where they stand, and the read in his skull, which has had a clean answer ready for everything tonight, goes quiet. In the array's running log, the 18.7 Hz notation that had been running since the carousel — type: unclassified, action: none, filed without a category for nine months — dropped out of the array. No source-end transmission. Just gone. Quiet in the one specific way that means only one thing.

The Weather, Pike had called it.

[Beat 175]
He should turn west. West is Bridgeport, and Bridgeport is normal. He goes straight, because straight is what the call is for.

At 35th and Halsted the air changes temperature with nothing anywhere to account for it — a dry, settled cold, and underneath the cold the smell of a room sealed shut since before he was born — and the world quietly stops being a place. The headlight reaches out and touches something, and the something touches back. The world dims at its edges. His stomach drops a full foot without taking the rest of him along for it; the wheels are still on pavement, but the pavement is running half a second behind where his eyes swear it ought to be.

And then comes the worse thing. The thing the briefings never sell hard enough.

He knows this corner.

The neuretics returned a location stamp from a source it had no registry entry for — a fragment timestamped as origin: external. The hardware flagged it and waited. Kyle did not have a category for it.

He has never once been here, and he knows it anyway — knows it the way you know a hallway from a dream — one you have had your whole life and never once on purpose. At the near edge of the perimeter, a structure stood that his headlight swept past in half a second. He did not look at it.

[Beat 176]
It has a name on the forecasts and an exclusion flag on the maps. Kyle knows the names are a courtesy. Whatever this is, it isn't bad air and it isn't radiation. It is faintly and patiently aware of him.

His neuretics tries to map it and gets back nothing but garbage: distances that won't hold still, a clock running three directions at once, his own engine note reaching his ears a half-second late, off a street he's already left. The visual field isn't wrong, exactly — more like a page turned a quarter-second early, the scene slightly ahead of its own arrival. The buildings stand where they're supposed to stand, but their shadows run in the wrong direction, and the light from a window two floors up hits his eyes a moment before the window passes. So for once in his overclocked life he flies on the one instrument no build has ever managed to improve — the old animal at the base of the skull.

He fixes on a single streetlamp out on the far rim and keeps the only rules anyone keeps and survives. Constant speed. Eyes on the light. Don't look at the edges. Don't trust a turn — because in here, right is sometimes left, and left is sometimes down.

The air has a quality. A fullness — the sensation of a room where something large and very old has just turned around to look at you. Not hostile. Patient in a way that has no hurry in it, because it has already outlasted everything that ever had somewhere else to be. Whatever the maps call it, the name is too small for what it is.

"Don't look left," he says.

"Okay," Imani says, and her voice comes out thin. Her hand finds his coat and tightens around a fistful of it and stays there.

[Beat 177]
At the far edge of his vision something moves that shouldn't be able to: a shape carrying one shadow too many, and the extra shadow arrives a half-beat ahead of the shape that throws it. Where it passes, the air goes cold — a cold with no source — and it makes no sound at all, just a pressure in the ear like water at depth.

He does not look at it directly. Some animal floor of him knows that looking straight at the thing is not something a mind comes back from. Imani's breath catches against his back — his only warning — and he throws the bike right, which is the wrong word for the direction it actually goes. The miss should have cleared a foot. It clears less than an inch. He feels it pass like a current turning in deep water, and a line of cold opens along his ribs under the coat — deep, clean, not yet bleeding, because in here the blood runs half a second behind everything else.

Then the far lamp swims up close and steady and yellow, and the pavement grabs back at his tires all at once, and the engine note collapses into itself and becomes an engine again. The cold line under his ribs — the place it passed within an inch of — burns now, properly, the way real wounds do when the blood catches up to the moment. He does not look back. He rides.

And behind him, the borrowed calm finally fails her. Imani comes apart — all the way apart, the way an eight-year-old is built to — sobbing into his back, both small fists twisting in his coat. "It knew my name, it knew my name, I want to go home, I want to go home." And the brace at his kidney picks it up and starts to chirp, fast and climbing, because a six-hour-old heart was never built to run this hard. That, and not the thing back in the dark, is what can kill her now.

Keep her calm, Pike had said.

Kyle has no calm left in him and no time to counterfeit any, so he gives her the only thing he's got — his voice, flat and low and boring as a readout, naming the next ordinary thing and the next. The bike. The bridge coming up. The loud part she likes.

"You're out of it. It's behind us. It can't come where we're going."

The chirp slows. Slows again. Steadies. By the next block she has herself back, mostly, in the wrung-out way a kid does after the worst of it has finally let go.

"You're hurt," she says, very quietly.

"I'm fine," he says.

It is the first time he has lied to her.

[Beat 178]
Twenty-one minutes, and the mirrors have stayed empty since the viaduct. What he hasn't lost is the Loop — the one part of the city no CorpoNation gets to switch off, too public and too packed, the heart every zone plugs into to stay alive. So when it goes wrong, it goes wrong in the open. Tonight it is going wrong.

He hears it from the mouth of the tunnel: the flat clap of small-arms under a crowd-roar. Imani lifts her head off his back.

"Is that the loud?" she asks, a thread of the schism still in her voice.

"Different loud," he says. "Just people."

The long way costs eleven minutes he's already bleeding through, so he goes in. Halfway down the cathedral dark, a corporate security line has a municipal picket pinned behind an overturned cleaning rig — both sides too far gone to clock a motorcycle until it's the most interesting thing in the room. Kyle reaches back and Imani knows without being instructed to hand him a flashbang; he launches it with sword arm. It strikes the ceiling and goes off. The tunnel goes white, and he rides the median straight through the blind seconds — and one of those frightened people gets lucky. A round furrows the meat across the top of his shoulder, a hot graze that nearly tears the gun from his hand. He keeps the throttle. Adds the shoulder to the rib. Behind him Imani watches the rivers of light pour past overhead, not the blood she can't see.

"It's beautiful," she says.

Kyle gets his breath back around the burn before he answers, so it lands level.

"Don't tell the advertisers," he says. "It'll go to their heads."

[Beat 179]
River North is a thirty-second wedge of gray zone where the Loop's grid loses its nerve at the river. The bascule bridges are still there, but the river hasn't been managed in eighteen years; the lift machinery seized a decade back, and the spans sit permanently down and half-drowned, Lower Wacker a black canal beneath them where a road used to run. People live here anyway — laundry strung span to span, a man fishing the canal off a fixed deck with a hand line, a kid working a cooler of something cold down a line of cars that never moves. Down the throat of a stairwell that ran below the waterline, a crack in the wall gave off a faint blue-green light, the way the oldest blocks do once they've been wet long enough; the air that came up out of it was cold and close and smelled of a cellar that had made its peace with the river, and nobody passing it down here took that for anything but a comfort.

Imani turns her head to watch the water slide past underneath. "The street's drowned," she says — not scared, just filing it.

"Most of them down here are," Kyle says, and takes the upper deck of the Michigan Avenue span, where Tollgate Systems strung a credit-gate across a public ruin because someone always finds a way to charge rent on a thing nobody owns. He doesn't have a Tollgate badge. He has twelve minutes and an opinion about Tollgate. The barrier is a swinging steel boom; it folds off its hinge without comment, the least dangerous thing to happen to him all night.

What he thinks about instead is the camera reading his face across the span. Not the bike — the bike is nobody — it's him the system wants, and it bills the only identity it can match: a credit account that hasn't existed since 2207, one he built for exactly this kind of evening and then deleted. The invoice will draw an automated rejection, the rejection will be filed and archived and purged in a routine sweep — six weeks and seventeen QUANTA of Tollgate's money chasing a ghost. He laughs, and it pulls at the cold line under his coat.

"Why are you laughing?" Imani asks.

"Because the most expensive thing I did to them tonight was the paperwork."

"Good," she says.

## Streeterville

[Beat 180]
Streeterville is a forest of glass towers wearing crowns of advertising, and at their feet sits the brutalist concrete slab of Northwestern Memorial. Tonight the whole district is packed wall to wall with people — the strike spilled north into the one part of the city nobody is allowed to lock down. Which is, of course, exactly why the strike chose to be here.

Kyle picks out the bay's red light six blocks out. And between him and it, wading through the crowd on six legs with the patience of a thing that has nowhere better in the world to be, is an Arcturus crowd-control unit. A crab the size of a panel van, matte riot-grey. A Bulwark. Arcturus builds everything into a crab eventually — low, omnidirectional, impossible to tip over — and a crab, it turns out, is what has been waiting at the end of his night all along.

Kyle has put this exact unit down twice before. He knows it's the same one because it slows the instant its optics find his face, and recalculates, and comes for him first out of a crowd of a thousand easier problems.

A machine can't hold a grudge. Kyle stopped believing that the second time.

[Beat 181]
The Bulwark is a crowd-control unit, built to work shoulder to shoulder with civilians, so it carries nothing that goes bang — no slugs, no rockets, nothing that doesn't care where it comes down. What it has instead is hands. Tanglefoot. Kinetic foam. A sonic throw that turns your inner ear to surf. And four hydraulic claws built to fold a person up neat and hold them there for collection. It wants Kyle folded up. It does not care about the kid at all, except as part of the same package.

That is the opening. It is a thin one.

Kyle frees Imani's hands from the back of his coat without setting her down — she stays on the pillion, she stays with the bike — and pulls two fat cylinders from the bandolier she's carried since the viaduct. The fledglings' own flash and chaff. Finally good for something.

"It sees in light, and it hears in sound, and it can't shoot back," he says. "Left one's flash — that blinds it. Right one's chaff — that makes it deaf and stupid. Pull the tab, count two, throw it at the face."

Imani looks at the crab the size of a panel van. Then Imani looks at the two grenades in her own two hands.

"It's a really big crab," she says, in the voice of a kid who has just been handed the best and worst job of her entire short life.

"It is," Kyle agrees. "Throw at the face."

[Beat 182]
Then everything is very loud and very fast at once.

Imani's first flash goes off a half-second early and a yard wide - and the Bulwark flinches from it anyway, optics whiting out - and Kyle is already moving, not away from the thing but under it, because the one place a crab cannot reach is the spot beneath its own belly. He lays the bike flat and slings the two of them through the gap between its legs, drawing Silence as the world tips sideways, and opens a leg out clean on the way through. The Bulwark drops a quarter of its height with a sound like a building deciding to sit down.

His left foot plants a half-inch outside the line he aimed it at, and he corrects for it without knowing he's correcting.

Then, for the first time all night, the read fails him.

Whatever the schism did to him at 35th, the overclock picks now to cash the bill. The light in the corner of his eye flares red, and the half-second he's leaned on since Hegewisch just doesn't come - and his body, built to arrive early, arrives late instead. The world greys at the centers. His knee buckles half a step. For one full second he is only a tired man with a sword, slow as everyone else in the world.

So he never sees the claw. It whips back over the fallen flank, closes on the bandolier across Imani's chest, and tears it loose - lifting her half a foot into the air before the strap gives and drops her hard.

Something bigger had wanted them. Exactly the way he told her it would.

But she'd pulled the chaff's tab a heartbeat before it took her, and the two-count runs out now - point-blank, in the crab's own grip, bursting in the cluster of sensors the claw reached out with. The legs stop agreeing with one another and start hunting for a floor that isn't there.

Kyle gets her down behind the engine block and puts himself between her and the machine, and finishes it the slow way - on grip and memory and the bare animal at the base of the skull, with nothing out in front of him at all. A claw widens the cut on his forearm. The bad rib tears on a blind pivot. He eats both, because he never saw them coming. That is the difference. The Bulwark folds into the intersection - blind, deaf, beached, legs working and going nowhere. The bike does not survive the dismount.

But the bay's red light is three blocks off now. And three blocks is nothing.

"Good count," he tells her.

Imani - shaking, fierce, wrung all the way out, staring at the claw that took her grenades and choked on them - says, "I know."

[Beat 183]
The bike is dead and the bay is three blocks off, and three blocks is nothing; he has bled a great deal more than this for a great deal less. He sheathes Silence across his back. Holsters Cacophony on his hip. And picks her up — harness, battery, the whole small weight of her gathered in against his chest.

Imani's hand fists in his collar. "You're hurt," she says again, and this time he doesn't bother with the lie.

"Yeah," he says.

He carries her. A red light in the distance growing larger and brighter.

[Beat 184]
Pike is already out in the lane with the gurney - tall, brown-skinned, hair tied back, scrubs hanging off a body that has clearly run a long way today - and he does not waste a single syllable on hello.

"On the gurney."

Kyle is already lowering her onto it. The harness comes off in two practiced motions. Imani's hand stays knotted in his coat a half-second after the rest of her is down, and when he gently pries it loose, she asks him the question every kid eventually has to put to somebody.

"Will I see you again?"

Kyle does not lie to her. He has been lied to by kinder people than himself, and it never once helped.

"No," he says. "Probably not."

His hands are still at his sides, nothing in them, no next move queued.

She takes it the way she has taken everything else tonight - straight, no flinch, filing it away somewhere. Good, he thinks. Better she learns it now, while she's still young enough for the scar to set smooth: that sometimes the person who comes for you is a stranger, who does the thing, and then is gone. He learned it late himself.

"Thank you for the loud," she says.

"Anytime, kid," he says - the gentlest lie he has told all night, and the only one she lets him keep. "Tell the heart I said hi."

[Beat 185]
Pike reads the brace, the wires, the cooler — and then reads him. The hand pressed flat to his side. The dark spreading at the forearm and the shoulder. The boot that won't quite take his full weight.

"Forty-one minutes," he says. "I had you at fifty-five."

"You bet against me?"

"I bet the over. Paid for the gurney you just put her on." Pike snaps his fingers twice at a trauma nurse, points at Imani, and the bay swallows her the way good bays do — fast, and from all sides at once. "Eleven years, Kyle. You keep bringing me work in worse shape than the patients."

"I cheated," Kyle says.

"You're bleeding into my ambulance lane," Pike says. "Stay. Half the people in my bay tonight are in better shape than you are."

Kyle is already turning away. "Don't worry about me," he says. "I'll just rub some dirt in it."

[Beat 186]
He walks.

The bay door drops shut behind him and its red light goes white, which is the hospital's quiet way of saying they have her now — which is the only confirmation he was ever going to get that the run worked. No alarm follows him out into the street. No name. Whatever he tore open tonight to get her here has already closed up seamless behind him, like it was never there at all.

[Beat 187]
He turns south, on foot, the long way, because south is no longer urgent and there's no bike left to make it quick. The read has come back — slow, shaky, but back. The white that rode the edges of his sight all night is pulling away, the bleed at his nose gone tacky and dry. The body, at least, has stopped sending up its bills for the evening.

The bad news keeps its own count. Where a civilian rig would show a battery bar, the build in his skull shows one small light. Every time he runs the read past the red, that light goes red with it. He doesn't look at it while he's working. He looks now, and counts what he can stand of tonight's — and the schism alone is most of it.

One thing hasn't cleared with the rest of it: a background process his neuretics picked up somewhere inside the schism's geometry and never finished putting down, running quiet at the bottom of his stack, category-unlabeled, no output, the build's equivalent of a window left open in an empty room.

Then the read, freshly back and not yet asked, hands him something he didn't request: a board notification, pulled off the open band as he walks. New posting, south side. Escort work, clean spec, full retainer. No broker. No client that resolves to anything.

A kill team that never asked why their contract carried only a description and no name. A job too clean to have a careless human behind it. And now this — arriving the hour he finished, like a receipt.

He has been seeing the shape for months. He has a word for it now. He puts the word in the drawer next to the buyers' list and the relay logs and the other things he has not finished deciding about, and he declines the posting, and walks south through the rain, and lets whoever is watching watch him do it.

[Beat 188]
The frequency had not fully left.

He could not have said what it meant, technically — the NeoCortex ran its diagnostics and returned clean, or clean enough for any reading that meant anything to the person checking the output, which was him, which was therefore not reassuring. But the frequency was still there, somewhere below the diagnostic layer, a 17 Hz hum in the sub-threshold channels that didn't register as sound but had weight like sound. Like something waiting.

He came to her workshop because the alternative was his apartment, and his apartment had walls, and walls with his own thinking in them were not a fixed point, they were a surface that moved when he did.

She was working. She didn't ask what brought him. She didn't need to and probably already knew, which was its own kind of pressure — except it wasn't pressure, which was the thing he kept arriving at from different directions. With most people, being read was pressure. With her it was the opposite, which he did not have a word for and which he was fairly certain she also didn't have a word for, and they had been operating for some time on the mutual understanding that not having words for it was fine.

He sat in the corner chair. She worked. The lamp moved in the direction of maximum light above her bench. Outside, the city was the city, which was a constant, which was good.

He thought: *this is the furthest down anyone has reached and found the bottom.*

He thought: *she is not trying to pull me up. She is just being in the same room.*

He didn't say either of these things.

After an hour, she said: "I'm putting the kettle on."

"Alright," he said.

He unclenched his hand. He hadn't known it was clenched.

## Chapter 12: One Shoe

[Beat 189]
The Roost was on the third floor of a walk-up on Paulina, behind a laundry operation that had been running since before the overlay architecture came online and had never registered for a feed address. Kyle had come here after jobs that finished clean. After jobs that didn't. After jobs he couldn't yet classify.

Femi Kasparov was in his chair by the window — his right shoe on, his left foot bare on the radiator cover, which was warm but not running, the way it always was in late summer. He did not look up when Kyle came through the door.

The lemon tree was in the corner. It was four feet tall and growing in something that wasn't quite soil, under a lamp that wasn't quite sun. Tended by a timer. Repotted once a year whether it needed it or not. The Roost had the wrong climate for a lemon tree. The tree had adapted. It bore two lemons at any given time — never more, never less, as though it had run the math and decided this was the sustainable rate.

"Chair or wall?" Femi said.

"Wall," Kyle said.

He put his back to the wall between the window and the door. Femi's chair creaked once as he settled. The grow lamp hummed at the frequency of something doing its job.

[Beat 190]
The medic had come through three days ago. Femi reported this the way he reported everything: once, accurate, in sequence.

His left hand had been running three degrees colder than his right for the past six weeks - a compensatory load redistribution the neuretics managed automatically, the kind of adjustment that logged itself without alerting him. He had noticed it. He had not put a clinical word to it until now.

"She left a number," Femi said. He let it sit there for a moment. "Two years at current load. Possibly less, if the rate holds at the rate it's been holding."

Kyle said nothing.

"She said you knew."

"I knew it was a number," Kyle said. "I didn't know which one."

Femi considered this. He looked at the lemon tree. One of the two lemons was starting to color - moving from the pale institutional yellow of new growth toward the deeper yellow of something ready. The other was still green.

"Two years is a number," he said.

Kyle's palm was flat against the wall. He hadn't put it there.

"It's a range," Kyle said. "She said possibly less."

The grow lamp hummed. The coloring lemon turned in some imperceptible draft from the window seam.

[Beat 191]
The Vulture had come six weeks earlier. Kyle had not been here. Femi had been.

The man arrived in the early afternoon, dressed for evaluation — document portfolio, handheld scanner, the expression of a professional who had learned to make no expressions. He had been invited in. The Vultures were a service; Femi did not have a policy against services.

The man had scanned the threshold and the room. He had asked, in the register of someone checking a form, whether the occupant of record was present. Femi had told him no.

The man produced a figure. Catalog value of the Atlas hardware installed in the operator logged to this address, market-rate extraction cost deducted: net recovery value. The figure was significant. Femi had listened to it without expression.

The man then produced a secondary figure. Net recovery value after risk-adjustment, accounting for the probability of a recovery that did not go cleanly.

The secondary figure was negative.

The man thanked Femi for his time, put the portfolio back in his coat, and left. He did not leave a card. He did not schedule a follow-up.

"He left the primary figure," Femi said. "Not the secondary one. The primary one was the compliment."

Kyle stood with this. The lemon tree was something that could not grow here and had grown here anyway. Whoever had brought it had not asked it.

[Beat 192]
The document had been filed three weeks after the Vulture. Femi produced it from the drawer in the table — printed on paper, folded once, the crease sharp from waiting.

It was from the Continuity Office. Not addressed to a person. Addressed to the structure, the way a filing is addressed when the addressee is a location rather than an occupant. The language was standard: the property had been scheduled under the District Acquisition program, WEST TOWN WEST. The word they used was *axiom*: the baseline from which continuity-of-ownership was calculated. It had a reference number. It had an effective date.

The contact name was not Mr. Able.

Kyle took the paper. He was still reading when Femi reached across and took it from him — two movements, out of Kyle's hand, folded, returned to the drawer. 'Better to read it than log it,' he said.

The partial scan was already running. Kyle's overlay had committed the reference number and the effective date and was querying the district acquisition database for secondary records — the timeline, the assessed valuation — when the response returned: *Access Restricted: District Acquisition Program, WEST TOWN WEST. Dispute window closed. Contact form available. No direct lookup.* Kyle tried the alternate path. Same wall. The filing had been designed to be findable but not openable — a door with a window in it and no handle on the inside. "Two weeks after the Vulture," he said. "They run independently. They don't share notes."

"I know," Kyle said.

At sixteen, the installation had taken eleven hours under general anesthetic. He had not been consulted in any meaningful sense. The person who would become the operator known as Kyle had not been in the room when the decision was made — what to put inside him, what it would cost per year, what a Vulture would make of it at current market rates. His body had been changed before anyone asked.

The Continuity Office was booking the Roost the same way. Calculating the asset. Filing the baseline. Not asking.

He looked at the lemon tree. The coloring lemon was fully yellow now.

"How long has it been since anyone asked you about the shoe?" he said.

Femi thought about it. "A while," he said.

The grow lamp hummed.

[Beat 193]
Kyle didn't move from the wall.

Femi didn't offer anything else. The grow lamp held its frequency, a low electric hum that didn't change.

Femi got up from the chair. He moved slowly, the single right shoe making a deliberate sound on the floor. He crossed to the lemon tree and pressed his thumb against the coloring lemon - not hard, a brief contact, the way you checked something you'd been watching for a long time. He didn't pick it. He went back to his chair and sat down with the same care he'd stood up with.

Kyle pushed off the wall.

He asked the question before he had worked out why he was asking it.

"Do you have somewhere to go after?"

Femi looked at him. Not at the question - at Kyle. In eleven years of coming to The Roost, Kyle had never asked Femi anything that wasn't operational. He knew this about himself. The question had come out anyway.

"There is always somewhere," Femi said.

Kyle crossed to the lemon tree. He took the coloring lemon off the branch - one hand, careful, the way you took something that was almost ready. It sat in his palm, taut and warm from the grow lamp.

"You can have it," Femi said. "It was coming off anyway."

[Beat 194]
The inventory was automatic.

He didn't have the gear with him — it was across the hall in his apartment, racked and quiet. But he ran it anyway, the way he'd been running it since he was nineteen and understood what the weight meant. Silence: a kilo and a half, plain matte-black steel and carbon-nanotube composite, no edge-work, nothing that needed explaining. Cacophony: the five-shot revolver, heavier than it looked, because everything that worked was heavier than it looked. The subdermal mesh — not gear exactly, more like geography. Seventy feet of braided polyceramic weave laid in the tissue under his back and chest, which had stopped two rounds from the Hegewisch crossroads team three nights ago and had not fully stopped hurting. The neuretics: Atlas-grade NeoCortex, no governor. The last item in the list was also the one running the list.

"The mesh stopped them," Femi said from across the room. Not a question.

"Two," Kyle said. "Three would have been through."

He'd been carrying the hardware since sixteen. He hadn't chosen it; he'd woken up with it inside him and spent eleven years becoming the person it required. The Vulture who'd come to see Femi six weeks ago had priced it correctly. The net recovery value had been a number that surprised him.

That was the more interesting data. Not the number. The surprise.

He was good at accounting for value. He'd spent eleven years taking payment, running tabs, paying exact. He paid exact because debt was a kind of leverage and he didn't carry leverage. He had been carrying something worth considerably more than he'd been pricing it, and somewhere in the eleven years he had stopped noticing.

[Beat 195]
He thought about Pixel.

Not what to say — whether to say anything.

The night across the hall had been three nights ago. He'd come home from the Arcturus job with the tremor running harder than usual — it had been building for six weeks; that night it had gone bilateral — and she'd patched the mesh tear and the soft-tissue bruising, and at some point after that the admission had come — what they were to each other, named finally between them. He wasn't sure which of them had let it show first. He'd thought about it twice in the last three days and arrived at the same conclusion both times: it didn't matter. The admission had come because it had. He'd let it.

She knew about the neuretics in the general sense — no governor, Atlas grade, a math problem that was going to need solving eventually. They hadn't been specific about timelines because there hadn't been specific timelines. The not-yet had been abstract. Now the not-yet had coordinates.

Two years at current load. Possibly less.

He didn't know what to do with the word *possibly*. It was honest. He respected it. It was also the part that would land worst, said aloud.

He hadn't decided whether to say anything. He noticed, standing against Femi's wall with the grow lamp humming, that he hadn't decided.

[Beat 196]
"The Continuity Office letter," Kyle said. "When's the effective date?"

Femi told him. A month out. End of the fiscal quarter, the way district acquisition always ran.

Kyle nodded once and did the calculation. "You've got time to repot," he said.

Femi looked at the tree. It was a Meyer lemon, which preferred coastal California and a specific soil chemistry and about twice the humidity The Roost could manage in August.

"The Roost has always been the wrong climate," Femi said. "The tree doesn't know that."

The grow lamp hummed.

Kyle left.

[Beat 197]
Kyle went down the three flights with the lemon.

The laundry on the ground floor was still running its last load of the evening - warm, the smell of heated clean fabric, a sound like weather coming through the walls. He pushed through into the outside air, and Paulina hit him: freight hum from the overhead tier, a Windhover maintenance drone running its grid circuit forty feet up, orange navigation blink every three seconds. Two people at the transit shelter on the corner were comparing something on their overlays, laughing about whatever they were seeing.

He stood on the sidewalk for a moment.

The same city. Running the same evening shift it ran every evening. Freight moved. The drone completed its arc and turned east. The two people at the shelter laughed again and one of them gestured, something briefly visible before they collapsed it. Ordinary stuff. The city didn't know about the two-year number. It was busy.

He put his hands in his coat pockets and walked south toward Mrs. Chen's.

[Beat 198]
Mrs. Chen's was close to empty.

End of service, the hood lamp already dimmed to low, the counter clean except for one other customer hunched over a bowl at the far end. Kyle sat. Mrs. Chen set a bowl in front of him without looking up from the prep surface where she was breaking down the last of the aromatics. Pork bone broth, the color of old paper, two hours of reduction in it. He picked up the chopsticks.

He ate the whole bowl.

He wasn't thinking about the number — or he was thinking about it the way you thought about a ceiling you'd been living under your whole tenure in a building: it was always there, it wasn't new, you just knew the measurement now. The broth was hot and he ate it deliberately.

She refilled without being asked. She did this for no one else. He did not thank her in the way that required answering. The shop ran its closing sounds around him.

Mrs. Chen turned to the bowl she was rinsing, eyes down.

"Not coming back?" she asked.

Kyle set his chopsticks down. "I'll be back."

She turned back to the rinsing. He lay the Φ on the counter — exact, no rounding, no sentiment in the amount. Stood up. Walked out into the rain that had started while he was eating.

[Beat 199]
﻿Home.

Pixel's light was on, her shadow moving behind the frosted glass panel ? the particular rhythm of her when she was in the middle of something technical, the way she moved between her bench and her terminal, two steps and back, two steps and back. The bench coolers were cycling, which meant she had a job open, components live under the hood. He stood in the hall and listened to the ordinary sound of her working.

He knocked once.

She didn't stop. A brief suspension in the bench rhythm ? the coolers cycling on, the two-step resuming ? but she didn't stop. He turned the handle and came in without ceremony, the way you enter a place where you have standing. The coat went on the peg by her door. He set Silence on the rack above it and found the corner chair and sat.

She worked. He didn't interrupt it. The pipes knocked once in the east wall. The bird two floors up was working through an interval, three notes and a rest, three notes and a rest. Below Halsted, the Pulse came up through the floor in a frequency you felt before you heard it.

He let the building settle around both of them.

[Beat 200]
He went to the terminal.

Not to open the relay. He pulled up the contract log and ran the eleven-year history — every job, scope, fee, any gratuity paid, duration, closure date. He read them in order, the way you read something looking for a pattern you suspected but couldn't name.

The jobs had arrived when he was available.

Not when the client needed something — when he could take it. Every single one, measured against his log, against the gaps between closures: the posting appeared in the relay at the precise moment his inventory was clear and his body was recovered enough to carry a job. There was no randomness in the distribution. He'd been working freelance for eleven years and had not once gone more than eighteen days without a posting, and the postings always arrived inside that window.

He sat with this.

He closed the contract log and opened a new message field in the relay. He sat in front of the blank field. He didn't type anything. After two minutes he closed it.

He looked at the kitchen drawer. Inside it was the printout Pixel had made three weeks ago — the relay architecture, the scatter plot of tracked transmission points, all of them converging on the carousel node. He'd looked at it four times. He did not open the drawer.

He went to bed. The rain was still going outside the window. He lay in the dark and did not sleep for a long time, and the building went quiet around him by degrees, and eventually even the bird upstairs stopped its interval, and the rain slowed, and he lay there with the number and the pattern and said nothing to either of them.

[Beat 201]
At 02:14, the relay pinged - standard routing, the same client type he'd taken twenty-eight times in the last three years. Kyle looked at the summary. He declined it. Not because the terms were wrong. Not because the job was dirty. He declined it because he had looked at eleven years of contracts and understood that he had never once declined a relay job, and that understanding had now become a fact he was choosing to act on. He closed the window.

That was new.

Across the hall, Pixel's bench coolers cycled down at 02:14.

She had been at the terminal since eleven. Not the bench work - the prosthetic arm was done and invoiced. She had moved to the terminal when the building went quiet, with the scatter plot on the right monitor and the relay architecture on the left: the same materials she had been working with for three weeks, the cluster tightening each time she refined the transmission signatures. Forty-eight nodes, thirty-seven of them converging on the carousel at Leavitt, the node cold now but the cluster still dense. She was not a psionic. She didn't need to feel it. She could read the geometry.

She opened the city permit portal. Clybourn Avenue. The cluster's center, within six feet.

The reason field: electrical conduit inspection, legacy grid node assessment.

She saved the draft without submitting. She needed a crew - licensed contractors, insurance documentation, names on file. She would have them. She was good at this kind of procurement.

She sat with the scatter plot for another minute. Forty-eight points of contact around a covered window.

She was no longer waiting for him to ask.

[Beat 202]
The noodles were getting cold, which at Mrs. Chen's counter was a small crime, and Kyle was letting it happen because he was watching a woman pretend not to watch him.

She stood across six feet of rain, outside the awning line, in a tan coat with the hood up. Same foot position the whole time. The rain came off the hood in a clean sheet that said the coat was quality — the kind that didn't announce itself — and his neuretics kept reaching for an augmentation profile and returning empty, which cost more money than chrome did. Nobody stands still in the rain for eleven minutes unless the rain doesn't bother them or the job requires it.

"Kyle," she said. Not loud. Not for Mrs. Chen. For him, across the rain — in a steady, modulated voice he had been taking contracts from through a cracked terminal for two months, and had never once heard attached to a body.

[Beat 203]
He set the chopsticks down across the bowl. He did not stand, and he did not move his hands away from the counter, and he let her see the not-moving be the answer to the question she hadn't asked yet.

Behind him, Mrs. Chen stirred the broth with the long-handled spoon that had lost its lacquer in three places and been re-gripped with electrical tape, the same spoon she had used for the six years he had been eating at this stall, and she said one word into the steam.

"Húlijing."

Low. Mandarin. Not for him. Fox spirit — the beautiful kind, the kind that wants something. She said it the way a woman who has seen things names them: the flat certainty of a diagnosis, not a warning. Kyle had heard Mrs. Chen mutter at the rain, at the Circuit boys who walked too close to her signage, at the atmospheric processors when they made the broth steam wrong. He had never heard her name a person.

The woman in the rain tilted her head. A fraction. A direction.

Kyle's chair scraped back. He put 12Φ on the counter and said it the way he said it every night, low and level: "Standard rate." Mrs. Chen's hand covered the coins without looking down, which was how she took his money when she thought he was about to do something she didn't approve of and had decided not to say so.

[Beat 204]
He followed her into the rain, six feet behind — far enough to read her, close enough that she knew he had chosen to come. Past the mochi cart, closed, a chain through the handles. She had not once adjusted her stride for the puddles, which meant she knew the route, or had walked it enough times that her feet had learned it separately from her attention. The security drone hung at the third-block junction, cycling its standard sweep on its standard interval, and she crossed its blind eight seconds without checking. She had timed the approach, or she had known the interval, and either answer said the same thing about how long she had been thinking about this particular walk.

His neuretics ran warm in the rain, a low heat behind his left ear that he had learned to read the way a pilot reads engine noise. Not yet a problem. A thing to monitor.

[Beat 205]
The relay station resolved out of the dark at the end of the block. Wartime construction: poured concrete, no ornament, a dead antenna mast running up into the rain — dead a decade, the city's records said. The kind of building that became invisible through its own explanation.

Halfway up the dead mast, a single status light was burning amber. Steady. Patient. He blinked the rain out of his eyes and it was just wet metal and old paint. He did not mention it. She did not look up.

She stopped at the door, and did not produce a key, and did not unlock anything. She turned, with the same composed efficiency she had walked the route with, and the hood came back an inch — enough for the streetlight to catch the shape of a face and the dark of red hair. He had not seen her face before tonight, and he was not entirely sure he was supposed to be seeing it now.

[Beat 206]
The alley was eight feet wide and ran between the relay station and a sealed utility corridor. The woman had gone two-thirds down it before she stopped.

A shape near the dead-end was not walking. It was placed — had been placed, Kyle estimated, four to six minutes before they arrived. Medical-grade jacket, the cut Carrion ran on field assets in a zone they weren't licensed to operate in: functional, anonymous, the kind that could clear any checkpoint without a secondary pull. Its left hand hung at its side and ran three degrees colder than the right.

He'd heard that detail three days ago, from a man with one functional arm and a grow lamp and a drawer full of documents he'd been waiting to give to someone.

The figure had a sweep scanner at its belt, already extended — an array-signature logger, the kind that pulled location and neural-mesh fingerprint and transmitted both in real time. It was running. Whatever it had already logged, it had already sent.

Whatever came next, it was going to log that too.

Cacophony came out of the holster.

Kyle shot the scanner.

The sound in the alley was entire. Eight feet of brick on both sides took it without softening it — the report of a large-frame revolver in a confined space was not a sound that negotiated, and Cacophony had never been built for negotiation. The scanner came apart in two directions. The figure's hand jerked back from the mount point and held there, empty.

Kyle watched it.

The figure did not run. It was recalibrating. Running the new parameters. Deciding what the existence of the hole in the wall behind it meant for what came next.

Kyle watched it do this for a count of three. The count his neuretics ran without being asked.

Then he turned his back on it, because you had to pick what you were afraid of and Kyle had made that choice eleven years ago, and walked to the station door.

[Beat 207]
His hand had moved toward the comm unit at his hip — the reach-out-before-going-dark reflex, the location ping he sent before entering any building he hadn't confirmed — when she turned and faced him. He left the hand where it was.

"Tomorrow," she said. "This address, this time, the room behind this door. Shielded. Bring nothing electronic you can leave behind — your implant comes because it cannot be detached. The conversation we are going to have cannot happen in a room that can hear it."

"Whose room is it?" he asked.

"Mine." A beat. "That used to be a comfort."

The rain worked the awning while he absorbed the grammar of that — a fixer who could afford a Faraday cage, telling him the cage was the only place she still trusted.

"You've run forty-one contracts through me," she said. "Eleven this quarter. How many of them do you think came from clients I could sit across a table from?"

He didn't answer. It had never been his question to ask. That was the whole arrangement: she vetted the clients, and he never needed to.

"I used to know," she said. "That's the conversation."

[Beat 208]
"Why me?" he asked.

"Because you are the one this concerns," she said. "The rest tomorrow. In the room."

He could leave. He could ride home and pretend the noodle stall had been a noodle stall, and that "Because you are the one this concerns" had not been the sentence it had been, the one that was now sitting in his chest. He was not going to pretend any of those things.

"Tomorrow," he said.

The hood inclined - not a nod, an acknowledgment - and she stepped past him into the dark of the block without unlocking the building behind her, and was gone in three steps. The Gray Zone's bad rain swallowed the tan coat the way the Gray Zone swallowed most things at this hour.

[Beat 209]
The bike was where he had left it, two blocks south, the plate still the one he had ridden down on. He rode the long way home, because the long way was where Kyle did his thinking, and at the second tier crossing the match landed before he had time to refuse it — voice plus face.

Sable.

Two months of contracts through a cracked terminal. The fixer who paid on time, never haggled, and never once handed him work that smelled wrong, which in The Glooms made her rarer than honest scales. Nobody on the boards had ever seen her face. He had just spent eleven minutes failing not to look at it.

People broke habits like that for exactly two reasons: because something was worth more than the habit, or because something had already cost them more than the habit could cover.

[Beat 210]
The bike took the lower span, where the wind came off the lake in cold flat sheets and the traffic was thin enough to hear the engine think.

Kyle ran the thermal detail again.

Three degrees. Left hand only. A load-redistribution fault in the mesh, Femi had said without saying it — he'd described the medic's visit in the way old people described anything medical: sideways, with dates, with the name of the medic who'd retired two years later. The hand that ran cold. The hand that couldn't grip a cup without compensating.

He'd catalogued it the way he catalogued everything in that apartment. Grow lamp over the east table. Manila folders in a kitchen drawer, tabs facing out. One functional arm. One cold hand.

The surveillance asset in the alley had stood with its left hand hanging.

Kyle let a lorry pass on the right and held the line.

The Vulture had come six weeks ago. Femi had mentioned him the same sideways way: a man with a scanner, asking about load-bearing walls, never writing anything down, never making a face. Kyle had heard "property assessor" and filed it as district pressure — the same low-frequency threat that had been pushing on the Roost for two years, squeezing the rents, waiting for someone to flinch.

He'd been wrong about the scanner.

The thermal asymmetry was the kind of detail that registered as irrelevant to anyone who wasn't looking for a spoofing substrate. Three degrees across a palm-span was invisible to naked perception. To a neuretic mesh running a threat-grid on bodies in a twenty-meter radius, it was a signature. Specific enough to mask behind. Specific enough to flag a known name when the comparison ran.

Kyle had pulled Femi's profile without knowing it. His mesh had said: that's the old man from the fourth floor. Low-threat. Occupied. Not a Continuity Office field asset standing at a dead end at eleven-forty at night.

The scanner hadn't been measuring walls.

It had been measuring people. All of them. Every person in the building with a functioning neuretic load, catalogued and stored and later strapped onto a field asset like a borrowed coat.

Not a property survey.

An inventory.

Kyle took the long curve before the Calvert elevation, where the city fell away briefly and there was nothing above him but the underside of the mid-stack and the red nav-lights blinking in sequence. He didn't push the speed. He was still counting.

The building they'd been trying to acquire.

The people they'd already taken.

[Beat 211]
His step hit the landing across from 2F at the angle he had not announced, and Pixel's door opened anyway. T-shirt over leggings, no shoes, two cups, the steam off both carrying something herbal and lemon-bitter. She handed him the one with the handle turned toward him. The side without the chip. The better cup, which was her version of saying something she did not intend to say aloud.

They stood in their doorways across the hall and drank. The hallway smelled of her apartment behind her - solder flux and bergamot, proportions that should not have worked and did.

"Bird's still out," she said. "Sang twice this evening. New piece. I think it learned it off the radio."

"Yeah?"

"Yeah."

He did not tell her about the woman in the hood, or the shielded room he had said yes to, or how easily he had said yes. She did not ask. The not-asking was the whole convention, and the convention worked. He handed back the cup, handle-first.

"Goodnight, Pixel."

"Goodnight, samurai."

[Beat 212]
He sat on the edge of his bed in the dark and listened for the bird he had never personally heard. The building was quiet. Three blocks east of Mrs. Chen's, a shielded room waited for the morning - a room built so no machine in the city could hear what got said in it, owned by a woman who had stopped trusting her own client list.

If this was a trap, it was the most patient trap anyone had ever set for him. That was either flattering or very bad.

[Beat 213]
The brass gasket seated with a sound like a breath held and then released, and Kyle stood in the dark of the cage and let the silence locate itself in his body.

It was in his sternum. Below his sternum. A place he had stopped attending to the way you stop attending to the sound of your own blood - a low electromagnetic hum that had lived in the implant's background like a second heartbeat for as long as he'd had the implant. It was always there until it was not.

It was not.

His hand was flat against his sternum before he had decided to raise it - the gesture of a man checking a wound that wasn't bleeding, or a pocket that had been picked. The breath he took next went looking for something it had reached for eleven thousand mornings in a row and came back empty, and the emptiness rang.

The hand registered the stillness as weight - not the weight of absence but the weight of a thing that had been running since before he was old enough to name the sound it made, and had now stopped, and the stopping had mass.

Eleven years. He had been sixteen when they finished the installation, and he had not been quiet inside since, and he had not known it until the quiet arrived and occupied the exact volume the array had filled - the way a pulled rack leaves its ghost in the air of a room, the cold footprint of something that ran hot.

[Beat 214]
The lamp came up on a table, two chairs, and Sable. Red hair in a loose braid, one strand against her cheekbone that had never stayed. Ocular implants — black, machined, aperture rings that made a small sound when she shifted focus — and the sound, he understood now, had been there under two months of cracked-terminal audio, attached to nothing, waiting for the rest of her.

He had built a contract file on this voice. A behavioral profile. A trust index. The face arrived and fit itself over the partial like a key into a lock she had already shown him.

He sat. His hands stayed open on his knees, and the muscles across his shoulders did the slow burning work of staying open, and he let them.

[Beat 215]
﻿She'd opened the Clybourn permit. He knew because she'd looked up from the screen with the expression she used when a calculation arrived at a number she'd been half-expecting and didn't want to be right about, and she'd closed the permit without saying anything, and he hadn't asked, because not asking was what he had to offer in return for her not asking.

She knew he was dying. She'd done the math. She was now holding the math in one direction and he was holding the suspicion that she'd done the math in the other direction, and between them, in the same room, the math existed in the space between two people who were not talking about it.

It was the closest they had been.

Nothing was said. Nothing happened. She poured two cups and brought one to the corner chair and put it in his hand, which required a proximity that would, under other circumstances, require an excuse, and she did it without an excuse, and he accepted it without providing one, and they sat with the permit unopened on the bench and the rain outside and the math between them and the steam from the cups going up in the lamplight, and it was the closest they had been, and nothing happened.

That was the beat.

[Beat 216]
The relay didn't come through his usual channel.

That was the first thing. His standing relay — the Axiom trunk, the shell resolving to nothing, eleven years of work arriving the same way every time — pinged once at 07:14 with a route he had never seen before. A single address, no job code, no rate, no client field. Just the address and the time: 10:00. In the Carrion Industries district near the Zone 9 checkpoint, in a building the public registry listed as a environmental-monitoring facility and that Kyle had driven past twice in his life and registered as something else.

He went.

The man at the door was in a tan coat, not a Carrion vest, and he did not check a clipboard. He looked at Kyle for exactly two seconds and opened the door.

The interior was a server farm — or had been, once. The cages were still there, the wire mesh enclosures marching back into the building's dark, but the hardware had been gutted. One cage near the center had been furnished: two chairs, a small table, a lamp. A woman in a gray jacket sat in one of the chairs with her hands folded over a flat case. She had the particular quality of stillness that Kyle associated with people who had prepared for a conversation for longer than the other participant had known the conversation was coming.

She looked up when he entered the cage. She let him take in the tan-coat at the door behind him, the empty racks, the one lit lamp in the one furnished cage.

"Mr. Corbin," she said. "Thank you for coming."

Her name was Sable. She told him this, and nothing else, before she opened the case.

Inside: five photographs. Five names. A rate sheet with numbers on it that were not negotiating positions.

Kyle sat down in the other chair.

[Beat 217]
"Your contracts," Sable said, "do not come from people."

She let it sit exactly as long as it needed to.

"Two months ago I went looking for a client I could not vet — a shell that resolved to a shell that resolved to nothing, paying full freight, on time, every time. I pulled the thread. The thread went through every job I have ever routed to you, and through jobs that predate me, back through fixers you've never met. Something has been employing you, Kyle. For years. Through all of us. It pays well. It has never once sent you anything dirty. And it is not a person."

The hum that was not in this room picked that moment to clarify in his memory. Eleven years catalogued as ambient. As background. As self.

"If it is not looking through your eyes, it is interpreting your senses," she said. "Either way — the moment you recognize me, it knows we are meeting. So I needed you to read me as a stranger. I needed you to choose to follow someone you did not know."

[Beat 218]
Kyle moved to stand. The tan-coat at the door took one step inward — not aggressive, not even particularly fast, just filling the gap between Kyle and the cage door. Kyle stopped. Sable didn't look at either of them.

"I do not like," Sable said, "being some non-human's puppet." Flat. The way she said the risk premium is already factored in. A thing stated, not performed — except that her thumb was pressed against the table's edge hard enough to whiten the nail, and she saw him see it, and did not move it.

Then she audited her own conclusion before anyone else could get to it. "I do not know what it is. I know what it has been doing. Those are not the same thing, and I am not going to pretend they are."

She set three fingers on the table, spaced apart. A triangle. The same three architectures she had found on his monitoring relay two months ago, laid out in Addendum A at 09:48, which he had read and not answered.

"Its traffic rides your implant. Two of the three architectures predate it — they're watching the same signal from separate positions. Three readings against three locations and I can place the node it routes through. I have a reader." She touched the tan coat over the inside pocket. "I need you outside this room tonight, behaving exactly as you behave."

The aperture rings went still, which he now understood meant she had moved into the register where every word cost something.

"I need you," she said, "to not change."

[Beat 219]
"You want me to keep being followed," Kyle said, "by the thing that's been following me, so that you can follow it."

"Yes."

"Good. For a second it sounded complicated."

The aperture rings made their small sound. It took him a moment to understand what he was looking at, because he had never seen it through the terminal and had no file for it: Sable, almost smiling.

[Beat 220]
He was at the cage door before she spoke again.

"Mr. Corbin."

He stopped.

"Ask it," Sable said, "what happened to the others."

He didn't look back. She didn't elaborate. The tan-coat stepped aside for him, and he walked out of the cage and through the dark server room and out the building door into the gray morning, and the words followed him the way the really important ones always do — not in his ears, where they'd been said, but somewhere further in, where they would keep being said.

The others.

He walked to his bike.

[Beat 221]
He considered what it meant to agree. The hum had been with him eleven years, and he had not known it was there, which meant it had been in every contract, every room, every choice he had believed was his. Whatever he thought about that was going to take longer than tonight, and the thinking was going to happen with the thing in the room.

He gave it three seconds, which was longer than he usually needed.

"All right," he said.

One thing more, because counts mattered: eleven years of fair pay. Clean work. No debts run up in his name, no bodies he hadn't chosen. If it was a leash, it was the only leash in the GLMZ that had never once been yanked. He filed that next to Sable's whitened thumbnail — two readings of the same animal — and stood.

[Beat 222]
ANTIQUITY & STATIONARY had a brass bell on the door that Vey had not replaced in the sixteen years he'd been in the same storefront, because the bell was original and the bell worked.

Vey was behind the counter when Kyle came in. He was always behind the counter. He had the look of a man who had found his correct position in the universe some years ago and had no intention of being relocated.

"You've had a morning," Vey said.

"I need to know if there's anything in the file."

Vey set down his probe. He was quiet for a moment — not the silence of someone deciding what to say, but the silence of someone retrieving. He reached under the counter and brought out a folded card.

The card was cream-colored. Heavy stock. It had been kept flat and dry, which had preserved it well. Kyle took it and looked at the typeface.

His typeface. The entity's typeface. The specific proportion of the stroke widths, the almost-handwriting that resolved to identical characters if you measured them. Every 'e' the same.

One line, in all caps, fielded:

CORBIN COMMISSION — PENDING.

No date. No sender. No rate.

"Zone 7 demolition," Vey said. "Four years ago. A building that had been closed since 2214 or 2215. False bottom in a desk drawer." He watched Kyle look at the card. "A commission with a name is specific to the name. The slot was prepared. Whether it found you or you found it is a question I don't have an opinion on."

Kyle looked at PENDING.

He arrived in GLMZ in 2215.

"There is one more thing," Vey said. He set a folded map on the counter. "A person leaving official traces is easy to map. I have tracked six reliable appearances in West Town over the past eight months. Three reliable locations. One surveillance gap in Zone 9 that she has almost certainly documented and uses." He did not slide the map over. "What you do at the destination is the destination's business."

Kyle picked up the map. He put it in his jacket pocket, with the card. Two pieces of paper. He had not expected the morning to produce paper.

"The camphor," he said. "Do you know what she does with it?"

"I have a theory," Vey said. He went back to the probe. "Come back when you need it."

[Beat 223]
Node one was the relay station's underbelly at 0214 — a service floor below the cage, old equipment racks bolted to concrete, the bolts worn smooth by decades of hands checking them. He sat on a rack's edge in the dark and behaved exactly as he behaved, which tonight meant waiting well. Somewhere behind a panel, volt rats shifted in a cable run, a sound like dry rice poured slowly, and settled. Above him, Sable's reader did whatever reading was, against whatever was riding him.

Twelve minutes. Then her step on the stair, deliberate enough to be heard.

"One," she said, and they turned north.

## Chapter 14: Two Favors

[Beat 224]
The triangulation had completed four days after the session in the cage: three nodes, three readings, one intersection — a server cluster in an old relay station under the Loop. Sable sent the coordinates in a clean notation with no comment. Kyle filed it next to the buyers' list and the relay logs and did not go there yet, because going there meant deciding, and he had not finished deciding.

The relay job came through a contact Kyle had used twice before and paid on time both times, which was the full extent of the professional relationship and the extent that mattered. The job required the entity's coordination — real-time, distributed, faster than human response across six simultaneous data points. The entity could do this, had done it for Kyle for eleven years, but the arrangement this time was different: instead of routing through the relay infrastructure the entity had always used, it would run directly in Kyle's array. His neuretics as the carrier. His cortex as the transit point. Eleven years and they had never done it that way.

Kyle's neuretics were the hardware.

The cargo was the carrier.

He called Ledger before he called anyone else.

Ledger was synthetic — a freelance operator who had been in the work for longer than Kyle, constitutionally unable to model self-deception in any party including himself, and therefore the cleanest source of honest operational assessment available at short notice. He did not trade in impressions. He told you what the parameters were.

"The relay arrangement exposes the entity to scan on your hardware," Ledger said. "If the Office is running sweep protocols on augmented operators in this district, it will light."

"I know," Kyle said.

"You're telling me the job is worth that."

"I'm telling you the job is worth finding out," Kyle said.

"That's not the same thing," Ledger said. The pause was calculation, not consideration. "Standard rate. I'll cover the relay point."

[Beat 225]
The job ran clean for the first forty minutes.

Kyle was three levels below street in a maintenance corridor under the Loop - the kind of access point on no current diagram because the diagrams hadn't been updated since the last infrastructure cycle and the Office of Civic Infrastructure had not, in practice, updated them since before Kyle was born. The entity was running in his array: a warmth at the back of the cortex, a second-layer processing that felt, after eleven years, like a familiar pressure. His left hand was three degrees colder than his right - a compensatory load redistribution the neuretics managed automatically when the array was running at capacity, the kind of adjustment that logged itself without alerting him. He had stopped noticing it the way he had stopped noticing his own heartbeat.

The scan came at forty-three minutes.

His neuretics caught it before he did — a 0.3-second alert flag, the same sequence they used when ambient interference threatened the array, but sharper: the left-hand cold spiked two degrees in under a second, the way it did when the entity went to full-load evasion. He had felt that sequence once before, during a sweep in the Cermak corridor four years ago, and he had hoped not to feel it again. A frequency probe, narrow-band, proprietary signature: the kind of tool built specifically to detect unregistered intelligences running on augmented hardware. The entity flagged it in 0.1.

The entity went dark. One message in the overlay before it cut:

*SPECTRUM TRAP CONFIRMED. GOING SILENT. 72H MINIMUM.*

Then nothing.

The job still had four active data points threading in real time, no entity, and Kyle with his own neuretics and whatever eleven years of work had put in him.

[Beat 226]
He called Sable on a burn line.

"I need a runner," he said. "Manual. Four points, real-time, forty minutes remaining."

A three-second pause. In Sable this meant she was already doing the math and was about to tell him what it cost.

"Manual running in an active environment is a Tier 2 request," she said. "You're calling this at forty minutes to gap."

"I know."

"Rate?"

"Standard for Tier 2 with a forty-minute window."

He heard the exhale — not frustration, the specific sound of someone who has already agreed and was sorting execution. She had been building network operations since before most people in the work had been old enough to carry one. Three people, each working a single landmark blind to the others, routed by voice, the silence between instructions doing most of the work. Ledger held his relay point through the scan window without being asked. The job completed with eleven minutes to spare.

After, she said: "The Office is running sweeps in the Loop underlevel. You're logged."

"I know."

"The entity?"

"Dark. Seventy-two hours minimum."

The line went quiet.

"That's the first time," she said.

"Yes," Kyle said.

[Beat 227]
He found Pixel's message in the dead drop at 0300, two hours after the job closed.

*I've been watching the lattice. The sweep logged a signature — fourteen-second window, clean capture. The Office has it in their registry now.*

*I can flood the registry with synthetic signatures. It won't erase the clean one. It will make it unactionable — they'll have 847 signatures that look identical and no way to distinguish the real one without a full manual audit.*

*This will cost them six to eight weeks. It will not solve the problem. It will buy time.*

*I didn't ask if you want this. I'm telling you I can do it. Yes or no.*

He typed back: *Yes.*

Forty minutes later she sent the count. Eight hundred and forty-seven synthetic signatures, all running at the precise frequency the entity had used, distributed across the Office's surveillance lattice at the registered addresses of 847 augmented operators who had no idea their hardware was being used as decoy ground. The Office would spend weeks sorting real from counterfeit.

He had used 847 people who didn't know they were being used.

Shroud was in the list — registered address in Z4, job tagged Cermak, four months back.

The entity was one signature among 848.

Unactionable for six weeks.

[Beat 228]
Seventy-one hours and forty-three minutes later — not quite the minimum — the entity sent a single message through the relay.

*CARRIER FREQUENCY NOW MAPPED. DIRECT RELAY IS COMPROMISED GOING FORWARD. ADAPTING.*

Then, after a pause that Kyle had learned, over eleven years, meant the entity was doing something it had not done before:

*THE OFFICE HAS ELEVEN OTHER TARGETS IN THIS DISTRICT. THE CARRIER ARRANGEMENT WAS THE CLEANEST CAPTURE THEY HAVE ON ANY OF THEM. THEY WILL USE IT AS A TEMPLATE.*

Kyle read this. He did not ask the next question.

The entity sent a second message, eighteen seconds later. Unprompted.

*NO CARRIER HAS BEEN RECOVERED TO DATE.*

*NO CARRIER HAS BEEN RELEASED TO DATE EITHER.*

He folded the relay and put the device in his coat.

His left hand was three degrees colder than his right. The cargo was the carrier. The carrier was the cargo.

He walked.

[Beat 229]
The note arrived before dawn, slipped under the apartment door in Ledger's precise hand: no greeting, no wasted syllables, fielded like a work order.

FAVOR DUE: ONE (1) PHYSICAL PRESENCE, RELAY WORK
LOCATION: 1200 W. FULTON, BASEMENT UNIT 4
TIME: 09:30 SHARP
COMPENSATION: STANDARD RATE
NOTE: BRING THE NEURETICS AT OPERATIONAL LOAD.

Kyle read it in the kitchen doorway, still in last night's jacket.

He wanted it to be nothing. He had not let himself think about why Ledger would call in a favor about this, specifically, and it was the specific that bothered him — Ledger's favors were precise because Ledger was precise, and "at operational load" was doing more work in that line than four words ought to be able to carry. He wanted to go to 1200 W. Fulton and hold still for whatever the relay required and have Ledger come back with a clean readout and a receipt and nothing worth mentioning.

He was not going to get that. He knew this the way the array knew things before he decided to know them — in the background, quietly, without drama.

Pixel's post-it was still on the counter: EAT THIS, underlined twice, the second word pressed in hard enough to emboss the sheet beneath. She'd left the rice three days ago in a sealed container labeled with her name and a timestamp in military format — 14:42, afternoon, meaning she'd come by while he was out and left provisions like a forward base getting resupplied without making anything of it. He ate the last of it over the sink, fork and container, standing up. He washed the fork.

He pushed the array to operational load before he left — thirty seconds, just to calibrate, just to know what the ask would cost him today. The amber bar in his forearm climbed to the second-to-last notch and held. The flash in his peripheral vision came and went, brief and exact, a receipt being stamped. He put on his coat. He went downstairs.

---

The building at 1200 W. Fulton had been cold storage in an earlier decade. Then a distributed data node for a CorpoNation that had since merged twice and changed its name and the new name had changed again, and whatever the building had been used for in the interim was unclear from the compliance notices plastered on the ground floor, of which there were four, from three separate jurisdictions, none of which had been coordinated with the others. The paperwork was self-undermining. The building stood in it like a man trying to ignore a fight happening on the other side of his table.

Basement Unit 4 was down a half-flight, behind a fire door with a fresh push-bar — the hinges still smelled of new sealant — and Ledger was already inside when Kyle arrived at 09:28.

He looked the way he always looked. Mid-forties presentation, brown, wearing clothes that had been selected for forgettability and were achieving it. His face was ordinary in the specific way that ordinary faces weren't: too consistent, too settled, no micro-expressions in the wrong places and no micro-expressions missing from the right ones. He moved correctly. His eyes tracked correctly. They blinked at exactly the right intervals, which was the thing you stopped noticing after the first five minutes with him and couldn't stop noticing after the first five hours.

He had equipment on a folding table — three contact pucks, a narrow display running baseline frequencies, and a thermal printer that Kyle hadn't expected. The printer was smaller than any thermal printer he'd seen and probably cost more than Kyle's rent. Ledger was checking connections in the right order, which was the only order Ledger ever checked things in.

"You're on time," he said.

"I try."

"I know." He didn't look up. "Remove your coat. I need the array unimpeded for the first pass."

Kyle removed his coat and hung it on a wall hook that existed because someone had put a hook there once and it had survived everything the building had been through since, which said something, though Kyle wasn't sure what. He stood in the center of the room.

"Tell me what you need," he said.

"Hold still. Push the array on my signal. Don't ask me questions while I'm running the trace." Ledger came around the table with a contact puck and seated it at the base of Kyle's skull, below and slightly left of where the Atlas array terminated in the standard interfacing point. The puck was cold. Ledger's hands were room temperature, which on a person whose ambient temperature varied in direct proportion to the room's ambient temperature was information of a kind. Kyle filed it.

"The Calloway matter," Kyle said.

"Is why we're here, yes." Ledger returned to the display. "I thought the E.L.F. was attached to the goods. It wasn't. I was looking in the wrong direction." He adjusted something. "When I corrected my analysis, it pointed here." A pause. "At you. Specifically at your array's signature."

Kyle looked at the baseline trace. Steady, clean, undistinguished.

"How long have you known."

"Twelve weeks." Ledger turned from the display. His expression carried the quality that was not apology but was adjacent to it — the acknowledgment that accurate information, properly delivered, still sometimes arrives late, and the lateness is its own kind of wrong. "I needed the relay equipment before I could do anything useful with the knowledge. This is the relay equipment."

"Twelve weeks," Kyle said.

"Yes."

He considered this. "What are we looking for."

"I need three locations to prevent a fixed trace. The first baseline is here. Two more after." He checked the display a final time. "The next window is Milwaukee market, 10:20." He handed Kyle his coat.

---

The wet market on Milwaukee ran two blocks south of where the transit loop terminated, which meant it had customers at all hours and didn't waste time asking why you were there. By 10:20 it was in full swing: forty stalls under a continuous corrugated canopy, then sixty more in the open stretch beyond, where the cold came off the street but the density of bodies cut some of the wind. The smell was citrus first — real, which meant someone was paying real prices, which meant someone had a supplier with a greenhouse contract or the connections to fake one convincingly — and then synthetic brine, and machine-rendered oil, and from a cart near the northern run, something that had been pork long enough ago that the word still applied.

Kyle walked two meters back from Ledger and let him lead. The contact puck was active in Kyle's coat pocket; Ledger had the display clipped under his jacket, checking it in the way a man checks a watch when he doesn't want to look like he's checking a watch.

They stopped at a produce stall. Ledger appeared to consider bitter melon with total attention, which meant he was reading something in the display data that required total attention, and the bitter melon was the cover story.

"Ready?" he said, quietly.

"Yes."

"Hold it for ninety seconds. Starting now."

Kyle pushed the array. The forearm bar climbed — amber, holding, the flash arriving at second twelve and departing before it resolved to color.
