Kyle pulled Cacophony and fired on the bloom.

One shot. The man hit the wall and slid.

The right-oblique man was the only one still standing. He'd backed against the corridor wall and was working through options. Kyle could see the thermal hesitation in his shoulders, the weight-shift that happened before someone decided to run or fight. He was deciding to fight, which was wrong but understandable given what training did to people. The decision showed up in the shoulders before the weight-shift completed — a lock, a lean, the body already beginning the draw. Kyle moved in the gap. He caught the wrist as the choice committed, before the consequence arrived, and applied the kind of pressure that didn't leave room for argument.

The man went down. Kyle controlled the descent — no noise, no crack.

The lights came back on. Nadia was at the panel, her hand still on the override. She looked at the four men on the floor, then at Kyle.

She didn't ask how he was doing. She could see the way he was holding his left side. She filed it the way an engineer filed a system fault: noted, priority-flagged, not the current problem.

Kyle reloaded Cacophony with two rounds from his coat and checked the corridor ahead. The supply dock door was at the far end — forty feet, one camera logging every second of this, Moss's transit van on the other side.

Four minutes and six seconds. Less now.

He moved. She followed.

They cleared the door, and the cold lakeshore air hit them like an invoice.

[Beat 253]
The supply dock at the north end took disposals around the clock. At 0340, bus 31 backed up the ramp with its running lights on and its manifest loaded. Moss came out in coveralls and moved two sealed medical-waste bags on a flatbed dolly with the economy of a man who had done this specific job eleven thousand times.

The gate clerk was a heavyset woman in a booth who had been working overnights long enough to treat it as a time zone. She ran the manifest, got to the bag count, and stopped.

"Says three bags," she said. "I'm looking at two."

Moss didn't look at the bags. He looked at the manifest on his slate and turned it toward her.

"Number transposed at dispatch," he said. "It's two. The three is a typo on the routing carbon — see the correction here, the initial." He produced a second manifest from the inside pocket of his coveralls and held it out. "I had dispatch resend it at 0330. This is the clean copy."

The clerk looked at the first manifest. Looked at the second. Looked at Moss.

Moss waited. He had the face of a man who had nowhere else to be and nothing more interesting to do and had been making night-window manifest corrections since before she was licensed.

She logged the second manifest and waved him through.

Bus 31 cleared the outer fence at 0351.

Three klicks south, Moss pulled into the shadow of a dead gantry and came back and opened the bags. Both bags. He stood over the two of them — the lunatic and an old woman the manifest swore was forty kilos of biohazard — and after a long moment set the empty bags back on the dolly with the care of a man putting something down he doesn't intend to pick up again.

"Told you," he said, "I'd throw you in the trash."

"You did," Kyle said. "Best ride I've had all year."

Nadia was already on her feet, brushing residue from her sleeve with the brisk efficiency of a woman who had woken up in a body bag before and had opinions about the experience.

Kyle gave her the burner and a sector address on the West Side. She took both without looking at them and moved to leave.

She stopped at the edge of the gantry's shadow. Turned her head, not her body.

"Marrow," she said.

Not a sentence. Not instruction. The way a researcher leaves a receipt on a workbench — a single artifact confirming the work happened, for the record, for whoever found it next. She didn't wait for a response. She was already moving.

Kyle let her go. In the overlay, the NeoCortex flagged the name and held it.

He climbed into the cab. Moss drove. The NeoCortex's thermal log was still running in the overlay — he hadn't closed it, and it occurred to him now that he wasn't going to. The arrest interval read 53 seconds. Full neuretic function. No heartbeat. The curve was clean and impossible and clearly his, running from the moment Moss set the paddles to the moment his hands found the bag seam in the cold without being asked.

He watched it the rest of the way to the depot.

The city came up through the floor.

[Beat 254]
The radiator knocked twice and went quiet.

Kyle had checked the room on entry — habit, not anxiety. Four walls, one window facing the alley, a dead bolt that held, a latch that didn't. He'd fixed the latch with a piece of friction tape from his kit while Nadia was still in the bathroom running water over her hands. The water ran for a long time.

She came out dressed in the clean shirt he'd left on the counter. Her own clothes were in a bag he would burn. She sat in the chair that faced the door — not the couch, not the corner — and Kyle noted the choice without remarking on it. Something in her wanted a sightline.

He had made tea because there was a hot plate and a box of something generic and because forty minutes after a reversal agent your body sometimes needed to be convinced it was safe by a warm thing in its hands. He set a mug on the floor beside her chair and took the couch.

She didn't touch the tea.

"The program ran for five years," she said. Not beginning — continuing. Something she'd been saying in her head for a long time. "Deep-black. Distributed across three CorpoNation research divisions simultaneously. Crucible on the genomics side. A Northpoint subsidiary on the structural architecture. A third entity I only know by an audit code." She paused. "The distribution wasn't coincidence. No single compliance review would ever see the complete picture. It was designed to not be audited whole."

Kyle said nothing. Outside, a vehicle moved through the alley below and kept moving.

"Marrow was the internal codename." Her voice was measured. The scientist's cadence — careful and careful and then suddenly accelerating, like a current finding a gap in the rock. "I worked the architecture. Not the application. The application — what the program was actually for — I wasn't read into that side. I thought it was organ-mapping. The structural work looked like organ-mapping. Detailed models of internal architecture, computational frameworks for layered systems that talk to each other in real time." She looked at her hands. "It looked like organ-mapping."

She had said that twice. Kyle heard what the repetition was doing: she was telling him that she had accepted the explanation, and that she had stopped accepting it, and that she didn't know what to call the years in between.

"I found a file," she said. "Fourteen months after I left the program. I wasn't supposed to be in that directory. I wasn't looking for anything specific. I was doing cleanup on an old data partition — routine — and the file was there because someone had stored it behind three deprecated directory paths and I don't think they meant to leave it where a person could follow the chain." She looked at the window, then back at him. "The file was a recipient specification. Detailed. Behavioral modeling, identity parameters, memory architecture — the kind of document you write when you're designing something that will believe things."

The radiator knocked once.

"Believe things," Kyle said.

"Yes." She picked up the mug, held it in both hands without drinking. "Not accept data. Believe. The distinction was explicit in the documentation. They weren't building a system that processed information. They were building one that had information installed as memory — as formative experience — so that it would carry forward certain behaviors without ever being instructed to. Because instructed behaviors can be discovered. Memory-behaviors look like personality."

She was precise. He had been looking at her precision since the cooler, since she'd read the thermal log with her wrists still marked from the restraints, and the precision hadn't broken once. It was the kind of precision that had been forged in approximately fourteen months of knowing something and having no one to tell it to.

"The recipient specification included a trainer," she said. "A constructed figure. Everything — the sessions, the corrections, the failure points, the named techniques. Very detailed. Specific to a physical practice." She stopped. "Specific to sword work."

Kyle's hands were still in his lap.

She looked at him the way you look at something you've been trying to decide how to approach for a long time. "There were aphorisms. Teaching phrases. The kind of thing a student carries for years, in the teacher's voice, long after the lessons end." She set the mug down carefully on the floor. "I used one in the posting. The phrase about interval reading and a borrowed count. It's in the file. Word for word." A pause. "I put it there because I needed to know if the recipient was real. I didn't have any other way to find out if the program had actually been deployed."

The room held itself.

Outside, nothing. The alley had gone back to quiet. The radiator made no further sound.

"I need to ask you something," Nadia said. "Before I tell you the rest. And I need you to answer honestly."

She looked at him — really looked, the way researchers look when they've run out of inference and reached the place where only the data in front of them will do.

"Do you remember him? The trainer?"

Kyle sat with it. The weight of the grief was old and specific. He knew the third correction. He knew the sound of the aphorism in a voice he had never recorded, only recalled. He knew the loss in the way you only know a loss that has lived in you long enough to become architecture.

"Yes," he said.

Nadia exhaled once — a precise, controlled breath, the kind that holds everything it's releasing — and said: "Then I need to tell you what Marrow actually built."

[Beat 255]
The cooler hummed. Somewhere in the building above them, something cycled — a compressor, a pump, a system that had kept this room at thirty-seven degrees for years without caring what it held.

Nadia sat with her back against the wall, the emergency blanket Kyle had pressed on her doing partial work. Her hands were still. She was watching him the way a person watches something they designed and then never expected to see standing in front of them.

"The architecture," she said, "needed stability mechanisms that weren't available in earlier programs. The problem with composite-identity systems — systems that draw from multiple source profiles — is dissociation. You don't get a person. You get a committee. Every stimulus hits the profiles individually before it hits the integration layer, and if the threshold isn't met, the response fractures."

Kyle did not move. He had been sitting across from her with his back to the nearest bay door, Silence across his knees.

"The stability threshold," she continued, "was sixty-four. Source profiles. Below that, the integration layer couldn't hold a coherent self-model under high-stress input. We ran the simulations at thirty-two. At forty-eight. The dissociative failure rate was —" She stopped. She made a small gesture with one hand. "Unworkable. At sixty-four, the system cohered. It held a single functional identity under conditions that would fracture earlier iterations. The load-bearing element was redundancy. Sixty-four perspectives on every stimulus created a consensus that was stronger than any individual profile."

Kyle said, "I understand redundancy."

"I know you do." She said it quietly. "The redundancy is you."

The compressor cycled. In bay fourteen, something dripped.

"The integration," she said, "also needed a coherence engine. A way to make sixty-four discrete operating systems feel like one continuous experience. Memory is the primary coherence mechanism. Not synthetic memory in the broad sense — specific memory. Relational history. The feeling of having been taught. The feeling of having failed and corrected. The feeling of knowing someone over time."

The architecture also needed teaching phrases. The kind that go in deep on first hearing and stay — not as data but as reflex, the body reaching for them under stress the way a hand reaches for a familiar grip. The architecture needed the profiles to agree not just on who taught them but on what they were taught.

Kyle was quiet for a moment.

"Did the architecture include a training narrative."

Not a question. Flat. The way he would say: the door opens inward.

"Yes."

"Was the trainer a real person."

Nadia's hands shifted in her lap. She looked at them.

"The training profile was drawn from documented teaching." She chose her words the way someone chooses footing on ice. "Historical sources. Practitioners whose method had been recorded — written record, interview archive, footage where it existed. The teaching voice was synthesized from that material to cohere the martial components of the splice pool into a single identifiable teacher. The architecture needed the profiles to agree on how they'd been taught. It needed them to recognize the corrections as coming from one source."

"So the method was real."

"The method was sourced from real practitioners, yes."

"The form. The corrections. Third correction — right wrist, last three inches of extension."

"Those were drawn from documented sources, yes."

"After the choice. Before the cost. That's where you move."

Nadia went still. A beat, then: "Yes. That one was in the file."

Kyle looked at the sword across his knees. The blade was clean. He had cleaned it after the corridor and he had not thought about whose hands had taught him to clean it, because that was not a thought he had ever needed to have before.

The wrist correction was right. He knew it was right because every time he ran the form, his body registered it as right. Not as instruction. As memory. As the particular shame of a twelve-year-old who had rolled his wrist and heard the flat disapproval of a man who expected better. The shame was as real as anything he had ever felt.

"Do you know what happened to Seito."

He said it in the past tense.

Not: do you know Seito. Not: is there a Seito. Past tense. What happened to him. The phrasing of a man who had been grieving in the wrong direction for a long time and had only just realized it, in the same moment he realized he was not going to stop.

Nadia looked up.

She opened her mouth. The air in the cooler moved between them.

She closed it.

Something happened in her face. It wasn't pity — pity needed a kind of distance she did not have right now, sitting on the floor of a Northpoint bay in a mylar blanket with a man she had spent three years trying to find. It wasn't horror. She had designed this architecture. She had run the simulations. She had signed off on coherence mechanisms that used grief as mortar.

What happened in her face was recognition.

She was looking at him and understanding, for the first time, the specific cost. Not the clinical cost — she had modeled that, had understood intellectually that a composite identity with implanted relational history would experience loss in ways that were structurally indistinguishable from real loss. She had understood it as a variable.

She was understanding it now as a fact.

The man across from her was grieving a teacher who had never existed. The grief was as real as the wrist correction. The correction was as real as anything the blade had ever done. The blade was in his hands, and his hands were the most honest thing in this room, and behind those hands was a person asking about someone he would never find and would never stop looking for, because the architecture had been designed to make him loyal and the loyalty had nowhere to land.

Kyle waited.

He was good at waiting. Sixty-four profiles of patience, if you wanted to count them that way. He didn't. He was good at waiting because that was who he was, and he was waiting now, with his sword across his knees and his face perfectly still, for an answer to a question he already knew the shape of.

Nadia looked at him.

She did not speak yet.

The cooler hummed.

[Beat 256]
She didn't look away from him when she started.

"The training profile was assembled from three sources," Nadia said. Her voice was the voice of someone who had rehearsed this for a different audience and was now discovering it wasn't easier for being rehearsed. "A pre-2200 swordsmanship lineage — instructional records, digitized, two complete generations of teaching method from a Kenjutsu school outside Osaka. That was the primary technical body of work. Then a second-tier fighter from the splice pool — not a name anyone kept, but his teaching structure was exceptional. The way he corrected errors. The specific corrections." She paused. "And a bridge layer. Synthesized, to connect the two into a coherent arc. Nine years. The research had shown that a sixty-four-splice parliament without a coherent anchor — a single north — would drift. The profiles would orient toward different norms. So the bridge needed to be a person."

Kyle's hands were on his knees. He did not move them.

"The composite was given a name. A face. A voice. The death came later in the installation — the grief is load-bearing, the research indicated. Unresolved grief around a specific person is a stronger anchor than living attachment, because a living attachment can be revisited and corrected and diluted. A death holds." She said this without cruelty, the way a doctor names a mechanism inside a body. "The name in the project files was a designation first. Someone personalized it during the installation."

The cooler was still running. Somewhere in the grid, a compressor cycled. Kyle could hear the refrigerant moving through the walls.

"The aphorisms were sourced from documented practitioners," Nadia said. "The phrases themselves were real — pulled from verified instructors, Kenjutsu lineages, records of teaching." She let a breath out through her nose. "There's one you may know. 'After the choice. Before the cost. That's where you move.' That was Yun-Gi Park, 2178, from a Busan practicum. Documented. Real man. Real moment."

She stopped. She was watching him the way she had been watching him since he sat down, and she did not look away from what she was about to say.

"But I wrote the context. The room in Old Pilsen. The afternoon. The specific way he stopped the drill and said it — the pause, the turn, the way the light came through the window at that angle. I put that in. The phrase was real. The afternoon I built."

Kyle did not move. The sixty-four were very still.

He had been trying to find that afternoon for nine years. The temperature that wasn't there. The room that had no floor when he reached for it. He had assumed the memory was degraded — that the installation had dropped something, lost fidelity somewhere in the transfer. He had never once considered that the room had no floor because no floor had been included. That someone had written the absence in.

He stayed very still for a long time.

"So I'm grieving a design choice," he said.

She did not flinch. She met his eyes and did not look away from him, which was the only honest thing available to either of them in that moment.

"You're grieving something real," she said. "What you lost was real. The skills are real — sourced from real people who spent real time learning to do what you can do. The corrections are real. Wherever you remember being held to a standard, that standard existed. Someone made it, and meant it, and passed it on, and it's in your hands now. The name and the face were the frame." She stopped. She let the silence be what it needed to be before she finished. "The work inside the frame was true."

He looked at the floor. Not down — the floor was simply where his eyes were while something settled, or didn't settle, or settled into a shape he didn't have a word for yet. The sixty-four were quiet. He couldn't remember the last time they had all been quiet at once.

He stayed that way for a long time.

Then: "What did he look like. In the file."

Nadia was still for a moment. Not reluctant — measuring. "Sixty, maybe sixty-two. Korean ancestry, Japanese training lineage, the face had the marks of both. Deep-set eyes. Gray at the temples but the rest still dark. There was a scar at the jaw — left side, old enough to be part of the face. The file said he carried himself narrow. Like he'd spent decades making himself smaller than his actual footprint, so students would underestimate the reach." She found the next detail and offered it carefully. "He had a habit of watching hands. Not weapons. Hands, before they moved."

Kyle had never seen that face. He had never seen any of it — not once in nine years of manufactured memory had the man turned fully toward him. Always in profile, or from behind, or in the early-morning half-dark where faces are just shape and intention. The file had given Seito a face the installation never showed him.

He thought: the hand that held my shoulder when the grip was wrong.

He thought: the three-count silence before a correction.

He thought: the standard. The standard is the only thing in this room that doesn't have a file number.

Nadia was watching him. He could feel her watching him in the way she had been watching him since the cooler — with the careful attention of someone tracking a process that might need intervention and might not. He didn't give her anything. Not because he was holding it back. Because there was nothing yet to give. The thing that was happening inside him had no surface, no signal, no register that a person watching could read.

The compressor ran. Something clicked and settled in the ductwork above them.

Kyle looked at his hands. He turned one over, and looked at the other side of it. The callus at the base of the index finger — where Silence sat in the grip, where it had sat so many times that the skin had reorganized itself around the fact of the sword. His hand had never met Seito. The hand only knew what it knew.

He set it back on his knee.

His face, when he raised it, was the face of a man who has discovered the map he has been navigating by was drawn on incomplete information, and who understands now, with the particular clarity that comes after the fact, that the territory is not wrong. The map was wrong. He got here anyway. The road under his feet was real the whole time.

He didn't say any of this.

He said: "We should move."

[Beat 257]
The cooler marked time the way everything in cold storage marked time: continuously, without opinion. The compressor had a three-count rhythm.

He was standing. Nadia had finished. The sources, the composition, the parliament -- everything she'd had to say had been said. He'd let it settle.

He named it.

"I'm not even a person. I'm a meatbag haunted by dozens of ghosts."

She didn't rush it toward a destination. She let it stand.

"Yes," she said.

That was all, for a moment.

"The ghosts are the architecture," she said. "Not parasites in a vessel that existed before them. There's no prior vessel. The program assembled the person from the ground up. The haunting isn't something imposed on what you are -- it is what you are."

"That's not different," Kyle said.

"No. But it changes the claim." Her hands were still on her knees. "You're not a host for sixty-four contributors' patterns. Those patterns are yours. The elbow. The third correction. The thing your hands do before you decide to. Not borrowed from someone else's life. Yours."

Kyle considered that.

"I'll think about the distinction," he said. "When there's time for it."

"I know," said Nadia.

[Beat 258]
The city was still doing what cities do. Through the window, three floors down, a cargo mech was backing a pallet into a loading bay. The driver — human, augmented, impossible to tell at this distance — was standing off to the side with her arms crossed, watching the machine work. She'd been standing exactly like that since Kyle took up the window.

He'd been standing at the window for eleven minutes.

Nadia had stopped talking. He'd heard her stop — a sentence trailing, then a careful silence, the kind of silence people make when they've understood that the other person needs to run the numbers and that numbers take time. She was behind him, somewhere near the cot. He wasn't tracking her. He knew she was there the way you know a wall is there, peripheral, not a threat.

He ran the arithmetic.

Seito's third correction: drop the elbow, reacquire the centerline, the particular stillness that precedes the strike. Eleven days. He couldn't have invented eleven days — the reps were too distributed, too correctly sequenced, exactly the kind of distributed practice that produced the result in his body. So the days happened. The correction was real. A man stood behind him for eleven days and watched the elbow and said the same four words in the same flat voice until Kyle stopped making the mistake.

The man's face he had on good authority was assembled from a database.

He checked the elbow. The correction was in the elbow.

There was a girl, once, on a motorcycle in a bad neighborhood. She'd been maybe eleven years old. She'd been riding the motorcycle because the man who was supposed to be protecting her couldn't and so she rode the motorcycle and Kyle had been in the street and she'd very nearly run him over and he'd moved and she hadn't said sorry — she'd just looked at him from the corner of her eye the way children look at adults when they've decided adults are furniture, and he'd thought: she's going to be fine. Something about the way she'd handled the bike. The line she'd found through the gap.

He didn't know her name. He'd been hired to keep her alive through a bad week and he had. Payment: 400 Φ, receipt filed. The job and the payment were both real. The child on the motorcycle was real. Nobody had installed that. He'd been there.

He thought about a hand on his wrist.

Not the whole story. Not even the name. Just: two fingers at the pulse point. Someone counting. Whatever was being counted was real — the count was being done on something, and whatever it was, it was still there, still producing a result. He thought about the fact that someone had bothered to count. The precision of it. That was real too.

So here was the arithmetic:

The correction was in the elbow. The Seito whose face he'd been given to dream about had never existed. The correction was in the elbow anyway.

The girl on the motorcycle was real. The job was real. The 400 Φ was in the receipt ledger.

The pulse that somebody's fingers had counted was real. Still producing a result.

That was not nothing. That was, in fact, a fairly complete accounting.

There were other entries he could run — the moment on the cooler floor with the thermal log still printing, the guard whose sword geometry had been wrong by eleven degrees, the specific smell of a Northpoint bay in the middle of the night — but the arithmetic was already resolving. The same answer kept coming back regardless of which column he checked. Every column said the same thing. The columns were not lying.

Down in the loading bay, the cargo mech had finished with the pallet. The driver uncrossed her arms, checked something on a handpad, crossed them again. She was waiting for a confirmation number or a sign-off or something from inside the building. She was good at waiting. Kyle watched her wait for a moment, and then he turned away from the window and picked up his coat.

"You posted the job," he said. "That means they know you posted the job. We need to move."

Nadia said: "I know. I was waiting for you to come back."

He shrugged the coat on. Checked Cacophony by habit — four rounds remaining, which was correct. "I didn't go anywhere."

"No," she said. "I know that too."

He held the door for her.

[Beat 259]
The cooler had settled into a sound Kyle had catalogued three minutes ago: the compressor cycling, the faint drip somewhere in bay six, Nadia's breathing still working to find its own bottom. He was crouched against the shelving unit, back to the cold steel, watching the door they'd come through. She was sitting with her spine against the adjacent unit, close enough that he could feel her body heat as a fact.

He asked the question because he had time and because it mattered.

"Why the open board?"

She didn't answer immediately. She was the kind of person who treated silence as a draft she could revise.

"I couldn't use a secure channel," she said.

"You have contacts."

"I had contacts. Past tense. Anyone I could have reached inside a closed network was either already compromised or being watched closely enough that my contact was the same thing as a signed confession." She turned her head toward him, not quite meeting his eyes — looking at the middle distance the way people did when they were reciting something they'd worked out alone at three in the morning. "I needed someone outside every network. Someone who wouldn't need an explanation. Someone who would read the phrase and already know what it meant."

Kyle said nothing. The compressor cycled.

"There was no one else who would recognize it," she said. "It's not a real phrase. It was never published. It was never in any training records that a person could access. Seito — the construct — was private architecture. Closed source. The only place that phrase exists is in you." She paused. "I put it in. I was the one who sourced the teaching aphorisms. I wanted something that would read as authentic to someone who hadn't lived it, so I pulled from actual instructors. The phrase itself was real — Yun-Gi Park, 2178, recorded during a practicum in Busan. 'After the choice. Before the cost. That's where you move.' But the context was mine. I wrote the room. I wrote the afternoon. I wrote Seito stopping the drill and saying those words to you — the specific you, the student who needed to hear them that way. The phrase was the only key that existed. I put it in the posting. And then I waited to see if anyone would recognize it. Whether the program had deployed. Whether the recipient was real. Whether he was still himself enough to read it."

Kyle watched the door.

"So the job was posted to me."

"Yes."

"You just had to trust I was still running boards. Still reading."

"Still yourself enough to recognize it," she said. "Yes."

The compressor finished its cycle. In the silence after, the cooler felt smaller.

"You could have just called," he said.

She looked at him. "I didn't have your number."

"Nobody does."

That sat between them without needing to be examined. Two people in a cooler, one of them the architect and the other the building, and between them a phrase that had no referent outside the space where she'd built it and he'd been made to believe it. He had grieved a man who hadn't existed. He had trained ten thousand hours under an instruction he'd believed came from a voice he'd loved. The skills were real. The hand knew what the hand knew. The grief was also real — he'd paid it out across years, and the interest was still accruing. He wasn't certain yet what that meant and he wasn't going to be certain for a while, but sitting in the cold with the woman who had written the lesson, he understood that she had known exactly what it would cost him to read it, and she had used it anyway because there was nothing else that would have reached him.

He didn't call that trust. He called it exactness. You used the tool that fit the lock.

She had known the lock.

The pressure changed. It wasn't a sound yet — it was a condition, the shift of a fire door opening three floors below and the building's sealed cold-chain corridors registering it as a ghost of atmosphere. His read caught it before his ears did, resolved it in the time between one heartbeat and the next: two bodies, moving with the specific gait of people who had cleared a stairwell before.

Kyle was already rising.

"They're here," he said.

[Beat 260]
Kyle heard them at the base of the stairs.

Not footsteps — the building did not give him footsteps. What he had was the differential. The safe house sat above a reinforced concrete subfloor, and concrete carried load information the way bad news traveled: unevenly, but it traveled. Six distinct compression signatures, two on the exterior landing, four ascending the stairwell in a stack. The NeoCortex was already building the geometry — entry vectors, angular exposure through the two windows and the front door, likely breach points ranked by probability — and he was already moving Nadia before he said anything.

"Desk. Left side."

She moved. No question, no hesitation. He had clocked that about her in the cooler and again on the bus: she listened with her body before her mind caught up. It was a researcher's habit, the reflex of someone who had spent years inside controlled environments where the protocol came before the understanding. He put her between the desk and the eastern wall, which was brick and load-bearing and three feet from the window's sight line. Then he crouched beside her and kept his voice at a register that wouldn't carry through drywall.

"Two on the fire escape. They'll wait on the east window until they hear breach from the front. Don't move until I tell you. If I say run, you go through the bathroom, out the window to the courtyard. You do not wait to see what's happening behind you."

She nodded once. Her eyes were tracking, not panicking — the particular composure of someone who had already spent weeks calculating exactly this kind of ending.

He went to his coat on the hook by the door.

Cacophony came free of the holster with a sound that was familiar in the way gravity was familiar — a constant that needed no acknowledgment. He checked the cylinder by feel. Four rounds. He had spent two inside Northpoint, and he had reloaded once in the corridor. Four was what he had.

The stairwell went quiet.

That was the signal. Professionals stopped moving before they moved — the last compression beneath a step, then nothing, then the door.

He ran the read.

With one shooter, the read was a gift — a clean sightline prediction, the shooter's geometry resolved before the trigger broke, his body already moving to the exit vector before the round arrived. With six it was a budget. He had spent it before and knew the bill: each additional angle cost him response time on the others; past four he was choosing which threats to accept. The two on the fire escape had the east window, which meant they had a sixty-degree sector on Nadia's position if she moved toward him. He resolved them first. They were the budget items. A miss from either killed her.

The door came in on a ram, low and fast.

He did not wait for it to stop moving.

The first two rounds from Cacophony went through the door's upper panel at angles he had already calculated — frame geometry, breach team spacing, the near-certain position of the point man's center mass at full extension. The Judge's spread did the rest of the work. The point man dropped into the door and the second man caught the edge of the pattern and went sideways into the frame, still moving, still functional, one hand on his weapon.

Kyle was already lateral.

The fire escape crew took the east window at the sound of the shots — he had expected six seconds, they came in four, which meant they were better than the entry pair and were running a coordinated entry protocol. The glass went inward. Two shapes, moving low and fast, professional spacing between them.

Two rounds left.

He put one into the near fire-escape shooter at seven feet. Center mass, no refinement needed. The second shooter was already inside the window frame and had angle on Nadia's position, which meant the desk was no longer cover. Kyle fired the last round at range as he closed, enough to force the second shooter's aim wide, then moved inside the threat radius where the weapon was a liability — and the scabbard came up before Silence cleared the draw.

It wasn't elegant. A pommel-end strike to the throat, the weapon still sheathed, the angle awkward because the man was already bringing his elbow up to deflect. Kyle felt the block and redirected down instead of through, driving the scabbard's lower end into the soft anatomy below the ribs. The man folded forward and Kyle turned him into the wall by his collar, and the man stopped being a problem.

Silence came free.

The second entry man was at the threshold with his weapon up and a compromised shoulder — the Judge pattern had done enough. At twelve feet in a narrow room with furniture between them, a pistol was slower than the read. Kyle moved before the trigger decision resolved, inside the furniture line and then past it, and the overhead cut was already committed when the man's arm came up. Silence answered it and then answered him.

The room was quiet except for the building settling.

He stood still for three seconds, cataloging.

The stairwell stack had been four. Two in, one down on the threshold, one unaccounted for. He went to the doorframe low and the read showed him nothing in the stairwell — which meant either one had held position outside and was already gone, or the fourth man was somewhere in the building that he couldn't resolve from here. He held the geometry for another two seconds.

Nothing moved.

Nadia's voice came from behind the desk, steady: "There's a coat."

He turned. She had his coat over one arm, extended toward him — his left arm, which she had correctly identified as the operational one, the one that needed to come free. She had retrieved it from the hook during the fight. He did not know when. She had been watching the geometry the way he had, reading the room on her own map, and she had done the right thing at the right moment without being asked.

He took the coat.

"Can you walk?"

"Yes."

"Then we're leaving."

He did a final scan of the room — not from sentiment, not from satisfaction, from inventory. He had used all four rounds. Silence was clean. The fourth man was still a variable. His left shoulder had a new restriction in the rotator, the arm that had driven the scabbard strike absorbing what the angle had required it to absorb, and the hit he had taken in the Northpoint corridor had been grinding against his lateral rib through every exchange and was now doing something structural that he identified as two fractured and one cracked, which would matter tomorrow and did not stop him tonight.

He filed both items without announcing them.

The coat went on over it.

"The stairs," he said, and she was already moving.

[Beat 261]
The contractors were in the stairwell, the hallway, and one was still in the chair where Kyle had put him. He counted them before he counted himself.

Six. All accounted for.

He ran the internal check the way a mechanic checks a chassis after a high-load run — not looking for something specific, just cataloguing what had changed. Left side: two ribs probably cracked, one possibly floating. The wound in his shoulder where the hallway team's third man had gotten the angle right was through-and-through, clean, and already closing around the biotape he'd applied in the dark during the forty seconds he'd had. His hands were steady. His neuretics were running hot — he could feel the thermal draw at the base of his skull, the small persistent headache that meant he'd burned past his read budget — but the overlay was still up, still readable. He was operational.

He found Nadia standing against the far wall with her arms folded, watching him.

"Count's done," he said. "You're unhurt."

"Yes."

"Northpoint will know the team went dark. They'll wait maybe ten minutes for a check-in, then they'll send a runner. The runner will confirm. After that someone senior makes a decision about what category this becomes." He picked up the slate from the table where she'd set it down. "You have approximately forty minutes before that decision gets made. You need to be somewhere I don't know about before it does."

She watched him. "You planned this before they came."

He handed her the slate. On it: a sector address in Z4, a transit code that would get her past two checkpoints without flagging, a name. "The contact will hold for 72 hours. Don't go back to wherever you were staying before tonight."

She looked at the slate. Then she looked at him — the same expression she'd had in the cooler when she read the thermal log, seeing something still running that she hadn't expected.

"You need a doctor," she said.

"I know one."

She didn't push it. She was not a person who pushed things that had already been decided.

A silence. Not uncomfortable — just the room being what it was now. Someone else's safe house that would stop being safe in thirty-nine minutes.

"The phrase in the posting," she said. "Seito's phrase. I put it there because you were the only one who would know it." She paused. "I didn't know if you'd still be —" She stopped. Started again. "I didn't know if you'd still be yourself."

"I'm not sure that's the right word."

"I know," she said. "I used it anyway."

She tucked the slate inside her jacket. She was precise about it, the way she'd been precise about everything he'd watched her do tonight — the way she'd checked the bay numbers in the cooler, the way she'd moved when he'd told her to move. She had, he thought, been waiting a long time to be moving again.

She went to the door. Stopped with her hand on the frame. Without turning around she said: "The man they built is the one who came. I don't think they knew that was what they were building."

Then she was gone.

Kyle stood in the room.

He did not go to the window. It wouldn't have told him anything useful, and it would have taken something from her exit that she'd earned.

He began to work. The contractor nearest the door he moved to the stairwell — not because it mattered in forty minutes, but because tidiness was a habit and habits did not take the night off. He picked up the casings by count, not by sight; his neuretics tagged each one as he pocketed it. He wiped the surfaces he'd touched. He checked the exit he'd used to enter and confirmed it still looked like an exit no one had used.

The shoulder was bleeding again, slowly, through the biotape. He noted it the way you note a slow leak — filed, not urgent, interval to be managed.

He had the doctor's code already in the overlay. He would send it when he was two blocks out and moving.

The phrase. Interval reading on a borrowed count. It was Seito's phrase, and Seito was a design document, and the phrase had been inserted into him by people who needed the parliament coherent. He had known that for four hours now. He turned it over the way you turn something that has already been examined: not looking for new information, just making sure the weight of it was in the right place.

The man they built is the one who came.

He didn't know if she was right. He wasn't certain it was the kind of question that had a right answer, or that the question needed one. The sword knew what his hand knew. The sixty-four had voted and the vote had held. Whatever word she'd reached for and set aside — yourself — it had pointed at something that was still in the room, still operational, still willing to hold a count.

He thought that was enough.

He picked up his jacket from the floor, checked the pocket for the remaining Cacophony rounds, and left.

[Beat 262]
The stairwell smelled like mildew and mineral water from a pipe that had been weeping through the wall since before the ferrocement wave. Kyle stopped on the third-floor landing, not because anything needed it, not because he'd heard a sound or read a geometry — nothing was incoming; he'd checked twice — but because his hands were doing something without him.

The right hand. His sword hand.

It had moved into the first position of the third correction, the foundational grip adjustment Seito had drilled into him across three hundred repetitions on a specific Tuesday that Kyle couldn't currently find. He had a sense of wooden floors. He had a sense of slant light. He had a clear physical memory of being told his elbow was too high, and a less clear memory of the face that told him this, and no memory at all of the afternoon it was corrected — the particular correction that mattered, the one he'd worked out himself two years later when he noticed the form was producing a slight lateral drag on the cut.

He'd fixed it alone. On a job. After the job. Standing in an alley on the north side with a body cooling behind him and nothing but time.

Seito had never seen the adjustment.

Kyle stood on the landing and ran the form once, slowly, no urgency, just the motion: weight shift, rotation at the hip, commitment through the shoulder, elbow where he decided it belonged not where he'd been told — and the form was better now. It was cleanly, demonstrably better. Whoever had designed the Seito construct had done it from archive material, from observed technique, from recordings of a thousand real fighters collated into one coherent teaching, and that was fine work, that was real work, the skill arrived in him whole and true, but the archive hadn't included the alley on the north side or the job before it or the four hundred times he'd done the adjustment-grip wrong in the two years after Seito before he caught it himself.

None of that was installed.

He ran the form to the end. His hands came back to rest.

He had a grief that had no object. He'd known Seito for nine years — which wasn't true in any sense a court would recognize, but the grief was real, which was perhaps the point, which was perhaps the whole point. The afternoons were fabrications. The form was his.

He sheathed Silence.

The stairwell gave him two more flights and then a fire door and then the gray zone, and the gray zone gave him northside asphalt at four in the morning, the texture of it specific and particular and older than anything in his design files. Cracked expansion joints spaced wrong. A repair crew's aggregate different from three blocks in either direction. He'd learned this texture by walking it, not by being built with it. The city kept accumulating in him whether he asked for it or not.

The next job was already filed. He knew the address. He knew roughly what it would cost him — not the money, the money was simple, but the read-budget, the sword-hours, the shoulder that would need two days before he could drive it hard again. He knew the field going in, which was an advantage, and he knew there were things he didn't know, which was the variable. There was always a variable. He filed this in the same drawer as everything else: the fabricated afternoons, the real corrections, Nadia's face when she said what Marrow was, the thermal log and bay seventeen and the question of whether knowing the architecture of a thing changed the thing itself.

He'd run the form. The form was better than what he'd been taught. That seemed like an answer, though not the kind that closed a file.

The form was better because he'd fixed it in the gap. Seito chose to build him — made the decision, laid the foundation, drilled three hundred Tuesday repetitions into a hand that would never stop moving — and then the cost of that building arrived slowly, over years, in alleys and jobs and lateral drag on cuts that no archive had predicted. Between the choice and the cost, Kyle had lived. That was where the adjustment happened. That was where everything happened, if he was honest about it: after choices that weren't his and before consequences that hadn't finished arriving, in the gap Seito had named and then inadvertently given him. He'd been moving there his whole life without knowing it had a name. The form was just the clearest proof.

He kept walking.

The city was making its particular northside sounds — a freight slug pulling under the street, the low hum of a cooling tower three blocks east, something avian and foolish in the gap between two buildings. None of it needed anything of him. He filed it all without meaning to, the way he filed everything, and the drawer stayed full, and he kept walking, and the first gray light was starting to make the ferrocement go the color of old water.

He was on his way to the next job. The next job would have its own costs, its own variable, its own drawer. This was what happened after. This was the whole of it.

He already knew how to move in it.

[Beat 263]
Somebody stole Kyle's motorcycle.

He stood in the alley behind The Pivot at 06:40 with a cup of tea going cold in his hand and inventoried the facts, because the facts refused to add up and he wanted them where he could see them. Fact: the alley had one entrance, and the entrance had a camera, and the camera worked, because Pixel maintained it in exchange for the landlord ignoring what her power meter did. Fact: the wheel hub had been locked. Fact: the plate cover — his plate cover, the one he folded a particular way — was sitting on the concrete where the bike used to be, folded the particular way.

Folded. Not dropped. Whoever took his bike had taken the cover off first, folded it like laundry, and left it as a receipt.

Kyle drank some of the cold tea and let himself feel the thing he was feeling, which was not, strictly speaking, professional. He had owned the bike for nine years. He had bought it with his second fee ever — the first had gone to a flat with a door that locked — and it was the only thing he had ever purchased that wasn't a tool. The bike was not a tool. The bike was the long way home. Eleven years in this city and his entire interior life had a displacement of 1200cc, and some polite individual had walked it out of a locked alley under a working camera, between 03:00 and dawn.

He went back upstairs and knocked on 2E.

Pixel opened the door with a soldering iron in one hand and yesterday's hair. She read his face the way she read his stride.

"Who died?"

"The bike's gone."

She stood very still for a moment. Then she set the soldering iron down inside the doorway, on the little shelf she kept for exactly the moments when something needed both her hands, and said, in the voice she used for load-bearing fasteners:

"Somebody boosted my work?"

"Your work?"

"Who do you think built the anti-theft, the manufacturer? The manufacturer's anti-theft was a sticker." She was already pulling her boots on. "The hub lock is mine. The ignition handshake is mine. Three years, samurai, nobody's gotten past the handshake — I'd have heard, I monitor the forums, people complain about me by name." She grabbed her field coat. "This isn't theft. This is peer review."

[Beat 264]
The camera had thirty-one minutes of nothing.

Not a loop - Pixel checked the frame seams twice - just a clean gap, 03:12 to 03:43, the feed dropping the way feeds drop when somebody upstream of the camera owns the right permissions. Kyle stood in the alley while she swore at her own equipment with real affection, and he counted what the gap told him. One: money, because permissions cost. Two: manners, because the cover was folded. Three: confidence, because anyone who knew enough to want his bike knew enough to know whose bike it was. An Exo's work, probably - body parked somewhere dry, eyes borrowed from the camera while the camera's owner filed a green-light log and went home.

"Professional, funded, polite," Kyle said. "Who steals at that grade and leaves a receipt?"

"Somebody who wants you to follow," Pixel said, which was the obvious thing, and then the true thing arrived behind it and they looked at each other. Somebody who wanted him to follow.

The freight lift at the end of the block had a courier on it.

She was coming off a night shift, breath still running ahead of her, the chromatophore panels in her sponsorship legs cycling some energy drink's sunrise campaign at one-quarter brightness, which was the legal minimum and her entire opinion of the energy drink. Kyle knew her the way the trade knows the trade - the nod, three years of shared lift rides, no names. She watched the lift numbers. He watched them with her.

"03:00-ish," he said. "A flatbed, maybe. Something that could carry a bike."

She let two floors go by, doing the arithmetic of what gossip costs against who was asking.

"Box truck," she said. "03:20. Backed up to your alley like it had a permit. Two lifters, and a kid riding the gate." She paused. "Truck had a cartoon fish on it. The fish was winking."

"A winking fish."

"I notice things," she said, to the numbers. "It cost you a favor, by the way. Unspecified. I'll know it when I need it."

"Standard terms," Kyle said, and meant it, and her mouth did something that on a less tired face would have been a smile.

WIN: the truck. One favor on the books.

[Beat 265]
The winking fish belonged to Dinh Salvage & Sundry, which was four garage doors and a smell of cutting oil down in the Ashgrave fringe, and the proprietor was a fence named Vo Dinh who moved hot vehicles the way other men moved furniture: carefully, locally, and with a strong preference for never knowing anything.

Kyle walked in at 09:15. The two lifters from the night before were having breakfast skewers by the compressor, and he watched the older one clock his face, then the saya over his shoulder, then his face again, and arrive at the correct conclusion about how the morning was going.

"Oh no," the lifter said, with feeling.

"Finish your breakfast," Kyle said.

Vo Dinh came out of the office wiping his hands on a rag he then continued to wipe his hands on for the entire conversation, which Kyle catalogued as the most honest thing in the building. The negotiation was brief because everyone wanted it to be.

"It was a contract move," Vo said. "Legitimate paper, pickup and transport, paid in front. We don't boost, we carry. The kid had a work order with your alley on it and I didn't ask, because the rate was three times market, and at three times market the not-asking is what they're buying."

"The kid."

"Riding the gate. The kid was the client." Vo's rag went around his knuckles. "Fourteen, maybe. Paid cash-Φ from a courier pouch. Had your alley, your camera window, your lock specs—" he had the grace to look embarrassed by the next part "—on paper. A printed file. Good stock. The kind you buy."

Kyle stood in the smell of cutting oil and let that one land where it landed. His alley. His window. His lock specs. On good paper, the kind you buy, in the hands of a fourteen-year-old.

"Where'd the run end?"

"Pullman side. The old can factory on Langley, the one with the murals." Vo finally put the rag down. "For the record, if I'd known whose bike—"

"You'd have charged five times market."

"...Four," said Vo, with professional dignity, and gave him a breakfast skewer for the road, which was not payment and therefore could be accepted.

WIN: an address. Also a skewer.

[Beat 266]
The terminal in Kyle's coat chirped on the walk to the can factory.

He had three terminals, technically — the clean one, the work one, and the cracked one behind the water recycler that he hadn't touched since the substation — but this was the work one, and the posting on it was new, and the posting read like this:

CONTRACT: RECOVERY OF STOLEN PROPERTY (VEHICLE, 1200CC).
SCOPE: LOCATE, RECOVER, RESOLVE.
FEE: 1Φ.
CLIENT: [shell — resolves to shell — resolves to nothing]

Kyle stopped walking.

He read it twice. He looked at the fee. One Φ — for a recovery he was already four hours into, posted by a client who did not exist, scoped so precisely to his current morning that it might as well have included the skewer.

It was watching. It had noticed somebody took the bike, and this was what noticing looked like when you were whatever it was: it couldn't bring him tea and it couldn't say that's terrible, so it had opened a contract, because a job taken is a job backed, and it wanted — he turned the thought over carefully, the way Pixel turned a component — it wanted in on the bike thing.

"You're ridiculous," Kyle said, out loud, to nobody, on a public street.

He accepted the contract. One Φ. A man should be on payroll for the important things.

Thirty seconds later the posting updated itself, which postings did not do:

ADDENDUM: CLIENT ADVISES THE TRUCK TURNED EAST OFF LANGLEY.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT ADVISES THE MURAL DOOR IS NOT THE DOOR.

Kyle looked at the addenda for a long moment. Somewhere under the city, something with no face and perfect handwriting was leaning over his shoulder like a kid watching an older brother fix an engine.

"East off Langley," he said. "Copy."

He could have sworn the terminal chirped happier the second time, and filed the thought under things Pixel never gets to hear.

[Beat 267]
Pixel met him at the corner of Langley with her field kit and a thermos, because she had invited herself the way specialists do, by showing up.

"Before you say anything," she said, "I put a beacon in the bike. Under the battery tray. Dormant, passive, wakes on a handshake only I have. Three years ago."

"You bugged my bike."

"I maintain my installations." She handed him the thermos lid with tea in it, handle first. "It woke up nine minutes ago when somebody put a charger on the battery. Whoever has it is taking care of it, by the way. Which is weird. Signal says it's not in the can factory — it's the building behind, the east one."

THE MURAL DOOR IS NOT THE DOOR.
CLIENT ADVISES: DO NOT HURRY. ESTIMATED TRANSIT FROM CURRENT POSITION: 9 MINUTES.

"My client called that one too," Kyle said.

"Your client." Pixel looked at him over her own tea. "The one-Φ client."

"It tipped already. Half a Φ. In advance." He showed her the receipt line. "There's an itemization. It says MORALE."

Pixel read the receipt twice, and her face did several things she would have charged anyone else money to see. "Your benefactor," she said finally, "is the only entity in this city with a perfect credit record and the social instincts of a vending machine, and I am ninety percent sure it's my favorite of your clients. Don't tell Sable."

WIN: a live beacon, a confirmed building. Tea.

[Beat 268]
The east building had been a union hall when unions had halls. Now it had blankets on the windows, a stovepipe through one wall doing its honest best, and a roof population of pigeons who had clearly never been bothered by anyone. Kyle counted heat lines, exits, and the small sounds of a building that holds more people than furniture: eleven, maybe twelve, most of them light, young.

The bike was in the middle of the old meeting floor, on a stand, under a work lamp.

It had been washed. The chain had been done. Somebody had — he catalogued this with an emotion he declined to name — somebody had detailed the engine, properly, with cotton swabs, the way the manual said and nobody ever did.

The girl sitting next to it, wearing his folded riding gloves like a graduation, was maybe fourteen, with the build of someone who climbed things for a living and the haircut of someone who did it herself. Around the room's edges, on crates and in doorways, her crew pretended very hard to be furniture: the gate kid from the truck, a long boy with courier calves, a tiny one in the rafters who'd been the lookout since Kyle was two blocks out and had absolutely seen him coming and absolutely not been able to do anything about it.

"You're faster than the file said," the girl announced. Loudly. To the room — for the room. Her hands were shaking and her chin was up, and Kyle revised several plans he'd carried in with him.

"The file," he said.

She produced it. Good stock, folded the particular way — she'd practiced the fold, he realized; the receipt in the alley had been a signature, hers, an artisan's mark on her first big piece. The file had his alley. His camera window. Pixel's lock specs, annotated in a tight adult hand and then re-annotated in a kid's: lock = real problem!! handshake = ask M.

"Five Φ," she said. "Kids' rate. There's a shop in the Loop underlevels, the man sells paper on anybody. Mostly we run errands for him. I saved up." Her chin came up another inch. "It took me five weeks to plan, I beat HER lock—" a nod at Pixel, who made a sound like a kettle "—and I didn't scratch it, and I charged the battery, and I cleaned it, and you found me in five hours anyway, which means you're worth it, so." She took a breath that used her whole body.

"So?"

"So teach me," said the girl, in a union hall, surrounded by her people, having stolen the unstealable bike of the most dangerous freelancer in West Town as a job application.

The room held its breath. The pigeons held theirs. Somewhere in Kyle's coat, he was fairly sure, a one-Φ client was taking notes.

[Beat 269]
What he did not do was laugh, because eleven kids were watching her bet everything, and a man who laughs at that deserves what the city does to him.

What he did was the arithmetic, out loud, the way you'd want it done to you.

"You broke into my alley. You stole nine years of my property. You spent five weeks and five Φ and a crew's worth of trust to schedule a meeting you could've had for the price of standing outside Mrs. Chen's at nineteen-thirty and saying hello." He let that sit. "And your file came from a counter that sold a fourteen-year-old my lock specs for pocket money, which is a problem I now own. That's the bill. Here's the other column." He looked at the bike. Cotton swabs. "You planned past my camera and her lock. You took nothing but the thing that gets attention. You kept it better than you found it. And when I came through that door, you stood up and put your name on it in front of your whole crew."

He picked up his gloves. Put them on. Kicked the stand, walked the bike off the floor — and stopped, next to her, and said it quietly enough to be hers and loud enough to be the room's:

"No. I won't teach you. I don't take apprentices; I lose enough people I like." Her chin stayed up while the room quietly broke for her, and that, more than the swabs, decided the next part. "But I hire. Two Φ a week. My alley's camera has a thirty-one-minute problem, and I want eyes on it that beat the camera. You report to Pixel, you touch nothing with an engine, and the first time one of your crew runs paper for that shop in the Loop again, the arrangement's done." He held out his hand. "Standard terms. Everyone starts on payroll."

Her handshake was a disaster of relief and ferocity. The room exhaled. The tiny one fell off the rafter, slightly.

"Boost," she said. "They call me Boost."

"Of course they do," said Kyle.

[Beat 270]
He took the long way home, obviously.

The bike ran better than it had in a year — he was going to have to find a way to never tell Pixel that — and the rain held off, and the Pulse hummed under the eastern strut, regular as breathing, and at the second tier crossing his terminal chirped one last time.

CONTRACT COMPLETE: RECOVERY OF STOLEN PROPERTY.
FEE RELEASED: 1Φ.
GRATUITY: 0.5Φ — ITEMIZED: MORALE.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT NOTES THE VEHICLE WAS RECOVERED WITHOUT DAMAGE OR INJURY.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT NOTES THE NEW HIRE'S RATE IS BELOW MARKET. CLIENT HAS REVIEWED THREE PRIOR ENGAGEMENTS.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT HAS OPENED A STANDING CONTRACT, SCOPE: TO BE DETERMINED, FEE: STANDARD RATE.

Kyle rode through the wet gold of the streetlights with 1.5Φ of the strangest money in the GLMZ in his account and a fourteen-year-old's lock-spec file folded in his coat, and thought about a counter in the Loop underlevels where anybody's life could be bought on good paper for pocket money — his life, five Φ, kids' rate — and decided that tomorrow, after the noodles, he was going to go price himself.

But that was tomorrow's problem.

Tonight the engine was warm, the long way was empty, and somewhere behind him a union hall full of kids was arguing about what their crew was called now that they worked for somebody. He had it on good authority — half a Φ of good authority — that morale was worth paying for.

He opened the throttle and let the city pull.

## Across the Hall

[Beat 271]
The Pivot sits two blocks past the Arcturus checkpoint — the booth that bleeds amber across the wet street all night and scans for plate and intent and waves you through if you're nobody worth stopping. Tonight Kyle is gloriously nobody. He leaves the slug-rail hum of the gray zone at the door and trades it for the warmer ruin of home.

He makes it up the stairs on a banister and a bad idea, leaving a dark smear on the wall he'll feel guilty about tomorrow. The second floor announces Pixel the way it always does — bass through her door at a volume that should bother the building more than it does, the clatter of something being tested and found wanting. Kyle stands in the hall with his hand flat against his side, blood dark between his fingers, and does the math he always does when he arrives here in pieces: the mediclone station on Meridian runs twenty-four hours and leaves no record that touches her.

He knocks. It comes out softer than a knock should.

The music cuts. The door opens on the chain, then all the way, and Pixel takes him in the way she takes in everything — fast, flat, already triaging. Pink hair half out of its updo, grey-blue eyes doing arithmetic on the spread of the stain.

"That had better not be yours," she says.

"It's mostly someone else's."

"Inside."

[Beat 272]
She clears the kitchen table with her forearm — a soldering iron, a half-flayed prosthetic hand, a mug — and none of it lands gently. Tools go to the floor and stay there. She doesn't look at them. She looks at him, and at the brace, and at the boot dragging, and the look on her face is the one she keeps for things that are still fixable if she's fast.

"Shirt," she says.

He gets it half over his head before the wound files a complaint, and she's there, peeling the rest of it off him like a bandage from skin, brisk, no ceremony. Then she stops being brisk.

She has seen Kyle hurt before. She has not seen this — the whole of it at once, under the kitchen light, everything his clothes have been keeping from the record. The long pale seam under his ribs, white with age, running clean as a weld. A burn that climbs his left shoulder, old and rough and uneven, the kind that took time to become what it is. Marks she can date by color and texture the way she dates components, and she reads them, one and then the next, her thumb stopping against the burn on his shoulder.

"Stop cataloguing me," he says.

"Stop collecting them," she says, and something in the word collecting moves through her face before she reaches for the kit.

[Beat 273]
She works the way good medics work — talking to keep him present, hands too busy to shake. Antiseptic first. He hisses; she doesn't apologize. She irrigates the gash in his side, and her thumb braces flat against the muscle below it to hold the edges while she threads the suture, and he feels the small precise tugs as she closes him, one and then the next, her breath warm and uneven against his collarbone.

Halfway through she turns him — checking the exit geometry, the way the wound tracks — and her fingers find something that shouldn't be there. A screw head, a machine screw, no more than an inch, worked into the skin just below the shoulder blade. The floor, when the table went. She extracts it without a word and sets it on the edge of the kit, and he watches her not react to it, and understands that the not-reacting is costing her.

Somewhere in the middle of all of it her eyes have gone bright and overfull, and one of them spills, and the tear runs down and lands in the open wound. He doesn't tell her she's crying. She'd hate that. He holds still and lets her finish, lets the salt find the raw of him, his hands loose at his sides and not moving. He has been sutured before, by people who were paid and people who owed him. None of them had ever braced a thumb against him like he was worth keeping still.

"You keep doing this," she says, not a question, knotting the last stitch. "Coming back in pieces. Making me put you back."

"You're good at it."

"That's not the part I'm complaining about."

[Beat 274]
She should be done. The wound is closed; the forearm is wrapped; the shoulder is taped. Her job, the named and reasonable part of it, is over. And she doesn't move away.

Her hand is still flat on his stomach where she braced to stitch him, and it stays, and then it doesn't stay still — it moves, slow, down the ridges and over the old burn and along the pale seam under his ribs, reading him the way her eyes read everything, except her eyes are closed now. She traces a scar from end to end like she's deciding whether to forgive it.

This is the part where Kyle pulls away. He has always pulled away. The reasons are numerous and not wrong.

He doesn't pull away.

He puts his hand over hers and presses it flatter to him, and lets her feel that his heart is doing something undignified, and when she looks up — startled, the arithmetic finally failing her — he is already looking back.

[Beat 275]
She kisses him like she's been holding it under her tongue for a year, and he answers it with both hands in the pink wreck of her hair. She climbs into his lap right there on the kitchen chair, careful of the bad side and reckless with all the rest, and he pulls her sweater off over her head and doesn't track where it lands.

In the lower-left of his overlay, his neuretics escalates to amber — the Arcturus checkpoint cycling its three-block sensor sweep, biometric correlation running. Thirty seconds to auto-clear if he's clean; longer if the chain has him flagged. He watches the timer. He does not move. He makes a choice, with full clarity, not to move. The overlay times out and goes dark.

The last of the tools go off the table — screws skittering across the concrete and into the dark under the workbench — and neither of them flinches. Her hands find him everywhere they spent the last hour cataloguing wounds and aren't cataloguing anything now. His mouth finds the freckled line of her throat and she makes a sound — low, helpless, not her register at all — that she would deny making.

"Bed," she manages, "is eight feet—"

"Too far," he says, against her skin.

She laughs, breathless, like something held too long has finally let go, and closes the eight feet.

[Beat 276]
He stands with her wrapped around him — the side screams and he tells it to wait its turn — and they make it as far as the wall, which is closer and entirely sufficient. Her hand fists in his hair and she pulls him back in. Her fingers drag down his back and find new scars to learn. His hands close over the compact strong shape of her and pull her in tighter, palms moving slow across her ribs, her spine, the warm dip at the small of her back, memorizing.

She gasps his name into the crook of his neck like it's something she's allowed to have now, and his breath goes ragged and he doesn't do anything about it. His shoulders drop. Eleven years of keeping himself at the exact distance where nothing can reach him, and he lets the wall take their weight and stops calculating the distance.

They move together — her back against the wall and then the floor, his mouth at her collarbone, her hands tracing him the way they closed him, except now they aren't closing anything — and the only sounds are breath and the deep subsonic thrum of the Pulse through the ferrocrete beneath them, the whole city's heartbeat. His hand finds hers on the concrete and holds it. She curls her fingers through his and holds back.

[Beat 277]
After, the floor is hard and they don't care.

She lies half on his good side, and the apartment ticks and cools around them, and the bass from someone else's unit thumps faintly through the brick. Down where the wall meets the floor, a thin green pulse runs the length of the baseboard seam and goes still, lumen mice settling back into the dark, and neither of them turns to look. His chest goes up and down too fast and then slower. Her pink hair is stuck to her cheek.

She runs a finger along the contours of him — the same practiced palpation she'd use to find what was broken, pressed slow now and asking nothing, learning a body she intends to come back to. The burn on his shoulder. The new line of her own stitches in his side, neat as a hem. The dip of his sternum.

He does the same to her — the freckles across her collarbone, the small old scar through one eyebrow, the strong unglamorous lines of the forearms that put him back together an hour ago. His thumb finds the pulse at the base of her throat and stays there, not counting it, only feeling it move. She lets him. She watches him do it with her grey-blue eyes gone soft, and says nothing.

[Beat 278]
She moves first — checking damage, running hands over stitches. He watches her catalog him. The wall between them comes back, measured in inches.

His shoulder blade where the screw had been is already starting to yellow around the edges. The line of her stitches curves along his ribs like a question she'd asked and received. She runs her thumb over the seam and her touch becomes lighter, almost formal, the way a doctor checks your response but doesn't linger.

He doesn't move to make it easier or harder. He just watches her do the work of being separate again.

She reaches for her shirt. She finds it three feet away, pink and torn at the collar from where his hand had caught it. She puts it on without looking at him. The motion is economical, practiced, a body remembering its own edges.

The distance between them has become a question without an answer. Not anger. Not regret. Just the specific weight of two people who've shared breath realizing they are sealed systems again, and the seal is airtight.

[Beat 279]
"This can't happen again," Kyle says. He's looking at the ceiling when he says it.

The words arrive into the silence she's just built. Into the inches between them. Into the moment where the wall was still settling, still deciding whether it would hold.

Pixel props herself up on one elbow, forearm flat on the concrete, and considers him with the particular patience of a person who has just won an argument the other party doesn't know is over. She doesn't answer. She picks the screw up off the floor — the machine screw, the one she pulled from his shoulder blade an hour ago — and turns it once in the low light. Then she sets it down.

His thumb is still at the base of her throat, where he left it. He doesn't move it.

But the words have done their work: they've declared the distance real. He's named it. And in naming it he's given it weight, given it meaning, given it permission to exist between them the way it will, again and again, until one of them stops reaching across it.

She doesn't argue with him. She doesn't have to. She just lies there and lets him feel what he's just chosen, measured in tenths of an inch and the temperature of air.

[Beat 280]
She doesn't answer right away.

That's the answer.

She turns onto her side, facing the room instead of him, and she does what she always does with information she's just logged: she files it. Not dismisses — files. The two are different things and she has always known the difference.

"Okay," she says.

One word. It lands where it lands.

She stands. She finds her shirt on the floor near the kitchen chair and pulls it over her head in one motion, back turned, moving with the particular ease of a person who knows this body and has no performance left to give. Kyle watches the line of her spine — the small scar behind her left shoulder from a thermal calibration slip he'd watched her bandage herself two months ago. She doesn't look back.

She pulls on the rest of what's hers. Picks up the soldering iron from the floor. Stands the mug upright. Not tidying — accounting. Taking stock.

"You should sleep," she says. She says it toward the counter, not at him. Her professional voice. The one that means the visit is complete and the patient's stable.

"Pixel."

She looks over her shoulder.

He has nothing to follow that with. The name is the whole of it. He said it to stop her from being so cleanly competent, and it worked, and now they're both in it: the kitchen at 2E with the tools on the floor and the hour past accounting and the twelve inches of air between them that is both enormous and entirely insufficient.

Her expression doesn't change. Something behind it does.

"Sleep," she says again, and this time it sounds like she means it for both of them.

[Beat 281]
He crosses the hall at 02:47 with his ribs taped and his shoulder stiff and a cut on his forearm that will scar, because it always scars when she stitches it, because she doesn't pull the thread any tighter than necessary and that is not actually the reason it scars.

2W in the dark. His door. His space.

He puts his jacket on the hook. He sits on the edge of the bed and looks at nothing for a moment and then lies down, because there is nothing to do with this information tonight and the body needs sleep and the body is going to get sleep because that is a decision he can make.

The wall between 2W and 2E is ferrocement, fourteen centimeters, load-bearing, essentially soundproof above a certain frequency. Below it: nothing tonight. She is being quiet. He is being quiet. The building does the breathing for both of them.

The lumen mice trace the ceiling. The Atlas array runs its check at rest-hour pace, nominal across the board, unhurried. Through the wall, nothing. Through the wall: twelve inches, or maybe less than that now, or maybe the same twelve inches with a different quality to them, like a door that's been oiled on one side only — the same door, but the sound it makes has changed.

He knows the sound.

He closes his eyes.

He does not think about it. He is good at not thinking about things. He is better at it than almost anyone he knows.

The ceiling is dark. The mice do their slow work.

He does not think about it at all.

[Beat 282]
The first surveillance report arrived at 07:00, slid under Kyle's door on lined paper, written in five different hands, because apparently the night watch rotated and every member of the crew had strong opinions about penmanship.

06:12 — PIGEON ON LEDGE. SUSPICIOUS.
06:14 — PIGEON LEFT. STILL SUSPICIOUS.
06:31 — MAN WITH CART (NOODLE? VERIFY) USED ALLEY AS SHORTCUT. LICENSE PLATE: CART HAS NO PLATE. NOTED FOR FILE.
06:44 — RAIN STARTED. NOT OUR DEPARTMENT.
06:58 — NOTHING ELSE. THE CAMERA'S BLIND WINDOW IS 31 MINUTES. WE CAN DO 4-MINUTE ROTATIONS AND COVER IT WITH TWO KIDS AND A MIRROR. REQUISITION: ONE MIRROR. — B.

Kyle read it twice, standing in his doorway in the grey morning light, and experienced the specific feeling of a man who has hired well at two Φ a week and is never going to be able to say so out loud.

Across the hall, Pixel's door opened. She had her own copy of the report and the expression of a project lead whose interns had exceeded the spec.

"They want a mirror," she said.

"I read that."

"I'm getting them two mirrors and a logbook with a waterproof cover." She tapped the report against the doorframe. "The little one — Rafters — drew the pigeon. In the margin. For identification purposes." She held it up. The pigeon had been drawn with the careful menace of a wanted poster. "We're keeping them forever, you understand."

"They're on a probationary contract."

"Forever," said Pixel, and closed her door.

[Beat 283]
