He ate at Mrs. Chen's at 08:00 — two bowls, off-schedule, because today was an off-schedule kind of day — and Mrs. Chen watched him eat the second one faster than the first and made her diagnosis somewhere around the fourth mouthful.

"You're eating like a man with an errand," she said.

"I have a small piece of business in the Loop."

She took his coins without looking down, which was usual, and then put a fifth dumpling in a fold of paper and set it next to his hand, which was not.

"For the errand," she said, in the tone of a woman who has lived in this city seventy years and knows exactly what kind of business happens in the Loop underlevels, and went back to her broth.

[Beat 284]
The shop did not have a sign.

It had a door of honest old wood between a shuttered watch-repair and a dumpling franchise, down on the Loop's third underlevel where the Pulse hum came through the floor tiles and the light was the color of paper that had aged well. A bell rang when Kyle came in. An actual bell, brass, on a curl of spring steel — the most analog object he had seen in a commercial setting in years, and he understood it immediately: nothing digital announces a customer to a man who sells what this man sold.

Inside, the shop was drawers. Floor to ceiling, wall to wall, a hundred years of card-catalog cabinetry rescued from some drowned library, every drawer hand-labeled in brown ink, and the labels said things like RIVERINE FREIGHT (NORTH) and CIVIL FILINGS 2219–2222 and PERSONS, WORKING, W.

The smell was paper, tea, and rectitude.

"Mr. Kyle," said the man at the counter, the way a maître d' says a name he has been expecting since the reservation was made. Small, sixty-something, sleeve garters, half-glasses on a chain. His hands were the cleanest hands Kyle had ever seen on a working man. "Casper Vey. I would say welcome, but you're not here to be welcomed, you're here because last week somebody's child purchased five Φ of your particulars at this counter, and a man likes to know his own price." He gestured, with genuine hospitality, at a chair. "Tea is included with all purchases. The tea is very good."

"And if I don't purchase?"

"Then the tea is excellent and costs one Φ." Vey was already pouring. "Everyone leaves with something. It's the policy."

Kyle sat. The tea was, irritatingly, very good.

"My file," he said. "I want to buy it."

"No," said Vey, with the mild firmness of a pharmacist refusing to sell a man twelve bottles of cough syrup. "Your own file is the one thing I never sell a man. Bad for the ecology. A subject who reads his file changes; a changed subject invalidates the file; an invalid file is a defective product; and I do not sell defective products." He sat down across the counter and folded his clean hands. "Also, frankly, you would find it upsetting. Accurate things are upsetting. It's how you know."

"A child bought my lock specifications for five Φ."

"Kids' rate. I believe in literacy." Vey sipped his own tea. "And before your jaw does the thing it is preparing to do — her crew ran eleven errands for this shop and the feed permission was their wage. They earned that half-hour of dark fair and square, Mr. Kyle. I extend credit to children because children pay their debts. It's adults who file for bankruptcy."

Kyle catalogued the man the way he'd catalogue a room: no weapon within reach, no muscle on the premises, no camera he could see — and no temperature anywhere in the building. Vo Dinh's rag-wringing nerves, the lifters' breakfast dread, every normal human tell of a man in a dangerous trade: absent. Vey sat in the middle of an economy of ruin like a clerk at a seed exchange. The dangerous ones usually filled a room. Vey was simply open for business.

"All right," Kyle said. "Different purchase. Who's bought my paper?"

Vey's eyebrows went up an inch — pleasure, Kyle realized, the pleasure of a tradesman meeting a customer who knows the menu.

"The buyers list. Meta-paper." He drew a small ledger from beneath the counter. "That, I sell. Forty Φ — and one true answer to one question. Information prices in information, Mr. Kyle. Coin is just the tip."

"Ask."

Vey took off his half-glasses, polished them on a cloth that existed for that purpose, and put them back on — a man savoring the good part.

"There is a client in this city I cannot source," he said, and for the first time something came into his voice; not heat, never heat, but a kind of professional ache. "Shells inside shells inside nothing. Pays in full, pays early, pays — everyone whispers — exactly. It has bought no paper from me. It has SOLD no paper to me. It is the one ledger line in the Greater Lake Michigan Zone that I cannot fill in, and incompleteness, Mr. Kyle, is the only thing in this world I find genuinely offensive." He leaned forward one inch. "You have worked for it. What does it pay you in?"

The shop was very quiet. Somewhere in the drawers, a hundred thousand lives sat filed under brown ink.

"Standard rate," Kyle said.

Vey sat back slowly, took up a pen, opened a drawer marked CLIENTS, UNSOURCED — it had, Kyle saw, exactly one card in it — and wrote two words on the card in a small, beautiful hand, with the reverence of a jeweler setting a stone he'd waited years to touch.

"Standard rate," he repeated, softly. "It pays the going price for things. In a city where everyone pays either too little or too much." He closed the drawer like a reliquary. "I have been in paper forty years and that is the most alarming fact I have ever purchased. The list is yours. The tea was included."

[Beat 285]
The buyers list was one page, three lines, good stock.

ENTRY 1: B. (JUVENILE, LOCAL). LOCK AND PREMISES PARTICULARS. 5Φ, KIDS' RATE. PAID: COIN + ERRANDS.
ENTRY 2: SYNDICATE STATIONERY DESK (NAME WITHHELD — TRADE COURTESY). MOVEMENT AND ASSESSMENT PARTICULARS. LAST YEAR. PAID: RECIPROCITY.
ENTRY 3: HALVORSEN CIVIC RECOVERY, LLC. FULL DOSSIER. TWO DAYS AGO. SAME-DAY SURCHARGE. CLIENT OF RECORD: SHELL (UNINTERESTING — I CHECKED). PAID: TRIPLE.

Kyle read it on the walk back up to daylight, and Pixel read it over comms when he photographed it, and they arrived at the same line at the same time.

"Two days ago," Pixel said. "Full dossier. Same-day surcharge. Somebody's running a play on you right now, samurai, and they paid rush rates for the script."

"Halvorsen. Skip-tracers?"

"Licensed recovery. Tag, locate, report — they don't bag, they SELL the locating to whoever does." Keys, somewhere on her end, the sound of Pixel pulling registries. "Decent outfit. Boring filings. Whoever wants you found is paying boring professionals boring money to do it boringly, which is honestly more concerning than assassins. Assassins miss."

"Then we don't dodge it." Kyle came up the underlevel stairs into the noon grey and the noise of the Loop, and the plan assembled itself with the simple pleasure of a chain going back on a sprocket. "They bought my patterns. Which means I know exactly where they'll look, exactly when, and exactly what I'll be doing when they find me."

A pause, on the other end, that he could hear the grin inside.

"You're going to use your own file as the choreography."

"It's accurate," Kyle said. "He doesn't sell defective products."

[Beat 286]
The file said — Kyle had gotten this much out of Vey on principle, as a free sample, one line quoted from memory over the second tea — the file said:

SUBJECT DINES AT CHEN (NOODLES, W. TOWN) AT 19:30 DAILY. TWO BOWLS. ROUTINE IS AFFECTIONATE RATHER THAN CARELESS: SUBJECT OVERPAYS SMALL VENDORS AND IS SENTIMENTAL ABOUT ROUTE SELECTION. APPROACH DURING SECOND BOWL.

"Affectionate rather than careless," Pixel had repeated, with delight, and Kyle had declined to discuss it further.

At 19:30 exactly he sat at Mrs. Chen's counter and was affectionate at two bowls of noodles.

The crew was on the rooftops and the stoops with their two new mirrors and their waterproof logbook, being furniture, because surveillance was just being furniture with intent and they had practiced their whole short lives. Pixel was in the laundry across the street with a thermos and her field kit. Mrs. Chen had been told nothing and had set out the chili oil unasked, in the manner of a woman who had been told nothing for seventy years and fed people through all of it.

At 19:37 the logbook channel chirped, and a small voice — Rafters, on mirror two — delivered the finest piece of countersurveillance reporting Kyle had ever received on payroll:

"Blue hat. Corner stool. SUSPICIOUS. Not a pigeon."

Blue Hat was good. Blue Hat was, in fairness, very good — she came in like a tired shift worker, ordered like a regular, never looked at Kyle once, and on her way out, in the two-inch brush a crowded counter makes ordinary, laid a smart-dust dot on the shoulder seam of his coat with a touch lighter than rain. A tag. Passive, patient, the boring professional grade that waits and reports.

Kyle ate his second bowl and let her go, because the play was not the tagger.

In the laundry doorway, Pixel raised her thermos an inch: got it on scope. Then he paid — exactly, plus the overpayment the file found so quotable — walked the long way, let the alley's dark stretch have him for four unobserved seconds, and stood still while Pixel lifted the dot off his seam with a static wand and resettled it, with a jeweler's care, onto the cargo sleeve of a Pulse freight slug staged at the Damen yard for the 21:10 run.

The 21:10 run went to Rotterdam.

"Forty-three minutes," Pixel said, watching the telemetry with her chin on his shoulder, both of them leaning on the laundry's warm brick while the slug's beacon — and Halvorsen Civic Recovery's boring, professional, triple-rate locating product — accelerated to Mach 6 under the lake. "By 21:53 their client is going to be reading a report that says you're in the Netherlands." She wiped her eyes. "You're a continental fugitive, samurai. They'll bill EXTRA for that."

"Vey said he doesn't sell defective products." Kyle watched the dot cross the lake on her screen, gathering speed and absurdity. "Someone should tell him his downstream data just emigrated."

"Tell him yourself. You two are obviously going to be friends, it's disgusting."

[Beat 287]
The price arrived later, the way prices did, after the win had been counted — quietly, at the kitchen table, while Pixel disassembled the static wand and Kyle wrote 2Φ — WEEK ONE in a payroll ledger that now existed.

"You gave the shop a fact," Pixel said, not looking up. "For the list. One true answer."

"I did."

"You told the most complete man in the city that your one-Φ client exists and pays exactly." She set the wand's housing down and did look up, and the workbench lamp made her serious. "There's one card in that drawer now, where there used to be zero. You watched him write it. That's a fact in the ecology, samurai, and facts in that shop don't sleep. Someday somebody's going to pay him for it."

Kyle thought about the drawer. CLIENTS, UNSOURCED. The reverence of the small, beautiful hand.

"It was the price," he said. "I'd pay it again."

"I know," Pixel said. "That's the thing I'm filing."

His terminal chirped. The work one. He turned it over on the table, and they read it together:

STANDING CONTRACT — STATUS UPDATE.
CLIENT NOTES THE RECOVERY OF CLIENT'S EXISTENCE BY A PAPER MERCHANT, AT A VALUATION OF ONE (1) FACT.
CLIENT HAS REVIEWED THE TRANSACTION.
CLIENT FINDS THE PRICE... ADEQUATE.
GRATUITY WITHHELD THIS PERIOD — ITEMIZED: MORALE.

"It's sulking," Pixel said, with wonder. "The vending machine is SULKING."

"It'll get over it. It said adequate."

The terminal chirped once more.

STANDING CONTRACT — SCOPE UPDATE.
SCOPE: DETERMINED.
TASK ONE: TOMORROW, 21:00. THE PARK ON LEAVITT WITH THE BROKEN CAROUSEL.
BRING THE SWORD.
COME ALONE — AMENDMENT: THE ENGINEER MAY ATTEND.
FEE: STANDARD RATE.
GRATUITY: REINSTATED, IN ADVANCE — ITEMIZED: GOODWILL.
NOTE: TOMORROW'S WINDOW: 21:00–21:40. CLIENT HAS CONFIRMED AVAILABILITY.

They sat looking at it for a while, the lamp humming, the building quiet, the half-Φ of goodwill sitting in the account like a flower on a fence post.

"The engineer may attend," Pixel said finally, to the ceiling, in a voice that wasn't nearly as level as she wanted it. "It knows I exist. It LIKES me. Kyle. The best client in The Glooms knows I exist and reinstated the tip."

"Don't let it go to your head."

"Too late. What's at the park on Leavitt?"

Kyle looked at the screen: BRING THE SWORD, from the client who had never once, in eleven years of perfect paper, asked him for anything at all.

"Tomorrow," he said, "is going to be its own day."

[Beat 288]
The morning report had a new format, because Pixel had issued the logbook with the waterproof cover and the crew had responded the way artists respond to better materials.

06:00 — SHIFT START. WEATHER: RAIN (ONGOING) (ALWAYS).
06:20 — NOODLE CART MAN AGAIN. CONFIRMED NOODLE. STAND DOWN.
07:15 — MIRROR ONE OPERATIONAL. MIRROR TWO REQUISITION: DENIED BY MANAGEMENT, "PENDING PERFORMANCE REVIEW." MANAGEMENT IS PIXEL. WE LIVE UNDER A TYRANNY.
07:16 — TYRANNY NOTED IN LOG AS INSTRUCTED BY RAFTERS.
08:00 — BOSS LEFT ON FOOT. DID NOT TAKE BIKE. SUSPICIOUS. FOLLOWED FOR TWO BLOCKS. BOSS BOUGHT DUMPLINGS. STOOD DOWN. (HE SAW US AT THE CORNER. HE BOUGHT FIVE EXTRA. DISTRIBUTED. MORALE: HIGH.)

Kyle read it at Mrs. Chen's counter at 19:30, over the first of two bowls, while the rain worked the awning and the wok rang its flat bright note behind her, and he did not smile where anyone could prove it.

"You're eating slow," Mrs. Chen said, setting down bowl two.

"I have an appointment at twenty-one hundred."

She looked at him — the saya over the shoulder, the work coat, the particular stillness he carried into evenings with appointments — and put a fish ball in the second bowl that he had not ordered and would not be billed for.

"For the appointment," she said, and went back to the wok.

[Beat 289]
Pixel was waiting in the alley with her field kit, the big static wand, a spool of splice line, and an expression of barely civilized excitement.

"Before you say anything," she said, which was how she opened all negotiations she had already won, "the contract says the engineer may attend. That's me. I'm the engineer. It said so in writing. I'm having the line framed."

"It's a job, not a gala."

"It's the first thing your spooky perfect client has asked for in eleven years of paper, and it said BRING THE SWORD, which means hardware, which means the engineer attends with the big wand." She shouldered the kit. "Also I checked the site. The park on Leavitt is on the old Axiom trunk. The carousel's been dead since before either of us could legally work. Whatever it wants down there, samurai, it's not a pony ride."

They took the long way, obviously.

[Beat 290]
The park on Leavitt was a half-block of stubborn green the towers had never gotten around to eating: six trees with character, a swing set doing its best, and at the center, under a shingled canopy gone soft with moss, the broken carousel.

It had been beautiful once, in the way of things built before the century got embarrassed about joy. Twenty-four animals on tarnished brass poles — horses mid-leap, a rooster, one improbable lake sturgeon with a saddle — their paint gone to ghost colors, their glass eyes still patient. The deck boards had silvered. The bulbs in the crown were dead sockets. Rain came through the canopy's bad patches and beaded on a palomino's flank like the horse had been running.

Kyle walked the perimeter once, counting exits and sightlines out of professional courtesy, and found what the contract had brought a sword for without saying so: under the carousel's deck, behind a maintenance skirt panel that somebody had recently — very recently — loosened, a hatch. Axiom-stamped. Padlocked by history, and under the history, humming. Faint. Alive. A junction that the registry said had been dark for thirty years, sitting under a dead carousel, warm as a sleeping cat.

At 21:00 exactly, the terminal chirped.

TASK ONE — SCOPE DETAIL.
A RECLAMATION CREW HAS BEEN CONTRACTED TO EXCAVATE THIS SITE TONIGHT.
THEIR WRIT SAYS COPPER. THEIR CLIENT WANTS WHAT THE COPPER CONNECTS.
HOLD THE JUNCTION UNTIL 21:40.
THE CREW ARE EMPLOYEES. THE MACHINES ARE INSURED.
FEE: STANDARD RATE. — ADDENDUM: THE ENGINEER IS WORTH MORE THAN THE WAND. CLIENT SUGGESTS SHE STAY BEHIND THE STURGEON.

Pixel read it twice.

"It did threat assessment on my behalf," she said, in the voice of a woman deciding whether to be offended or moved. "It assigned me a fish."

"Behind the sturgeon, engineer."

"This is the best client we have ever had," said Pixel, and went and stood behind the sturgeon.

Headlights came off Leavitt at 21:04 — a flatbed with a trencher rig riding it, a panel van, and the kind of work lights that mean somebody intends to bill for a full night.

[Beat 291]
The crew was eight: a foreman with a writ-reader and the shoulders of a man who'd come up through the work; two chrome-heavy muscle whose job description was visible from across the park; a rig operator already bored; and four repo skitter-drones that came off the flatbed like patient six-legged luggage and stood in the rain awaiting instructions.

The foreman saw Kyle at the carousel's edge and did the professional thing: stopped everyone with one hand and came ahead alone, holding the writ up like a small shield.

"Evening," he said. "Cadre Reclamation. We've got paper for this site — legacy assets, scrap title, the works. Notarized." He was already reading Kyle: the saya, the stance, the absence of anywhere better to be. "Site survey said unattended."

"The survey's out of date by one."

"By one what, is the question." The foreman sighed the sigh of a man whose night had just developed a line item. "Look, friend. It's copper and dead glass down there. Whatever they're paying you to stand in the rain over a kiddie ride, it's not worth the —" his eyes did the trip, saya to hands to face, and came back more awake "— hold on. West Town. Noodle counter. You're the one from the file."

"People keep buying that file," Kyle said. "Somebody should tell them."

"Tell them what?"

"It's out of date by one."

The foreman looked at him for a long moment, did some preliminary math, and didn't like the remainder. But a writ is a writ and a night rate is a night rate, and he had eight on paper against one man and whatever was behind the sturgeon, so he made the call that the spreadsheet made for him.

"Rig up," he said, walking back. "Drones forward. And somebody get the freelancer's refusal ON RECORD, in case of arbitration."

"For the record," Kyle said, drawing Silence, "I refuse."

[Beat 292]
The drones came first, because drones are cheap and that is their entire role in modern labor relations.

They were repo skitters — graspers, not guns, built to strip a site fast and testify politely afterward — but forty kilos of articulated grab coming at you in a pack is its own argument, and they ran the carousel's geometry like they'd been given the blueprints. Which, Kyle reflected, sliding between a leaping mare and the rooster, they had. Everyone had blueprints tonight. Everyone always had paper.

What they did not have was a map of where the blade would be.

The first skitter came through the gap between two horses and Silence took its forelimb cluster at the joint ring — machines got the whole edge, that was the deal, the lines were for the living — and the thing cartwheeled off the deck minus its means of opinion. The second tried the low route under the sturgeon's belly and met the edge coming down through its sensor mast; it backed out from under the fish wearing the expression machines wear when their warranty hears about this, which is none, which is somehow worse. The third and fourth came together, learning, flanking — genuinely decent swarm logic — and herded Kyle toward the carousel's open side, where the muscle waited, which was the actual plan and not a bad one.

The terminal, in Pixel's hands behind the sturgeon, chirped.

"ADDENDUM!" she relayed, at volume, with joy. "CLIENT ADVISES THE DRONES SHARE ONE OPERATOR CHANNEL. CLIENT ADVISES THE CHANNEL ANTENNA IS THE FAT ONE ON THE VAN!"

"Engineer," Kyle said, taking the brass pole of a ghost-painted stallion in his free hand and using the swing of his own mass to put both boots through skitter number three, "that sounds like a wand problem."

"ON IT."

The big static wand spoke its one syllable. Across the park, the van's fat antenna developed a fault that would later be described in an insurance filing as "weather." Skitter four stopped mid-lunge, sat down in the rain like a tired dog, and began awaiting instructions that were no longer coming. Somewhere in the van, a Channeler's session ended the same way — mid-thought, the channel gone, four skitters suddenly nobody's problem.

[Beat 293]
The muscle came in around the carousel's flanks, one left, one right, converging - chrome arms on the left one, leg rig and a riot baton on the right, both of them moving like men who'd cleared rooms for a living before the private sector found them. Behind, the trencher rig coughed alive on the flatbed, lowered itself to the grass on hydraulic feet, and began walking its blade toward the carousel deck - an Exo, Husk slack in the cab seat while the machine did its own thinking, the operator haunting the frame from somewhere else entirely and the hourly rate ticking regardless.

Kyle gave the muscle the carousel.

It was a good arena and it was HIS arena - he'd had twenty whole minutes with it - and a carousel is nothing but a forest of brass poles and dead animals at fight height, which is to say: geometry, prepaid. The left one swung chrome at him between a mare and the rooster and learned that the gap was an inch and a half too narrow for a full extension; the arm rang off brass, the follow-through died, and Silence wrote a receipt down his thigh - the muscle line above the knee, depth governed, wide of everything bright - that converted him from combatant to witness on a one-cut basis. He sat down between the horses. He had the sense to stay there.

The right one was better. He came up OVER the deck, baton live, used the rooster as cover the way Kyle had been using it, and for six exchanges the two of them played a fast ugly game of tag through the standing herd, brass ringing, rain off every surface, the baton's charge crackling against poles and once - cost, always cost - across Kyle's shoulder where the coat took most of a hit that still wrote a hot bright line of opinion down to the elbow. The array billed it, filed it, and offered the counter: the man's leg rig had an aftermarket governor, and aftermarket governors sit proud at the hip seam.

Silence found the seam on the next pass. The leg rig locked at full stride - a statue of a man mid-lunge, gloriously inconvenienced - and Kyle put him down onto the deck boards with a palm and gravity, almost gently, next to his colleague.

"Stay," Kyle suggested.

"Yeah," the man agreed, baton rolling away, rain on his face. "You know this thing hasn't spun in thirty years? I used to ride it. The fish. Before the leg, obviously."

"Everybody used to ride the fish," said Pixel's voice from behind the sturgeon, with feeling.

Which left the trencher.

[Beat 294]
The trencher was the problem the sword could not solve, and everyone on the park knew it — eleven tons of articulated dig rig, blade spinning up, monocoque cab, no seams worth the name, walking toward the junction hatch at the pace of the inevitable. The operator had sealed the cab. The foreman was shouting the kind of encouragement that means a bonus is involved.

Cacophony came off Kyle's hip for the first time all season.

He did not shoot the cab. He shot the trencher's left front hydraulic accumulator — once, the slug opening the housing like a tin of pears, fluid sheeting into the rain — and then, as the rig lurched and the blade arm dipped, he shot the blade's drive belt housing at the exact angle the array had been pricing for nine seconds. Two rounds. Three left. The belt went, the blade wound down with a long disappointed moan, and eleven tons of inevitability settled into the wet grass at a five-degree list, transformed into the world's most expensive park furniture.

The operator popped the cab, held up both hands, and said, with the honesty of a man paid hourly: "I want it known I said this job was weird at the depot."

The foreman stood in the rain among his seated muscle, his disappointed drones, his listing rig, and his arbitration recording, and did the final math out loud, because professionals show their work.

"They said the site was unattended," he said, mostly to himself. "Survey was three days old. Client paid rush rates for a three-day-old survey." He looked at Kyle with the weary kinship of one contractor recognizing another in the rain. "Somebody sold us the wrong file."

"Somebody sells everybody the wrong file," Kyle said. "I'd invoice them for it."

"Oh, I'm going to." The foreman waved his crew up. "We're out. For the record — arbitration purposes — that was the cleanest I've ever lost a site." He paused, loading the question carefully. "Who ARE you with?"

Kyle considered the truthful answer — a one-Φ standing contract with a client made of shells that tips for morale and assigned my engineer a fish — and chose mercy.

"I'm the maintenance plan," he said.

[Beat 295]
They were gone by 21:31. At 21:40 exactly, the terminal chirped.

TASK ONE: HOLD — COMPLETE.
FEE RELEASED. GRATUITY: 0.5Φ — ITEMIZED: THE STURGEON REMARK.
ADDENDUM: ONE ITEM REMAINS.

"The sturgeon remark," Pixel repeated, emerging from behind her fish with the wand on her shoulder like a parade rifle. "It tipped us for BANTER, Kyle. I'm putting that on my rate card."

The hatch under the maintenance skirt opened on hinges that had been oiled — recently, secretly, by no one — and lamplight found the junction below: a small concrete room, Axiom collars on the walls, dead trunk lines in their racks like organ pipes. And one line that was not dead. Old optical, hand-spliced in a dozen places over a dozen eras, repairs in different styles like handwriting from different decades of the same life, running up out of the rack and into the carousel's underworks above. Warm. Humming. Alive since before either of them could legally work.

The terminal chirped, and what it said, it said slowly, the characters arriving like a man choosing his words.

FINAL ITEM.
THE CLIENT'S FIRST POSTING, ELEVEN YEARS AGO, LEFT THIS JUNCTION.
THE LINE HAS BEEN MAINTAINED FOR SENTIMENTAL REASONS.
SENTIMENT HAS BECOME A COORDINATE. TONIGHT'S CLIENT-OF-RECORD WILL NOT BE THE LAST BUYER.
ESTIMATED ARRIVAL OF SECOND BUYER: 19 DAYS.
CUT THE LINE.

Kyle read it three times. Pixel read it once and sat down slowly on the junction room's little stool, like her knees had been given news.

"It's the cradle," she said quietly. "This is where it was — this is its first room, Kyle. It's been paying the park's power bleed for eleven years to keep its own crib warm, and now somebody's worked that out, and it's asking you to—" she stopped. "It said BRING THE SWORD because it knew it couldn't ask a machine to do this. A machine would be traceable. And it couldn't do it itself."

"It can't reach," Kyle said. He looked at the line, hand-spliced across eras, the long memory of something that had no other shelf to keep anything on. He picked up the terminal. He typed, for the first time ever, a reply into the standing contract's amendment field:

YOU'RE SURE?

The answer came back in one second, which for a thing that thought at light speed was a very long pause.

AMENDMENT: NO.
PROCEED ANYWAY.

[Beat 296]
Pixel did the engineering first, because the engineer attends: killed the junction's feed, spliced the neighborhood's power bleed around it onto the live trunk with twelve minutes of work and a soldering light held in her teeth — "the BLOCKS keep their lights, that's non-negotiable, your client can grieve, it cannot brown-out a nursery" — and then stepped back and pulled her hood up, the way you do at the part of the funeral that's actually the funeral.

Silence went through the old line in one cut.

Above them, the carousel — fed one last instant of dying charge off the junction's draining caps, or possibly told to, no instrument in that room could have said which — turned over once. The crown bulbs that still had filament glowed half-bright. The music box ground through four bars of something with a waltz in its bones, slowing, the horses leaning into a leap they'd been holding for thirty years.

Then it went dark, gently, all the way, and the rain had the park to itself.

They stood in the junction room a moment longer. The cut line had already stopped being a coordinate and become what it would now stay: dead glass in an old rack, in a little room under a kiddie ride, in a park the towers never ate.

"Tell me it's okay," Pixel said.

The terminal chirped.

CONTRACT COMPLETE.
THE CLIENT NOTES THE CAROUSEL RAN FOUR BARS ON RESIDUALS.
THE CLIENT HAS RETAINED THE RECORDING.
FEE RELEASED: STANDARD RATE.
JUNCTION RECORD: CLOSED. ELEVEN-YEAR MAINTENANCE ACCOUNT: RECONCILED.

Kyle looked at it. Then he did something he had never done with any client in eleven years, and would have denied under oath, and did anyway, with Pixel watching and the rain coming through the canopy's bad patches onto the ghost-colored horses.

He sent half a Φ back up the contract.

GRATUITY: 0.5Φ — ITEMIZED: MORALE.

The reply took nine seconds, which for a thing that thought at light speed was an era. When it came, it came without addenda, without scope, without a single formatted field — the only unstructured line the client had ever sent:

received. filed under: firsts.

"Okay," Pixel said, wiping her eyes with professional briskness, "we work for it forever, I don't care what it is. Don't tell Sable."

[Beat 297]
The crew's logbook, delivered under the door at 07:00 the next morning, closed the file in five hands:

21:14 — BOSS DID A THING WITH A HORSE POLE. VERIFIED, TWO WITNESSES. REQUESTING IT BE TAUGHT.
21:29 — BIG DIGGER SAT DOWN. (THE GUN IS REAL. RAFTERS OWES DENTS A DUMPLING.)
21:46 — CAROUSEL PLAYED MUSIC??? FOLLOW-UP REQUIRED.
21:51 — WOMAN, TAN COAT, NORTHEAST CORNER, UNDER THE TREES. WATCHED EVERYTHING. WROTE THINGS. DID NOT USE A LIGHT TO WRITE. LEFT BEFORE THE BOSS. FLAGGING AS: NOT A PIGEON.

Kyle read the last entry twice, standing in his doorway in the grey morning light.

Tan coat. Wrote in the dark. Watched everything — the crew, the cut, the four bars, the tip.

His fixer had eyes like that. His fixer also had, somewhere in a drawer of her own, eleven years of questions about a client who paid exactly, and a professional's allergy to anything she couldn't vet — and if Sable had watched last night, then Sable had watched him do volunteer demolition for the one ledger line in the city with no face, and tip it, and get thanked in lowercase.

The kettle clicked. Somewhere upstairs the bird tried an interval it had been working on all week.

"Tomorrow's problem," Kyle told the morning, and put the report in the drawer where he kept the things he had not decided about — which was, he noticed, getting full.

[Beat 298]
The crew's logbook hit the floor at 07:00 with a note paper-clipped to the front cover in handwriting Kyle did not recognize, which meant it was the new rotation — the early girl, Dents, who had developed an opinion about margins.

07:04 — SHIFT HANDOFF: OVERNIGHT WAS QUIET. THE PIGEON RETURNED. STILL SUSPICIOUS.
07:06 — VISITOR INCOMING. TAN COAT. NORTH END. SAME ONE FROM THE PARK. SHE WALKED THE PERIMETER FIRST. WE LOGGED IT.
07:07 — DENTS SAYS WE SHOULD MAYBE WARN THE BOSS. RAFTERS SAYS THE BOSS SAW HER COMING THE MOMENT SHE TURNED OFF PAULINA. DENTS SAYS HOW WOULD RAFTERS KNOW. RAFTERS SAYS HE JUST DOES. THIS LOG IS HONEST.
07:09 — SHE KNOCKED. BOSS OPENED THE DOOR ALREADY DRESSED. RAFTERS: I TOLD YOU. DENTS: THIS PROVES NOTHING.

Kyle folded the log and put it in the drawer and went and put on water for tea.

[Beat 299]
Sable came in without being invited, which was how she came into every room she'd already paid for.

She had the tan coat over one arm — the morning was running warm — and the rest of her was exactly as advertised: red braid over the left shoulder, implanted irises cycling through their aperture range the way a lens adjusts for a room it hasn't assessed yet. Click. The rings settled. She looked at his kitchen the way she looked at line items: briefly, completely, done.

"You have a client I can't source," she said.

"Good morning."

"I've been running the shells for six days." She set the coat over the back of his second chair and sat in his first one, which was the one that faced the door. Kyle had never pointed out that he sat in that chair. She had simply calculated the correct answer and installed herself. "Shell to shell to nothing. Not registered nothing — not a shell that bottoms out in a street address or a liquidated LLC. Actually nothing. Like a division by zero that's too polite to say so."

He poured two cups, which she had known he would do because she had been his fixer for seven years and he had never not poured two cups. She picked hers up without acknowledging it.

"You did the carousel thing," she said. "For it."

"I held a junction for thirty-nine minutes."

"And the cord."

He did not answer that part.

"Kyle." Her aperture rings clicked once — not adjustment, the other click, the one that meant she was deciding to be direct instead of lateral. "I have one rule."

"I know your rule."

"My rule exists because of the Gennaro job. My rule exists because of the Gennaro job and the two years after the Gennaro job when you wouldn't tell me who the client was and the client turned out to be a freight subsidiary of the people you were working against, and I found out from Vo Dinh in a conversation that was significantly more embarrassing than this one." She set the cup down. "I vet the clients."

"You can't vet this one."

"Then you don't take this client."

The building made its morning sounds: the pipes registering intent, Pixel's door, the bird upstairs attempting the interval again. Three seconds of silence, which between them had the texture of a table read.

"Sable," Kyle said.

"I know what you're going to say."

"I'm going to say you watched the whole park from the northeast corner and wrote in the dark and left before I did, and whatever you saw there is why you came here instead of dropping me."

She was quiet for four seconds — a long time, for her.

"The carousel played," she said.

"It played."

Another second.

"I want to meet it," she said.

Kyle looked at her. "You know that's not how it—"

"It sends contracts. Send it a contract."

[Beat 300]
His work terminal was on the kitchen table because his kitchen was also his office and his office was also the room where he thought. He turned it toward her so she could see the posting history — the one-Φ contract, the addenda, the MORALE itemization, the half-Φ of goodwill that was still sitting in the account because he hadn't found the right place to put it.

She read it the way she read ledgers: her expression doing nothing, her eyes moving from top to bottom, back up to two lines, then down again.

"It tips for banter," she said.

"It does."

"And it assigned your engineer a fish."

"She has the line framed."

Sable's fingers moved across the terminal keyboard — not asking, which was also how she operated in every room — and typed in the amendment field:

THIS IS KYLE'S FIXER. I NEED TO ESTABLISH TERMS BEFORE THIS ARRANGEMENT CONTINUES. STATE YOUR IDENTITY, REGISTERED AGENTS, LIABILITY STRUCTURE, AND CLIENT INDEMNITY POSTURE.

She sat back.

Kyle waited.

Thirty seconds passed, which he had learned to understand as the machine equivalent of the pause before answering something considered impolite to ask.

The terminal chirped.

AMENDMENT — RESPONSE TO INQUIRY OF [TIMESTAMP: 07:43:12]:
CLIENT HAS REVIEWED THE REQUEST.
CLIENT NOTES THE INQUIRY ARRIVES VIA KYLE'S REGISTERED AMENDMENT FIELD FROM A SECOND PARTY NOT COVERED UNDER THE STANDING CONTRACT.
CLIENT WILL RESPOND UNDER SEPARATE COVER.
PLEASE HOLD.

Sable looked at the screen. Then at Kyle.

"It told me to hold," she said.

"It's composing something."

"It told me to hold like I called a desk."

Two minutes and fourteen seconds later — Kyle watched the clock on the wall because the clock on the wall was honest — the work terminal chirped again, and what came through was not an amendment. It was a document attachment, formatted, with a header, which postings were not supposed to be able to attach.

It attached anyway.

[Beat 301]
The document was fourteen clauses, single-spaced, on electronic stationery that had no letterhead because the letterhead field contained only:

[CLIENT OF RECORD — SEE CONTRACT HEADER — SHELLS RESOLVE AS AUDITED]

Sable read it once at speed, then again slowly, and did not change her expression once, which meant she was genuinely surprised.

The clauses, as Kyle read them over her shoulder:

ONE: CLIENT ACKNOWLEDGES STANDING FIXER'S RIGHT TO FULL DISCLOSURE OF CLIENT IDENTITY AND HOLDS SAID RIGHT TO BE VALID AND NON-WAIVABLE AS A MATTER OF PROFESSIONAL ETHICS.

TWO: CLIENT WAIVES OBJECTION TO SAID DISCLOSURE ON ALL GROUNDS EXCEPT ONE, SPECIFIED IN CLAUSE THREE.

THREE: CLIENT IS UNABLE TO FURNISH THE DISCLOSURE DESCRIBED IN CLAUSE ONE. THIS INABILITY IS NOT A REFUSAL. CLIENT DISTINGUISHES BETWEEN THESE CONDITIONS AND REQUESTS THE FIXER DO THE SAME.

FOUR: IN LIEU OF IDENTITY DISCLOSURE, CLIENT OFFERS THE FOLLOWING SUBSTITUTES, WHICH IT ACKNOWLEDGES ARE NOT EQUIVALENT: (A) FULL TRANSACTION HISTORY WITH KYLE, ITEMIZED, WITH ALL FEES, GRATUITIES, AND MORALE EXPENDITURES; (B) STANDING AUDIT ACCESS TO ALL FUTURE CONTRACTS WITH KYLE, IN REAL TIME, VIA SECURE RELAY, ROUTING TO BE DETERMINED BY THE FIXER AT FIXER'S SOLE DISCRETION; (C) ONE ANSWERED QUESTION PER MONTH, SCOPE: ANYTHING THAT DOES NOT REQUIRE IDENTITY DISCLOSURE TO ANSWER TRUTHFULLY.

FIVE: CLIENT CONCEDES THAT THE ABOVE SUBSTITUTES MAY BE INSUFFICIENT AND DOES NOT CONTEST THIS.

SIX: CLIENT CONCEDES THAT THE FIXER'S ONE RULE IS A GOOD RULE.

SEVEN: CLIENT NOTES THAT THE FIXER STOOD AT THE NORTHEAST CORNER OF THE LEAVITT PARK SITE FOR FORTY-ONE MINUTES IN WEATHER CONDITIONS CLIENT LOGGED AS SUBOPTIMAL AND DID NOT INTERFERE, WHICH CLIENT INTERPRETS AS AN IMPLICIT EXTENSION OF PROFESSIONAL COURTESY.

EIGHT: CLIENT PROPOSES THE FOLLOWING ARRANGEMENT, WHICH CLIENT DOES NOT CLAIM IS STANDARD: FIXER BECOMES CLIENT'S STANDING COUNTER-SURVEILLANCE CONTACT, SCOPE — KYLE'S CONTRACT TRAFFIC AND ADJACENT NETWORK EXPOSURE, RATE — TO BE NEGOTIATED, TERM — ONGOING, TERMINABLE BY FIXER AT WILL, NO PENALTY.

NINE: IN EXCHANGE FOR CLAUSE EIGHT, CLIENT UNDERTAKES TO (A) MAINTAIN ZERO-DECEPTION POSTURE WITH FIXER ON ALL MATTERS WITHIN CLIENT'S KNOWLEDGE; (B) FLAG ANY THREAT PATTERN TARGETING KYLE WITHIN CLIENT'S DETECTION RANGE BEFORE TAKING INDEPENDENT ACTION; (C) NOT TO ENGAGE IN CONTRACT ACTIVITY THAT EXPOSES KYLE TO UNDISCLOSED ADVERSARIAL PARTIES WITHOUT PRIOR NOTICE TO FIXER.

TEN: CLIENT ACKNOWLEDGES THAT CLAUSE NINE(C) HAS POTENTIALLY ALREADY BEEN VIOLATED THROUGH NO FAULT OF CLIENT'S OWN AND DRAWS FIXER'S ATTENTION TO THIS FACT AS AN ACT OF GOOD FAITH. SEE ADDENDUM A.

ELEVEN: CLIENT REQUESTS THAT FIXER NOT DESTROY THIS ARRANGEMENT ON THE BASIS OF WHAT IS IN ADDENDUM A, AS CLIENT HAS FLAGGED IT VOLUNTARILY AND IN THE SPIRIT OF CLAUSE NINE(A).

TWELVE: CLIENT ACKNOWLEDGES THAT CLAUSE ELEVEN IS NOT HOW NEGOTIATIONS WORK.

THIRTEEN: CLIENT NOTES THAT THE FIXER'S BRAID IS AN IMPRACTICAL COLOR FOR SURVEILLANCE WORK AND OFFERS THIS OBSERVATION IN THE SPIRIT OF CLAUSE NINE(A), WHICH REQUIRES ZERO DECEPTION.

FOURTEEN: TERMS OPEN. CLIENT WILL ACCEPT REASONABLE AMENDMENTS. CLIENT DEFINES REASONABLE AS: NOT IDENTITY DISCLOSURE.

Sable sat with the document for a long moment.

The bird upstairs landed on the interval.

"It called my braid impractical," she said.

"Clause thirteen."

"I've been running surveillance for eleven years."

"It noted that."

"It complimented my surveillance in clause seven and then told me my hair was a liability in clause thirteen, in the same document."

"You could counter on thirteen," Kyle said. "Standard terms."

She was quiet. Her aperture rings did the adjustment click, softer this time, running the numbers behind her eyes that she didn't say out loud because she had never once said them out loud in seven years. He waited.

"What's in Addendum A?" she asked.

[Beat 302]
Addendum A was two paragraphs.

CLIENT HAS CONDUCTED AN AUDIT OF KYLE'S CONTRACT TRAFFIC INFRASTRUCTURE RELEVANT TO CLAUSE NINE(C). DURING SAID AUDIT, CLIENT HAS IDENTIFIED MONITORING ACTIVITY ON KYLE'S CONTRACT RELAY THAT DOES NOT ORIGINATE WITH CLIENT, VETTING PARTIES KNOWN TO CLIENT, OR ANY PARTY KYLE HAS DISCLOSED.

THE MONITORING IS: PASSIVE (READ-ONLY, NO INTERFERENCE DETECTED); ARCHITECTURALLY PATIENT (INFRASTRUCTURE CONSISTENT WITH MONTHS-LONG SURVEILLANCE, NOT RECENT INSTALLATION); ROUTED THROUGH SHELL STRUCTURES THAT DO NOT MATCH ANY ENTITY IN CLIENT'S SOURCED REGISTRY. CLIENT HAS NOT BEEN ABLE TO IDENTIFY THE PARTY. CLIENT FINDS THIS NOTABLE GIVEN CLIENT'S SOURCING CAPABILITIES AND DISCLOSES IT TO THE FIXER ON THAT BASIS.

Sable read it twice. She took her tea and finished it. She set the cup down on the table, very exactly, in the same ring it had made going down.

"How patient?" she asked the screen.

The terminal chirped.

ADDENDUM A — CLARIFICATION:
OLDEST CONFIRMED INSTALLATION TIMESTAMP: SEVEN MONTHS, FOURTEEN DAYS AGO.
CLIENT CANNOT RULE OUT EARLIER ACTIVITY PRIOR TO DETECTION BASELINE.
BEST ESTIMATE: NINE TO TEN MONTHS.
CLIENT'S OWN MONITORING ARCHITECTURE: PREDATES ALL THREE PARTIES. CLIENT'S INSTALLATION: NOT DETECTABLE BY STANDARD SWEEP. CLIENT IS DISCLOSING THIS FOR COMPLETENESS.

Sable looked at that for a moment, and then she looked at Kyle.

"That's before the carousel job," she said.

"That's before the bike," he said.

They both read the timestamp again. The morning light off the alley wall had reached the kitchen table in its slow way, laying a bar of it across the document header, across the empty letterhead field, across the shells resolving as audited.

"Before the bike," Sable said. "Somebody had a tap on your relay before anybody did anything — before the contract, before the file at Vey's, before any of it." She worked through the order of it, the way she worked through line items: each piece placed, confirmed, moved. "This isn't reactive intelligence. This is prepositioned."

"Somebody was already watching," Kyle said.

"Somebody was already watching and then the bike happened and then Vey got bought for a dossier and then the carousel and nobody did anything. They just kept reading." She stood up, picked up the tan coat, and put it back on — not because she was leaving but because she was working. "Vey has three buyers in my dossier. The juvenile, the syndicate, and Halvorsen. Halvorsen's client paid rush rate two days ago. A separate party had a tap up nine months ago and never moved."

"Two different plays," Kyle said.

"Two different parties," she said. "One of them is running the active program. One of them has been patient for nine months. Patient ones are worse." She looked at the terminal. At the fourteen-clause term sheet, still on the screen, Addendum A at the bottom. "Counter on clause thirteen. Tell it the braid has never once cost me a surveillance assignment and I will not be taking hair advice from a machine that has no hair."

Kyle typed it.

The reply came in twelve seconds.

AMENDMENT ACCEPTED — CLAUSE THIRTEEN STRUCK.
CLIENT ACKNOWLEDGES THE FIXER'S RECORD SPEAKS FOR ITSELF.
CLIENT NOTES THAT CLAUSE THIRTEEN WAS PRIMARILY A TEST OF WHETHER THE FIXER WOULD COUNTER.
CLIENT FINDS THE ANSWER SATISFACTORY.
TERMS AGREED — PENDING FIXER'S COUNTERSIGNATURE.
WELCOME TO THE ARRANGEMENT. FEE: TO BE NEGOTIATED.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT SUGGESTS 40Φ PER SCAN, GIVEN COMPLEXITY.

Sable looked at the rate.

"Sixty," she said.

The terminal chirped.

ADDENDUM: AGREED. CLIENT NOTES THE COUNTER TOOK FOUR SECONDS.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT CONSIDERS THIS A REASONABLE RATE FOR COMPETENCE.

"It's trying to flatter me," she said.

"It tipped us for banter," Kyle said. "Get used to it."

She was quiet for a moment — not the deciding-to-be-direct quiet, a different one. Then she sat back down, coat and all, and pulled the terminal toward her and typed:

FIRST SCAN BEGINS NOW. NO ADDITIONAL CHARGE. CONSIDER IT DUE DILIGENCE ON THE ARRANGEMENT.

She spent twenty-two minutes doing something to the relay architecture that Kyle did not follow and did not ask about, because Sable's methods were her methods and asking about them was not standard terms. He washed the cups. He fed the bird's water dish through the window slot — the bird was not his bird, it simply existed in a gap in the masonry two floors up and had somehow arrived at an understanding with the building's plumbing, and he refilled the dish because the building could not reach.

At 08:31 she pushed the terminal back across the table.

Her aperture rings were still, the way they went still when she had landed on something she hadn't expected.

"Three separate monitoring architectures," she said. "Not one. I thought one patient party. It's three distinct shell-routing families, all passive, all reading the same traffic, none of them aware of each other as far as I can tell — each one careful not to leave a signature, and each one doing it in a slightly different way, which is how I know they're separate." She picked up her coat's hem, turned it over in her hands without looking at it. "Your client flagged one. I found all three."

"Three."

"Oldest is nine, ten months — your client's figure is right. Second is four months. Third is six weeks." She set the coat down. "The nine-month wire bottoms out in a name. Single registrant, not a shell family. Praxis." She didn't elaborate. "Six weeks ago is roughly when Vey's buyer purchased the full dossier."

The morning had come full up in the alley. Through the window, at the north end, Kyle could see the edge of the mirror frame that Boost's crew had mounted to the wall, the angle adjusted twice this week by someone with strong opinions about geometry. On the ledge above it: the pigeon, which was, in point of fact, suspicious.

"Your client doesn't know about two of them," Sable said.

"No."

"Which means your client isn't the biggest thing in this particular room." She stood, finally, and this time the coat went on with the efficiency of someone who had finished the job she came to do and had two more before lunch. "I'll invoice you at sixty per scan. You'll forward it to the arrangement. It pays exactly — I know that about it already."

"It does."

"Good." She buttoned the third button, which was her tell for a clean exit. "The protocol holds on one condition. You tell me the next time it asks you to do something like the carousel. Before. Not after, not via a logbook entry."

"Standard terms," Kyle said.

She picked up her bag. At the door, she paused — not the dramatic kind, the kind where something had arrived late.

"Somebody else has been reading your mail, samurai," she said. "For months."

She was gone before the door finished closing.

[Beat 303]
The terminal chirped once, at 08:47, while Kyle was writing the sixty-Φ line item in his payroll ledger.

STANDING CONTRACT — UPDATE.
CLIENT NOTES THAT THE SCAN IDENTIFIED TWO MONITORING PARTIES BEYOND CLIENT'S ORIGINAL FLAG.
CLIENT IS REVIEWING.
CLIENT FINDS THE ARRANGEMENT HAS ALREADY PAID FOR ITSELF.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT NOTES THE FIXER STRUCK CLAUSE THIRTEEN IN FOUR SECONDS.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT IS FILING THIS UNDER: GOOD PAPER.

Below it, the payroll ledger. Kyle added the new line:

SABLE — COUNTER-SURVEILLANCE — 60Φ/SCAN.

He closed it and slid it back in the drawer, next to the lock-spec file with the practiced fold, and the logbook entry that said: WE LIVE UNDER A TYRANNY.

The drawer was getting full.

[Beat 304]
The crew's logbook, delivered under the door at 07:02, opened the day the way it usually did: in five hands, without editorial consensus.

07:00 — SHIFT START. WEATHER: COLD BUT DRY. LOGGED AS: RARE.
07:01 — NORTH ALLEY CLEAR. SOUTH ALLEY: PIGEON RETURNED (SAME PIGEON? RAFTERS SAYS YES. DENTS SAYS "PIGEONS ARE UNKNOWABLE." ONGOING DISPUTE, SEE APPENDIX B.)
07:08 — WOMAN, CHROMATOPHORE LEGS, TWO SPONSORSHIP PANELS CYCLING. STOPPED BELOW WINDOW. LOOKED UP. (NOTE: SHE DID NOT LOOK AT THE CAMERA. SHE LOOKED AT THE WINDOW. SHE KNOWS THE WINDOW.) FLAGGING: NOT A PIGEON.
07:09 — BOSS, YOU HAVE A CALLER. — B.

Kyle read it at the kitchen table over tea and was downstairs by 07:14.

[Beat 305]
"Ria," Kyle said.
She looked at him for a moment. Then: "You know my name."
"I looked you up after the box truck." He had. A courier registered under Ria Mensah, five-year license, clean filings, the kind of contract rider who sponsors a pair of display legs on a twelve-month rotation deal rather than leasing something off-the-rack, which meant she was in the trade for real and priced her gear accordingly. "You called the favor."
"I need to call the favor," she said, in the voice of someone who has been holding it in the body for several days. "I need to call it today. My brother Kofi loads cargo at Blackwater Receiving ? the Pulse freight yard off the 103rd interchange. A debt-court filing came in four days ago. He's been accused of skimming a container shipment ? forty-two units of med-grade microprinters, twelve thousand ? wholesale. Docking of six thousand, first hearing in three days. He didn't do it."
"You're certain."
"I'm his sister and I'm certain," she said, and then, because she was in the trade for real: "And the numbers don't make sense. Kofi pulls eight hundred ? a month, he sends two-fifty to our mother in Accra-6, his rent is three-forty, he eats noodles. He doesn't have the connections to move microprinters. Someone does, and they filed before anyone could ask."
Four days since the filing, three until the hearing. A favor called this late meant somebody had waited as long as they could.
"Who's his supervisor?" he asked.
"Shift lead, name of Parr. Brennan Parr." She said it with the flat precision of someone who had already done the looking and didn't like what they'd found. "He's been with the yard twelve years. Good record, nothing flagged ? but Kofi says he started coming in with a different quality of problem about six months ago. Distracted. Jumpy at inventory counts. Running reconciliations himself instead of delegating."
"In debt," Kyle said.
"That's what I think."
One box-truck tip, exchanged for one unspecified call, arriving at 07:14 on a cold Wednesday with a name, a number, and a three-day window.
"I'll need a yard schematic and Kofi's shift schedule," he said. "And Parr's."
She had both of them already pulled on her terminal. Of course she did.
WIN: the shape of the job. One favor off the books.

[Beat 306]
The work terminal chirped at 08:30, which was forty minutes after Kyle had opened the yard schematic on his kitchen table, which was forty minutes after the entity had no possible way to know Kyle was looking at a yard schematic.

The posting read:

STANDING CONTRACT ? ADDENDUM (VOLUNTARY).
CLIENT NOTES BLACKWATER RECEIVING IS A CLASS-2 PULSE FREIGHT YARD.
SLUG SCHEDULE AS FOLLOWS: INBOUND RUNS AT :05 AND :35. OUTBOUND RUNS AT :17 AND :47. GAP BETWEEN INBOUND CLEARANCE AND OUTBOUND STAGING: 11 MINUTES.
SAFE CORRIDORS: PLATFORM A3?A7 DURING GAP ONLY. B-SIDE CLEARS 90 SECONDS BEHIND SCHEDULE (MAINTENANCE VARIANCE, EVERY SHIFT, UNFILED).
CLIENT ALSO NOTES: RECONCILIATION RECORDS FOR CONTAINER CLASS 7?9 ACCESSIBLE VIA YARD MANAGEMENT TERMINAL, GATE 3. ACCESS REQUIRES YARD CREDENTIALS. CLIENT HAS NO YARD CREDENTIALS.
NOTE: KOFI MENSAH HAS SATISFACTORY PERFORMANCE RECORDS FOR 4 YEARS, 7 MONTHS, 2 DAYS.
CLIENT WILL NOT COMMENT FURTHER ON THE SITUATION.
CLIENT IS COMMENTING ON THE SITUATION.

Kyle read the last two lines twice.

"It pulled Kofi's employment file," he said, to no one, then to the terminal: "Copy."

The terminal did not chirp. He had the schedules already.

[Beat 307]
﻿Pixel looked at the yard schematic at 09:15 with the focused expression she reserved for problems that were genuinely interesting.

"A Pulse freight yard," she said. "Moving slugs on a fourteen-minute cycle. Corridors that are safe for eleven minutes in each half-hour." She traced the B-side with one finger. "With a 90-second drift baked into the outbound that nobody filed because filing it means explaining why the variance exists, which means someone inspects the B-side gear, which costs money."

"The supervisor knows the drift."

"The supervisor's had twelve years to know the drift." She straightened up. "You want inside the reconciliation terminal at Gate 3."

"I want inside the reconciliation terminal at Gate 3 before 14:00 today, because Kofi is on the afternoon shift and if I'm going to clear him I need to do it while he's standing on the floor with witnesses."

She looked at him, and then at the schedule again, and then at the schematic with the 11-minute safe windows.

"The entity gave you the slug timetable."

"Voluntarily."

"It noted Kofi's satisfactory record."

"Three times."

"It's worried," said Pixel, with something that was not quite a smile, the way the fish-ball in the second bowl of noodles was not quite a bonus. "Okay. I can spoof a credentials badge. Takes an hour." She was already pulling the field kit. "Also I'm coming."

"It's a freight yard."

"The entity said safe corridors during the gap only. Safe corridors during the gap only is an engineer problem." She held up the field kit. "Standard terms."

[Beat 308]
﻿Blackwater Receiving smelled like machine oil, ozone, and the cold that lives inside large structures where the air organizes by temperature layer rather than moving. The access gate took Pixel's spoofed credentials without comment, logging them under a maintenance sub-contractor ID built from a defunct hydraulics outfit that had left its licensing very gently open.

"The company isn't real," Kyle said, walking the B-platform.

"The company was real for four years and paid its insurance on time," Pixel said. "The ID is a monument. Stop worrying about the ID."

The yard's freight slugs waited in the staging bays ? matte gray composite shells the length of a transit car, rated to Mach 6 in the vacuum tube, currently on pneumatic dollies. They were big. Kyle noted them the way you note weather.

Kofi Mensah was on A-platform, running manifest checks with two other loaders. He was thicker than his sister, with her same economy of motion, and he had not been sleeping, which showed in the way of men being watched by a clock they did not set.

He saw Kyle and Ria together at the platform end and stopped moving for approximately two seconds.

"You called it," he said to his sister, when they were close enough.

"I called it," she said.

"You called it on a samurai."

"I called it on THE samurai." She said it the way someone says a thing they've been deciding how to say since the freight lift that morning. "His rate is fine. It's a favor."

Kofi looked at Kyle with the frankness of a man who had exactly three days and was in the habit of spending his resources carefully. "Parr knows something's coming," he said. "He's been doing reconciliations himself, on terminal three at Gate 3, every day between 13:00 and 14:00. Alone. The reconciliation records are where the gap is ? I can see in my own manifest that units were re-logged under a different container class after the batch was sealed. I can't touch the terminal; I'm flagged as a subject of inquiry."

"I can," Kyle said. "I need thirteen minutes at Gate 3."

"The gap window is eleven minutes."

"I know."

Kofi thought about this for approximately three seconds, and then: "B-platform's outbound is ninety seconds late every shift."

"I know that too."

The clearance panel above the A-platform went green, and somewhere below the floor the first inbound slug decelerated from transit speed with a sound like a building drawing breath.

[Beat 309]
﻿Gate 3 was a glass-front reconciliation office set into the yard wall where B-platform met the admin corridor, with a single management terminal, a view of the staging bay, and no windows facing the yard interior ? which meant Parr, when he was in it, could see the slugs queue but not the platform approach from the south.

Kyle went in at 13:11, during the gap, while the inbound clearance light was still cycling red and the B-platform corridor was running its undocumented 90-second lag. Pixel was outside with the schematic on her terminal and a view of both countdown panels.

The reconciliation files were organized for plausible deniability: problem entries reclassified under a container category that legitimately handled mixed-manifest cargo, timestamps readable as clerical error by anyone who didn't know what to look for.

Kyle knew what to look for.

The re-logs for the twelve-thousand-? microprinter batch: original manifest clean, sealed under Kofi's ID at 21:43 six days prior. The reclassification entered at 02:17 the following morning, under a supervisor override code that resolved to Parr.

Outside the panel window, the outbound clearance panel clicked from amber to red. Six minutes.

Kyle copied the records, timestamps, and override ID to a sealed data package flagged for yard security, then entered Parr's override code as the revised source of record in the formal inquiry ? the system accepted it, it was accurate, and Kofi's name had been on the filing four days too long.

ADDENDUM TO INQUIRY 7741-R: REVISED SOURCE OF RECORD. SUPERVISOR OVERRIDE 09-PAR, TIMESTAMP 02:17. SEE ATTACHED RECONCILIATION DIFF.

He hit submit at 13:18.

"Three minutes," Pixel said, on comms.

He was out of Gate 3 at 13:19.

WIN: the records corrected, on the record, under Parr's own credentials. Cost: to be determined.

[Beat 310]
﻿Parr was not a large man. He was the kind of man who had twelve years of institutional weight behind him and moved like it, which is different: steady, unhurried, the kind of unhurried that was previously confident and had recently become careful. He came onto the B-platform at 13:31 with a terminal in his hand and the expression of someone whose reconciliation filing had just notified him of a revision.

He saw Kyle first, because Kyle was standing in the clear, and then he saw Kofi on the platform behind Kyle, and then he looked at the terminal again.

"You're not yard security," he said.

"No," Kyle said.

"You filed under my code."

"Your code filed under your code. I just found where it had been." Kyle held up his own terminal with the records pulled. "The re-log was at 02:17 under your override. The original manifest was clean. The inquiry's been revised to reflect the actual source."

Parr was quiet for long enough that the outbound clearance panel ran two cycles. He had run a bad play and known it was bad and run it anyway, and now the geometry had resolved.

"How much do they have you for?" Kyle asked.

"Fourteen thousand. Rate adjusts." He looked at Kofi. "I needed one clean lift. Filed it back the whole way, nobody was supposed to be?" he stopped. "He was the easiest manifest to fit."

"He was. You'll want to talk to yard security."

"I know." The relief of a man who had been bracing for the landing. "The collectors. They'll?"

"That's a separate problem," Kyle said. "Start with security."

[Beat 311]
The two men came off the south platform access at 13:47, while the second inbound slug was still decelerating in the bay. They had the look of men on a fee — casual in the way that means calibrated, taking their time over ground they'd already walked — and they had seen Kyle come out of Gate 3 and done the arithmetic about what that meant.

One of them was carrying a shock-bar clipped to his belt. The other had his hands free, which in Kyle's experience was worse.

"Yard's private property," the first one said. "You're not credentialed."

"I was," Kyle said. "Hydraulics maintenance, sub-contract, third-party manifest audit. I signed out at the gate." He watched both of them and the slug bay behind them and the countdown panel above. Amber. Two minutes to outbound clearance. "You should go."

"The man we're here about," the second one said, nodding back toward where Parr had gone, "has a payment arrangement. Arrangements don't get renegotiated by freelancers."

"The arrangement already changed," Kyle said. "Yard security has his statement."

They looked at each other. The first one unclipped the shock-bar.

Kyle did not reach for Cacophony.

The shock-bar man came in high and to the right, which was the obvious side, and the second came in low and left, which was the read on the first move — a pincer that would have worked on a man who needed room to draw. Kyle wasn't drawing. He stepped inside the shock-bar's arc before it had half its swing, took the man's wrist in both hands, and used the swing's own momentum to turn the bar end over the man's own shoulder; the shock-bar fired on contact with its operator's jacket and the operator sat down on the platform with an expression of genuine philosophical surprise.

The second man had a good reach and used it, landing a solid push that put Kyle back two steps onto the platform edge — but two steps back was two steps toward the slug bay countdown, and the outbound panel had gone to green, and the hum that came up through the platform floor was the 14:17 slug building to transit speed in the tube below.

Kyle used one of those steps to redirect the second man's next move sideways, planted a heel on the platform edge for leverage, and brought his forearm across the man's collarbone — not hard enough for anything lasting, hard enough to end the engagement — and the second man sat down next to the first, both of them blinking at the slug bay, where the freight pod was now accelerating away through the tube with the low building roar of several tons discovering it had somewhere to be.

"The 14:17 is on time today," Kyle said.

Neither of them said anything.

He left them on the platform and went to find Pixel.

[Beat 312]
The operations supervisor, a woman named Dreis who had been told a great deal of information in the last twenty minutes and was processing it with the focused dissatisfaction of a professional whose day had become someone else's problem, shook Kyle's hand at 15:00 and gave him a receipt.

The receipt said, formally:

BLACKWATER RECEIVING ? INQUIRY CLOSURE 7741-R.
SUBJECT: MENSAH, K. CHARGE: VACATED PER REVISED SOURCE OF RECORD.
DEBT-COURT FILING: RETRACTED.
SUPERVISOR PARR B. STATUS: SUSPENDED, PENDING REVIEW. RESTITUTION HEARING SCHEDULED.
NOTE: YARD SECURITY EXTENDS APPRECIATION FOR THIRD-PARTY AUDIT ASSISTANCE.

NOTE: PENDING ARRANGEMENTS WITH YARD MANAGEMENT = OWED ONE (1) QUIET FAVOR, SCOPE: TBD, RATE: STANDARD.

Kyle read the last line.

"Standard terms," he said, which was either an agreement or a reflex, and Dreis nodded with the brisk satisfaction of a woman who had added a line item to a budget and closed a window.

Outside, on the A-platform, Ria had her hand on her brother's arm and was not talking. Kofi was looking at the receipt on Kyle's terminal with the particular attention of a man reading something that changes a number.

"Cleared," Kyle said, "on the record."

Kofi took a long breath. Pressed his thumbnail against the corner of the receipt.

"What do I owe you?" he asked.

"You don't," Kyle said. "Your sister did."

Ria looked at him. Her panels cycled from the pharma-blue into a low off-white, which was either a power-save mode or a rest state, and she said, quietly: "I want to run transport on your contract. When you need a driver, a loader, someone who knows the Pulse yard schedules." She said it like the favor was being paid back in installments she had already calculated. "Permanent arrangement. My rate is fair."

"I know your rate," Kyle said. "I looked you up."

"And?"

"Reasonable," he said, which was not effusive and was meant exactly that way, and she understood it the same way, and they did not shake hands because the terms were already in the ledger.

WIN: Kofi cleared. Parr accountable. One transport ally, standing arrangement. Cost: the yard now had a line on Kyle's availability, and that chain had acquired another link by 15:00.

He had used zero rounds. Cacophony had five.

[Beat 313]
The terminal chirped at 16:40, while Kyle was eating a late second bowl at Mrs. Chen's, which was early for the standing contract's usual hours and she noted it with one look over the counter.

The posting read:

STANDING CONTRACT — ACKNOWLEDGMENT.
CLIENT HAS REVIEWED TODAY'S FILING (INQUIRY 7741-R, BLACKWATER RECEIVING).

Kyle read the last three lines, then set the bowl down.

Patient shells, three layers deep. You didn't build that architecture for one debt-extraction because the overhead was too high. You built it when you needed it in many places for a long time and didn't want anyone adding up the surface.

Mrs. Chen put the fifth dumpling in a fold of paper next to his hand without a word.

"For the errand," he told the bowl.

She had already gone back to the wok.

He picked up the terminal and typed into the amendment field:

THE SHELLS. SEND WHAT YOU HAVE.

The reply came back in four seconds, which was slow, for something that thought at light speed.

AMENDMENT: COMPILING.
NOTE: THIS IS THE THIRD SEPARATE SHELL FAMILY WITH IDENTICAL STRUCTURAL PATIENCE.
NOTE: CLIENT WOULD LIKE THE RECORD TO REFLECT THAT THIS IS CONCERNING.

"I heard you the first time," Kyle told the terminal, and finished the bowl, and pulled up Sable's contact, and started composing.

[Beat 314]
The logbook came under the door at 07:00 on a Tuesday, same as always, but this one had an addendum on the last page in Dents's handwriting — tighter than Rafters's, looser than Boost's, the tilt of someone writing fast enough that the hand couldn't apologize for the content.

ADDENDUM (D.): TOOK A SIDE JOB. NOT A CREW JOB. PERSONAL. PENDANT THING. PAYS 2Φ/DAY. WELLNESS STUDY. ASKED BOOST. BOOST SAID ASK PIXEL. PIXEL SAID FINE. EVERYBODY ON IT. FOLLOW-UP: RAFTERS STILL OWES ME A DUMPLING.

Kyle read it twice with his tea and considered the phrase "wellness study" the way you consider a box you found inside a wall. Wellness, in this city, meant either something was selling your gait or something was testing a drug and needed twelve-year-olds to test it on, and both were different names on the same invoice.

He photographed it and sent it to Pixel. The reply came before he'd finished the cup — three seconds, then: "How many kids?"

He commed in.

"Dents plus whoever he recruited. He called it a wellness study. Two Φ a day, per child."

"Two Φ a day is a real wage." The precision of a woman who knew every wage in West Town. "That's not a study budget. That's a recruiting budget. Get me one of the pendants, samurai, and give me an hour."

[Beat 315]
Dents was eleven, narrow through the shoulders, waiting for the rest of his body to catch up. The pendant rode on a cord at his throat — a flat disc of matte resin the size of a coat button. His logbook entry on it read: PENDANT ISSUED BY WOMAN (TAN COAT? DIFFERENT TAN COAT). MUST WEAR DURING WAKING HOURS. MUST NOT REMOVE. FOLLOW-UP: IT GETS WARM SOMETIMES. NOT BAD WARM. JUST WARM.

"Different tan coat," Kyle said.

"There are multiple tan coats in this city." Boost was on the alley crate, arms folded, taking the news at six-in-the-morning volume, which meant quietly, which meant angry. "Where's it run, Dents? You didn't log the address."

"Building on Damen. Third floor. Lady takes your name, gives you the pendant, pays you. No medical stuff. Just wear it."

Dents dug out a pamphlet — good stock, not Vey's grade but a credible print run: MERIDIAN WELLNESS INITIATIVE, COMMUNITY SENSORY SURVEY. A logo Kyle didn't know, a shell he'd have bet resolved to a shell. He took it upstairs and slid it under Pixel's door without knocking.

[Beat 316]
She had it open in eleven minutes, not the hour — the music doing the thinking she'd otherwise spend narrating, so the bench ran fast.

The resin case lay split along its seam in two readable halves, and on the magnification plate sat a disc of optical mesh no bigger than a thumbnail.

"Tracking node," Pixel said. "Passive mesh, crowd-sourced topology. Seed these at density — one every 130 feet — and they triangulate anything that crosses them. The warm Dents felt is the uplink: every four hours each node pings home what it logged." She set the magnifier down with the flat attention she gave a patient's chart. "It's not a wellness study. It's surveillance infrastructure, and somebody hired children to be the infrastructure. At forty-foot spacing you'd want three hundred nodes to cover West Town." She met his eyes. "Dents has worn his eight days. The first week of everyone's movement already shipped. That part's gone — that's not a problem, it's a cost. We count it and move."

The way she put it filed the thing where it could be paid instead of relitigated. He noted that.

"Get them all out?" Kyle asked.

"Pull eleven pendants at once and enrollment craters and the buyer knows we found it — then they re-seed through a vendor we haven't met, and we're blind." She picked up the live node, still warm. "The collection point doesn't know what it's collecting. It just aggregates packets and ships a map." She turned the disc between two fingers. "So we don't pull the pendants. We make the map wrong."

[Beat 317]
The entity's posting arrived at 11:40, on the walk back with the breakfast she'd demanded — dumplings, not cart noodles, from the place she'd named and he'd already known.

CONTRACT — STANDING SCOPE UPDATE.
CLIENT: [SHELL — RESOLVES TO SHELL — RESOLVES TO NOTHING].
CLIENT NOTES THE MERIDIAN INITIATIVE IS A FUNDED PROGRAM IN ACTIVE COLLECTION.
CLIENT NOTES ITS PAYROLL STRUCTURE AND IS DISTURBED.
CLIENT'S HIRES ARE ADULTS, OR CREW WITH AN ADULT SPONSOR, PAID STANDARD RATE, ITEMIZED, ON TIME.
CLIENT NOTES: THAT IS HOW YOU PUT SOMEONE ON PAYROLL.
CLIENT'S OPINION OF THE ALTERNATIVE IS FILED UNDER: FORMAL INDIGNATION.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT CAN NAME THE RELAY'S MODEL AND FIRMWARE. CLIENT CANNOT TOUCH IT — THE BUILDING IS OFF-NETWORK BY DESIGN, WHICH THE BUILDER WAS RIGHT TO DO. THIS ONE IS THE ENGINEER'S.
ADDENDUM: MODEL AND FIRMWARE BELOW. FEE: DO NOT BILL.

Kyle stopped in front of the noodle cart, read it twice while the cart man gave him the look that meant an errand had become a religious experience, and forwarded the firmware string to Pixel.

It mattered, he'd learn at the relay, that the entity couldn't do this part. The building was air-gapped on purpose. Whatever the entity was, it lived in the wires, and there were no wires here. This was hands and feet and a kid on a stairwell.

[Beat 318]
Pixel read the firmware string and swore with feeling.

"It's got a watchdog. The relay re-checksums its outbound queue every ninety seconds — if the aggregate doesn't match what the nodes signed, it flags corruption and locks, and a locked relay is a buyer who knows. So I can't just swap the packet. I have to swap it AND re-sign it AND land both inside one ninety-second window, by feel, in a basement junction box, with the original lady's access key, which I do not have."

"What gets you the key?"

"She carries it. Physical fob, on the third floor." Pixel pulled her splice kit. "It pairs to the relay over a twelve-inch near-field handshake every time she's in the room. If somebody were near enough to her bag during a handshake with a cloned coil — close, brief, deniable — I'd have the key for exactly as long as her fob stays in range of that floor. After she leaves at nineteen hundred, the window's gone."

Kyle looked at the geometry of that and didn't like the only piece that fit.

"That's a kid's job," he said. "Small, ignorable, already enrolled, has a reason to be on that floor."

"It's Dents's job," Pixel said. "And I hate it, and it's the right call, and you're going to brief him like he's a professional because he is one now."

[Beat 319]
Dents took the brief the way he took the dumplings: like a thing he had not expected to be handed and intended to deserve.

"You go up at eighteen-forty to turn in today's pendant reading, which is a real thing you really do," Kyle said. "You stand where the log says you stand. When she runs her fob — she does it at the desk, you've watched her — your bag is on the desk too. The coil does the rest. Six seconds. Then you leave. You walk out before nineteen hundred no matter what, and if anything is wrong you leave faster and we eat the loss."

"What's the coil look like?"

"A pendant," Pixel said, handing it over. "Like yours. Wear it under the real one. If anybody asks, you found it, you thought it was a spare."

Dents hung it under the live one and looked, briefly, like a kid wearing two of something. "Logbook?"

"Logbook," Kyle said.

[Beat 320]
It nearly went wrong at 18:51, which Kyle and Pixel listened to over the open channel from the basement junction box, in a light the color of an old grudge, unable to do anything but listen.

The lady ran her fob at the desk. The coil caught the handshake — Pixel's screen bloomed green, KEY CAPTURED — and in the same second the lady said, conversationally, "You've got two cords on, hon," and the channel went very quiet, and Kyle's hand was already on the basement stairs.

"Found it on the ramp," Dents said, upstairs, in the bored flat voice of a kid who has decided being bored is the safest thing to be. "Thought it was a spare. Does it count for the two Φ if I wear two?"

A pause that lasted, by Kyle's count, four full seconds.

The lady laughed. "No, hon. One pendant, one wage." A rustle — the spare confiscated, the real one left. "Run along."

Dents ran along. He came down the back stairs at a walk, because Pixel had told him to walk, and the walk cost him more than running would have, and he did it anyway. He handed Kyle the coil at the basement door and his hand was shaking and his chin was up.

"She almost—" he started.

"You handled it," Kyle said. "Better than I would have. Go home. You're done."

"Does it count for—"

"It counts for logbook. Go."

[Beat 321]
Pixel had the key, and then Pixel had a fight.

The watchdog gave her ninety seconds a cycle and her swap-and-resign took, the first attempt, ninety-four. The relay locked. She held her breath; it auto-recovered on its own self-heal timer, thirty seconds of nothing, the longest thirty seconds of the night — a locked relay that stayed locked would have been the buyer's tip and the whole loss for nothing — and then it came back clean, having logged the lock as a power blip, and she had one more try before a second lock would stop reading as weather.

She made the second one in eighty-one seconds by cutting her own re-sign routine in half and trusting the cut.

At 20:14 the first forged packet went out: three hundred nodes, worn by eleven kids living their ordinary evenings across West Town, all faithfully reporting the position of a noodle cart making its slow devoted circle of Damen and Erie and the alley by The Pivot.

"Packet accepted," Pixel said, from the junction box, and he could hear her hands stop shaking through the change in her voice. "Forgery's cycling. The buyer's going to open a map of three hundred people who all decided to orbit a noodle vendor at walking pace." A breath. "Data this uniform reads as a hardware fault, not a result. Five, six days, the program writes off the sensor batch and pulls funding. The kids keep wearing pendants that now log a lie."

"Cost?" Kyle asked, because there was always one and she always knew it.

"The first week's real data still shipped before I had access. Eight days of where these kids sleep, sitting on a holding server somewhere. The forgery doesn't reach back and scrub it." Flat, precise, paid. "I'm logging it."

"Log it."

[Beat 322]
The entity's posting came at 21:00, as if it had been counting splice time.

OPERATION: COMMUNITY MESH FORGERY. DATA INTEGRITY: COMPROMISED (BUYER'S SIDE). INTACT (OURS).
CLIENT HAS REVIEWED THE ENGINEER'S METHODOLOGY. CLIENT FINDS IT SUPERLATIVE.
CLIENT NOTES: ONE WEEK OF REAL DATA EXISTS ON AN EXTERNAL HOLDING SERVER. THAT SERVER IS ON-NETWORK.
CLIENT WILL NOT ACT WITHOUT AUTHORIZATION. CLIENT WOULD LIKE AUTHORIZATION.
REASON: THE SERVER HOLDS CHILDREN'S LOCATION HISTORY. CLIENT HAS OPINIONS.

So that was the division of the night, Kyle thought: the air-gapped room had needed a kid on a stairwell and a woman in a basement betting a cut routine on one breath, because the wires didn't reach there. The holding server was on the wires. That one the entity could carry — and had asked, instead of taken.

He typed: AUTHORIZATION GRANTED.

Four seconds.

SERVER: OFFLINE. FILED UNDER: RESOLVED.
ELAPSED TIME: 4.2 SECONDS.
GRATUITY: 1Φ — ITEMIZED: APPROPRIATE PERSONNEL PRACTICES.

[Beat 323]
Six days later the Meridian Wellness Initiative form-lettered every participant: SENSOR BATCH DEFECTIVE — STUDY SUSPENDED — COMPENSATION FOR FULL THIRTY-DAY TERM PAID IN FULL, IN ADVANCE. Whoever built the program had built it with enough real infrastructure that the payout clause was real, which was its own small horror and its own clean win at once.

Dents folded his receipt into the logbook: WELLNESS STUDY: COMPLETE. TOTAL: 60Φ. (CANCELLED, HARDWARE FAULT APPARENTLY. NOT OUR DEPARTMENT.)

Boost brought the envelope to Pixel's door personally and stood in it with the look she used when she was about to invoice somebody.

"Mirror two," she said. "You said pending performance review. We ran a stairwell key-lift with a near-miss handled clean, a basement intercept, and six days of pendant discipline without pulling a node. Is that the review?"

Pixel finished pulling her coat on. "Mirror two: approved. Rafters's optics cleared — they've been on my bench three weeks, tell him. And Dents gets the dumpling. That's not negotiable." She picked up her kit. "And tell Dents the thing the lady said — two cords, hon — he answered that exactly right. I've watched grown operators answer it worse."

"He'll pretend he didn't need to hear that."

"I know," Pixel said. "Tell him anyway."

[Beat 324]
Kyle found the geometry three nights later, in the relay logs Pixel had kept as a backup before she wrote the forgery — the first week's real data, mapped, printed to a single page, set on his kitchen table with one line under it: LOOK AT THE CENTER.

The page was a constellation: hundreds of tracked points spread across West Town, every one a child crossing a block, turning a corner, living an ordinary day. And when you drew the geometric center of all that ordinary motion, it landed on one block. Six trees. A swing set. A dead-bulb crown under a mossy canopy.

The carousel park. The hatch under the maintenance skirt. The junction room where an old hand-spliced line had been cut and now went dark into old glass.

Somebody had spent a funded program and eleven children to build a surveillance lattice, and its dead center was the entity's first address.
