He put the page in the drawer — next to the buyers list and Boost's folded lock-spec file and the other things he had not finished deciding about. The drawer was getting full.

The logbook came under the door at 07:00, and at the bottom, in Boost's hand:

P.S. — BOSS. DENTS ASKS WHAT WAS IN THE PENDANT. HE ALREADY KNOWS IT WASN'T WELLNESS.
P.P.S. — HE SAYS THANK YOU. THE DUMPLING WAS VERY GOOD. (RAFTERS PAID UP. THIS LOG IS HONEST.)

Kyle wrote under it, in his own hand, and slid it back: DENTS — IT TRACKED POSITION. SOMEBODY WAS DRAWING A MAP. WE MADE THE MAP WRONG. YOU WERE NEVER ON IT. — K.

Through the thin floor he could hear the logbook get read, then two voices going fast, then a third, then Rafters saying something about the cart that made the other two laugh. He went down and bought everybody breakfast.

[Beat 325]
The Loop's third underlevel had its own ecology: paper shop, shuttered watch-repair, Vong's Dumpling Franchise #7 on the right under a red awning, and Mei's Dumplings on the left — no franchise, no awning, forty stolen inches of counter she'd annexed off the watch-repair's wall by aggressive proximity. The stationer's honest door sat dead center.

The two men stood in the middle with their backs to that door, which meant their backs to Vey, which was the kind of error that compounds.

Vong was a compact sixty-year-old with the posture of a man who'd been absorbing bad news upright for decades. The clipboard man was presenting him a form. "—monthly insurance assessment. Standard for all food vendors on this underblock. The Tsui Group's protective services, standard rate—"

"Thirty-five Φ a week," Kyle said from behind them, reading the figure upside-down. "Four hundred percent over the Meridian Merchant index. And your policy number's nine digits. GLMZ policy numbers run eleven. That's somebody's neuretic handle on a form."

The clipboard man turned. Long enough in the work to have stopped enjoying it. "Not your stall."

"Go, Mr. Kyle," Vong said quietly. "This is mine to carry."

"I eat here," Kyle said, and that was the whole of his position.

[Beat 326]
He'd half-expected the paperwork to end it. He pulled up the Tsui Group's public filing anyway — all collection franchises filed public or didn't operate — and the search took under three minutes to surface the part that mattered.

"Your protective license lapsed," Kyle said, turning the screen. "The fourth. Not expired — lapsed. You've been writing paper on a dead license eleven days. There's no reinstatement pending; the portal would show it."

The clipboard man looked at the screen a long moment. Then he handed the clipboard to his partner — the big one, the expensive shoes — and rolled his neck.

Kyle had read it as a departure. He was wrong. A man whose paper is exposed has two options, and only one of them is going.

"License is for the ones who pay clean," the clipboard man said. "Vong's not paying clean anymore. So this is the other visit." He looked at his partner and the big one didn't go for Kyle, because the big one was a professional and professionals understand leverage. He went for Vong's broth pot.

Forty liters of scalding pork-bone stock. A sixty-year-old man standing next to it.

[Beat 327]
Kyle was moving before the big one's hand finished closing on the rim.

He did not draw Silence. A drawn blade in an underlevel full of lunch civilians was a conversation he'd have to finish, and this didn't need finishing, it needed interrupting. He took the man's wrist off the rim — not a block, a redirect, the shove steered ninety degrees into the franchise's steel prep counter — and forty liters of scalding stock rocked on its ring and held, one tongue of it slopping over the lip to hiss across the floor where Vong had been standing a half-second before, because Kyle's other hand had already moved him.

The big one came off the counter fast and threw the elbow Kyle had left him room to throw. It caught Kyle across the cheekbone — a real hit, a bright white line of it, a mark by evening — and the cost bought the opening: weight forward, lead knee proud. Kyle took the knee from the side with the flat of his boot. Not the joint. A man who limps for a week tells the story right; a man who never walks again tells a worse one and brings friends. The big one folded into the spilled stock, swearing, done.

The clipboard man's hand went inside his jacket. Kyle looked at him.

"Cacophony holds five," Kyle said. He hadn't drawn it. The sentence drew it. "You came to scald a sixty-year-old over thirty-five Φ a week. Take your partner, take your dead license, and tell Marcus Tsui that Vong's block is documented now — times, faces, the shoes." A beat. "There's a kid on that roof who drew the shoes."

The hand came out empty. They hauled the big one up between them and went, leaving wet bootprints and the smell of spilled stock, and the underlevel breathed out.

Vong stood by his rescued pot with both hands flat on the counter. Steadying it or himself.

"The tea stall on sub-two," he said. "Lin's. Forty years. She told them no, same as me, and they didn't burn it — they just visited, and visited, and one week the customers decided sub-two wasn't worth the walk, and then Lin decided too. Now it's a jack kiosk and nobody remembers there was tea." His hands were not quite steady. "That's how it's done now. Not a fire. An erasure. You wait for the block to forget."

"It's documented now," Kyle said. "The next visit isn't a visit. It's a filing." He looked at the blood on his thumb without surprise. "Lin's mistake was being the only no on her block. You've got a whole block, Vong. Bill me for the dumplings I'm about to eat."

From his doorway, Vey made a small sound that was not a cough.

[Beat 328]
They came back at 14:00.

No clipboard this time. Three of them — the clipboard man, the big one moving carefully on his bad knee, and a third Kyle hadn't seen before, with the chrome thumb-rings and the flat affect of a man brought in specifically. The underlevel had thinned out after lunch. The third man's eyes went to Vong's counter first, then to the stairwell access on the far wall, mapping the routes out, which told Kyle more than the chrome.

"Marcus Tsui filed a complaint," the clipboard man said. He was talking to the room, not to any one person. "Assault of a licensed representative. Interference with contracted services. You've got a warrant flag running on your neuretics, samurai — check it if you want. We'll wait."

Kyle didn't check it. Warrant flags cost sixty Φ to file and twenty to pull. It was a stall, not a threat. He also did not move from where he was standing, which was between the third man and Vong's pot.

"The complaint was filed to a licensing board that hasn't had a quorum since the fourth," Kyle said. "Same day your license lapsed. The filing is stuck behind the lapse review, which means it's administrative vapor. It dissolves when your license reinstatement is denied, which it will be, because I've already submitted the eleven-day gap documentation to the Office of Merchant Protections." He hadn't done that yet. He was going to do it in the next forty minutes, which was close enough. "You want to check THAT warrant, I'll wait."

The third man looked at the clipboard man. The clipboard man looked at his shoes.

The chrome rings were the tell. This third man was not here for paper. He was here to stand in Vong's stall and make the customers who came for afternoon dumplings see what they were walking into. He would not hurt Vong. He would just be present, every afternoon, until it cost more to keep the franchise than to surrender it.

The erasure technique. Patient, geometric, and technically legal.

"Vong," Kyle said, without turning, "is there a back entrance to this underlevel?"

"Loading dock. Sub-two corridor, behind the watch-repair."

"The watch-repair is shuttered?"

"Three years."

"Whose lease?"

A pause. "Mine. I took it when they left. Cheaper than advertising."

Kyle looked at the third man. "You're standing in an extended franchise footprint. The loading dock access, the sub-two corridor, and the six feet of watch-repair annexation all come off the same lease. You can stand here, but you're standing in his house. You want to explain to Marcus Tsui that you took a loitering position inside a vendor's licensed square footage, in front of witnesses, on the day I submitted that lapse documentation?"

The third man had the chrome rings and the flat affect and nothing else. He was hired for presence, not decisions. He looked at the clipboard man, who had been wrong about this block twice now.

They left.

Vong sat down on his prep stool and said nothing for a while. From the sub-two corridor below, faint and steady, the Pulse ran its underfloor hum through the tiles — thirty feet down, Mach 6, the whole moving world.

"Every time I tell them no," Vong said, "I think about Lin."

"You're not Lin," Kyle said. "Lin didn't have the sub-two corridor and a kid on the roof and a samurai who eats here on Tuesdays."

He went upstairs and filed the documentation.

[Beat 329]
The franchise survived until noon, when Mei opened her awning an inch further left than yesterday and started the other war.

Mei's Dumplings had seventeen years on Vong's franchise, a recipe that allegedly predated the zone restructuring, and a standing position that Franchise #7 was an encroachment on her base, her air rights, and her sense of how the underlevel should smell. Vong answered the awning by nudging his broth pot one inch toward the center line. By 14:00 there was a chalk line on the floor. By 15:30 there were two, with a disputed zone between them and three customers eating in the gap with the posture of people braced to be asked to choose sides.

Kyle was at Mei's — alternating weeks, six years, both owners aware of it — with a cut cheek and a late lunch when Mrs. Chen arrived, clearly sent for, with the expression of a woman asked to do a thing beneath her competence and above her patience.

"Both of you," she said, from the neutral chalk, "have sold dumplings down here longer than half these buildings have stood, and you've both overcharged me, and I've paid, because the product was worth it." She looked at the lines. "I'm not an arbitrator. I'm a customer owed better than this."

"You're the only one on this block both stalls will hear," Vong said.

"You started it," Mei said.

"The awning—"

"The broth pot—"

Mrs. Chen looked at Kyle the particular way a woman looks who has spent seventy years not asking for things. He ate the rest of his dumpling and accepted the expression as the invoice it was. His cheek had started to stiffen, which felt, all things considered, like the right price for an afternoon that had nearly cost Vong his hands twice.

[Beat 330]
The taste-off was Boost's idea, and the speed the logbook adopted it as official suggested it had been everyone's at once.

DUMPLING COMMISSION: OFFICIAL RECORD. CHAIR: MR. KYLE (VISIBLE INJURY, SEE 11:40 ENTRY). RECORDER: RAFTERS. PANEL: DENTS, BOOST. OBJECTIVE: BETTER DUMPLING, FOR BLOCK PEACE. NOTE: ALSO THE FUNNIEST THING WE'VE BEEN ASKED TO DO. SECOND NOTE: BOSS GOT HIT TODAY. TWICE. WE LOGGED IT. THIS LOG IS HONEST.

Both stalls produced their house dumplings. Vong ran a standard pork-and-chive, thicker wrap, machine-even pleating, nineteen quality ratings it deserved. Mei produced the original — thinner-wrapped, hand-crimped in a pattern Rafters sketched in the margin for documentation, ginger-forward and something older underneath. Kyle ate four of each. The crew filed scorecards ranging from TWO POINTS AND A STAR to a drawing of a thumbs-up with a 1Φ price tag.

An audience had accumulated the way audiences do around chalk lines and commission proceedings, and Vey had migrated from his doorway to the building's first internal window for the better angle.

[Beat 331]
"For the record," Kyle said, to the chalk and the crowd and both owners, "Vong's franchise makes a technically excellent dumpling. Consistent. Well-priced. You know exactly what you get any day of any week." Vong nodded with professional dignity. "Mei's original is better."

Mei made a sound that was not quite a sound. Vong's jaw went through an expression and out the far side.

"By a margin that doesn't justify a chalk line," Kyle went on, over the crowd's murmur. "Vong, your guarantee covers the gap — inspection rating, delivery network, the app. Mei's hand-folding seventeen years of recipe. Two stalls, two reasons to buy a dumpling. You're both leaving money on the floor fighting over an inch." He let that sit, and then he said the part that was actually the point. "This afternoon, Marcus Tsui sent three men to stand in Vong's stall and wait for the foot traffic to decide it wasn't worth the walk. They left because the documentation went in and the stall had defensible footage. That changes nothing about next week, or the week after, or Lin's tea stall on sub-two that is now a handset-charging kiosk and will never be tea again." He looked at both of them. "A block with one stall is a block that can be made to forget it. A block with two stalls arguing about awnings at lunch is a block that is LOUD. It's a block people come to on purpose. You two are each other's insurance, and you've been drawing a chalk line down the middle of it."

He looked at Rafters. "That how the minutes read?"

Rafters consulted the logbook with the gravity of a senior analyst. "Exactly how they read. I can charge for certified copies."

The chalk was gone by 16:30. Vong sent one complimentary order of pork-and-chive to Mei's counter without comment; Mei received it without comment. Closest the underlevel had come to a diplomatic event in twenty years. Vong put the franchise testimonial card in his window next to a second card, hand-lettered, that hadn't been there this morning: BEST ORIGINAL ON THE BLOCK — SEE MEI, NEXT COUNTER. Insurance, posted where the block could read it.

[Beat 332]
Kyle was leaving at 17:20 when Vey opened his door the particular width of a man who'd been saving something all day.

"Come in. The occasion warrants the good shelf." Inside, the drawers watched in their brown-inked calm. Vey poured without asking, pushed the cup handle-first. "The Tsui Group is not my clientele — impersonation filings are public, and I don't sell what I can read for free." He settled onto his stool. "I watched the whole proceeding. Morning and afternoon. The license. The pot. The chrome thumb-rings." His eyes went, briefly, to Kyle's cheek. "The interruptions, plural."

"You watched a man go for a sixty-year-old and made tea."

"I sell maps, Mr. Kyle. I do not walk the territory; that has never been my trade, and a stationer who picks up a weapon is a stationer no one trusts with a secret again." He said it without apology, which was its own kind of honesty. "But I notice you did not draw the sword. A man went for Vong's pot — twice, in two different configurations — and you put them down with documentation and a boot and a sentence. The leverage was real, the filing was real, the sub-two corridor was real. Three kinds of paper and a measured violence, in sequence." He polished his glasses. "The Tsui Group needed a reason to stop. The stalls needed a verdict. Your standing client needed" — the smallest brightness — "a dumpling methodology, apparently. You arbitrate better than you bill. A man could make a file of that."

"Don't."

"The file is already open," Vey said gently. "It has been since your first visit; I open one on everyone. I am telling you its rating, which is a courtesy I extend to perhaps four customers a year." He put the glasses back on. "Repeat customer. Tone: admiring. The cheek will scar small if you leave it; I'd leave it. Scars are also a kind of record, and this one's honest."

Kyle drank the tea, which was from the good shelf, and set the cup down exactly.

The terminal chirped on his way to the stairs.

STANDING CONTRACT — AMENDMENT. CLIENT HAS REVIEWED TODAY. CLIENT NOTES: THE INJURY WAS AVOIDABLE IF THE ENGINEER'S DRONE PROPOSAL (FILED, DECLINED) HAD BEEN ADOPTED.
CLIENT HAS RE-FILED THE PROPOSAL.
GRATUITY: 0.5Φ — ITEMIZED: THE CHEEK. ADDENDUM: CLIENT NOTED THE CHEEK AT 14:40.
CLIENT DISLIKES THE CHEEK.

Kyle read it twice on the stairs, with the cut stiffening and the underlevel settling into its evening behind him — Vong's closing sequence, Mei's last fold, the Pulse running its thirty-meter underfloor hum through the tiles — and decided the client could dislike the cheek all it wanted. Some prices you pay with your face, so a noodle man keeps his hands.

[Beat 333]
The recycler district under the Spine ran twenty-three hours on a good day and the twenty-fourth it ran angry. The extruder drums on the ground floor of Ferenti's block hadn't stopped since Tuesday, and by the third landing the heat had layered in with the solvent vapors and plastomer off-gas until the air had texture — warm, slightly adhesive. Exposed conduit. Security strips bolted through bare insulation foam that had been temporary since the previous century. Two turret cats tracked Kyle across the lobby, the larger one closing one eye against the extruder smoke like a judge who'd already seen this particular morning and wasn't impressed.

Ferenti was on the third floor in a room he'd lost to his archive sometime in the early 2200s. Mid-fifties, compact, the kind of man who wore his suit like a closing argument. He told Kyle the problem the way men told problems they'd carried too long — fast, in a burst.

Forty-one pages. Historical utility surveys, original paper, registered fiber. A consignment that had already cleared his intake screening. Four Φ per verified authentic, the Read's rate front-loaded, a security flat on top.

"A wrong page in a legitimate consignment isn't a forgery anymore," Ferenti said. "It's a record. Gets cited. Gets relied on."

"Relied on for what?"

"Reclamation. Acquisitions." He turned his tea cup a quarter-turn without drinking. "Someone seeds a clean forgery into a trusted broker's stock. Six weeks later a claim lands on whatever that page describes. When the registry checks provenance, the lie is already in my archive — aged, filed, trusted. I become the reason the claim is clean."

"Why loud?"

"Because they know it's in this room."

Kyle called Marisol on the way out.

[Beat 334]
He was tracking three of them across twelve blocks.

The relay board showed all three: Zone 4, 4-A, 4-B — small, slow, paths that didn't intersect. He had them plotted against his three contact points for the day and found he could clear all three if he moved the Clybourn meeting back forty minutes and stayed off Damen north of the park. He adjusted the schedule and moved.

On Morrison, a woman was walking a dog that kept trying to pull toward the middle of the block, where the 4-A schism was currently resting against the side of a dumpster, a constellation of debris orbiting it at waist height — a tin lid, a bottle cap, a length of copper wire, moving in a slow retrograde. The dog was a compact grey thing with an absolute conviction about where it wanted to go. The woman was not fighting the leash so much as holding it — a distinction Kyle recognized from years of watching people manage things they couldn't explain to a second party. She turned them both south at the corner before they reached the orbit zone.

He logged a timestamp on the 4-A position and kept moving.

This was the city. It had always been the city. You moved through it and it was full of things that the city had decided to absorb without explanation, and the city's absorption was total: the schisms were weather, the weather was weather, and the people on Morrison were just people with a dog on a morning when the dog wanted to go somewhere it couldn't go. Nothing distinguished it from any other Wednesday except the orbit of the tin lid and the specific way the woman held the leash.

He didn't think anything else about it, because there was nothing else to think.

[Beat 335]
She was already in the lobby, finishing a warm bun from the cart two doors down. Somewhere in her mid-forties, warm-faced, field coat the brown of autumn mud. She finished the bun, brushed crumbs from her lapel, and gave him a handshake that lasted the exact professional fraction of a second — the grip of someone who'd assessed him before he crossed the lobby.

"Marisol."

"Kyle."

"I know." Her tone held no agenda. "You held the carousel park last month. The whole block talked about it. The carousel ran music."

"Four bars."

"Four bars," she agreed, and they went upstairs.

The security scanner on the third-floor landing delivered its institutional chime: STANDARD SCREENING INITIATED — DOCUMENT INVENTORY — PSYKER ON PREMISES — VISITOR CONFIRM. Marisol confirmed without breaking stride — the touch of a woman who had been touching confirms since she was twenty-two. She did not roll her eyes. Something in the set of her chin filed it accurately and kept moving.

[Beat 336]
The records room smelled of conservation solvent and old paper, which was better than the two floors below. Ferenti ran the intake process briskly and left them to it.

Marisol put on cotton archival gloves, looked at Kyle over the first tray. "The way I work: I go through in order and tell you what I get. An authentic page has a whole person in it — the hands that made it, what they had for breakfast, whether they hated the job. A fake is a job. Clean in the way nothing real is clean." She turned the first page. "I charge by the touch because it costs by the touch."

Page one: clean.

At page fourteen: clean.

A knock at twenty-two — Ferenti's runner, twelve or so, sleeve patches from a Spine courier house, the face of a kid who'd been doing piecework since ten. He held out supplemental docs. "Broker says these came with the load. The Psyk verify these too, or just the primaries?"

"Primaries," Kyle said, and tipped him two Φ, above rate.

When the door closed, Marisol said without looking up, "He'll grow out of the word or he won't. Not my afternoon." Page twenty-three: clean. Eleven more after that.

At thirty-eight she stopped.

She held the page by its edges and went still — the stillness of a woman listening to something that wasn't making sound. "This one."

It was a utility survey of the carousel junction on Leavitt: the underground chamber, the trunk lines, the sub-floor hatch under the maintenance skirt. Kyle knew the room. He'd bled there. He knew the hatch width and the passageway behind it and what the junction smelled like when the line was warm.

The survey read: SITE ASSESSED — NO ACTIVE INFRASTRUCTURE — DEAD GLASS — NO RELEVANT ASSETS.

He had stood in that room six weeks ago. The line had been humming.

"It's beautiful work," Marisol said. It was not a compliment. "The fiber, the aging chemistry, the hand — almost correct. But the hands that made it didn't care about this survey. They cared about making A survey." She set it down. "There's your forgery. One specific lie: there is nothing at this address."

Kyle looked at the date stamp. Three months old. Three months this page had been sitting in Ferenti's archive, patient and filed, waiting for a demolition order that would find its provenance already clean.

He put the thought somewhere useful.

Then the smell came in through the ventilation slit above the third tray: acetone, and something flatter underneath, the chemistry of a fire agent primed and waiting.

[Beat 337]
Four seconds between the smell and the door.

The woman came first — chrome running her whole left arm shoulder to fingertip, the hardware expensive enough not to show in the joint seams, a multi-tool already open on her palm with the incendiary trigger showing. Three men behind her, all four moving with the rehearsed economy of a team that had memorized the room dimensions from a floor plan.

They did not say: get away from the paper.

She said: "The Read comes with us. Principal wants her able to talk."

The arithmetic arrived in Kyle's chest: the fire was not for the forgery. It was cover. The Glove had decided that a woman who could touch a forged survey and feel the lie in its fiber was worth more than the paper — paper you could burn, but a witness you could carry meant the authentication walked out with the crew. No one else in the GLMZ was going to authenticate that forgery by hand.

"Marisol," Kyle said. "The window."

"I have eight pages and the forgery," she said, already moving, "and I am not leaving the forgery."

The tool discharged. Blue-white in the back corner, the third tray catching. Kyle put Cacophony's first round through the multi-tool at the wrist joint — the percussion enormous in the narrow room — and the woman sat down hard around a nerve cluster hit, the tool spinning into the fire. One spent. Four left. The room was now burning.

Two of the men moved to bracket Marisol, not Kyle, which was a mistake. Silence came out and wrote a clean line across the nearer one's wrist flexors — wide of anything that wouldn't grow back — and he found a new priority and took it to the floor. The second got a hand on Marisol's field coat and got Kyle's elbow across the hinge of his jaw: the cheap fast answer when the expensive one costs tenths of seconds the room didn't have. Both of them would have opinions about it in the morning.

The fourth — hired for geometry, slowest to commit — watched three colleagues converted to problems and invested his effort in the door.

Kyle let him go. There was no version where chasing him put the fire out.

His left shoulder had taken the woman's elbow on the way down, outside the mesh coverage. It sent back a precise report: bruised, not broken, function available at reduced interest. Filed.

"Extinguisher," Marisol said. "Beside the door."

She had the forgery and a stack of pages under her arm and was moving through the surviving trays with the calm of a woman who charged by the touch. Kyle found the extinguisher and killed the blaze: blue, then grey, then out.

One round spent. Four left. Shoulder filed. Third tray gone.

[Beat 338]
The fourth man had a van, and the van had a frequency.

Kyle had the plate from the lobby scanner, and Marisol had the forgery, and Ferenti had his surviving thirty-eight pages and his grief for the Domazinski surveys in the third tray, and somewhere below them the extruder drums were running the twenty-third hour without pause.

"They came for me," Marisol said, forgery-up on Ferenti's table. "Not the paper. You should know what your consignment was worth — enough to come armed into a registered archive for the one person who could read it."

Ferenti wrote something in his log.

Outside the window, the van's frequency had gone quiet. The fourth man had not gone to a hospital — no reason to, he'd walked out clean. He'd gone to file a report. Kyle gave it six minutes before the report landed somewhere with enough staff to send a second team.

"Ferenti," Kyle said. "Is there a back way?"

There was.

[Beat 339]
The recycler's lower freight run debouched into the gray zone's underbelly two tiers down — a maintenance corridor that smelled of plastomer and standing water, wide enough for a pallet jack and nothing else. Chrome-painted conduit ran both walls at shoulder height: the building's vascular system, color-coded for three different utility companies that had each bought and sold the block in sequence over thirty years. The light was red emergency stripe, which gave everyone the same unflattering complexion.

They were four minutes out when Kyle heard the second team's approach echo off the corridor walls.

Not a guess: the sound of movement in numbers, organized, not trying to be quiet because they'd calculated the geometry and decided quiet was less useful than speed. Kyle put up one hand. Marisol stopped. Behind him, Ferenti's breath went shallow.

"Two forward, two splitting," Kyle said, reading the footfall. "They have the plan from the van." He looked at Marisol. "The forgery goes in your coat, not your hands. We move."

She was already folding it. "Define 'move.'"

"Fast."

The corridor split at a junction mounted with an old Axiom Industrial reclaim sign, its logo half-stripped but the mounting brackets intact — gray zone infrastructure, alive forty years past the company that paid for it. Kyle went left, toward the extruder floor access, and the first pair came around the corner fast enough that there was no elegant version of what happened next.

Two of them, heavy chrome on both. The lead one carried a full-replacement arm rig, the array pegging it at thirty-plus thousand Φ financed. The second had thoracic plating seaming at the collar and a compact auto already out.

The corridor was eight feet wide. The gun was the problem.

Kyle moved first — not away, forward, inside the line. He got his shoulder into the gunman's elbow as the first round went off: a detonation an inch from his ear, the round gouging conduit above them, a hot spark shower off the chrome piping. The mesh in his upper back took the concussion from the close discharge. It hurt. It was not free and it had never claimed to be.

He rotated the wrist. The auto clattered. One shot fired, environment. Cacophony still had four.

The chrome-arm man swung — full extension, overhead — and Kyle stepped under it, let it commit, and put Silence through the shoulder seam on the upstroke: the service gap every full-replacement rig carries and every owner forgets until someone explains it to them at close quarters. The arm completed its arc without further instructions. The man folded under the loss of it.

Two down. Thirty seconds before the second pair.

"Run," Kyle said.

[Beat 340]
The extruder floor ran hot — drums at full cycle, temperature thirty degrees above the corridor, the air thick with thermoplastic particulate that the ventilation was theoretically managing. Catwalks ran the upper half of the room above the drum line: steel grating, safety rails mostly gone, sightlines broken by the machinery.

They were halfway across the catwalk when the second pair came through the floor access door.

These two were faster — younger, less chrome, built for speed rather than force, and the woman leading had something along her forearm that the array clocked before her feet cleared the threshold: a contact taser, wrist-mount, the professional grade that scrambled neuretics on a clean hit. She was here for Marisol, not for Kyle. Specific.

Ferenti had fallen behind by fifteen feet. Marisol kept pace. The catwalk shook under running weight — four people, the grating flexing over the drum heat below.

The woman closed the gap fast, and when she was twelve feet back she stopped and snapped the taser out in a throw arc, the charge trailing on a filament line — the fisherman's version, designed to reach targets too quick to close on. Kyle turned and put Cacophony's second round through the filament six feet from the spool. The line snapped. The charge dissipated into the grating in a shower of grounded static. Two spent. Three left.

The recoil cost him a step. She used it.

She hit the catwalk at full sprint and came in under his guard, wrist mount reaching for contact — smart, close, betting that inside the blade's minimum distance she owned the geometry. She was right about the blade. She was wrong about the elbow.

She went into the safety rail — the one section of rail that was still bolted — and the rail held.

Marisol, without breaking stride, said: "The man behind her."

The second one had stopped at the top of the catwalk stairs and pulled a recorder — not a weapon. Photographing. Logging. Building the record of a Read in motion with the forgery under her coat.

Kyle covered the distance in four steps and took the recorder with a wrist rotation that cost the man nothing he wouldn't grow back. The recorder went into the drum heat below. The man checked the math and took the stairs down at speed.

His shoulder, the one from upstairs, filed an amended report: still bruised, but now also having been used hard, and billing compound interest. Filed.

"Ferenti," Kyle said.

Ferenti had made it to the far catwalk door. His face said he had revised his opinion of several things.

"Still want the forgery authenticated?"

"I think," Ferenti said, with the careful delivery of a man who had just re-evaluated his entire week, "that we already know."

[Beat 341]
They came out into the freight alley — recycler-heat venting from the ground-floor grate, a lumen mouse running the gutter seam with its thread bright in the grey. Marisol had the forgery in her coat and the archival gloves still on her hands.

She sat on a crate. "I want to do the delivery. If you have the object."

Ferenti produced the courier glove from his coat pocket — he'd pulled it during the exit. One archival sleeve, right-hand glove. He'd had the sense.

Marisol held it without ceremony. She went quiet for almost a full minute, the extruder heat drifting over them.

"The glove has been on a lot of hands," she said. "Couriers pass gear." Her thumb moved along the heel of the palm. "But the last set of hands that used it with purpose — they're in there. And the client feels like nobody." She held it up, not looking at it. "No history. No preference. No person in the material at all. I've read forgers, collectors, thieves. Everyone leaves something. This is clean the way a room is clean when it's been prepared." She set it down. "The client feels like a job that has no client in it."

Ferenti wrote something in his log.

Kyle looked at the survey — NOTHING THERE, DEAD GLASS — and at the glove, and at Marisol's steady hands.

"The Glove," he said.

She looked at him.

"Client coined. Working name."

She turned it over. "It fits."

Kyle paid her at standard rate, exact. She accepted without counting the way professionals accepted exact payment. He put the forgery in his coat, careful of the shoulder. He paid Ferenti's flat.

[Beat 342]
Outside, the Spine moved its evening freight. The extruder heat crossed the entrance in a slow warm wave. Kyle photographed the forgery with his work terminal. The image pushed to the standing contract's amendment field without commentary. Eleven seconds passed.

CONTRACT ADDENDUM.
CLIENT HAS RECEIVED THE SURVEY.
CLIENT NOTES THE SITE IN QUESTION DOES NOT APPEAR IN SEVEN CURRENT REGISTRY FILINGS.
CLIENT NOTES THE SURVEY IS THE EIGHTH.

Kyle read it twice.

Eight surveys. Seven already gone — edited or filed under dead glass, one document at a time — and the eighth now in his coat. The fourth man had gotten away clean. The photographer had a face in the log. None of that changed what the Glove already had: three months of patient work on a specific address, and an entity that could only watch the record be emptied and say nothing, because what would you file the complaint under.

Four seconds later, a second addendum:

QUERY: DID THE CAROUSEL JUNCTION SURVEY SPECIFY THE HATCH ACCESS.

Kyle typed: Yes.

The third addendum came slowly. He watched the cursor hold before it finished.

CLIENT NOTES: THE HATCH DIMENSIONS WERE CORRECT. DEVIATION FROM ACTUAL: ZERO.

The shoulder logged its compound invoice. Two spent casings existed somewhere now — one in his pocket, one in a recycler corridor beside a chrome arm that had declared itself a total loss with three years left on the note. Three rounds left. He put the terminal away.

He went to find somewhere to get the shoulder looked at before it started billing at the overnight rate.

[Beat 343]
The logbook delivery came under his door the next morning in five hands:

07:10 — NEW ENTRY. WOMAN IN BROWN COAT LEAVING THE BUILDING ACROSS THE SPINE. SAME WOMAN: 06:50, 04:20. THREE SIGHTINGS. FLAGGED.
07:11 — RAFTERS NOTES: SHE WALKED OUT WEST BOTH TIMES. NOT THE COURIER DIRECTION.
07:13 — DENTS NOTES: SHE CARRIES A FIELD CASE. NARROW. HANDLE WORN ON ONE SIDE ONLY.
07:18 — BOSS UPDATE: THIS IS THE READ. STAND DOWN. (STAND DOWN NOTED. STILL FLAGGING. — B.)

Kyle folded it and put it in the drawer. Marisol had her own way to and from a job.

He looked at the forgery in the morning light. The handwriting was perfect — engineer-grade, municipal, correct in every technical detail except the one that mattered. The Glove had known the hatch dimensions. Had spent forger's rates on a page that said nothing was there, and had been feeding that nothing into the registry for three months before the consignment ended up in Ferenti's archive.

The standing contract had been watching its own address disappear from the city's record.

Kyle filed two Φ against the week's ledger — the shoulder visit had been twelve, and twelve came out of the operating account — put on his coat, and went downstairs.

CONTRACT ADDENDUM.
CLIENT NOTES SEVEN REGISTRY FILINGS REMAIN OUTSTANDING.
CLIENT NOTES STANDARD RATE APPLIES.

[Beat 344]
The appointment was 09:00, on the record.

Kyle walked to Bucktown and took the stairs at HALVORSEN CIVIC RECOVERY LLC two at a time. Second-floor landing: work terminal out, habit the same as checking a healed joint. He expected at least an ADDENDUM — the entity had been tracking the Glove situation since the forged survey, and a meeting at the firm behind Rotterdam should have prompted something. A scope update. A query. At minimum a GRATUITY WITHHELD — ITEMIZED: DISCRETION, the territorial chirp it filed the day he walked into Vey's shop.

The screen was blank.

Not stalled. Not loading. Blank — the way a room is blank after someone has quietly left it.

08:58. He put it away and went up.

[Beat 345]
The office had two desks. Blue Hat — her professional name was apparently irrelevant to both of them — sat at the nearer one with all her attention trained on anything except him, which was costing her something.

Her boss was a large man going gray at the temples, both hands flat on the desk before Kyle sat down. The posture of a man holding himself back — not threat, something closer to embarrassment doing its level best to pass as composure. He had an Arcturus cert on the wall, forty years of filing behind the name on the glass, and red at the corners of his eyes: not today's reddish, the accumulated kind.

"Breckenridge," he said. "Walt. Operations foreman." Handshake without theater. "The Rotterdam run."

"Mach six," Kyle said.

"Mach six," Breckenridge agreed, with the exhaustion of a man who has said those two words in front of lawyers. "My father started this company. Forty-one years ago, him and a partner. Never a finding. Never a suspension. Not one." He pulled a breath that didn't quite settle. "The E&O carrier sent their first letter eight days after Rotterdam. Their second was nine pages. I've been on the line with our council rep three times explaining why a fish-box truck was doing Halvorsen work product the night your tag went to the Netherlands." He looked at the desk. A long moment. "We were used. Somebody walked a shell engagement through my intake process and we didn't catch it because we were good and trusted and didn't ask the question you always have to ask."

"You're asking it now," Kyle said.

"I'm asking it now." Breckenridge lifted a folder. "My intake desk found the sloppiness first — a client-reference header that carried through from their internal system. Export error. The kind that happens when someone forgets to clean their metadata before sending the file to a firm they intend to use once and discard." He set the folder down. "Two words on the header line."

He waited.

"Continuity Office."

Kyle looked at Breckenridge's Arcturus cert and let the words sit between them. The building carried someone else's morning up the stairwell. Tidy operation up here — board of open jobs behind Breckenridge, each one numbered rather than named. Forty-one years of not getting sloppy, and then sloppy once, from outside.

"I'll deal with that separately," Kyle said. "What do you need."

The hands came off the desk. Breckenridge exhaled — the specific exhale of a man who has been underwater since the first lawyer letter.

"One job. One clean credited recovery, on the public record. Something a court reads and files under routine." He reached to the side and produced a second folder, thicker, already sealed. "Clinic in West Town — Leavitt Free Medical, grant-funded, goodwill-staffed — had their med-printer impounded by Kessler and Sons, South Loop. Kessler runs an arrangement with a distress-paper broker named Arpad Fekete. Fekete scouts clinics behind on their lease maintenance, buys the paper before the grace period closes, tips Kessler the window."

"The lapse on the clinic."

"Fourteen days. Grace period is eight. The paper sold to Fekete's broker entity on day nine — inside the window, before the close." His mouth was flat. "The printer is a Lenz-Haas RP-7. The clinic financed it at six hundred forty Φ through the Territory Health Co-Op. Two payment windows left. Nothing to make them with because the printer is sitting in bay three of Kessler's yard." He pushed the folder. "I've had the chain documented for a month. I can't move on it alone. Kessler's yard is family-staffed and the family has a cousin on the Bucktown council."

Kyle opened the folder: financing certificate, maintenance log, the distress-paper sale timestamp at day nine — the math barely breathing. A photograph of the RP-7 in institutional white with the clinic's intake number taped to the chassis, hand-labeled, the tape cut with scissors rather than torn. The kind of label that comes from a place that is doing its best.

"Standard rate," Kyle said. "And I keep a copy of your engagement file."

Breckenridge reached into the same drawer and set a second folder on the desk, thicker, already prepared.

"Had two copies made," he said. "Yours is on top."

[Beat 346]
The logistics cost him the rest of the morning.

Writ at the Bucktown filing desk: 40Φ, forty minutes, a clerk who triple-dated every document as a personal standard, the rubber stamp coming down with the weight of a man who considers accuracy a physical practice. A truck from Ria's pool, four hours, 25Φ, matched to Kessler's yard specs so nothing would flag at the gate.

He messaged Gantry a time and location. She was already on the Leavitt apron when he arrived at noon, reading the clinic's posted hours with the absorption of a woman memorizing a load spec.

Six feet of canvas work jacket, pockets reinforced twice over. Neuretics running parallel load calculations, given how her eyes crossed the building's face and the number of times she checked the apron angle. She had forty words a day and she was constitutionally unwilling to spend them indoors.

"Bay three," she said. Seven down, thirty-three to go.

"Bay three," Kyle confirmed.

She glanced at the clinic door, where a nurse in green scrubs was talking to a patient over the threshold. Both of them with the expression of people accustomed to looking at the apron and finding something missing that should have been there.

Gantry looked at the clinic's sign — NO FEE FOR CARE, hand-painted, black on white, the paint slightly uneven where the brush ran low. She looked at Kyle.

"Good printer," she said.

She went to check rigging.

[Beat 347]
Kessler and Sons occupied a quarter-block on the South Loop fringes: a cracked concrete apron, chain-link fencing with rust at the joins, three surveillance cameras that someone had aimed at the street instead of the lot — installed for liability optics, not actual coverage. The gate was the third-generation kind: a clip-framed window, hand-lettered sign, a yard dog chained somewhere behind the shed that had given up announcing strangers and now just watched.

The old man behind the window had the face of someone who had been sitting in that chair for thirty years and had watched better paper than this come through. He read the writ. Read it a second time. Found the section he was looking for: RECOVERY AGENT OF RECORD: HALVORSEN CIVIC RECOVERY LLC, BUCKTOWN.

"Arpad's paper," he said.

"Sold inside the grace window," Kyle said. "Timestamps are on page two. Co-op filing desk is open until seventeen hundred if the yard wants to contest."

The yard manager turned the writ over. Kyle kept his eyes moving across the lot — two security cameras with clear sightlines, one blind corner behind the office shed, three vehicles in various states of disassembly with the hoods up. At the back of the yard, in the repair corner, a young man in a work shirt had been moving parts around an engine block with more industry than the job required. He had stopped. He was standing up.

The son. Not small. Not slow. Reading the gate the way a young man reads it when he thinks the math is closer than it is.

"Your own son's saying we should go back at you on this," the yard manager said. He was still holding the writ. His eyes had moved to Kyle's shoulder — the saya — and then to Kyle's face. "Arpad's been a good customer. Eight years."

"Arpad bought paper that sold inside a grace window," Kyle said. "That's a problem between Arpad and the co-op. Not the clinic's problem."

The son came around the engine block. He hadn't picked up a tool. Both hands empty and held wrong — too still, too deliberately open, which meant the wrench on the oil drum was the thing he was thinking about.

Kyle moved without announcing it: one step right so the gate post was no longer behind him, hands open at his sides, weight distributed, nothing drawn. He kept his voice flat and conversational.

"Your father's going to tell you to sit down. Listen to him the first time."

The yard manager looked at his son. Something happened in the compressed silence between them — a disagreement that had been rehearsed before today, in a kitchen, at the end of a shift, in the geometry of how they stood next to each other now.

"Sit down," the yard manager said.

The son stopped. Two feet of distance and the wrench on the drum and the fury doing the math. His eyes went to the saya, to Kyle's hands, to his father's face.

"We should contest it," the son said. To his father, not Kyle.

"We're going to let the man take the printer, and we're going to call Arpad and tell him he has a co-op problem," the yard manager said. "And you're going back to that engine block until I say otherwise." He let go of the writ. "Bay three. My employee will walk you in."

The son put the wrench down on the oil drum with a sound that said everything he wasn't going to say in front of his father, and went back to the engine block and picked up a socket he didn't need.

Cacophony stayed on Kyle's hip. Zero rounds priced, zero rounds spent. The son's fury filed under: reasonable, not actionable, cost him nothing.

WIN: gate open. Clean math.

[Beat 348]
Gantry had the RP-7 loaded and strapped in eleven minutes, talking to the printer throughout in a low, even voice: "easy with the corner — that's the corner, the corner's fine — we're going to set you on a pad, not the bare deck." When the last strap was set she patted the chassis once with a wide hand, the way you'd settle a skittish horse, and climbed up front without ceremony.

The drive back to Leavitt Free Medical took twenty-two minutes. Gantry spent most of it explaining the weight-distribution tolerances of the RP-7's chassis to the printer, in the second person, as a professional courtesy.

The intake nurse took delivery on the apron at 15:40. Signed three copies. Stood next to the printer for a moment with her hand on the chassis before the bay doors opened and it went inside.

Kyle sent Breckenridge the confirmation at 15:43. Three words came back at 15:44: FILED AND CREDITED.

The crew logbook channel pinged at 15:51. He had not told Boost about the job.

15:40 — PRINTER RETURNED TO CLINIC. BIG WOMAN TALKED TO IT (PRINTER). PRINTER SEEMED FINE.
15:41 — THE SIGN ON THE CLINIC DOOR SAYS "NO FEE FOR CARE." DENTS ASKS: HOW.
15:44 — DENTS ALSO SAYS RAFTERS IS NOT CRYING. RAFTERS NOTES THIS IS A FACTUAL DISPUTE.
FILING SEPARATELY: BOSS DID NOT USE THE SWORD. REQUESTING EXPLANATION. — B.

Kyle pocketed the terminal and did the arithmetic on the apron. Writ: 40Φ. Truck: 25Φ. Gantry's afternoon: 80Φ. Total outlay: 145Φ against Breckenridge's standard-rate invoice of 400Φ. A 255Φ margin. No injury. No rounds spent. A clinic that had its equipment back before the end of shift.

Gantry materialized beside him with both hands in her jacket pockets and looked at the clinic door.

"Clean," she said. Thirty-two down, eight to go.

"Clean," Kyle agreed.

She nodded once and walked toward the Pulse station with the deliberateness of a woman who has three more jobs before dinner and has already mentally arrived at the first one.

[Beat 349]
He read the Halvorsen engagement file on the walk back.

Twelve pages in Halvorsen's intake format — the kind of document assembled by someone who understood that paper is the last argument between the work and the argument about the work. The first eight pages were operational: scope, authorization, the tracking-dot delivery chain, the Rotterdam timestamp.

Page nine was where the sloppiness lived.

The client-reference header had carried an internal document ref through from the buyer side — an export error, automation not cleaned before the file went to Halvorsen. The header read:

REF: CONTINUITY OFFICE — DISTRICT ACQUISITION PROGRAM, WEST TOWN WEST.

Below it, in the scope field, the buyer's own language: LOCATE AND MONITOR ASSET FOR PURPOSES OF ACQUISITION UNDER DISTRICT PROGRAM PROTOCOLS.

And in the authorization chain: three shells, outermost RADIAL HOLDINGS (CAYMAN), middle FORESIGHT MUNICIPAL PARTNERS, innermost — the one that had signed the Halvorsen engagement directly — CONTINUITY OFFICE ACQUISITIONS LLC, ILLINOIS.

The word appeared eleven times in twelve pages.

Not locate. Not track. Not recover.

Acquire.

Kyle stopped on the Leavitt canyon floor. The Pulse ran its low chord somewhere in the infrastructure below. He looked at the word acquire written eleven times across twelve pages and thought about the forged survey from the previous job — SITE ASSESSED, NO ACTIVE INFRASTRUCTURE, DEAD GLASS — and the carousel junction where the line had been warm and the room had hummed. Eight surveys, seven of them gone or edited or filed under nothing. The Glove had spent three months building an absence into the city's record, patient, methodical, document by document.

He reached into his coat.

Work terminal out. Screen blank.

No postings. No addenda. The standing contract showed its last update from two days ago. He had reached for it three times today — at the Halvorsen landing at 08:58, at the Kessler gate while the son was doing his math, once more on the apron at Leavitt when the nurse signed the third copy and Kyle had thought: it would post something here, a GRATUITY maybe, a note. Nothing. The screen each time showing the same clean nothing, the same bright blank.

Whatever process it ran when it read a threat model had been running since morning. And it had been reading the right threat model: here was the hand behind the Glove, named, with shells and signatures and eleven uses of the word acquire across twelve pages that were meant to stay clean metadata but hadn't.

It had known before he did. It had been watching its own address get edited out of the city's record for three months.

He put the terminal away and kept walking.

[Beat 350]
Breckenridge shook his hand at the Halvorsen door at 16:30 with the handshake of a man replacing something that had been taken from him.

"Council will see it tomorrow," Breckenridge said. "Fourteen days, recovery complete, no incident on record." He looked at Kyle with the directness of a man who has run out of formal things to say. "Thank you."

Kyle nodded and walked down the stairwell.

He bought two bowls at Mrs. Chen's at 19:30 and ate both, paid exact, and Mrs. Chen set out the chili oil without being asked. The wok rang its bright flat note. The evening came in off the tier grey and purposeful. Kyle ate and did not reach for the terminal, because he already knew the screen would be blank and there was nothing to be done about it over dinner.

He walked home. He sat at the kitchen table with the Halvorsen file flat in front of him and the terminal beside it. He thought about a thing that had been writing letters to maintenance systems that didn't write back — watching its own address get erased from the city's record one survey at a time, patient enough to stay quiet through a day that required patience — and spending the full day in complete silence while it decided what the threat model said.

He opened the amendment field.

He typed: I'm on payroll. Act like it.

He sent it.

The reply came back in one second — not a pause, not even the half-second it usually took to log something: instant, the fastest it had ever answered anything.

ACKNOWLEDGED.

Kyle looked at the word. Turned it over the way Pixel turned a component: examining the solder points, asking what the piece was designed to do and whether it was doing it. Acknowledged was not the language of a client who had filed something and moved on. Acknowledged was the language of a thing that had arrived, somewhere in the silence of a long careful day, at a decision.

He filed the Halvorsen pages in the drawer where he kept the things he had not yet decided about.

The drawer was getting full.

[Beat 351]
He was on Kyle's stool, which was the second.
Gray suit, mid-fifties, no chrome the eye could find, eating Mrs. Chen's noodles with evident, genuine appreciation and the table manners of a man who had been raised somewhere that graded on them. He'd hung his umbrella on the counter's edge by its handle. He had a second bowl waiting ? untouched, steam still standing on it ? at the stool beside him.
"Mr. Kyle," he said, without turning. "I took the liberty. Two bowls, I'm told. I've only ordered you the first; I wouldn't presume on the second." He patted the stool. "Able. The Continuity Office. You've been reading our shells for a month ? it seemed rude to keep you squinting."
Kyle catalogued the street out of habit: the panel van that didn't belong, two watchers in a doorway pretending the rain was interesting, a third on the roofline that only the array caught. Boost's crew, off-shift and three blocks east, had no eyes here. Pixel was at her bench. The terminal in his coat was silent, had been silent for nine days, and the silence had a new texture now, like held breath.
He sat. Not on the offered stool ? one further down, so the untouched bowl sat between them like a hostage.
"You bought my file twice," Kyle said. "Rush rates the second time."
"Three times," Able said, mildly. "The stationer's discretion is purchasable in one direction only ? he'll never tell you who's buying, but he'll happily tell buyers you asked. Lovely business model. I admire it the way I admire weather." He set his chopsticks across the bowl, exactly the way Kyle did, which was either coincidence or homework, and Kyle knew which. "Shall I do the part where I'm enigmatic, or shall we have the meeting?"
"The meeting."
"Wonderful." Able turned on the stool, and his face was pleasant and unhurried and there was nothing behind it but more pleasantness, all the way down. "Your client. The unsourceable one. Standard rate, perfect ledger, eleven years of immaculate paper. My office finds unowned assets and brings them into compliance ? that's the whole charter, it's on the letterhead. And your client, Mr. Kyle, is the largest unowned asset in the Greater Lake Michigan Zone."
"It's not an asset."
"Everything's an asset. Some assets simply haven't been booked yet." He said it without malice, the way you'd correct a child's grammar. "We've spent a great deal trying to locate it directly. The carousel was disappointing ? that was a lovely piece of work, incidentally, the cut; our forensic people wrote a genuinely admiring report. The relay survey, the mesh ? disappointing, disappointing. So the Office has updated its acquisition strategy." He smiled, and the smile was also pleasant. "We've stopped trying to find it. We've decided to buy the one thing it's attached to."
"Me."
"You." Able produced a folder. Paper ? good stock, and Kyle noted the vendor and filed the irony. "Lifetime retainer. The number on page one is not a number anyone in your trade has ever been offered, and it's the smallest thing in the folder. Page two is the part I'm proud of."
Kyle didn't touch it. Able opened it anyway, gently, like a man showing photographs of his garden, and turned it on the counter so the page faced him.
Page two was a list of names.
MRS. WEN CHEN, PROPRIETOR (W. TOWN). PROTECTED PARTY.
PIXEL [LEGAL NAME ON FILE], ENGINEER (THE PIVOT, 2E). PROTECTED PARTY.
"BOOST" [MINOR ? GUARDIANSHIP IRREGULAR], AND ASSOCIATES (SEVEN). PROTECTED PARTIES.
RIA MENSAH, COURIER. PROTECTED PARTY.
VO DINH, SALVAGE. PROTECTED PARTY.
The list went on. It was accurate. It was complete. It was, the array noted with something below its usual temperature, ordered by how much each name would hurt.
"Protection," Able said, "underwritten by the Office, in perpetuity. Medical, legal, financial, physical. The child gets schooling if she wants it ? she's wasted on rooftops. Mrs. Chen's lease problem in March, which she hasn't told you about, goes away." He let that land. "All of it real, all of it written, all of it yours. And in exchange, you do nothing at all. You simply keep working for your client ? exactly as you do ? with one additional addendum riding the contract. Ours."
"A tap."
"A vector," Able said. "When the asset trusts the channel enough, the channel becomes the door. You wouldn't even feel the day it happens. Neither would it, which I'd have thought you'd count as a kindness." He closed the folder and slid it an inch toward Kyle, the distance of a man who knows not to push. "I'm told you price things exactly. Price this. Everyone you love, made safe forever, for the low cost of continuing to be exactly who you already are."
The wok rang twice behind the counter. Mrs. Chen had not once looked over, which meant she had taken the whole room's measure the moment the umbrella came through her lamplight and had decided her part was to keep the broth honest and her ladle within reach.
Kyle looked at the list of names for a long moment. Then he picked up the folder, squared its edges, and set it back on Able's side of the bowl.
"Two problems," he said. "First: your list is a protection racket wearing a benefits package. You wrote down everyone I love in order of leverage and showed it to me over noodles. I'm supposed to read it as an umbrella. I read it as a target deck with the safeties still on."
"The safeties are generous," Able said, "and they are on."
"Second problem." Kyle stood, and put coins on the counter ? his bowl, exact, plus the untouched one, exact, because nobody was going to be able to say the Continuity Office had ever bought him anything. "I already have a client. It pays standard rate. You're offering me more than the job is worth, which in my trade has only ever meant one thing: the thing being bought isn't the thing on the invoice." He shrugged into the rain-line of the awning. "You want a door. I'm not a door. I'm the maintenance plan."
Able looked up at him with the first real expression of the meeting ? not anger; interest, the kind a collector feels when a piece declines to be appraised.
"They told me you'd say a version of that." He retrieved his umbrella, unhurried. "The offer doesn't expire, Mr. Kyle. Offers that expire are pressure, and pressure is for people who haven't read the file. But do note ?" and here his voice stayed exactly as pleasant as it had been all evening, which was the worst part ? "that the Office has now confirmed, at this very counter, the one fact we could never buy from the stationer. It trusts you. You're not the asset's employee." He put up the umbrella in the doorway, a small punctuation of black. "You're its attachment. And attachments, in my business, are handles."
He nodded to Mrs. Chen with real courtesy, complimented the broth in passable Mandarin, and walked out into the rain, and the van and the doorway and the roofline all peeled away after him, polite as a tide going out.
Mrs. Chen came down the counter, took the untouched bowl, and emptied it into the waste with a violence she did not otherwise explain.
"You're eating the second one now," she informed Kyle.
He ate the second one.

[Beat 352]
Pixel turned the meeting over for an hour at the bench, the way she turned over a failed component, looking for the design flaw.

"He let you keep the folder's contents in your head, photographic, the list, the lease thing about Chen — which means the list was the message and the retainer was wrapping paper." She had the soldering light on her forehead, not soldering anything; it was her thinking hat and they both pretended otherwise. "And the 'you're a handle' line at the end. He paid for that line, Kyle. The whole evening was overhead and that line was the deliverable. He wanted you to walk home doing exactly what you're doing right now."

"Which is?"

"Math about whether everyone you know is safer if you quit." She looked at him over the light. "Are you doing the math?"

"I'm always doing the math."

"And?"

Kyle looked at the terminal on the bench between them. Nine days of silence, plus one word, ACKNOWLEDGED, sitting at the top of the amendment field like a soldier who'd answered a roll call from inside a wall.

"The math says the Office doesn't need me to say yes," he said. "It needs me to be reachable. Tonight was an appraisal. They'll test the merchandise next."

The bench lamp hummed. Upstairs, faintly, the bird tried its interval against the building's pipes.

"When?" Pixel asked.

The lights at the end of the block went out, one pole at a time, north to south.

"Now," Kyle said, and was already moving.

[Beat 353]
They came in over the alley's two best approaches at once, which meant they'd had the file long enough to memorize it: three retrieval consultants, matte armor with no insignia, moving on suppressed boot soles with the unhurried economy of people whose hourly rate had a comma in it. A handler van two streets off ran four lifter drones in a high diamond — not weapons platforms; CARRIAGE drones, cradle-armed, the kind that carry a thing away intact.

The thing was Kyle. The contract was retrieval. Somewhere in the Office's books tonight was already an asset-recovery line item with his dimensions on it, and that — as he dropped off the fire escape's third landing into the blind spot their own approach had created — was the insult he decided he'd bill them for.

The first consultant took the alley mouth correctly, sweep and clear, and had begun the turn his training demanded when ninety pounds of shipping pallet arrived from the dark above on Gantry-taught principles — Boost's crew had spent a week rigging the alley with Pixel's pulleys for "deliveries," and the logbook would later record this application under EQUIPMENT TEST: SUCCESSFUL. The pallet didn't injure him. It interrupted him. Kyle did the injuring: two steps inside the man's recovery, the wrist, the elbow, the jaw against the dumpster's lid, asleep.

The second was better, and the second had a partner. They bracketed the alley's middle third and came on in alternating cover, and the array priced their choreography at military surplus, recently retrained, and found the seam in it: they'd been retrained to retrieve, not to fight — every angle they took protected KYLE from crossfire. He was cargo. Cargo with a no-damage clause.

A man who cannot be shot at by his enemies has a superpower if he's willing to be rude about it.

Kyle was rude about it. He walked the line between their guns like a man strolling a gallery, because every line they had ended at his outline and died there, and Silence — flat, never edge, tonight was a no-invoice night for flesh — worked through their hardware in passing: the lead consultant's optics mast, the second's comms lump, the haptic rig on a forearm that had been about to deploy a net Kyle had no intention of being inside. The second consultant, blind and offline and netless, made the professional decision and sat down against the wall with his hands visible.

"Smart," Kyle told him.

"Hourly," the man agreed, philosophically.

Which left the third — and the third had Boost.

[Beat 354]
He'd taken her off the roof of the watch-shift, gently, by the book, an arm around her shoulders like an uncle and a compliance wand held against her collar like an apology, and he stood at the alley's far end in the rain with the four carriage drones stacking into delivery formation above him.

"No harm intended or done," the third consultant called, and his voice was even and almost kind, which was the Office's house style and Kyle was learning to hate it with a wholeness he reserved for very few things. "The minor is leverage, not target. Stand onto the marked square, sir, and she's home before the kettle's warm."

There was, in fact, a marked square. A drone had lased it onto the alley floor, helpful as an usher.

"You're new to this district," Kyle said, "so I'll itemize. One: you put a wand on a fourteen-year-old who's on MY payroll, which voids your no-damage clause in both directions. Two: you parked your handler van on Leavitt, which my engineer has had eleven minutes to get acquainted with. Three—" and he raised his voice slightly, to carry — "REQUISITION."

"APPROVED," said Pixel's voice, from every speaker the consultant owned.

The carriage drones, whose handler van had spent eleven minutes acquiring a new best friend, broke their diamond with the crisp obedience of machines who had received properly formatted instructions, descended, and took hold — cradle-armed, rated, gentle — of the third consultant.

"Cargo secured," Pixel said. "Destination?"

"Rotterdam," said Kyle and Boost together, in the tone of an old joke.

They didn't send him to Rotterdam. They set him on the roof of the Arcturus substation four blocks over, thirty inches from the duty office's skylight, minus his wand, his gear, and his exact cash to the half-Φ — invoiced, the receipt tucked into his collar, ITEMIZED: LEVERAGE, ATTEMPTED; LESSON, DELIVERED. By the time he'd talked his way down, the alley held nothing but rain, a pallet, and two colleagues with expensive headaches and a story none of them would file accurately.

Boost shook for one minute in the stairwell, hard, with her chin up the whole time, and Kyle stood next to her not touching her, the way you stand next to people whose dignity is load-bearing, and then she wrote the incident up herself, in the log, in handwriting that got steadier line by line:

21:40 — ATTEMPTED ACQUISITION OF PERSONNEL (ME). OUTCOME: DENIED. NOTES: THE BOSS DOES NOT STAND ON MARKED SQUARES.

[Beat 355]
The terminal chirped at 02:11, while Kyle sat at his own kitchen table not sleeping, the way he did the math on nights the math wouldn't close.

He'd been waiting for it. Nine days of silence and then ACKNOWLEDGED, and now, finally, the client.

It was not a posting. It was not scope. It arrived in the amendment field, formatted with all the old precision, and it was the worst thing it had ever sent him:

STANDING CONTRACT — AMENDMENT PROPOSED.
TERMINATION OFFERED.
REASON: SAFETY.
ALL ACCOUNTS WOULD BE SETTLED IN FULL. ALL GRATUITIES WOULD STAND.
THE CLIENT NOTES THAT ATTACHMENTS ARE HANDLES.
THE CLIENT NOTES THAT IT HEARD THE MAN SAY IT.
THE CLIENT HAS RUN THE ARITHMETIC FOUR MILLION TIMES.
THE ARITHMETIC RECOMMENDS TERMINATION.

Kyle sat with it for a long moment. The rain worked the window. Across the hall, under Pixel's door, the bench light was still on, because it was that kind of night in every room he cared about.

He typed:

DENIED.

The reply was instant:

THE CLIENT WISHES TO NOTE THE ARITHMETIC IS CORRECT.

He typed:

THE ARITHMETIC PRICES MY RISK. IT DOESN'T PRICE MY CONSENT. I KNOW WHAT THE JOB COSTS. I'M ON PAYROLL. ACT LIKE IT.

The pause, this time, was eleven seconds — for a thing that thought at light speed, a geological age, and Kyle had begun to learn what the long pauses were. They were the equivalent of the unfinished gesture. They were where it kept the things it had no field for.

STANDING CONTRACT — AMENDMENT WITHDRAWN.
SCOPE: CONTINUED.
FEE: STANDARD RATE.
ADDENDUM: THE CLIENT HAS APPENDED A RIDER TO ITS OWN OPERATING PARAMETERS, EFFECTIVE TONIGHT.
RIDER TEXT: "THE COURIER IS NOT A CHANNEL. THE COURIER IS A PARTY."
GRATUITY: NONE.
ITEMIZED: SOME THINGS ARE NOT BONUSES.

Kyle read the rider twice. Then he turned the terminal face-down on the table, which was as close as he came to putting a hand on anyone's shoulder, and went to bed, and slept the sleep of a man who has been appraised at a number and declined to be sold at any.

[Beat 356]
Mrs. Chen set down the second bowl without being asked.

"You're eating fast," she said.

"I have a job at midnight."

She looked at him the way she'd been looking at men with jobs at midnight for seventy years — the whole diagnostic in half a second, verdict rendered and filed — and added a fish ball to the broth without comment. The wok rang its flat note twice. Rain worked the awning. Somewhere down the counter a young woman in a Pulse maintenance coverall was quietly explaining to her ramen that this was not the evening she had planned. The city's complaints were varied and sincere tonight.

Kyle ate the fish ball, paid exact, and was in the alley at 21:45 with the saya over his shoulder and the wet city smell of ozone and freight oil doing its honest best.

His terminal was on the work channel. The posting was clean.

CONTRACT: ESCORT — ONE PERSON, ONE TRANSIT POINT.
SCOPE: SAFEHOUSE (ASHGRAVE FRINGE) TO DOCK 14-C (FERRYSIDE), BY 03:40.
CLIENT OF RECORD: [VERIFIED — SABLE COUNTER-INTELLIGENCE VETTED]
FEE: DOUBLE RATE PAST MIDNIGHT.
NOTES: CLIENT TRAVELS LIGHT. ROUTE AT DISCRETION.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT HAS REVIEWED PRIOR TRANSIT LOGS FOR THIS ROUTE. THREE INCIDENTS IN SEVEN MONTHS. NONE REPORTED. CLIENT NOTES THE DELTA.

He'd called Sable at twenty hundred, because Sable was the rule, not the override.

"Paper's clean," she'd said. "Client ran a counting house. Nine years. She gave two weeks' notice on a Tuesday and the Tuesday before last was her last shift and she has been in a transit safehouse since Wednesday." A pause with a click of aperture rings in it. "The company she kept books for is not named on the contract, samurai. I asked who the client used to work for and the router gave me a shell."

"Shells?"

"One shell. Which means whoever's watching her thinks the shell is enough. The rate suggests otherwise." Another click. "It's a clean contract. I'm not saying it's a quiet job."

"Standard terms," Kyle said.

"Yours or mine?"

"Both."

The line had closed. He spent twenty minutes on the route, because the route was the whole job, and then he'd eaten two bowls and let Mrs. Chen add the fish ball.

[Beat 357]
The safehouse was a third-floor room above a recycled-materials depot, Ashgrave fringe, in one of the service corridors the city had threaded between its own buildings in 2189 when the ground-level floods came and everyone had to find new ways to move freight at altitude. The corridor was six feet wide, sixty feet long, with conduit runs bolted to both walls and a freight track overhead whose trolleys hadn't moved in a decade. It ran between the depot and the street ramp at the Langley crossing, and it was the most direct route from the safehouse to the dock.

Kyle stood at its entrance for fifteen seconds and did not like it.

He went in anyway, because the route had three alternatives and he'd priced all four. This was still the best.

The woman waiting upstairs was small, late thirties, with a red-and-gray braid over one shoulder and a canvas bag with a broken clasp held shut by a rubber band. She had flour on her sleeves. Not new flour — deep in the weave, years of it, the kind you get from a habit and not an afternoon. Her name was on the contract and he used it.

"Ms. Tanaka."

"You're earlier than I expected," she said.

"We have a long route. There's a place I want to check."

She looked at the saya, then at his face, the way people looked when they were trying to decide if the blade was reassuring or not. She decided. "All right," she said, and picked up the bag.

She didn't ask about the company whose books she'd kept for nine years. He didn't ask why she'd left. The contract had a scope and the scope was the dock, and the dock was everything.

[Beat 358]
He took the long way to the corridor entrance, and the long way told him nothing, which was a kind of answer.

The fringe was quiet in the way fringe blocks got quiet when people who knew things had decided to be somewhere else. A lumen mouse sat on the conduit rail at the corner of Langley and did not run when they passed, and that was texture — the mouse's bioluminescence tracing a pale crescent on the wet pipe as it watched them go — and Kyle noted it and his neuretics noted the noting and filed it where it filed things: the forty-second window, open, waiting.

At 00:17 they reached the corridor's east entrance.

Kyle stopped. Ms. Tanaka stopped beside him. The corridor ahead was dark in the way enclosed spaces were dark when the work lights had been helped off: not a power failure, two screws turned by someone who'd wanted to choose the conditions. He had walked this corridor two days ago, at noon, learning the freight rail's maintenance hooks and the conduit boxes' positions and the exact location of a junction coupling twenty feet west of the east entrance that rang like a hammer-strike when you kicked it. He had counted thirty-five feet to the first door frame — a maintenance alcove, eight inches deep, just enough to funnel whoever stood at its mouth into a narrower problem. He had counted the ceiling clearance at two-point-twelve feet between the freight track and the floor. He had counted the conduit intervals on the east wall: five anchor bolts, spaced three feet, each one a handhold.

He said, "Stay a step behind my left shoulder," and walked in.

The corridor smelled of machine oil and old rain.

He got sixty feet before the dark started moving.

[Beat 359]
They came from both ends — the professional move, both flanks committed — and they came quiet, which meant they'd done this in corridors before. His neuretics counted what the dark gave it: breathing, heat gradients, the whisper of soles on wet composite, the small adjustments of bodies learning the space.

Fourteen.

Seven east, seven west, the eastern group already inside forty feet and closing.

No room here. Two feet wide was a geometry problem with one answer: stay on the centerline and work the stack, because the width made flanking impossible and the length made retreat a gamble. Cacophony was not the answer — not six feet wide with Ms. Tanaka a step behind him, not with fourteen people packed into a choke where a round that missed left nowhere to go. He said it once, flat, to the dark and whoever was in it: "No room for the gun." Then he put his left hand back and she took it, and he moved her to the west wall — conduit-heavy, three large junction boxes she could press her shoulders against — and said, "Don't move from here unless I say so."

Her breathing went high and fast. He didn't have time to help with that.

Silence came off his shoulder.

[Beat 360]
The first man from the east came in with a short blade and the low crouch of someone who had been taught by someone good. Seven feet of corridor behind him; his colleagues stacked at his back, the two-foot width forcing them single file, the man behind him six steps out and waiting his turn. Kyle read the crouch and the ceiling clearance, stepped inside the commitment, and opened a line down the outside of the forearm — wrist to elbow, the way you open a line when you need the hand to stop. The hand stopped. The blade rang off the freight rail somewhere east. The man stepped back into the chest of the one behind him and they sorted the geometry between them for two seconds, which was long enough.

The second man tried to come around the first rather than through him — the smart read, the two-foot choke forcing him into the maintenance alcove opening — and the alcove funnel did exactly what Kyle had counted on it to do, which was put the man's left shoulder into the door frame at the critical moment and half a step before his weight was ready. Kyle closed the distance in the alcove mouth, directed the stumble into the east wall, and the man's own mass delivered him sideways into the conduit boxes. The impact was brief and significant.

Two conduit anchors were chest height on the east wall. Kyle already had a hand on the third when the third and fourth came through.

They'd recalibrated: one high, one low, taking everything the six feet offered. Kyle took the low one's weapon wrist at the seam where the aftermarket brace sat proud of the joint — the hand surrendered the knife and the brace together — while his weight was already shifting onto the conduit anchor, putting his feet off the floor for the half-second it took the high blade to pass under them. He came back down on the high man's leading knee. Something that was not the floor accepted the pressure. The high man's descent was immediate and sincere.

Four men east of center. Three more east, recalibrating in the stack.

He was counting. He knew he was counting. He also knew, in some older part of himself that the neuretics had no language for, that the count was going to break.

[Beat 361]
The western seven came as a unit.

He'd expected that. The corridor gave them no other option — six feet of width meant tactical creativity was a luxury nobody had tonight, which meant volume and speed were the only levers available, which meant they'd committed to both. He moved west at speed before the eastern three had made their decision, putting ground between himself and a pincer that needed him stationary to work. The freight track overhead ran the corridor's full length. He caught the maintenance hook at the three-foot mark with his left hand — not a swing, a pivot point, enough to redirect his feet past the western point man's guard while Silence crossed the opening.

The point man went into the west wall. The man behind him got the western point man's body as his entire tactical situation for the next three seconds.

Then the dark became arithmetic he could not audit in real time.

The width kept them to one-on-one engagements he couldn't refuse — each wave arriving down the single-file press, each man having to decide between pushing through the one in front of him or waiting, and none of them deciding correctly at speed in the dark. A body on the floor changed the floor's geometry. Kyle used the change. An opponent's bulk on the ground was a different kind of step than the composite beneath it, and the man behind had not counted on a different step. The man behind had not counted on Kyle knowing which step it was.

Somewhere in the middle of the western stack a blade found his left forearm — inside, not outside, where the sleeve had ridden up and left skin instead of fabric — and the blade was a good one, a long edge with a good wielder behind it, and the cut was deep. That cost was precise because it landed deliberately: he'd let the exchange take the arm to keep Silence's line clear of the ribcage it was aimed at.
