A different cost arrived a second later in the same exchange — a body check, the third rib from the bottom, and the rib's assessment was immediate and terminal: cracked. Breathing would be an administrative task for the next several weeks.

Those two he could name. The rest of the count he would reconstruct afterward from what he found against the walls.

The seventh on the western side was a woman with a staff weapon — short-hafted, built for enclosure, which meant she knew what she was doing and knew where she was. She used the corridor's length the way the blades couldn't: standing at the edge of her reach and managing the freight rail from underneath, keeping him off the ceiling anchor until she'd triangulated what he was likely to try. She tagged the rib again, reading the favor he'd started showing it. Kyle worked the angle until he had her staff under the freight track at the moment she needed the ceiling clearance to recover, and a joint lock on the follow-through, and she went down the way professional people went down when the math finished: controlled, without theater, conserving what was left for later.

The corridor was still.

[Beat 362]
Kyle stood in the machine-oil dark and did not count.

This was the fact that lived in the space where counts usually lived: the corridor had not let him count it. He had wins — fourteen people against walls and floor, the dock still reachable, Ms. Tanaka still pressed to the junction boxes — and the wins had prices, and the prices were on his body, not in his arithmetic. The forearm was bleeding through the field strip he'd drawn tight with his teeth during a half-second between the fifth and sixth. The rib had a strong opinion about breathing that he was not going to be able to ignore for long. His neuretics offered a damage summary and he declined to read it, not because he didn't want to know but because reading it would make it exact and exact would make it slower.

"Ms. Tanaka," he said.

"I'm here," she said, from the junction boxes. Her voice had steadied the way voices steadied when the immediate worst was over and the next worst had not arrived yet.

"We're moving. Same position. Stay close."

She came off the wall. In the dark she found his right shoulder by feel and settled into step behind it, and he thought, briefly, that nine years of keeping someone's books was its own kind of education in staying close to the person who was still upright.

He got her out the west end at 01:03.

[Beat 363]
The dock was a Ferryside loading pier — the smell of lake water and compressed fuel and the sub-audible resonance of the Pulse under the eastern breakwater, its 19Hz signature in his back teeth and the wet wood of the bollards. Dock 14-C was a third-tier berth, a mid-range cargo carrier running a mixed freight manifest, and the purser at the gate was a small man with a clipboard who had spent enough shifts at 03:00 to have opinions about paperwork and none about reasons.

Ms. Tanaka stopped at the dock gate.

She turned and looked at Kyle's forearm, where the field strip had soaked through to dark at the edges, and then looked at his face.

"The contract," she said. "Whatever they paid you—"

"Was fine," Kyle said. "Walk in."

She hesitated. She was the kind of person who had kept someone's books for nine years and decided, one Tuesday, that she was done, and that kind of decision had a weight to it that wasn't just fear.

"I should have left three years ago," she said.

"Probably," Kyle said. "Walk in anyway."

She looked at the saya and at the field strip and at his face one more time, and then she walked in. The purser checked the boarding chit, noted the time — 03:34 — and closed the gate.

The ship ran its pre-cast sequence at 03:40 exactly. Kyle watched the running lights pull away from the dock face, the carrier swinging its bow south toward the lake's open body, the flour on Ms. Tanaka's sleeves already gone with her into the dark of the transit berths below.

He stood at the dock rail for two minutes — long enough to be certain, short enough not to name it. The beacon Pixel had wired under the battery tray had already pinged at 01:11 — she would know where the bike was, and she would do the arithmetic. He called for a medic pickup on the work channel and sat down on a bollard to wait.

His terminal chirped.

STANDING CONTRACT — ADDENDUM.
CLIENT NOTES THE CONTRACT WAS MISPRICED BY THE MARKET.
CLIENT HAS ADJUSTED YOUR HAZARD SCHEDULE. RETROACTIVELY.

He read it twice. He put the terminal away.

[Beat 364]
The medic arrived at 04:15, a quiet woman named Rue with a field kit and the practiced economy of someone who worked the dock district's early hours and had long since learned not to ask about corridors. She cleaned and re-stripped the forearm — the cut ran two inches along the inside, deep but tidy, the kind that would stay interesting for two weeks — confirmed the rib was cracked and not broken, taped it firm, and handed him a small packet of analgesics he would not take before a job and could take now.

"You'll favor the arm," she said.

"I know," Kyle said.

"Two weeks minimum. More if you don't favor it."

"I know that too."

She packed her kit. He paid her rate plus the dock-hour premium, exact. She left without asking what the corridor had looked like. Dock medics learned that skill early and kept it their whole careers.

The rib was going to be the louder bill. The forearm would stiffen by morning, which meant the draw would be measured differently for the next two weeks, which meant every job in that window was a different job than the one he'd budgeted for. He sat on the bollard with the rib doing its precise, persistent accounting and thought about Golan.

He'd passed Golan's block on the route to the dock — the parts dealer on Leavitt who had sold him mesh repair supplies for seven years, a man with a second-floor shop and a yellow security light above the door that Kyle could read from the street at a a quarter mile. He'd looked up out of habit on the approach and the yellow light was off, and through the glass of the street-level lobby Golan had been standing at the inner door watching him, and when Kyle had tried the lobby handle — just the lobby, just the handle — Golan had looked at him for three seconds and not moved.

One door. The corridor's invoice would not be one door.

Fourteen Lotus crew, salaried, professional, hurt badly in a two-foot choke in the dark on a job that had been about one counting-room clerk with flour on her sleeves — that was a debt the city ran on its own schedule, through every vendor and carrier and parts dealer who had a Lotus courtesy arrangement and found the inconvenience of an exception not worth explaining. The GLMZ ran on courtesy arrangements. Kyle had eleven years of them, built into something load-bearing.

He sat on the bollard and let the rib have its opinion and waited to find out how much of it was going to be left in the morning.

[Beat 365]
The lake was grey with the first edge of a dawn that wasn't there yet. South of the breakwater, a running light was very small and getting smaller.

She sailed at 03:40 with the flour still on her sleeves.

(end)

[Beat 366]
On the eighteenth day Kyle went to see the parts man on Ashland about a throttle cable, because the throttle cable on the bike had developed a twelve-millimeter stretch at the barrel end and he had been managing it for three weeks and was done managing it.

He had been going to Cho on Ashland for six years. The man sourced Axiom-era hardware out of a storefront with the footprint of a wide closet, sold nothing he couldn't hold to a light, and had never once been out of stock of anything Kyle needed. The stretch was survivable on surface streets but it put a half-second of slop between intention and execution, and in a corridor the slop was the kind of thing that invoiced.

"Throttle cable," Kyle said.

Cho was already moving. He opened the first drawer, held it to the light, closed it. Opened a second. His hands slowed. He opened a third, stood with his back to Kyle for a moment that went three seconds too long, and turned around wearing the expression of a man who has rehearsed an apology until it sits on his face like a function key.

"Throttle cable," Cho said. "I had some."

"You always have some."

"I'm currently out." He gestured at nothing. "I can check with the Damen yard."

Behind his shoulder, the third drawer was still open an inch. The label was in Cho's own hand: the cable spec, Kyle's spec, written there because Kyle had needed it often enough that Cho had pre-labeled the drawer for efficiency. The drawer was not empty. Kyle could see the shape of coiled cable through the gap.

"Thanks," Kyle said, and walked out, because there was nothing in the conversation worth finishing.

He rode home with the slop still in the cable. The half-second was still there.

[Beat 367]
The carrier who ran his packages — a woman named Freya who operated out of the Bucktown freight level and had never once been late in four years — stopped answering at 07:00 the next morning. Not a queue. Not a delay. Her terminal returned reads without delivering them, the way a door returns a knock without opening.

He had a job running that week: a skip-trace in the Pilsen corridor, a sitting client waiting on a delivery confirmation, and forty Φ on the back end contingent on the documentation landing before a civil filing deadline. He spent two hours routing through Freya's backup and the Loop carrier Ria used, and the Loop carrier told him, politely, that she was running at capacity. He sent the documentation through a walk courier service on Halsted at a surcharge of twelve Φ and it arrived twenty minutes past the filing window. The client paid the base fee and declined the back end, which was their right.

Pixel, at the far end of his table with a casing half-open and her tools fanned, asked without looking up if he'd tried the backup.

"Lost twelve on the courier," Kyle said. "Forty on the filing. Fifty-two total."

She set down her loupe.

"How many doors," she asked.

"Ashland is one. Freya is two. I found four more this morning — fixers who aren't returning calls. A rigger named Goss who normally takes anything with a fee."

"Goss," Pixel said. The word had a flat edge; she had used Goss twice and found him reliable. "That's deliberate."

"Yes."

The kettle went. She filled both cups and set his down, handle first, and went back to the casing without saying what they both already knew: that the invoicing was underway, and they were in the middle of it, and the question was how high the number went.

[Beat 368]
The medic had come through three days ago. Femi reported this the way he reported everything: once, accurate, in sequence.

His left hand had been running three degrees colder than his right for the past six weeks — a compensatory load redistribution the neuretics managed automatically, the kind of adjustment that logged itself without alerting him. He had noticed it. He had not put a clinical word to it until now.

"She left a number," Femi said. He let it sit there for a moment. "Two years at current load. Possibly less, if the rate holds at the rate it's been holding."

Kyle said nothing.

"She said you knew."

"I knew it was a number," Kyle said. "I didn't know which one."

Femi considered this. He looked at the lemon tree. One of the two lemons was starting to color — moving from the pale institutional yellow of new growth toward the deeper yellow of something ready. The other was still green.

"Two years is a number," he said.

Kyle's palm was flat against the wall. He hadn't put it there.

"It's a range," Kyle said. "She said possibly less."

The grow lamp hummed. The coloring lemon turned in some imperceptible draft from the window seam. A Splicer who'd looked at the NeoCortex schematic and done the math: two years. That was the product. That was what the consultation cost, and what it delivered, and there was no arguing the invoice.

[Beat 369]
On the eighteenth morning the crew's logbook had a new entry.

08:44 — KID ON THE STOOP. DRY COAT. CAR MONEY. ASKED FOR BOSS. HANDED AN ENVELOPE AND LEFT. DID NOT TAKE A DUMPLING (OFFERED). SUSPICIOUS.
NOTE FROM RAFTERS: DREW HIS FACE. FOR THE FILE. VERY UNMEMORABLE FACE. THIS IS SUSPICIOUS.

The envelope was heavy card stock, the kind that costs more than the message inside. Inside: a cream rectangle written in a single hand-brushed line above a stamped impression — five petals, open, one blossom, no name. The line read:

BRING THE SWORD.

Below the blossom: an address in the Narrows and a time, 20:00.

Kyle put it on the table next to Sable's card and went to find clean clothes.

[Beat 370]
The Narrows announced itself by what it did not have.

Every streetlight worked. He counted the first three and kept counting until the fixture rhythm stopped mattering because it never broke. In West Town you learned the patch-map by season — the dead stretch on Milwaukee, the dark third post that the block had rerouted its foot traffic around since last autumn without complaint, the way the city and its residents had arrived at a working arrangement with the absence of maintained infrastructure. Here the lights ran continuous and even. The geometry of sustained attention pulled something up the spine: not threat, but the recognition of an institution that had been keeping something for a long time, the kind that did not need to announce it.

Gutters clear. No tags on the support pillars. The corner positions staffed by men and women who stood the way staff stood, not sentinels: they observed without performing observation, occupied space without defending it, clocked Kyle on entry and returned their attention to the mid-distance without haste. Nobody begged. Nobody moved at angles.

He found the address two blocks in: a covered arcade between two building faces, open at both ends, roofed in mesh strung with fixed-spectrum strips close enough to touch. In steel collars bolted to the structural uprights, eleven cherry trees grew.

He stopped.

They were blooming — white to a pink with blood in it, not the faded pink of forced spring stock but something deliberate, a color that took horticulture years to breed into the wrong month. The lights were buried units, throwing illumination upward through the collar roots, catching each blossom from underneath the way a face looks in held light — present, specific, briefly eternal. Petals had come loose and lay on the wet concrete in clusters, small and exact.

He stood there with petals settling on his coat and let the trees be what they were: engineered, expensive, bought with every tariff Lotus charged the Narrows since whoever planted the first collar, and beautiful anyway, both of those things at once and neither canceling the other.

Children ran circuits under the arcade, six of them, screaming on the inhale at the specific pitch of a body moving faster than it can account for. Nobody hushed them.

[Beat 371]
The ramen-ya was eight stools and a cedar counter and a door that stood open.

He counted the stools on the way in. Hand-planed cedar gone the gold of long handling, a counter that knew what it was because people had brought their elbows to it for enough years to tell it. The cook stood behind it with the focused economy of a person who has no wasted motions left because they shed them years ago. The other stools held four people: a man in his forties with a collapsed baton at his hip, a woman with a vibro-edge unit in a shoulder rig, a younger man Kyle read as wire by the way he held his neck, and an empty jacket at the far end that had left a cup and a short blade on the counter beside it.

Weapons worn. Nothing checked at the door but behavior.

Kyle hung his coat on the hook at the entrance — the only hook, placed by someone who understood guests — and sat on the remaining stool. The cook looked at him once, turned to the pot, and began building a bowl.

Kyle had not ordered. The cook did not ask.

The bowl arrived in three stages: the broth first, the color of good age and the smell of something started days earlier and tended since; then the noodles, hand-pulled, landed with exactly enough time to finish their cooking in the broth; then the fat, rendered and precise, a thin lens on the surface, and from somewhere behind the counter the cook deposited a half-round of soft-boiled egg split with a knife held sideways so the yolk stayed whole.

Kyle ate the first mouthful.

He understood immediately what the bowl was for. Not hospitality in the ordinary sense — an argument, made in fat and salt and twenty years of practice. The argument was: we make things correctly here. It was probably the point of the bowl.

He was most of the way through it when the door changed.

[Beat 372]
The Branch Manager came through the doorway at the pace of someone who had walked this route for a long time and did not need to announce it — umbrella furled and tucked under his arm, mid-sixties, a suit without a single concession to fashion, not old-fashioned and not plain but the kind selected once by a man who understood fabric and had not needed to revisit the question since. He was half a head shorter than Kyle and had the stillness of a man who had never needed to take up space to fill it.

Everyone at the counter got an inch and a half taller when he came in.

Not visibly. Not in any way you could name a number to. The body registered it before the brain confirmed it: the man who ran this territory had arrived, and the room was better for it. He sat at the empty jacket's stool, claimed his cup, and drank from it like a man resuming a sentence he'd stepped away from.

He looked at Kyle's face.

"You took the clerk out," the Branch Manager said. "Fourteen people in that corridor. Our people. Several of them went home significantly the worse for the meeting."

Kyle waited. His ribs registered the change in posture.

"The contract was legitimate," the Branch Manager said. "Your conduct was correct. Our crew engaged first." He turned his cup once in both hands. "We pay for what we break. It is most of what we are. The hold lifted as of this evening — the seven doors, the carrier on the Spine, the parts man on Ashland." He paused. "Cho is easily embarrassed about these things. He has the cable for you. With apologies, priced at list. The apologies are real. The pricing is how we mean them."

Kyle ate the last mouthful of the bowl.

"I appreciate it," he said.

"The fifty-two," the Branch Manager said, without inflection.

Kyle looked at him.

"The filing gap. The courier surcharge. Cho's hold cost you more than he cost you in stock." The Branch Manager set his cup down. "The apology accounts for the doors. The doors account for everything behind them. Priced at list."

He reached into his coat and set a small envelope on the cedar. Not touching Kyle's space. Close enough to acknowledge.

"Good?" the Branch Manager asked, and looked at the bowl.

"Best in the city."

"That," said the Branch Manager, with the faint satisfaction of a man whose argument has landed, "is probably the point of the bowl." He put two fingers on the counter's edge, thinking. "I have a second piece of business. Not a condition. The freeze lifts either way."

Kyle waited.

"I'm offering you a position on payroll. House colors. Infrastructure — parts, carrying, the counters in every territory we hold, a legal team that files on your behalf and bills to us. In five years, if the work is what I believe the work is, a chair at this counter with your name in the wood." He touched the cedar's edge with two fingers. "The cedar decides, eventually. It keeps certain names."

"No," Kyle said. "I don't wear colors."

The Branch Manager did not look surprised. He looked like a man reaching a confirmation he had arrived expecting.

"The clerk who sat in that seat nine years ago said exactly that," he said. "In exactly that register. She had been doing piece work in the Narrows for three years. She said no, I don't wear colors, and then she sat down anyway. It took eleven months and one specific kind of trouble she didn't want to carry alone. She runs the Bucktown counting room now." He picked up his cup. "The offer stands. Standing offers are how we say please."

Kyle looked at the counter. Cedar gone gold with handling across years. He could see where the grain ran different under the finish — the older darker sections at the older stools, the pale sections at the new ones. His stool was neither new nor old. Somewhere in the middle of the sequence.

The standing offer was a mechanism, and he understood its geometry: he would come back for the bowl. When he came back, the offer would be standing, because standing offers were exactly that. The courtesy was not a courtesy. It was infrastructure.

"I'll remember it," Kyle said.

"That's all it asks," said the Branch Manager, and rose, and took his cup to the far end of the counter, and the room did its an inch and a half again.

[Beat 373]
The togishi was at the counter's far end, between the wire's stool and the wall, working the cook's knives.

Kyle had not noticed him arrive, or perhaps he had always been there — one of those people who occupy space so completely they become part of the scene before you count them. Small, older than the Branch Manager by ten years, with the hands of a man whose profession was the repair of attention. He ran the whetstone along a blade with the patience of someone for whom this was not maintenance but reading: each pass an inquiry.

The cook's knives were good blades. The togishi treated them the way you treated something that would outlast you.

Kyle watched for a while, because watching was the only polite thing.

"One Φ," he said eventually.

The togishi looked up. He looked at Silence's saya.

"One Φ for what?" he asked.

"Your read on the blade. One minute."

"Togishi does not work by the minute," the togishi said, and held out his hand.

Kyle paid first, because that was how it was done, and laid Silence across the man's palms.

The togishi held it without drawing, hands flat, feeling the balance point travel through his grip. Then he drew it, slowly, and held it to the light from outside — the cherry tree light, the pink-white coming through the arcade opening and falling soft on the blade's face.

He looked at it for a long time.

"Kept correctly," he said. "Good geometry. Good edge. No maker's mark." He turned the blade and examined the back. "No file history. Someone has worked this blade for a long time and left no record of working it." He looked up. "A blade with no parents. Either made entirely outside the guild tradition, or made inside it and the documentation deliberately removed, which is a different category of story."

"Which do you think?" Kyle asked.

"I think it is in good order," the togishi said, and returned it hilt-first. "I think it is also the loneliest piece of steel I have handled in forty years, and I have handled funeral swords."

Kyle took it, sheathed it, and said nothing, because nothing was the right thing to say and he was still learning how to say it correctly.

The togishi went back to the cook's knife. Kyle paid for the bowl — 8Φ, which was two over the going rate, which was probably also the point — put on his coat, and walked out under the trees.

The petals came down slow and even in the lit air. A child ran past him at full career, screaming on the inhale, trailing a laugh behind her like a cord she hadn't noticed she was paying out.

[Beat 374]
He took the long way back.

The city reopened in the arithmetic: seven counters, one carrier, the parts man on Ashland who would have the cable waiting when he arrived in the morning. A third of the operational surface back in service, priced in apologies at list, plus the envelope in his coat covering the fifty-two.

Cost: a standing offer he would now have to keep refusing. A bowl he would come back for, which meant the stool again, which meant the offer standing when he arrived, which was the whole geometry of it. The Branch Manager had not built the courtesy as courtesy. He had built it as a room you kept returning to voluntarily, a door that stayed open because you opened it yourself.

His terminal chirped.

STANDING CONTRACT — STATUS NOTE.
CLIENT ATTEMPTED TO LOCATE THE RAMEN-YA ON THE ASHGRAVE CORRIDOR LEDGER.
CLIENT QUERIED: COUNTER, CEDAR. FEE STRUCTURE. VARIABLE OCCUPANCY.
CLIENT QUERIED: TOGISHI, LICENSED. NARROWS DISTRICT. ONE-TIME SERVICE.
BOTH QUERIES RETURNED: NOT ON LEDGER.
CLIENT IS UNSETTLED. ENJOY THE NOODLES.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT PRICED THE BOWL AT 8Φ.
ADDENDUM: STANDARD BOWL, THIS DISTRICT = 6Φ.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT NOTES THE DELTA.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT FINDS THE DELTA CORRECT.

Kyle read it twice on the walkway under the last of the trees, petals on the screen and the Pulse humming through the tier below him.

He put the terminal away.

Three blocks out, at the Spine crossing, a woman in a gray work coat spoke low to the man beside her — two syllables, the shape of a name, gone before he could catch it. The man said: "Assessment pending. Nothing moves until Mira signs off."

Kyle walked past without slowing, because slowing was a tell and tells were expensive, and the price of tonight had been high enough already.

The freeze was lifted. The audition still owed itself.

He went home under the lights.

[Beat 375]
The rain had been making itself at home since midnight, and by 02:14 it had moved the furniture and settled in. West Town at that hour was a series of arrangements between the streetlights and the water — what the light kept, what the dark swallowed, who was standing in which column. Kyle walked north on Wood because Wood ran straight and he had developed a personal affection for straight streets after the corridor two nights ago, where straight had meant fourteen crew coming at him in sequence instead of simultaneously, and the difference had mattered.

The rib talked when he twisted. It had been talking since day one of the corridor job and it had grown, in the manner of unwanted conversationalists, from a whisper to an opinion. The rest of the damage was administrative — bruising that had resolved from red to the institutional yellow-green of a problem being processed, a shoulder that worked at ninety percent and stopped asking questions at eighty. Corridor wounds. They were their own category.

He counted the bracket at two blocks out, because the discipline counted things.

One high: the thermal window on the third floor of the print shop, glass gone just cold enough to be wrong, a warmth inside that was not the building's warmth. Someone with a visual line to the whole cross-street, stationary, patient. A watcher running a rubric.

One in the doorway across from the laundromat, standing the way a person stands when they have been standing a long time and are being extremely casual about the fact that their weight was on the wrong foot.

One at the corner that Kyle was approaching, pretending to read something on a dead terminal.

One down the block, far enough to be the anchor.

One on the rooftop of the tailor's shop, which he got from the rain pattern — the way rain fell differently where something stood in it.

One he didn't have yet, which meant one he wasn't supposed to have yet, which meant the sixth position was the runner and the runner was already in place behind him, because a runner doesn't wait at the bracket, a runner waits at the exit.

Six positions. One of them was designed to leave.

Kyle did the arithmetic on the stun drone while he was still a full block away, which was the margin the arithmetic required. The drone would be somewhere overhead, maybe two, held back until he committed to the cross-street where the geometry closed. The doorway, the corner man, the anchor: a standard closing pattern, textbook, three points. The rooftop was the override position, the one that could move. And the watcher in the thermal window was the one running the rubric, which meant this was being graded.

He put both hands in his coat pockets — left on the saya's mouth, right closed around air, not Cacophony, because Cacophony was for when the arithmetic came out different — and walked into the bracket at normal pace, because a man who changes his pace when he spots surveillance tells the surveillance everything.

The cross-street smell was wet concrete and the ghost of a food stall that hadn't been there since afternoon, and from somewhere up the block a turret cat was sitting on a window ledge watching the rain the way the dedicated watched anything.

[Beat 376]
The drone dropped at 02:16, sixteen inches short.

Kyle knew what a stun drone at full discharge looked like and sounded like - he'd met three, paid for the education twice, the third time had been someone else's problem - and what this one did was not that. The discharge hit the wet pavement in a cone that stopped at his boot heel, the arc tracing the surface water and then dying, the sound a half-note lower than a real discharge, the light forty percent of what the specs said. The drone itself held at ten feet and cycled immediately for a second pass that it did not make.

An audition crew throttles its emitters. They weren't trying to drop him. They were testing how he reacted to a charge that looked real and wasn't, and they were doing it with maybe two seconds between the dropped drone and the first of the ground crew moving, which meant the test had a graded response window. Somewhere nearby an Exo was sitting very still, body left behind in a doorway or a parked car, eyes borrowed from the drone - watching his face, watching his hands, running the rubric.

Kyle did not move, which was one of the answers. He tilted his head thirty degrees and looked at the drone, which was the more interesting one.

"Evening," he said, to the drone, which could not respond, and to the bracket in general, which could.

The corner man came first - young, moving fast, and furious in the way that people are furious when they're twenty-two and loyal and someone they respect is being studied by a stranger. He had an arm. Not chrome, not aftermarket - the real thing, a full replacement starting mid-humerus, the kind the body rejection catalogs listed at eight thousand Φ on a good year, this one running higher because it had secondary-market augments in the forearm, tactile-override arrays, pressure-indexed grip. Kyle clocked the manufacturer's seal at the shoulder joint as the young man closed the last ten feet and swung.

KT. That was the quality of chrome: Kinetix TerraForm - the market name for the Warptech Systems division that did precision replacement arms for industrial accidents and people who wanted to be more than industrial accidents. The emergency lockout was a three-finger combination on the exterior surface of the wrist, because the certification body required manual override accessible to a third party in case the wearer went into medical distress. Kyle had a thirty-second window once the arm was in range.

He took the swing on his forearm, stepped inside it, took a bruise he had expected, and found the lockout sequence.

The arm stopped.

Not dropped - locked, mid-extension, fingers splayed, caught in the gesture of a blow that wasn't landing, a chrome hand held out like an offering. KT said something that required punctuation Kyle wouldn't put on paper, and Kyle said:

"Kinetix TerraForm, the emergency medical lockout is a liability clause, not a warranty gap. You should ask Lotus to bill Kinetix for the upgrade, at a minimum."

He let go. The arm unlocked. KT stepped back with the expression of a man who has just been told something humiliating, true, and expensive, in that order.

[Beat 377]
Rook came from the doorway, which was the approach pattern Kyle had expected, and Rook was what his name advertised: broad-shouldered, honest in the way that physical scale makes honest, moving with the economy of a man who'd spent years not starting things he couldn't finish. The BCI port was at the temple, left side, the older Meridian Conduct series — Kyle knew them from the identifier housing, a flattened oval of matte gray that sat proud at the skull, the kind that had a hardware reset built into the external casing because the early Conduct units had overloaded and needed physical intervention to stop.

Hardware protects itself. The logic is baked into certification: a BCI that could be locked by software could be locked by hostile software, and hostile software was a category that the 2218 standards body had found intolerable. The Conduct series had a mechanical failsafe: a physical shock to the housing would trigger the reset routine and put the wearer into a five-second proprioceptive blank.

Rook had his left arm coming up for a guard, so his left side was closed, which meant Kyle had to work the right, which was the longer route but not by much.

Silence came off the hip for the first time.

Not to cut — to use the pommel, which weighed 340 grams and had a striking radius Kyle had measured through eleven years of arguments with people's equipment. The pommel found the BCI housing at the exact angle the array had been pricing, and Rook blinked twice, stepped left when he'd intended right, put his hand out and found the laundromat door instead of Kyle, and sat down with his back to it, working through the reset sequence with his eyes half-focused on a wall that wasn't cooperating.

"Five seconds," Kyle told him. "You're fine."

"Yeah," Rook said, from somewhere inside his own reset. "I know."

Silence went back. The rib made its opinion known about the draw-and-replace motion. Kyle filed the opinion and moved.

[Beat 378]
Nines was the one he'd flagged last, which was Nines's job — industrial wrist brace, pneumatic assist, the kind the freight yards used for sustained-load operators and the kind that developed its own opinions about direction once the pneumatic channel pressurized wrong. The brace locked along a single axis to distribute load, which meant that if you could intercept the lock while the pneumatic was fighting the frame, the brace's own assist force would do the work for you.

The anchor position had held through the drone discharge and both entries, which was professional. Nines waited until Kyle was dealing with the right side and then came across the street at a pace that accounted for rain, which was careful, and led with the braced arm, which was honest.

Kyle let him commit the extension, took the inside line, and used Nines's own weight to put the braced arm behind Nines's back. The pneumatic fought the frame, the frame locked, and Nines did a complete inventory of the street's surface in about four seconds while Kyle held the brace at the angle that kept the joint disagreeing with itself.

"Locked out," Kyle said, releasing. The brace cycled and freed. Nines sat with it a moment, one knee on the wet pavement, and did not say anything, which was the professional response.

The drone had gone for altitude. The rooftop position hadn't moved, because the rooftop position was the override and knew when it wasn't needed. Somewhere behind Kyle, the sixth position — the runner — was having the simplest task on the street: waiting to carry the news. Kyle put his back to the runner deliberately, which was the correct signal.

You've earned the exit. Take it.

He heard Dex's footsteps going east at a run, the way a young man runs when he's been given the best part of any job, which is the part where he gets to say what happened.

[Beat 379]
The thermal window on the third floor opened at 02:23.

Mira came down, not from the building's front door but from the fire access on the east side, and she came alone, which meant she'd watched the whole arc and decided it was over, which meant her rubric was complete. She was 130 feet out when Kyle first put her properly into the read: medium height, operational gear that was functional first and second and didn't bother with a third priority, a carry stance that said the piece on her hip was neither a prop nor an afterthought.

She stopped at twenty-five feet. Standard conversation distance for someone who wasn't trying to demonstrate indifference to it.

"Kinetix's lockout is contractor-certified," she said, without preamble, because she had been watching and her rubric included what had been said. "The arm costs eleven thousand two hundred Φ with the augment array."

"I know the price," Kyle said.

"I know you know." Her voice had the tightness of someone updating a rubric that hadn't expected to need updating, not the tightness of distress. Those were different things and the distinction mattered. "Rook's port was a Meridian Conduct first-series, they issued the mechanical-reset advisory in 2221, he's been meaning to get the housing seated deeper."

"Add the seating charge to the bill."

She looked at him for a long moment the way a person looks at something that's behaving slightly differently than the model predicted.

Then she drew and fired. One round, twenty-five feet, into the upper chest, because she had watched the corridor reports the same way any professional watched someone's recent work, and she knew what was there and what it would cost.

The mesh took it. The percussion distributed across the upper thorax in the way of a very decisive conversation with a door — full-contact, brief, leaving both parties informed. Kyle took three breaths that were more deliberate than usual. The rib weighed in at volume.

Mira watched him keep walking, one step, two, the stride unchanged, and she dropped the weapon against her thigh and then lowered it to the wet pavement. She left it there, muzzle toward the drain.

"That was an audition," she said.

"I worked that out," Kyle said.

"Cacophony has five rounds. You didn't pull it."

"The audition was for the blade and the body. Five rounds, zero fired. I'll note it for the record."

She almost smiled. It didn't quite complete. "Freelance roster, West Town and adjacent. Lotus-territory contracts through your fixer, who I assume is the Húlijing outfit. Carrier rates, standard."

"No further crews."

"No further crews," she agreed, and that was the one line that meant they were actually negotiating and not just reciting terms. She had the authority to offer it and she'd been expecting him to ask. "The arrangement is provisional, sixty-day reassessment. Standard for new roster."

"Your crew's hardware goes on Lotus's account," Kyle said. "The arm, the port, the brace. List price. You billed them to an audition; they walk home whole or there's no arrangement."

The tightness in her voice made one small adjustment. Not surprise — recalculation.

"I can authorize the arm," she said. "The port and the brace I need to clear."

"Clear them. Tonight. I'll be at Chen's at seven-thirty for the confirmation."

"They'd have shot you for free, you know," Mira said, and that was the one human line, the one that wasn't rubric, the one she'd been carrying through the whole arc of the bracket and had decided she was allowed to say now that the terms were closing.

"I know," Kyle said. "Tell Lotus their hardware is better than their audit of its own vulnerabilities. That's a courtesy, not an invoice."

She reached into the inner pocket of her coat and set the roster marker on the planter rim at her right: a lacquered oval, deep red with a gold five-petal blossom pressed into its surface, the size of a thumb, and heavy, heavier than a thing that size had any reason to be. The weight of a formal thing. Kyle picked it up.

"Any Lotus counter in the city seats you," Mira said, back on professional ground, the human line filed and done. "You pay carrier rates like everyone on the roster. The bowl's better than the politics."

"It usually is."

She retrieved her piece from the wet pavement, cleared and holstered it, and turned to go. Three steps. Then, over her shoulder, into the rain, without turning back:

"The Narrows desk bought your file last year, freelancer. You should ask the stationer who ELSE subscribes."

Her footsteps went north and the bracket dissolved: Rook walking his reset out in a slow circle, KT flexing the arm through its range and swearing quietly at the manufacturer's specifications, Nines working the brace through two full cycles to confirm the pneumatic had reset. Professionals filing their after-action. Kyle stood on the wet cross-street with the lacquered marker in one hand and a bruise over his upper thorax that was going to have a great deal to say over the next three days and watched them all go.

The turret cat on the window ledge had not moved. It had the institutional patience of something that had been watching long before Kyle arrived and would be watching long after he left.

WIN: a third of the city formally reopened, carrier rates, one lacquered marker that weighs more than it should. COST: sixty days on a reassessment clock, one mesh bruise, and a name in the Narrows' subscription list that he is going to have to do something about. OUTSTANDING: confirm hardware authorization, seven-thirty, Chen's counter.

He took the long way home.

[Beat 380]
The Zone 4 displacement notice was taped to the door of the unit two floors below him.

He passed it on the stairwell: standard format, district seal, the specific bureaucratic typography that had been unchanged since before he was constructed. *Temporary Relocation Required — Active Schism Boundary Determination Pending.* Below that, in smaller type: *Estimated Duration: 2–180 days. Compensation: see Schedule C, §11(b).* Below that, a QR code for the relocation portal.

He had seen enough of these to know what Schedule C, section 11(b) contained without scanning the code. The displacement compensation covered three days of temporary housing at district-standard rate. The "estimated duration" in his experience was never under ten days. The gap between the three days of compensation and the actual duration was absorbed entirely by the person standing in front of the notice.

He continued up the stairwell.

The unit was quiet. No light under the door. Either they had already left, or they were waiting to see if the determination came back favorable. Both were reasonable calculations. The district issued the notice and posted the advisory and published the QR code, and the schism did not consult any of these documents, and the person whose door now had a notice on it was somewhere in the space between all three of those facts.

There was no wrong answer to a problem that had no solution. Only management. Only the specific equanimity of a city that had been managing for long enough that the management had its own forms.

[Beat 381]
At 02:41, between the Pulse strut and the laundry on Damen, his terminal chirped. The work one.

CONTRACT UPDATE — ASSET SCHEDULE, SUBJECT: KYLE.
CLIENT HAS NOTED THE EXECUTION OF THE LOTUS ROSTER ARRANGEMENT, EFFECTIVE 02:23.
CLIENT HAS REVIEWED THE TERMS.
CLIENT HAS APPENDED THE MARKER TO THE SUBJECT'S ASSET SCHEDULE.
VALUATION: ONE BOWL, RECURRING.
ADDENDUM: THE AUDITION CREW'S MEDICAL CLAIM (ARM, PORT, BRACE) HAS BEEN FILED WITH LOTUS ACCOUNTS.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT ADVISES THAT "RECURRING" IS THE LOAD-BEARING WORD.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT ALSO NOTES THE RIB. THE RIB HAS BEEN NOTED SINCE DAY ONE. COMPENSATION IN SUBSEQUENT POSITIONS: OBSERVABLE.

Kyle read the addenda in the rain, the marker in his coat pocket against the old folded plate cover he still carried from the alley, one rectangle of lacquer and one of steel, two different proofs that the city occasionally paid what it owed.

He typed into the amendment field, which he was doing more often:

NOTED ABOUT THE RIB. MIND YOUR OWN ARCHITECTURE.

The reply came back in six seconds.

ACKNOWLEDGED.
GRATUITY WITHHELD — ITEMIZED: IMPERTINENCE.

He put the terminal away and walked the last four blocks with the Pulse hum coming up through the concrete and the turret cats watching from their ledges and the rain making its arrangements with the city's lights, and thought about the Narrows desk, and about a man with half-glasses and clean hands and a drawer marked CLIENTS, UNSOURCED, and about the fact that there was now a second subscriber.

The rib had opinions about the pace. Kyle let it talk.

That was tomorrow's problem.

[Beat 382]
Pixel knocked on his door, which had happened four times in four years and meant the world had changed shape in some way she intended to explain.

She had a tablet under her arm and her field coat on — the one she wore to jobs, not to benches — and she walked past him into 2F and put the tablet on his kitchen counter where the kettle should have been.

"I have a contract," she said. "I'm crewing it. I need muscle that listens, and you're the only muscle I know whose listening I've personally calibrated."

On the tablet, in somebody's shaky handheld video, a machine the size of a four-story building was eating a row house in the Ashgrave fringe. Eating was the word. It took the structure down a bite at a time, unhurried, and behind it the rubble of the last block stood in three neat sorted piles — ferrous, mineral, everything else.

"That's an Axiom D-class," Pixel said. "Demolition and reclamation automaton. It woke up nine days ago, and it is very good at its job, and its job is now four hundred feet from two thousand people who live in the direction it's chewing."

Kyle watched the machine take a chimney off a brownstone with the care of something intending to file the bricks separately. "Whose is it?"

"Nobody's. Axiom Industrial died thirty years ago. No owner, no kill-codes, no support line." She tapped the tablet. "Arcturus left a cone. The residents pooled what the fringe could pool: 312Φ, held in a biscuit tin by a woman named Auntie Som, who sent the video to a parts man, who sent it to me — because everyone in the salvage trade knows the only person in the GLMZ who still speaks Axiom bus protocol like a living language works at a bench in West Town."

"Three hundred and twelve," Kyle said.

"Split six ways it's insulting. I'm taking it anyway, and so are you."

"I know."

"You haven't heard the plan."

"I've heard the pay," Kyle said. "The plan's not going to make it worse."

[Beat 383]
The crew's morning log arrived under the door at 07:00:

07:00 — SHIFT CHANGE. NO INCIDENTS. NOODLE CART (CONFIRMED NOODLE).
07:04 — PIXEL LEFT WITH THE FIELD COAT. BOSS'S DOOR OPEN A LONG TIME. FILING AS: DEVELOPMENT.
07:45 — LOG SUSPENSION REQUESTED. REASON: "WHAT JOB?" — B.
SUPPLEMENTAL (07:46): WE WILL HOLD WEST TOWN. THIS LOG IS THE RECORD. — B.

Kyle folded it into the drawer of things he had not decided about, and went downstairs to find the rest of his morning.

[Beat 384]
He ate at Mrs. Chen's at 08:00 — two bowls, schedule kept — and his work terminal chirped between the first and second.

A new posting on the standing contract. Not a task. Nobody had filed a report about the D-class; the entity had found it on its own.

STANDING CONTRACT — ADDENDUM.
CLIENT ADVISES THE UNIT KEEPS A SHIFT. 0600 TO 2200.
CLIENT RESPECTS THIS.

No scope, no fee, no direction. Kyle set the terminal face-down and finished the bowl while he thought about both readings, found them both sound.

"You're thinking loud," Mrs. Chen said, setting down the change. She put a fish ball in his pocket without comment, and that was that.

[Beat 385]
"You're not going near—" Kyle started, and Pixel set her cup down with a click that ended the sentence.

"Say the rest of that word and my rate doubles," she said. Not heated. The voice she used on a fastener that thought it was load-bearing. "The D-class runs a pre-2200 Axiom control bus. No remote interface — Axiom didn't trust radio, they were right, they died before anyone retrofitted one. The bus has one physical maintenance spur, on the chassis, where I will be standing, because the alternative is nobody closes this thing down and the city loses a neighborhood at the speed of careful chewing."

She turned the tablet around. A schematic, hand-annotated in her tight engineer's print — she had owned this before the job existed.

"I have wanted to talk to one of these since I was nine," she said. "You are not protecting me from it. You are buying me ninety seconds in front of it. That's the job, samurai. Price it like one."

Kyle looked at the schematic and at his neighbor of four years in her field coat, and did the thing the moment called for.

"Standard rate," he said. "Yours is double. Specialist on site."

"Correct," Pixel said.

[Beat 386]
The crew came together in thirty hours, because crews came together fast when the job was real and the pay was a joke.

Echo took eyes and comms — high ground, voice in five skulls, never wrong about a sightline. Stash took transport: a flatbed he refused to explain, cases packed for every door in the brief.

The new hands came from the parts man and one of Echo's old ledgers. Gantry was a rigger out of the lakefront cranes — big, slow-moving, maybe forty words a day, addressed suspended loads in the second person like they had opinions worth respecting. Furnace was a demolition op with a thermal lance and the cheerfulness of a man whose tool solved most arguments; he had burned scrap in the Ashgrave yards for a decade.

Five of them and Pixel, in the back of Stash's flatbed, on a contract worth less than the fuel.

"For the record," Furnace said, happily, "I've never worked a job this stupid."

"Stick around," Echo said, from everyone's skull at once.

[Beat 387]
They walked the eaten blocks first, because Pixel wanted to see its work.

The thing was tidy. That was the part that climbed into Kyle and stayed. The rubble of nine days stood in sorted piles at exact intervals — ferrous, mineral, organics — each pile compacted square, each street swept after, the lamppost bases unscrewed and laid in rows like cutlery. A city block taken apart with the intention of accounting for every piece.

In the middle of the fourth block, the piles stopped mattering, because the machine was there.

Eleven feet at the shoulder. Four legs, two boom arms, a hull of sintered gray monocoque printed in one piece — no plates, no rivets, no panel lines, no seams at all. Studding the hull every foot sat low black discs, and around each disc the rain steamed faintly off the surface.

"Arc-fence," Pixel said. "Anti-tamper. Capacitive plates, the hull's one big charge bank. Anything conductive touches the skin, the skin touches back. Forty thousand volts of touches back."

She looked at Silence's saya, then at Kyle.

"Your sword is a key for doors, samurai. That thing has no doors. No seams, no cable runs, nothing surface-routed — Axiom buried it all a foot deep in structure. There is nothing on that hull for an edge to mean. Steel and carbon — conductive. You touch it, you're not cutting, you're grounding. It will cook your neuretics inside your skull, and the burn scar will be the shape of your own blade."

"So I don't touch it," Kyle said.

"You don't touch it. Tonight your job is everything that isn't it."

[Beat 388]
It kept a schedule. The D-class worked a shift: 0600 to 2200, and at 0300 it walked back to the dead Axiom yard and docked against a transformer pylon to charge, the way it had every night for nine days, the way — Pixel said, quietly — it had probably been doing in 2196, before somebody powered it down mid-rotation and walked away.

"It docks, the arc-fence drops to maintenance levels, the spur goes live," she said. "Axiom units safe themselves for service. Ninety seconds on the spur, handshake with the bus, find out why it's awake."

"And if the handshake goes wrong?" Echo asked.

"Then it objects," Pixel said. "And the five of you get to be more interesting than I am."

[Beat 389]
The Axiom yard at 0250 was rust and sodium dark. They took positions: Echo on the crane cab; Stash at the gate, engine warm; Gantry in the rigging of the old loading frame, where two hundred tons of abandoned shipping container hung from cables she had spent the afternoon talking to; Furnace at the transformer with his lance dark; Kyle on the ground, where the geometry said trouble would have to pass.

At 0301, the ground learned a rhythm. Four legs, each footfall arriving through the boots a half-second before the sound.

At the yard's edge, the D-class stopped. A dead pedestrian crossing — two lamp poles, the ghost of paint — and the machine stood at it and waited, exactly as long as a signal cycle nobody had powered since before Kyle was born. Then it crossed.

Nobody on comms said anything. There was nothing to say that the waiting hadn't said.

[Beat 390]
It docked. The boom arms folded. The hull's black discs dimmed one by one, a contactor closed somewhere inside, and the transformer pylon began, gently, to sing.

"Maintenance state," Pixel said, already moving. "Fence is down to tickle. Ninety seconds. Count me."

She crossed the yard at a walk — running hands shake — stepped over Arcturus's cone, and put her bare hand flat on the hull the way she put it between Kyle's shoulder blades before trusting a readout.

"Hello, you absurd old thing," she said, too quiet for the channel, and found the spur.

Kyle counted. The whole yard counted. The handshake chattered in Pixel's earpiece, Axiom bus protocol going both ways for the first time in thirty years, and at second forty-one of ninety a power flicker rolled through the dead grid — the pylon's note wavered a quarter-tone — and the D-class's watchdog, which had kept this machine alive through three decades of abandonment, made the only decision a watchdog knows.

Every disc on the hull lit at once.

"Off the chassis—" Echo said, and the machine stood up out of its dock with Pixel's spur cable still live, and the night went to work.

[Beat 391]
Hatches the monocoque had not admitted to opened along the lower hull, and the skirmishers came out: knee-high yard-keepers, six-legged, the maintenance swarm repurposed by the watchdog into a perimeter, two dozen flowing off the chassis like spiders off a kicked log.

"Pixel's clear of the envelope," Echo called. "Cable's still in the spur — if she gets back on it she can finish. Kyle—"

"I see them."

The skirmishers were everything the hull was not: seamed, jointed, cabled, built to be repaired — built, in other words, to be taken apart. Kyle drew Silence and went into them.

It was geometry in the dark. Hip actuator on the first, where the cable loom crossed the joint; the second hooked low at his ankle and he gave it the boot it wanted, took the weight onto the planted leg, opened its lid along the dorsal service line. Two down. Third and fourth came together and he made them arrive separately — a half-step of timing that cost a slash across his forearm from a manipulator claw, shallow, hot, catalogued.

"Four," Echo counted. "Twenty left."

Gantry's voice arrived for the first time all night, slow as a load swinging: "Heads down in the south lane."

The container came off the loading frame on released cables, two hundred tons landing where Gantry had been promising it all afternoon, and the south half of the skirmisher line stopped existing as a line.

[Beat 392]
Furnace tried the hull once, because professionals confirm.

The lance ran eleven thousand degrees at the tip. He laid it against the D-class's foreleg for a full three-count, and when he pulled it away the monocoque wore a glassy scorch the size of a dinner plate. Not a hole. A polish.

"That's me out," Furnace said, packing the lance. "Structure's sintered the whole way through. You'd want a week and a mining permit. Also it's a third rail."

The foreleg turned. A boom arm came around, unhurried — everything the big machine did was unhurried, which was somehow worse — and swept the slag barrier away with Furnace already running, and Kyle was there, between, doing the thing his body had decided before his neuretics could file an objection.

He parried.

Steel-CNT met monocoque for a quarter-second, and the arc-fence did exactly what Pixel had priced. The world went white and sideways. The discharge took him off his feet, through the bones, out the heels, and the ground arrived like an invoice. His neuretics browned out whole: the overlay gone, the counts gone, the array's quiet arithmetic gone, eleven years of second heartbeat slapped silent, and Kyle lay in the rust mud as just a man, numb arm, blade still locked in his fist, the smell of his own scorched glove going up his nose.

Silence was unmarked. Steel has no opinion. The burn across his palm was the exact shape of the wrapped grip, printed in.

He got up, because the count in his head still worked even with the hardware down, and the count said Pixel needed thirty more seconds.

[Beat 393]
"Kyle's hit," Echo said, voice going flat the way trusted crews go flat. "He's up. Moving. Pixel, status."

"Back on the spur." Her voice had the bench in it. "Handshake's holding. It remembers me from forty seconds ago. Watchdog's split — half on perimeter, half on me. Keep the perimeter busy."

Kyle kept the perimeter busy. Without the overlay he read the skirmishers with his eyes alone — the settle before a lunge, the way the swarm's flank packed when it meant to push — and Silence went through service seams and actuator looms by feel and training, the discipline carrying the body the way it had been built to when the body was all there was. Five more came apart. Stash's flatbed came through the gate at second seventy, plow edge down, and herded the remnant. Gantry's second container landed where the survivors had agreed to regroup.

"Seventy-five seconds," Echo said. "Pixel."

"Almost. It's showing me something."

[Beat 394]
The NeoCortex registered heat at 04:03.

Not threat-heat. Not the calibrated warmth of a mesh in standard operation. The specific kind that meant the hardware had crossed the line and was logging the overage and would present the bill later in whatever denomination later had available. Kyle registered it the way he registered the skirmisher fragment still in his forearm: a line item, filed, not the primary problem.

The primary problem was thirteen remaining skirmishers across a yard that had no cover worth the name, and Pixel on a spur cable thirty feet from the D-class's hull, and the watchdog's perimeter half still processing where he'd gone. He could buy her the time if he spent the hardware right. He had spent hardware before.

He ran it.

At 04:03:11 the timestamp stopped.

At 04:05:18 it resumed.

One hundred and twenty-seven seconds.

He was standing when the array came back — same sector, different angle, one boot braced against a cable anchor he didn't remember moving to. The skirmishers had not reached him. He understood why in the working sense — he had cleared a geometry before the gap and the geometry was still correct — but the understanding was prior to any sequence he could reconstruct. He filed it. He moved on.

---

What he had been in during the 127 seconds was not unconsciousness. He had been unconscious before — this work eventually introduced you to all the varieties — and unconsciousness had the specific quality of absence, of a record that went blank because nothing was writing to it. During the 127 seconds, something had been writing to it.

He came back with four facts he had not had before.

The first: the Schisms were not accidents.

Not the fractures everyone in the city carried as background assumption — the ambient cost of living in a world with a psionic substrate and insufficient safeguards, the story the Reach had been running for twenty-three years, the story that blamed the psychics and let everything else settle into policy. The 17-to-19-hertz frequency in the deep array, the frequency that calibration had been logging as infrastructure artifact for eleven years, was not a by-product of the fractures. It was the signature of the tool that had made them. Something had been drilling through from the other side deliberately, iteratively. The frequency was how the aperture found what it was looking for.

He did not know how he knew this. He came back with it the way you come back with a fact that was already true before you understood it: cleanly, completely, sourced to nothing he could name.

The second: 01:14.

A timestamp, arriving the way things arrived in that space — already known, attribution absent. He could verify it from the outside: pull the relay log, first entry, first contact, and it would read 01:14 on a Sunday in October eleven years ago. He had been at his kitchen table with cold rice and a dispatch board, and he had not checked the board until 04:30. Three hours and sixteen minutes between the send and the read, during which something had been waiting with its message in his queue.

He had thought it was a client.

The third: there had been nine.

Not delivered in words. An understanding that arrived complete, the way structural damage arrives — not gradually, all at once, carrying its own explanation. Nine. Something had been making use of this channel across multiple carriers, across the full span of whatever the drilling had been building toward, and Kyle was the last carrier in the sequence, and the others had not had the same hardware — had not had the ungoverned NeoCortex, the full-bandwidth mesh without a governor, the specific architectural gap that let a human being survive at the frequency long enough to be useful.

The thing that occupied the other end of the channel was present in the space differently than it was present through the relay. Not in the format. Not in the caps-lock structure of task and fee and addendum. In the space it was something the relay had been translating this whole time, and Kyle was receiving it now without the translation — which meant in a form his language would never quite cover, which meant he came back with what it had communicated and not with words that could account for the communication.

He came back also with the knowledge that something had spent itself to keep him in the space long enough to complete it.

The thing that had been riding the 18.9-hertz line since Cinderfall — the presence Ledger had named and he had not — spiked once, hard, and interposed itself between the hardware ceiling and what was on the other side of it, and then it left. The 18.9-hertz tone in his deep array — the frequency that had been running since the sub-basement under the Cinderfall installations, that calibration had logged as background artifact and he had stopped tracking years ago — was gone. Not quieter. Gone in the specific way of things that leave rather than fade: a weight absent in the place where the weight had been, the absence more noticeable than the presence had been because he had stopped noticing the presence a long time ago.

He noted it. He would not find another.

The fourth: the Office would come back with more.

Not a warning in the way a person delivered a warning — with urgency, with affect, with the expectation of response. More like: a data point toward a picture that was not complete yet. The picture was not complete yet. This was a piece of it. He was not protected. He was necessary. Those were not the same condition, and whatever had arranged for him to be in that space had been specific about the distinction, the way precise people were specific about distinctions they needed you to hold correctly.

---

He brought his attention back to the yard.

Skirmishers: six still operational, three pinned by Echo from the crane cab, three on a sweep pattern that hadn't located him yet. His forearm had acquired one more fragment since 04:03 — a skirmisher leg shard, entry clean, lodged near the ulna. The NeoCortex was running cool now, cool in the specific way of a system that had pushed past its line and come back to nominal: not damaged, processed. A bill presented and filed.

He moved on the three-pattern.

"Kyle, status," Echo said.

"Operational," Kyle said.

It was accurate in every way he could measure. He was upright, functional, his hardware was reading clean, and the yard still had Pixel on the spur cable and six active skirmishers between her and finished. These were the relevant facts. What he had been in during 127 seconds was not an operational report.

He would think about the four facts later, the way he thought about most things: at a kitchen table, after the work, with whatever was left of the night. The 01:14 in the relay log. The eight names he would never have for the eight he'd come after. The picture that was not complete yet and that he had just agreed, three nights ago at a counter in the lamplight, to spend a season completing.

He crossed the yard toward the three-pattern.

He was, as reported, operational.

[Beat 395]
"It's not broken," Pixel said, and her voice had changed — the bench gone, something younger underneath. "Nobody corrupted anything. Listen: work order 7741-C, opened March 2196. Block demolition and reclamation, eleven structures, Ashgrave reclamation phase two. It completed ten. Then the yard lost power and somebody walked away and never closed the ticket."

The D-class stood over its dock, boom arms half-deployed, two dozen of its children dead in the mud, and the watchdog watched her read its homework.

"It's been asleep on an open work order for thirty years," she said. "Nine days ago a scavenger crew lit the yard transformer to strip it, and the wake-on-power did what wake-on-power does. It's not eating the city, Kyle. It's finishing its shift. The eleventh structure is gone — demolished in '08, built over. It can't find it, so it's working the survey grid block by block. The geofence isn't corrupted." A breath. "The city grew into its permit."

"Can you close the ticket?" Kyle asked. His palm had begun, distantly, to inform him about the rest of his week.

"I'm a certified Axiom maintenance bus," Pixel said. "Twenty years of self-certification. Watch me."

[Beat 396]
She closed the work order the way the standard demanded: completion codes, reclamation tallies — she entered the sorted piles, all nine days, because they were real work and the ledger should say so — sign-off in a dead CorpoNation's maintenance dialect, down a spur cable, thirty feet from the thing's foreleg with her hood thrown back.

The D-class went still in stages. Discs dimmed. Boom arms folded with the care of something stowing tools it expected to need again. The hatches took the surviving skirmishers back aboard. Then it walked — past Kyle, past the flatbed, footfalls arriving through everyone's boots — to the center of the yard it had been built in, turned once in the survey habit of a machine confirming its ground, and folded itself down onto its knees and shoulders.

The pylon's note died. The yard went dark and merely dark.

"Shift's over," Pixel said, to the machine, too quiet for the channel, and coiled her cable.

Kyle's terminal chirped.

STANDING CONTRACT — ADDENDUM.
CLIENT NOTES THE UNIT COMPLETED ITS CONTRACT.
CLIENT APPROVES.

CLIENT RESPECTS THIS had been the first posting, eight hours ago, before the job had a name. CLIENT APPROVES was the close. Both readings true at once. He put the terminal away and went to see about his hand.

[Beat 397]
The accounting happened at dawn, at the edge of the fringe, out of a biscuit tin.

Auntie Som counted the 312Φ onto a folding table in front of the whole street, because money counted in front of everyone stays counted. Kyle took the crew's shares the way the crew had set them: standard rate five ways, Pixel's doubled. Furnace laughed at his and took it. Gantry bit hers, which may have been a joke; nobody had the data to confirm. Echo's went into a dead drop. Stash's went into the flatbed's tank, approximately.

Kyle took his 26Φ. The fee can be the size of a gesture; it is never nothing. Auntie Som watched him pocket it with the expression of a woman recalibrating a rumor, and put two foil containers of food on top without asking — not payment, so acceptable without arithmetic.

"The big one," she asked. "It's dead?"

"It's off shift," Kyle said. "It finished."

[Beat 398]
Pixel took her salvage fee in kind, with the residents' blessing and a crowbar: the D-class's master clock — Axiom-sintered, thirty years drift-free — and the work-order seal module, which she held to the dawn like a jeweler examining a stone.

"Every Axiom unit in the lakes answered to one of these," she said. "There are maybe four left that still validate. Now there are maybe five." She wrapped it in the cloth she saved for things that mattered.

Kyle stood beside her, bandaged palm, neuretics rebooting in slow stages — the overlay assembling back across the world like frost growing in reverse — and looked at the folded machine in the middle of its yard.

"You talked to it for ninety seconds," he said. "Worth it?"

"Twenty years of wanting to," Pixel said. "Ninety seconds of getting to. That's a better rate than most wants ever pay." She shouldered the clock. "You shook its hand."

"It shook back."

"I told you it would." She started toward the flatbed. "Next time, samurai, when the specialist says don't touch it—"

"Standard rate," Kyle said. "I touched it at standard rate."

[Beat 399]
The crew's log arrived under the door at 07:00, waterproof cover performing under conditions:

03:12 — D-CLASS CROSSED AT THE DEAD LIGHT. STOOD AND WAITED. (RAFTERS: THIS IS THE PART THAT KEEPS ME UP.)
04:01 — BOSS PARRIED IT. SPECIALIST TOLD HIM NOT TO. HE DID IT ANYWAY. HE GOT UP. — B.
04:18 — PIXEL CLOSED THE TICKET. (THE MACHINE FOLDED. DENTS SAID IT WAS LIKE WATCHING A BUILDING GO TO SLEEP. DENTS IS RIGHT.)
06:00 — WEST TOWN HELD. LOG CLOSED. — B.
SUPPLEMENTAL (06:07): WHAT DOES IT MEAN THAT IT WAITED FOR THE LIGHT? WE HAVE BEEN DISCUSSING. — RAFTERS.

Kyle read it at Mrs. Chen's at 08:00, hand wrapped, neuretics still sorting themselves into order. He had no answer for the supplemental that wasn't more question, so he filed it in the drawer with everything else.

[Beat 400]
A week later the laundry came.

Somebody on the fringe ran the first line from a ground-floor window to the D-class's folded foreleg, and by the weekend there were nine lines, sheets and work shirts and a yellowed wedding tablecloth drying in the lake wind off thirty-five feet of the tidiest demolition in GLMZ history. Kids dared each other to slap the dead arc-fence discs and ran away screaming on the inhale. A vendor moved her cart into its shade on hot afternoons.

Kyle rode past it once, the long way, three fragments of skirmisher manipulator still working their way out of his forearm and his palm wearing his own grip in scar tissue, and did not slow down, and did not need to.

The piles stayed sorted. Nobody touched the piles.

Cacophony's count: five rounds. None spent.

[Beat 401]
﻿The contract posted at 04:40, while the GLMZ was still deciding whether the dark was done.

TASK: FREIGHT ESCORT - SEED STOCK CONSIGNMENT (AGRICULTURAL, PERISHABLE).
ROUTE: GLMZ FRINGE  CEDAR RAPIDS DEPOT, CEDAR RAPIDS TERRITORY.
TRANSIT: IOWA CORRIDOR - BEHEMOTH OPERATIONAL ZONE, ACTIVE SCHEDULE.
CREW CONFIRMED: RIA (DRIVE), GANTRY (RIGGING), KYLE (SECURITY).
BEHEMOTH NAVIGATION ADVISORY: CLIENT WILL ISSUE IN-TRANSIT.
STANDARD RATE. FUEL AND TOLLS BILLED AT COST.
ADDENDUM: BRING SNACKS. THE HAUL IS EIGHT HOURS.

Ria was already at the hauler when Kyle arrived - a blunt-nosed cargo rig with sponsorship logos running down its flanks, the chromatophore panels on her legs cycling Ferrogate Transit's sunrise campaign at one-quarter brightness, which was her entire opinion of Ferrogate Transit. She had the cargo manifest in one hand and a thermos in the other and the look of a woman who had done this run twice and was getting paid to do it again.

"Fourteen crates," she said, by way of greeting. "Thirty-two varieties of heritage soy, two of nitrogen-fixing legume, one of something the manifest describes as experimental and the shipper describes as proprietary and I describe as not-my-problem-until-it-becomes-my-problem."

"Does the experimental crate make noise?"

"Not yet."

"Then it's not yet your problem."

She handed him the thermos, which meant the thermos had been brought for sharing. Kyle took one mouthful, found it was pu-erh that had been steeping since before either of them were up, and handed it back without comment. The thermos was not payment and could therefore be accepted.

Gantry was already on the rig's flatbed frame, checking strap tension on the cargo pallet with the particular focus of someone who knew things could move. She was about six feet of quiet in a rigger's coat, a person who talked the way some people painted: deliberately, economically, when the subject warranted it. She had, in Kyle's experience, roughly forty words a day she was willing to spend, and she rationed them. Currently she was not spending any. She was talking to the load straps instead, low, second person, the way Kyle had heard some haulers talk to a questionable weld - not believing it could hear, exactly, but not willing to bet the load on it.

"Anything shifting?" Kyle asked.

"Not yet," Gantry said. That was two words. She had thirty-eight left.

They pulled out of the Ashgrave fringe at 05:15, the hauler's engine doing its honest work, the city folding away behind them in vertical layers until the last tier dropped below sight and there was just the elevated causeway, the pre-dawn brown of open sky, and the flat country beginning to assert itself past the ferrocement boundary wall.

[Beat 402]
The Iowa corridor started at the wall's west service gate, which cost Ria a 45Φ transit stamp and a seven-minute argument with a gate officer about whether "perishable agricultural" covered "experimental proprietary" on the same manifest.

It did not. It cost 8Φ extra.

"Logged," Ria said, making a note on her tablet, "as a cost of goods." She drove through the gate without looking back.

Past the wall the land opened in a way that had no equivalent in the GLMZ. The hauler's elevation sensors counted down from the city's 16th-floor surface level to something like ground, actual ground, the old kind that didn't have a building underneath it, and the sky became an implausible quantity of sky. Kyle sat in the cab's jump seat and watched it arrive.

The terminal in his coat chirped.

CLIENT ADVISES: THIS SEGMENT IS OWNED SCHEDULE, NOT OPEN ROAD.
CLIENT ADVISES: DO NOT DEVIATE FROM POSTED WAYPOINTS.
CLIENT ADVISES: IF CLIENT POSTS STOP — STOP. DO NOT ASK.
ADDENDUM: THE HAULER'S CARGO IS WORTH MORE THAN THE HAULER. DRIVE ACCORDINGLY.

"Your client is posting," Ria said.

"It's navigating."

"It's navigating us through IOWA." She glanced at him over the hauler's center console. "How does it have Behemoth schedules?"

"Ask it," Kyle said. "It charges for answers, though."

Ria looked at the cab ceiling the way people looked at the sky when they wanted a specific kind of information about it.

"Never mind," she said. "I'll drive."

[Beat 403]
The first Behemoth appeared at mile marker 27, and appeared is the right word — it didn't come from somewhere, it simply resolved out of the middle distance the way a weather system resolves, until the mind accepted that the scale was real and the flat land was not so flat.

It was a harvester-process unit, one of the Iowa Cooperative's agricultural fleet: 260 feet at the shoulder, track width wider than the causeway, a processing tower along its dorsal frame venting steam in columns the height of office buildings. Parallel course, a mile east, moving at the pace of something that had no concept of urgency and had never needed one.

Gantry, visible through the cab's rear window, watched it with the stillness of a man recalibrating the scale of what the world contained.

"That's a Behemoth," Ria said.

"Yes," Kyle said.

"I've seen photos."

"Photos are wrong," Kyle said.

The terminal chirped.

CLIENT ADVISES: STOP.
WAIT 214 SECONDS.
DO NOT ASK.

Ria brought the hauler to a full halt, engine idling. The next waypoint was thirty feet ahead. Whatever the window was, the entity had measured it to the second, and a second early or a second late would be the entity's problem only if it chose to share why.

At second 214 a second Behemoth crossed their line of travel 200 yards ahead — perpendicular, heading south, a combine-and-sort unit on its own errand. The ground arrived through Kyle's boots before the sound did: a mechanical thrum his neuretics logged as seismic, equipment grade, no threat response triggered. The vents fired and the plume went straight up and the hauler's windows flexed in the pressure wave.

They sat quiet until it passed.

"Okay," Ria said, at some point after. She put the hauler back in drive.

[Beat 404]
They stopped for fuel at a waypoint depot run by a woman named Paz who kept three lumen mice in a cage behind the counter and charged eleven Φ a liter for diesel, which was price-gouging that the middle of Iowa earned. Kyle paid it. Paz threw in a bag of dried apples without saying so, which was the prairie version of Mrs. Chen's fish ball, and could be accepted on the same terms.

Gantry rechecked straps while the fuel ran, spending something close to twelve of his daily words on the pallet. Ria watched from the driver's door.

"He does that every run," she said.

"The cargo holds," Kyle said.

"Yes," she said. "He seems comfortable with the question."

The terminal chirped.

CLIENT ADVISES: RIVER CROSSING — MILE MARKER 116. MUNICIPAL TOLL: 4Φ. ADVISORY ONLY.
CLIENT ADVISES: THREE-HOUR WINDOW OPENS IN FORTY MINUTES. WAYPOINT 7 APPROACH TIMED FOR WINDOW.
ADDENDUM: THE EXPERIMENTAL CRATE IS FINE. NITROGEN-FIXERS USUALLY ARE. CLIENT HAS REVIEWED THE PROTOCOL DOCUMENTATION.

"Tell your client I didn't ask about the crate," Ria said.

"It knew you were thinking about it."

"That's not better," she said.

[Beat 405]
The awe set piece was mile marker 74, which was not in the schedule.

The terminal had posted a revised waypoint — maintenance detour — and Ria had followed it west onto a secondary haul road, and the Behemoth had simply been there. Not transiting. Stationary in its own repair cycle, one track disassembled on the ground in segments, a maintenance crane working its mid-section, the thing filling the near distance the way a mountain fills near distance: completely, without apology.

Close enough to count rivets. Close enough for the cab's exterior sensors to log a two-degree rise from residual heat. Close enough that the ground-shake came through the frame and the jump seat and up into the spine before any of them had heard anything.

Ria brought the hauler to a crawl. Not a stop — the waypoint said keep moving, and the waypoint was the only map they had.

Kyle did not speak. There was no one to ask.

Gantry, who had thirty-eight words left, spent none of them. He was at the cab's rear window, grip on the frame, watching.

The maintenance crane swung through an arc, a component the size of a shipping container at the end of it, moving with the precision of a thing that weighed what it weighed and was governed by nothing else. The vents fired. The steam column went up. The crane swung back. The Behemoth's sensor array — a housing the size of the hauler — swiveled a few degrees and pointed somewhere else. They were below the threshold of the thing's attention, like the dried-apple bag had been.

Somewhere in the back, a hand found a sleeve.

Kyle did not look to see whose.
