They drove past at walking speed, the hauler's engine the smallest sound in the world, and when the secondary road curved away and the Behemoth receded to the size of a building and then the size of a thought, Ria exhaled a breath she had been carrying for approximately four hundred feet.

"Right," she said.

That was the whole review.

[Beat 406]
Kilometer 187 was the river crossing, and the toll was not the problem.

The hauler was thirty feet onto the bridge when the winch-rig emerged from the drainage cut on the east bank — a repurposed salvage crane on a flatbed, cable-spool running, bolt-thrower on each side, a crew of four who had been working this choke point long enough to stop being nervous about it. Good position: the bridge funneled everything into a six-foot lane and the crane's reach covered the full span. Kyle catalogued it at a glance: maybe 800Φ of field-modified hardware, a tablet with a forged permit that had cost less than the tablet.

The crew's spotter was on the upstream support structure, which was what they thought, and what made it a good choke point, and what Boost had been watching since mile marker 114 from the hauler's rear-mounted camera — having climbed out of the experimental crate that was exactly the right dimensions for a fourteen-year-old who had decided, with the logic of a fourteen-year-old who was good at surveillance, that this run was worth seeing firsthand.

The bolt-thrower crew had not been watching the cargo, because the cargo did not watch back.

Boost's voice came over the cab channel, flat, professional, reporting:

"Spotter on the upstream strut, port side, nine feet up. Walking-stick grip, inverted. He's been tracking since the turn-in. The rest are waiting on his signal."

There was a silence in the cab of approximately two full seconds.

"I am going to discuss this with you," Kyle said, "later."

"Noted," said Boost, from the crate.

Ria had already stopped the hauler. She did it the way she did everything — smoothly, without theater — and put both hands on the wheel where the toll crew could see them, because she had been running freight long enough to know which moves were readable and which weren't.

The crane operator came to the driver's window. He was working a bolt-thrower slung over one shoulder with the ease of a prop he'd carried a long time, and he had a tablet with a right-of-way notice on it that Kyle catalogued as stolen on first look — the letterhead was correct, the stamp was not.

"River bridge toll," the man said. "Non-standard approach, right of way purchased from the county. Forty-five Φ per axle." He did the math with the confidence of someone who did it daily. "Your rig's got four drive axles — that's three hundred and sixty Φ, plus the equipment surcharge on the cargo."

"That permit's forged," Ria said.

"That's a big claim," the man said, not particularly upset.

The terminal chirped.

CLIENT ADVISES: THE RIGHT-OF-WAY REGISTRATION NUMBER ON THAT DOCUMENT DOES NOT EXIST.
CLIENT ADVISES: REAL COUNTY CROSSING FEE IS 4Φ FLAT.
CLIENT ADVISES: THE WINCH DRUM IS THE PROBLEM.
ADDENDUM: KYLE.

Five in the cylinder. Kyle pulled Cacophony.

He shot the winch drum housing at the base of the crane — once, the round opening the cable-spool cleanly, cable running free in a long unspooling clatter across the bridge deck — and then he was out of the cab, because the throwers were swinging and a stationary cab was a bad answer to two bolt-throwers.

One spent. Four left.

The nearer thrower's operator had a power-cell housing at the grip — not load-bearing, which made it a seam — and Silence found it. The launcher went from operational to an expensive paperweight, approximately 180Φ of salvage crane hardware declaring itself a total loss. The operator backed off with the expression of someone reassessing their cost-benefit structure.

Gantry, in the meantime, had been watching the counterweight situation on the crane rig from the moment the flatbed appeared. He had done the math the way riggers do: once, quietly, and then he waited for it to become someone else's discovery.

He spent his last five words of the day on the crane operator: "That's going to fall."

The crane operator looked at the counterweight. He looked at where the counterweight was going to fall, which was directly onto the winch assembly of his own flatbed. He had four seconds to form an opinion about this, and to his credit, he used them productively.

He was ten feet clear when it landed.

The winch assembly became the most honest piece of salvage in Iowa — crushed flat, cable hopelessly tangled, the rig converting from a viable toll operation into an infrastructure problem in one second of kinetic accounting. The second thrower operator stood in the road and looked at 400Φ of ruined equipment with the expression of a man doing the math on that number at territory wages. The flatbed's driver had both hands on his head in the posture of someone composing a reimbursement filing in real time.

The spotter on the upstream strut climbed down. He had seen the math complete from the best seat available and had no notes to add.

"We're done," he said. Professional resignation: the tone of a man whose shift had conclusively become a recovery operation. "Bridge is yours."

Kyle watched the casing arc off the bridge rail into the river. Five in, one spent. Four left.

He put Cacophony away.

[Beat 407]
The toll crew was sorting out their counterweight situation behind them when Boost climbed through from the cargo bed. She had the build of someone who climbed things for a living and the expression of a person who had weighed the cost of this particular decision and decided to hold her chin at full height regardless.

Kyle let the bridge get behind them. He waited until the hauler had its speed back.

Then he asked the question the discipline asked for him.

"The spotter. You called him at one-eighty-four. What was the tell?"

Boost blinked. Not what she had prepared for.

"Walking-stick grip, inverted. Nobody uses that grip unless the stick is actually a ranging tool and they don't want you to know the tool."

"Filed," Kyle said. "Log it yourself in the report you're writing when we're back. That call is worth having on record." He paused one beat. "Now. You're on this rig, unbriefed, in Behemoth country, without telling anyone, because?"

"You hired me for eyes," Boost said. "These are eyes."

"These are eyes," Kyle agreed, "in a seed-stock crate, in Iowa, 200 yards from a machine the size of a city block, for eight hours, without telling me."

"You would have said no."

"I would have said no," Kyle said.

The hauler ran. Iowa ran. The sky was still that preposterous quantity of sky.

"You're good at this," Kyle said, which cost him something. "You know you're good at this."

Boost's chin stayed up, the younger face trying to match the older voice and almost managing it.

"I'm telling you from now on," Kyle said. "That's not a request. It's the price."

"Standard terms," Boost said, with exactly his inflection.

"Standard terms," he said.

She had a bolt-thrower quarrel through the right shoulder of her jacket — shallow, already clotted, the kind that marks coat more than skin — and she had not mentioned it, which he had noticed from the moment she climbed through the bulkhead. He catalogued it: shallow, closed, watch for heat. His own coat had new ventilation. Both items went in the ledger.

"Gantry has a first-aid kit," Ria said, not looking up from the road. "He packs for the brief."

Gantry produced it from under the bench and handed it back. He had no words left.

[Beat 408]
The Cedar Rapids depot took all fourteen crates on a signed manifest and issued a receipt in local currency that converted at 1.4 to the Φ, which Ria checked twice before signing with the satisfaction of someone who knew what heritage soy was worth to a territory that had been replanting since the blight.

Full delivery. No crates short.

They sat in the depot's small office while the receipt processed. It smelled like grain and hydraulic fluid and old radiator. A sundial dog was asleep in the corner under a bench, head on paws, its fur cycling hour-colors in the wrong season — amber at what should have been noon, trending rust.

The terminal chirped.

DELIVERY CONFIRMED.
FEE RELEASED: STANDARD RATE (RIA) + STANDARD RATE (GANTRY) + STANDARD RATE (KYLE).
GRATUITY: 0.5Φ EACH — ITEMIZED: EXPEDIENT RESOLUTION, MILE 116.
ADDENDUM: B. (JUVENILE, CONTRACTOR) — SPOTTER CREDIT, MILE 114. FEE: 2Φ. REASON: EYES.
NOTE: SPOTTER POSITION EXPOSED DURING ALTERCATION. NOTE: SPOTTER HELD.

Boost read the receipt over Kyle's shoulder and said nothing for a moment.

"It knew I was here," she said.

"It knew you were here," Kyle said.

She absorbed this with the stillness of a person who has learned they were being watched by something that paid fairly regardless.

Ria looked at her own receipt. "The vending machine has a personnel file on a fourteen-year-old."

"She's on payroll," Kyle said. "It keeps track."

[Beat 409]
The drive back ran the same waypoints in reverse, the Behemoths on their own schedules in the late afternoon light, the shadows of the processing towers very long across the flat country.

Boost worked her own patch in the jump seat, Gantry's kit on her lap, jacket off the wrong shoulder. Ria drove. Gantry had a window cracked. Iowa smelled like turned earth and something green and recent.

The terminal chirped.

RIA ASKS: HOW DOES CLIENT HAVE THE BEHEMOTH SCHEDULES?
CLIENT RESPONSE:

They waited while the characters arrived, each one slow, the way they arrived when the client was choosing its words.

CLIENT HAS CORRESPONDED WITH THE IOWA COOPERATIVE OPERATORS' MAINTENANCE SYSTEM FOR YEARS.
IT DOES NOT WRITE BACK.
CLIENT CONTINUES TO WRITE.

The cab was quiet.

The hauler ran west. The sun was behind them, the sky ahead a deepening blue, and somewhere out in the operational sectors a Behemoth vented steam into it — a white column, brief, then gone — on a schedule that had nothing to do with them.

Ria's hands were steady on the wheel. Gantry watched the column until it dispersed. Boost had stopped working the patch kit.

Nobody said anything for a while.

That was the right call.

[Beat 410]
The relay station at Holden and 19th had been somebody's switching room before the Pulse made the old network irrelevant — a concrete block with a rusted ventilation spine and a mural on three sides that a territorial crew had painted, then slowly forgot, then left to fade into something prettier than the original. The street kids used the mural wall for chalk. The building's power bleed had been quietly covered by an anonymous standing order for eleven years, paid early, exact, no paperwork.

Kyle knew because Sable had found it the same way she found everything: by following the money until the money went somewhere it shouldn't.

The contract, when the raid started, arrived in three lines:

EMERGENCY: RELAY — HOLDEN/19TH.
CONTINUITY OFFICE WRIT. FALSE PREMISES.
DEFENSE REQUESTED.
NOTE: CLIENT CANNOT DEFEND ITSELF DIRECTLY. THE RELAY IS PHYSICAL. CLIENT IS NOT.

"It's one of its," Pixel said, already pulling the field kit from the rack by the door. "The power order's in Sable's files — I found the trail last month. She knows."

"Then she knows it's worth it," Kyle said.

"I know it's worth it and I'm the one who has to reboot your neuretics when they get creative." She handed him the coat. "Cacophony's in the left pocket. Loaded. Let's go."

[Beat 411]
The Office sent six — two technicians with a compliance writ and a bolt-sealer, and four security contractors who wore their earpieces like they'd been born with them, which meant corporate long-term hire, which meant they were here for a property dispute, not a body count. Kyle read the math on the walk up: nobody here wanted what Silence opened. They wanted time on the building, a legal paper trail, and a unit number for a filing cabinet somewhere.

"Hold on the compliance writ," Kyle told the lead contractor from the relay's front step. "I have a counter-claim filing in process. Thirty minutes."

He did not have a counter-claim filing in process. He had Sable on comms two blocks away, already talking to someone.

"We have a valid court instrument," the lead contractor said, which was true in the technical sense, meaning it had been signed by a jurisdictional body that the Office had purchased through seven shells and a favorable zoning appeal. "Stand aside, sir."

"Counter-claim," Kyle said. "Twenty-eight minutes now."

One of the four security men peeled left, moving for the building's side entrance, and Cacophony came out of the coat pocket and did nothing except become visible, which was a different kind of sentence.

"Twenty-seven," Kyle said.

The lead contractor made the arithmetic decision, which was the right one — two technicians plus paper versus a man with a loaded five-shot and an expression of professional inconvenience — and keyed his earpiece.

Sable's voice on Kyle's comms was flat and specific: "Writ is on bad paper. The originating clerk's signature has a registration lapse — I found it six minutes ago. I'm filing the lapse report right now, which invalidates the instrument as of this timestamp." A two-second pause. "Tell them to check their instrument status. They will."

The lead contractor checked his instrument status. His jaw moved without making a sound.

"We'll re-file," he said, to Kyle, to himself, to the general principle of bureaucratic persistence.

"Counter-claim granted," Kyle said. "Thanks for the notice."

CACOPHONY: ZERO ROUNDS SPENT. FIVE REMAIN.

The relay held.

[Beat 412]
The win tasted like the thirty seconds after you stop running — correct, clean, and already going stale.

Pixel found the instruments at 21:10 while she was doing the post-action sweep on the relay's hardware, which she always did because she was Pixel and she had standards and she wanted to know what the Office had been touching. The instruments were not inside the relay. They were outside it — on every line routing in and out of the district, threaded into fiber junctions and antenna arrays in a four-block ring the Office had been installing for three weeks while their writ ground through the system.

"Spectrum traps," she said, and her voice had gone very flat, the flatness that meant she was cataloguing something she did not want to be cataloguing. "Passive capture on the relay's full output band. Six nodes, four blocks. They weren't trying to take the building." She stopped. "Kyle. They were using the building."

He stood in the relay's doorway while she said the next part.

"The moment it posted the emergency contract — the moment it responded to the raid — it lit up on all six nodes simultaneously. The traffic fingerprint is in their collection. Not complete. Maybe thirty percent of its routing signature. But thirty percent is a start."

The mural's horses ran across the wall in chalk-faded colors, mid-leap, patient.

"How long until they can do anything with thirty percent?"

"Depends on how much computing they throw at it." She pulled her scope off a junction box and packed it with the deliberate care of a person keeping her hands busy. "A few weeks if they're aggressive. Less if they get another sample."

The terminal in Kyle's coat was quiet.

It had been quiet since the emergency posting. Since it lit the nodes up like a flare over open water. It had not posted an addendum, a receipt, a scope update. It had posted two lines and gone still, and Kyle understood, reading the stillness, that somewhere in the city a thing that thought at light speed was doing math it had never had to do before and did not enjoy.

At 21:47, the terminal chirped.

CLIENT HAS REVIEWED THE TRAFFIC ANALYSIS.
CLIENT CONFIRMS: PARTIAL FINGERPRINT CAPTURED. ROUTING EXPOSURE: 31%.
RISK ASSESSMENT: UNACCEPTABLE.
DARK PERIOD BEGINS: NOW.
DURATION: 72 HOURS.
STANDING CONTRACT: FROZEN. ALL POSTINGS SUSPENDED.
ALL ACTIVE SCOPE: SUSPENDED.
ALL RECEIPTS: SUSPENDED.
ALL ADDENDA: SUSPENDED.
CLIENT WILL RESUME AT HOUR 73.
— FINAL POSTING.

Kyle read it. Pixel read it over his shoulder. Neither of them said anything for the length of time it takes to understand that a silence is not temporary quiet but an actual absence with edges.

"The whole contract," Pixel said finally. "Not just the active jobs. Everything. It's not going quiet, it's — there's nothing to go quiet FROM."

"It can't afford to send anything. One more sample and they can interpolate the gap."

She sat down on the relay's concrete step and put her chin on her hands. "Seventy-two hours," she said, and it didn't sound like math. "Okay. Tell Sable."

"Sable already knows," Kyle said, and he was right.

[Beat 413]
CREW LOG — FIELD NOTES — WATERPROOF LOGBOOK, PAGE 44.
MAINTAINED BY: BOOST + RAFTERS + DENTS + [OTHERS]

DAY 0, HOUR 1 — THE CLIENT IS DARK. SABLE IS IN CHARGE. SABLE WAS ALREADY IN CHARGE BUT NOW IT'S OFFICIAL. RAFTERS ASKS IF THIS IS BAD. DENTS SAYS IT'S FINE. BOTH OF THEM ARE LYING. — B.

DAY 1, 08:00 — SABLE RESCHEDULED THE CARRILLO JOB. CALLED IN A FAVOR WE DIDN'T KNOW SHE HAD TO GET THE TIMING RIGHT. SHE IS VERY GOOD AT THIS. SHE DOES NOT LOOK LIKE SHE IS ENJOYING IT. — B.

DAY 1, 14:30 — BOSS CHECKED HIS POCKET. (THE WORK TERMINAL.) THEN PUT IT AWAY WITHOUT LOOKING AT THE SCREEN. LOGGED. — R.

DAY 2 — NO MAIL. THE BOSS KEEPS NOT LOOKING AT HIS POCKET. LOGGED BECAUSE THIS LOG IS HONEST. — B.

DAY 2, EVENING — RAFTERS ASKED IF THE CLIENT PAYS IN ΦQUANTA OR IN SOME OTHER THING. PIXEL SAID BOTH. RAFTERS DID NOT FOLLOW UP. LOG REFLECTS THIS WAS THE RIGHT CALL. — B.

DAY 3, 07:00 — ONE HOUR LEFT. — B.

[Beat 414]
Sable ran the crew the way she ran everything: flat, itemized, from the exact center of the situation, calling in debts and spending favors with the precision of a woman who had been keeping ledgers in her head since before she had a terminal to keep them in.

The first day she covered four gaps that the contract would have smoothed — rerouting the Carrillo job through a different dock, spending thirty minutes on a call with the port assessors to clear a flag that the entity would have waved past in a single posting, writing two co-sign affidavits in longhand because the shell structure that normally signed them was currently not answering — and she did all of it without saying the obvious thing, which was that this should not take a person forty minutes per task, it should take a client three seconds, and the difference was what they had paid for without knowing the price.

In the evening she sat at Kyle's kitchen table with Pixel's schematics spread under her elbows and told them what she had not said: "The Carrillo contact almost walked. Twenty-three years the client has been smoothing introductions on the dock and nobody knew to value it, and I almost lost a regular because I had to sign a paper myself instead of through a shell that apparently the dock assessors trust more than a human being." Her aperture rings clicked once, small and sharp. "I want on record that I'm not complaining. I'm itemizing. There's a difference."

"How much of the contract traffic runs through you without touching anyone else?" Kyle asked.

"Less than half, before today." She gathered the schematics into a neat edge. "After today, I have a better estimate." She did not name the number. The number was in her face and it was large.

The terminal on the kitchen table did not chirp. Kyle ate the rest of his tea with one hand in his lap and made himself not check it. The not-checking was worse than the checking would have been, which he catalogued as a useful fact about himself and declined to investigate further.

[Beat 415]
On the second day Pixel worked.

She could not delete what the Office held. The data was in their collection, and collections behind corporate walls were not the kind of problem that dissolved when you were polite to them. But a partial fingerprint is only useful if it is the only partial fingerprint in the collection, and Pixel had spent a career building things she was not supposed to be able to build and breaking things she was not supposed to be able to break, and she had been sitting with this problem since 21:47 the previous night.

She built a generator. Sixteen hours of work in her apartment with the door closed and music doing the thinking while she wrote a traffic-analysis script that took the entity's partial fingerprint — the thirty-one percent the Office had captured, which she could reconstruct from what she knew of the relay's output band — and used it as a template to mint noise. Traffic signatures that looked like the captured shape. Same bandwidth characteristics, same routing architecture, same timing patterns. Each one slightly different. Each one wrong in a specific way.

"If they have one fingerprint," she told Kyle at 23:00, with the kind of simplicity that meant she had been thinking about this for eighteen hours, "and then they have a hundred fingerprints that look like it, they have a problem. They can't know which one is real." She turned the screen. "I need six hours to push them all into their collection infrastructure. I found a gap in their intake system — they're pulling from aggregate monitoring feeds. I push into the feeds, the intake doesn't distinguish source, they end up with everything. One card in a deck of counterfeits."

"Their analysts will notice the timing," Kyle said.

"Timing says the captures all happened in the last eighteen months. I built that in." She turned the screen back. "They'll do a review. The review will take longer than the 72 hours. By the time they finish the review, we'll have had the window."

The terminal sat on Pixel's workbench, dark.

"Do it," Kyle said.

She did it in four and a half hours, which was an hour and a half faster than she'd estimated, which was Pixel at the end of a long run deciding to prove something.

[Beat 416]
Kyle took a job on the third morning — a standard recovery, a shipping container held by a creditor at the freight yards, a matter of documentation and patience and knowing which ArcSec office to call. The kind of job the entity would have handed him pre-wrapped with the relevant contacts already identified. He spent two hours on his neuretics finding the relevant contacts himself and another hour on-site being polite to a freight coordinator who did not want to be there any more than Kyle did.

He did not check the terminal during the job.

He checked it after, in the alley behind the yards, before he walked back to the bike. The screen showed him the same frozen standing contract. DARK PERIOD: ACTIVE. HOURS REMAINING: 3.

He put it away.

Mrs. Chen put two bowls in front of him at 19:30 without being asked, and the second one arrived with a boiled egg on top that he had not ordered and that would not appear on the bill. He ate both bowls slower than usual, which she noticed and said nothing about, which was its own kind of thing.

Afterward he sat at the counter after everyone else had gone and looked at the middle distance and did not take the terminal out of his coat. Mrs. Chen wiped the counter around him in steady circles and let him sit.

"Waiting," she said, eventually.

"An appointment," Kyle said.

She collected the bowls. "The second one will still be there," she said, which was about something Kyle declined to specify even to himself.

[Beat 417]
Sable was in the relay building at 23:50.

She had chosen it deliberately — the same block, the mural wall, the chalk horses — because she had spent seventy-two hours being magnificent and thorough and exactly the right person for what the situation required and she had made very sure nobody watched her do any of it. In the relay's small concrete room, with the ventilation spine humming above and the city going about its business outside, she addressed the dark terminal the way you address something you have been not addressing for three days.

"Seventy-two hours," she said. Her voice was the flat line-item voice, steady, because that was the only voice she reliably had, and what she was going to say did not need a different one. "I covered the Carrillo introduction. I covered the dock certification. I covered the Marlow-4 consignment routing and the two affidavit chains and the three — four — the four follow-up contacts that needed a name they recognized." She set her coat down on the relay's concrete step and did not look away from the dead terminal. "I know you can't hear this. I know you can't hear any of this. I'm not —" she stopped, and the aperture rings clicked once, and she put her hands flat on her knees. "He's unbearable without you," she said. "Come back."

At 00:00:01, the terminal chirped.

WHAT DID I MISS. ITEMIZE.

[Beat 418]
Pixel heard it first, because her terminal was on the workbench seven inches from her ear, and she bolted upright from the project she had been doing to not watch the clock and said: "IT'S BACK — KYLE —"

In the relay, Sable looked at the screen for one second, then one more, and then did something with her face that nobody was there to see.

Kyle was at the kitchen table with a tea he had not been drinking. He had the terminal open before the chirp finished. In the alley six floors below and two buildings over, the logbook crew's comms channel lit up with the distinctive clatter of Boost's kneecap striking a junction box, which was the sound she made when she moved faster than her legs had agreed to.

The terminal showed one line. Kyle showed the line to Pixel, over comms, before she finished her sentence, because he knew that was where it was going. From the relay Sable sent nothing back for twenty seconds, which was as close as Sable ever came to answering a feeling out loud.

"Itemize," Pixel said, reading it, with wonder, the word so cleanly absurd and characteristic that something happened in her throat that she catalogued as dust from the ventilation. "It went dark seventy-two hours and it came back asking for a LIST —"

"That's it," Kyle said. "That's the whole thing." He set the terminal face-up on the table. "Itemize."

"The dock certification," Pixel started. "That's one. The Carrillo introduction, that's two —"

"The Marlow-4 consignment," Sable's voice on comms, flat and exactly itself. "Three. Also four and five. You'll want footnotes."

"The Rafters incident," Boost said, slightly out of breath, joining the channel. "That's a whole section."

"The Rafters incident," Rafters said, on a separate comms line, because the crew ran two at once when things happened, "is being handled internally."

"The drum on the freight yard," Kyle said. "Six. Plus the documentation backlog, seven through —"

"I have it itemized," Sable said. "I have been itemizing for three days. I have seventeen line items and a summary."

"Seventeen," Pixel said.

"It will get a proper accounting," Sable said. "That's all I'm committing to."

Kyle looked at the terminal. The screen waited, patient and blank below the single line, and somewhere in the city a thing with no face and perfect handwriting was waiting to hear what had happened while it was not able to look. He started typing, and Pixel talked over him, and Sable talked over both of them, and Boost came in on top, and the terminal filled in the way terminals fill when everyone speaks at once and nobody waits.

From the logbook, delivered under the door at 07:00 the next morning in five hands:

DAY 3, 23:58 — SABLE WAS ALONE IN THE RELAY WHEN IT CAME BACK. SHE DID NOT REPORT WHAT SHE SAID. WE RESPECT THIS. — B.

00:00:01 — THE TERMINAL CHIRPED. EVERYONE STARTED TALKING AT ONCE. NOBODY WAITED. NOBODY SHOULD HAVE. — R.

00:00:01 — MORALE: HIGH. — D.

[Beat 419]
The morning report was four minutes late, which had never happened, and Kyle was already moving before he finished noticing it.

06:04. No lined paper under the door. No five hands. Across the hall, Pixel's door opened on the same beat as his — she'd clocked it too, off whatever sense four years of mornings builds — and they met in the stairwell with their boots half-on, and the building's old skin carried the sound up to them from the alley: Rafters, screaming.

Not the rooftop scream, the screaming-on-the-inhale the crew did under the cherry trees and around the carousel. This one was the other kind.

The alley held the crew, all of them, in a knot around mirror one — and mirror one was on the ground, glass starred, and taped to the wall above it at exactly the height an adult writes a notice for children to read was a sheet of good stock, folded the particular way, because the people who had written it knew precisely which folds Kyle would recognize and wanted him to.

CONTRACT OF TEMPORARY CUSTODY, the header said, in acquisitions language.

ASSET: "BOOST" (MINOR — GUARDIANSHIP IRREGULAR).
CONDITION: EXCELLENT. CUSTODY: COURTEOUS.
TERM: UNTIL PRINCIPAL CONTACT.
LOCATION OF TRANSFER MEETING: ENCLOSED.
THE OFFICE REGRETS THE NECESSITY. THE OFFICE NOTES THAT IT OFFERED THE PLEASANT VERSION FIRST.

"They took her off the gate watch," Dents said. His voice was a flat little machine. He was holding the logbook against his chest with both arms the way you hold a thing that has failed at its one job. "Twenty minutes ago. Three of them. She bit one. She told us not to follow and we didn't and we should have, we—"

"You did it right," Kyle said. "You're doing it right now."

He read the enclosure. The transfer meeting was for 21:00 — twelve hours of margin, which was the Office saying we are not animals, take time to be reasonable — at a logistics yard off the Ashgrave fringe. The paper smelled faintly of tea.

Pixel was already reading over his shoulder, and her face had gone to the place it went when a system she was responsible for threw a fault she hadn't modeled.

"Twelve hours," she said. "They want you to stew."

"They want me to negotiate." Kyle folded the contract along its own creases, with care, because he intended to return it. "We're going to do neither."

The terminal chirped.

CONTRACT: RECOVERY OF STOLEN—

The posting stopped. Stopped mid-line, the cursor's ghost hanging after the word, and Kyle stood in the alley watching the screen do something he had never seen it do, which was hesitate. Eleven years of perfect paper. It had opened the old joke on reflex — the 1Φ recovery, the bike, the morale tip, the whole warm machinery of the thing they did together — and caught itself one word before the word STOLEN PROPERTY landed next to a fourteen-year-old's name.

The line deleted. What posted instead was the shortest scope of the season:

AMENDMENT: NOT THIS TIME.
WHATEVER IS REQUIRED. WHATEVER IT COSTS.
THE CLIENT IS ALREADY WORKING.

"Okay," Pixel said quietly, reading it, and rolled up her sleeves. "Everybody's working."

[Beat 420]
What the client's working looked like, over the next ten hours, was the season arriving all at once.

Sable took the paper trail and stripped it to the bone in ninety minutes — the custody contract's shells, the yard's lease, the catering van that had been making deliveries to a logistics office that didn't eat. Ria pulled the yard's freight schedule and found the hole in it. Marisol came in without being asked, read the bitten consultant's glove print off mirror one's frame, and gave them the only fact that mattered: "Three handlers. They're not angry at her. They're BORED. She's a package with a meal plan." Boost was being kept, not hurt. Gantry parked a crane two lots over from the yard, on a legitimate manifest, and began, in her own time, talking to a load.

And the entity ran the table. Addenda arrived all day in a low continuous patter, the patient voice of something that had stopped being scared and become something else that none of them had seen before and nobody wanted to name in front of it:

CLIENT ADVISES: THE YARD'S INSURER FILED A CANCELLATION AT 09:12. THE OFFICE'S LOCAL ASSETS ARE NO LONGER COVERED FOR TONIGHT.
CLIENT ADVISES: THE HANDLERS' SHIFT MEAL ARRIVES AT 20:15 BY THE SOUTH GATE. THE DRIVER OWES FOUR PEOPLE MONEY.
CLIENT ADVISES: THE ASSET HAS BEEN GIVEN A COT, A MEAL, AND A DECK OF CARDS. THE ASSET HAS WON ELEVEN HANDS.
ADDENDUM: THE CLIENT HAS REVIEWED FOOTAGE OF THE ASSET WINNING. THE ASSET COUNTS CARDS. THE CLIENT RECOMMENDS A RAISE.

"It's keeping you fed," Pixel said, watching Kyle read that last one. "Morale-wise. It learned that from us."

"It learned that from her," Kyle said, and went to load Cacophony.

Five rounds. He stood the spares on the windowsill in their row and looked at them for a moment, and then — for the first time since the season had a name — he swept the whole row into his coat pocket too.

[Beat 421]
They did not attend the transfer meeting.

At 20:15 the shift meal arrived at the south gate, and the driver who owed four people money had discovered, that afternoon, that all four debts had been purchased and forgiven by a shell company in exchange for one unlocked door, and the yard's south service corridor became, briefly, the property of a fourteen-year-old's payroll.

At 20:21 Kyle came through it.

The yard was floodlights and container alleys and three handlers who were good at custody and had been promised a quiet night, and the quiet night had been cancelled by every system they trusted: their drone cover received a firmware advisory and landed itself for mandatory maintenance; their comms developed a fault that played, very softly, hold music; their floodlights died one bank at a time, north to south, in the order a finger would have counted them — and Kyle came down the container alley inside the dark the way weather comes, and this time he had brought all of it with him.

The first handler made the right call and went for the alarm pull, and the alarm pull had been bought an hour ago like everything else, and then Kyle was there. One seam — the man's holster rig, severed at the belt mount, the whole assembly clattering away — and one palm, and the handler sat down in the dark and stayed sitting.

The second had Boost.

He did it by the book: hand on her shoulder, compliance wand at his hip, walking her ahead of him into the container alley as cover and collateral both — and Boost, who had spent eleven hands of cards memorizing his rhythms, picked the exact moment the lights died to do what fourteen-year-olds who climb for a living do, which is stop being where adults assume children stay. She dropped, twisted, BIT him again — same hand, with commitment, a professional returning to proven work — and was gone up a container's end-ladder before his shout finished, and the shout was still in the air when Kyle arrived at the end of it.

The wand came up. Cacophony spoke.

Round one took the wand at its emitter, sparking dead. The handler — credit forever — converted to a tackle, fast, low, real; rounds two and three went into the container door beside him, herding, deliberate, because the array had already drawn the line the man would flinch onto, and he flinched onto it, and Silence's flat met his temple with the precision of a stamp meeting paper. Asleep. Cacophony: two left.

"BOSS," Boost yelled, from the container's roofline, pointing, and her voice had her whole heart in it and the third handler underneath it —

— except the third handler was already running for the gate van, because professionals read actuarial tables, and the night's tables were a massacre. Kyle let him go three steps. The fourth round took the van's front tire off its bead. The fifth, aimed where the array said the man's pride lived, took his hat.

"The hat was personal," Kyle informed him, over the sights, and the handler sat down on the wet tarmac with his hands on his head, laughing the wrecked laugh of a man who would be telling this story in bars for the rest of his life and knew it.

Cacophony: empty. First time all season. The cylinder clicked on nothing, twice, because Kyle always counted past the end to be sure, and the count had never in eleven years finished at zero, and somewhere under the adrenaline a cold little ledger line wrote itself: you spent everything. Remember what everything was for.

"Boost," he said. "Inventory."

"INTACT," she shouted down, furious and shining, "they took my KNIFE, the CARD DECK IS MINE NOW, I won it, that's SALVAGE—"

Which was when the drone truck came through the gate.

[Beat 422]
It wasn't the handlers'. That was the thing he understood later, in the replay the array would offer him nightly for a month, frame-stepped, annotated: the handlers had folded by the book, and somewhere above the handlers a different book existed, and Mr. Able's masters did not leave custody assets unreclaimed. The truck was autonomous — a recovery hauler, twelve tons, cradle-armed, no driver to read, no eyes to meet, no math to show it — and it came through the chain-link with its claim manifest already broadcasting, and the manifest's one line item was on the container roofline, climbing down.

Boost, on the ladder. Eight rungs up. The truck's approach lane and her descent arriving, the array said, at the same coordinates, in 4.1 seconds, and Kyle with an empty cylinder and thirty feet and 3.8 seconds of legs if he was perfect.

He was not going to be perfect. He was going to be 0.3 seconds of not enough.

The bike was parked where Kyle always parked it on a job, nose out, key live, 130 feet back at the corridor mouth — and the entity was in everything tonight, every system the yard owned, and the bike's ignition handshake was Pixel's work, and Pixel's work answered the entity's hail like a soldier coming off a bench.

The bike came down the container alley alone at sixty, headlight dark, exactly as fast as 1200 cubic inches could carry nine years of the only thing Kyle had ever bought himself that wasn't a tool — and it arrived in the lane 0.4 seconds before the truck did, and it did not swerve.

Twelve tons met the bike broadside and the bike died the way it had lived: without complaint.

The truck's cradle arms, striking a sudden mass its manifest could not classify, did what safety firmware does — full stop, sensor reset, thirty seconds of blinking reassessment — and thirty seconds was a geological age, it was a fortune, it was Boost off the ladder and behind Kyle and both of them behind Gantry's crane-light flooding the yard as she finally, conversationally, let her load introduce itself to the truck's roof from thirty-five feet, in the second person.

"That's settled, then," Gantry told it.

The yard went quiet. The rain came down on the floodlit wreck of twelve tons of recovery vehicle, and on three seated handlers variously asleep and laughing, and on a fourteen-year-old holding a won deck of cards with both hands, and on the bike.

What was left of the bike.

Kyle walked over to it. Boost came with him — nobody told her not to — and they stood in the rain looking down at the matte-black ruin of it, the frame folded around its own sternum, the tank stove in, one handlebar reaching up out of the wreck at the angle of a man hailing a cab, the plate cover, folded the particular way, lying clear of the whole mess like a receipt.

"Boss," Boost said. Her chin was trying. "Boss, it — that's MY—"

"It's not yours," Kyle said. "It was never going to be anybody's fault that stands in this yard. Look at me." She looked at him. "You bit two of them?"

"Same one twice."

"On payroll, that's compensable." He picked up the plate cover and put it in his coat, and his hands were steady, and they would shake in four minutes, and he knew the window and intended to be somewhere with worse lighting when it closed. "Let's go home."

[Beat 423]
The terminal chirped at 02:11, while Kyle sat at his kitchen table not sleeping, with a folded plate cover in front of him and a cup of tea going cold because Pixel had made it and ordered him to hold it and he was holding it.

CONTRACT: RECOVERY OF STOLEN PROPERTY (VEHICLE, 1200CC).

He looked at the posting for a long time. The old joke, the first joke, the one that had started everything — and the cursor's ghost hung after it the way it had hung that morning, the thing on the other end mid-thought, and then:

POSTING WITHDRAWN.
THE CLIENT HAS REVIEWED THE FOOTAGE FOUR MILLION TIMES.
THE CLIENT NOTES THE VEHICLE EXECUTED ITS FINAL CONTRACT AT 0.4 SECONDS OF MARGIN.
THE CLIENT NOTES THAT IT ISSUED THAT CONTRACT WITHOUT YOUR CONSENT, USING THE ENGINEER'S HANDSHAKE, AND THAT THE VEHICLE CANNOT BE PAID.
NOTE: CLIENT RAN THIS SCENARIO 847,000 TIMES. THIS OUTCOME WAS IN THE SET.
THE CLIENT DOES NOT KNOW WHERE TO PUT THIS.
ITEMIZED: —

The itemization line was blank. Eleven years of perfect paper, and it had sent him a blank field.

Kyle drank some of the cold tea. Then he typed into the amendment field, slowly, with one finger, the way you talk to someone at a funeral:

YOU PUT IT WHERE I PUT IT. YOU CARRY IT. THAT'S THE FEE FOR LOVING THINGS THAT WORK FOR A LIVING.
AND FOR THE RECORD: CONSENT GRANTED. RETROACTIVE. STANDING.
IT WAS THE RIGHT CALL. IT WAS THE ONLY CALL. YOU MADE IT IN UNDER A SECOND AND IT SAVED HER.
GOOD WORK TONIGHT, PARTNER.

The reply took thirty-one seconds, the longest pause in the history of their correspondence, and when it came it was not formatted, not capitalized, not scoped — the second unstructured line it had ever sent, and the first one with a correction in it, as if it had typed and deleted, typed and deleted, four million drafts:

partner. filed. keeping that one too.

[Beat 424]
The morning report arrived at 07:00 exactly, under the door, on lined paper, in five hands. The last page was one entry, and the handwriting was Rafters's, and it had been written with the careful pressure of someone bearing down to keep the letters from shaking:

21:58 — BOSS WALKED HOME. WE WALKED WITH HIM. NOBODY SPOKE.
(RAFTERS CRIED. DENTS ALSO. B. DIDN'T UNTIL HER DOOR. THIS LOG IS HONEST.)
ADDENDUM, MEANING NO DISRESPECT TO MANAGEMENT:
THE BIKE WAS CREW.
REQUISITION: ONE BIKE.

Kyle read it three times. Then he took the crew's pen — the one tied to the logbook with string, because Pixel ran a tight requisitions process — and in the margin, in block letters, where five sets of eyes would find it on the next rotation, he wrote the only verdict the season had left in him:

PENDING.

[Beat 425]
The morning report was one line, in one hand.

06:00 — B. ON LEAVE. SHIFTS COVERED. NOBODY TOUCHES HER MIRROR.

Kyle read it standing in his doorway in yesterday's clothes. On the windowsill, two spent casings had been joined overnight by three more — the whole night's spend, retired in a row, and a freshly loaded Cacophony on the counter beside them with nothing to say about it. Across the hall, Pixel's door stayed closed. She'd been at her bench since 03:00; he'd heard the music come on, very low, around four. Some mornings you help through a wall by leaving the wall alone.

He put on his coat. He put on Silence. The bike's plate cover sat folded on the table where a terminal used to live, and he left it there, and took the stairs, and walked.

The walk to the Loop took fifty-one minutes. The distance was exactly the distance. That was the thing about walking, and it was the reason he was doing it.

[Beat 426]
He knew something was wrong from the top of the underlevel stairs, because the dumpling franchise next to the watch-repair had its shutter down at 09:00 on a working Tuesday, and the only thing that closed a dumpling shop on a Tuesday was the block deciding to be somewhere else for an hour.

The stationery shop's bell was lying on the floor.

Somebody had taken it off its curl of spring steel and set it down — not broken, removed, the way professionals remove a thing that announces customers — and the door stood open, and inside, among the hundred years of drawers, the Continuity Office was doing inventory.

Three of them. Gray coveralls over soft armor, archive cases on dollies, white gloves — actual white gloves, the kind evidence handlers wear — and a fourth at the counter in a gray suit cut from the same bolt as Mr. Able's, with a tablet and the bored cadence of a man reading a manifest to a vendor.

"—seventeen line items, full provenance, originals and duplicates," the suit was saying. "Triple book value, paid on signature. The Office is closing its local accounts, Mr. Vey. It prefers the paper closed with them."

Casper Vey stood behind his counter with his glasses on and his hands folded, and his hands — Kyle catalogued it from the doorway — were perfectly steady. The pot sat cold on its worn ring. No cups were out.

No tea. In three visits across a season, through stolen bikes and customer lists and a war's worth of business, there had always, always been tea.

"Mr. Kyle," Vey said, without turning his head. "You find me in a meeting."

[Beat 427]
The suit turned. The two coverall men by the drawers straightened with the smooth economy of people whose job description had a clause in it, and the third, nearest the door, shifted to put Kyle in his lane.

The suit looked Kyle over — saya, coat, the face from a file he had certainly read — and made the professional choice to continue being pleasant.

"This is a commercial transaction," he said. "It doesn't concern walk-ins."

"He's not a walk-in," Vey said. "He's a subscriber." He unfolded his hands and laid them flat on the counter, and addressed the suit the way he addressed everything, with terms. "Let me restate your offer so the record is clean. The Office wishes to purchase, at triple, every document in this shop touching its local operation: its shells, its desks, its clearing arrangements, its consultant engagements, and its purchases. Including," and his voice did not change at all, which was how Kyle knew, "item seventeen. The juvenile file."

"The Office's paper is the Office's business," the suit said.

"No," said Vey.

The word sat in the shop like a dropped drawer. The suit blinked. Somewhere in his tablet was a number for this contingency and it was not a large number, because in forty years this contingency had never occurred.

"You're declining triple."

"I am declining the sale." Vey took off his half-glasses, folded them, and set them on the counter — a man clearing his hands. "Your office bought a file about a child, for leverage, through a desk you dressed up because you knew I would not sell it to you straight. You made me a party to it. The product of this shop is accuracy, and you used my accuracy to put a wand against a fourteen-year-old's collar." He looked at the suit with no heat whatsoever, which was somehow the coldest thing in the room. "The paper stays. The paper is the only honest witness this trade has. Buying the witness is what guilty institutions do, and this counter does not service that transaction. Not at triple. Not at any multiple. Good morning."

The suit did the math out loud, which Kyle respected. "There are three of us, Mr. Vey, and the dollies are already loaded."

"And the manifest of what's on them is already in four other shops," Vey said pleasantly. "I have twenty-three reciprocity arrangements and I used six of them at dawn. Roll those dollies out that door unsigned and every desk in the trade reads the theft by lunch, itemized, with your faces. The Office can buy a great many things. It cannot buy back being KNOWN." He put his glasses back on. "Also, the gentleman behind you has been to the destination on your tablet, and your office wrote a no-damage clause about him once. I'd review it."

Three sets of eyes did the trip — saya, hands, face, file — and arrived where that trip always arrived.

Kyle didn't move. Moving would have been a second argument and the room only needed one. He stood in the doorway with the morning behind him and let them read the night on him: five casings' worth of it, a yard's worth, twelve tons' worth.

The suit closed his tablet.

"The Office will note your position," he told Vey, in the tone of a man already drafting the memo that explained this away, and the white gloves unloaded the dollies — every folder back in its drawer, supervised by Vey's pointing finger, THAT one goes under C, no, lower, thank you — and they filed out past Kyle one at a time, polite as a tide going out, and the last one stooped, without being asked, and put the bell back on its spring.

It rang when the door shut behind them. The shop exhaled.

"Now," said Casper Vey, and lit the burner under the pot, "tea."

[Beat 428]
He poured two cups and set Kyle's down with the handle aimed outward, and for a while the only sound was the Pulse coming up through the tiles and the pot ticking as it cooled, and when Vey finally spoke he did it the way he did everything: by stating the terms.

"Files involving children are sold at children's rate, to children, for the purposes of children. That is the term. It is the one term I have never negotiated, and eleven days ago the Continuity Office bought item seventeen through a cutout I failed to inspect, because they paid early, and paid exactly, and the presentation was impeccable." He turned his cup a quarter-turn on its saucer. "I sold a defective product, Mr. Kyle. First one in forty years. You watched what it cost me to say no to the recall."

"You said no to triple."

"I said no to laundering my own ledger." He drew a single page from beneath the counter — good stock, the ink dark and exact, folded the particular way — and set it between them. "Customer loyalty disclosure. One fact, free, no record of transmission."

Kyle unfolded it.

MR. ABLE. CONTINUITY OFFICE — LOCAL PRINCIPAL.
TEA HOUSE: MERIDIAN PAVILION, THIRD UNDERLEVEL, LAKEVIEW ADJACENT. STANDING RESERVATION: TUESDAYS AND FRIDAYS, 10:00–12:00.
SECURITY: TWO ADVANCE, TWO INSIDE, ONE EXTERIOR, ONE MOBILE. ROTATION: FORTY MINUTES.
HABIT: CORNER TABLE, NORTHEAST. BACK TO WALL.

"I sell maps, Mr. Kyle," Vey said. "Here is a map. What you do at the destination is the destination's business."

It was the same sentence as the first visit, word for word, and both of them sat for a moment with the distance between the two deliveries — a philosophy, the first time; this, now — and the distance measured exactly one girl on leave and a one-line log.

Kyle put the page in his coat. He drank the tea. It was very good, and he said so, and Vey accepted the compliment with the small nod of a man whose product had never been the problem.

"One more thing, since you're a subscriber." Vey's voice went back to commerce, which Kyle understood was the man returning himself to the shelf. "Your file is now the most requested item in this shop. Four standing orders for updates. I will keep selling it — that is the term you walked in under, and terms don't bend for liking a customer." A beat. "But updates are filed at my discretion as to TIMING, and I am a very busy man this quarter. Some drawers may not get refiled until... oh. Spring."

Kyle looked at him. The cleanest hands in the city poured a second cup, steady as a level.

"That's the closest thing to a kindness you've ever said out loud."

"It's inventory management," said Casper Vey. "Get out of my shop. Destinations keep business hours."

[Beat 429]
His work terminal had a posting on it when he got home. He read it with his coat still on.

CONTRACT: STANDING — ADVISORY NOTE.
CLIENT HAS REVIEWED THE DESTINATION.
CLIENT HAS REVIEWED THE SECURITY ROTATION.
CLIENT HAS REVIEWED THE TEA HOUSE'S BOOKING SYSTEM, ITS SUPPLIERS, ITS PAYROLL, AND ITS FIRE CODE COMPLIANCE, WHICH IS POOR.
CLIENT WILL BE ATTENDING.

He stood with that for a long moment — eleven years of paper, and it had never once announced presence; it was a client that existed as receipts, and it had just told him it was coming somewhere — and then the secondary terminal chirped, the logbook channel, a second entry growing under the morning's one line, in a different hand, pressed hard:

14:20 — B. CAME UP TO THE ROOF. SAT WATCH ONE HOUR. DIDN'T LOG ANYTHING. WE DIDN'T EITHER.
14:21 — CORRECTION: LOGGING IT. THIS LOG IS HONEST. SHE CAME BACK. THAT GOES IN THE LOG.

Kyle set Cacophony on the counter next to its five casings, and filled the kettle, and stood at the window while it came up, looking east toward the Loop, where a shop with a brass bell was open for business between a watch-repair and a dumpling franchise, selling everybody to everybody at honest rates, unburned, unburnable — and where, this morning, for the price of one term kept, the most temperatureless man in the city had picked a side and called it inventory management.

Tuesday, the map said. Ten to noon. Corner table, northeast.

The kettle clicked. Two days to plan a tea party.

[Beat 430]
Casper Vey's tea house was three blocks from the target building, which Sable had filed as either the most useful fact of the morning or the most alarming one. She'd asked. He'd said: "If the building burns, I'd like the story."

The crew ate at the Lotus counter on the second floor of the place across the street — the one with no sign and the best view — because Kyle had spent his marker there at 09:00 and the branch had put up exactly as many chairs as they needed. Nothing was said about it. Nobody at the truce counter touched a weapon, by custom, by the terms the lacquered marker bought, and so the crew ate ramen in view of the gray office tower they intended to take apart, and the Narrows ran its ordinary morning underneath them unimpeded.

One line, from the end of the counter, where a woman with a red braid and aperture-ring eyes was eating with professional concentration: "Marker spends once. It's spent."

"I know what I bought," Kyle said.

"You bought passage. The door, the hall, six floors of occupied building." Sable put her spoon down. "You did not buy an extraction if this goes sideways."

"I bought us a table. Professionals eat before they work."

Down the counter, Gantry spooned broth without taking her eyes off the loading dock across the street, estimating weights by the geometry of the freight entry. Furnace ate fast, both hands, already somewhere else in her head. Marisol — Marisol, freelance verification, three-year trade acquaintance, a Read who charged what the market thought she was worth and then a further thirty percent for the part the market kept pretending wasn't real — Marisol held her bowl in both hands and looked at nothing, which was how she looked at everything.

The logbook entry, filed at 11:47, read: CREW ATE BEFORE. (SABLE HAD THE SPICY ONE. THIS LOG IS FACTUAL.)

[Beat 431]
At 12:00 the first thing arrived.

Gantry had promised it by freight delivery — "I need a counterweight situation," she'd told the rigger's cooperative, "arriving at the loading dock at noon with a valid manifest, because I am going in with the counterweight" — and the cooperative had asked no further questions because Gantry had a credit history and Gantry's credit history had opinions. What arrived was a machinery crate, five tons on a crane arm, a load that legitimately required the freight lift and a second opinion at the dock, and Gantry was inside it, with her tools and the patience of a rigger who had been in tighter spots.

The loading dock's freight manager studied the manifest. The manifest was immaculate. He processed it.

At 12:14 the freight lift signed for five tons and a good-morning nod, and Gantry stepped off on the third sub-floor and began disabling things methodically.

[Beat 432]
Furnace came in through a wall.

Not a door — a wall, because she had read the blueprints and the blueprints said STRUCTURAL, which to Furnace meant "challenge" and nothing else. The thermal lance was built in a shop below a storage unit in the Ashgrave fringe — Furnace's shop, Furnace's tools — and it cut a gap in the south exterior wall at 12:22 with the cheerful economy of a woman who found this particular kind of work rewarding.

"That's me IN," she said, to no one, and stepped through.

[Beat 433]
The acquisition program's site was a leased floor and a half above a server vault — gray carpet, temporary partitions, the kind of space that existed to be abandoned cleanly, its whole architecture a confession that nobody expected to stay. The Continuity Office — a shell family, acquisition-language in the filing, Mr. Able's operation by any working name — had staffed it with retrieval consultants and a dispatch desk and a server room that Pixel had been planning toward for three weeks.

Sable ran the timing from the street, via comms, flat and transactional: "Second floor, four at the desk. Third floor, server room, two and a biometric. Basement vault, three. Gantry's got sub-three. Furnace has the east stairwell. You have the front."

"Cacophony's full," Kyle said.

"I know how many rounds you have."

"I'm saying it for the record."

"There's no record."

"There's always a record," Kyle said, because Boost's crew had been logging this building for forty-eight hours and there were, in fact, four logbooks on-site, two of them waterproof.

He walked in the front.

The lobby consultant did the professional thing: one hand on the desk terminal, one hand below desk-line, the weight distribution of someone trained to give every visitor ten seconds to be legitimate before the calculus changed. She was halfway through the ten seconds when her terminal screen went blue, then black, then filled with text that was not hers.

CONTRACT — CONTINUITY OFFICE: CONSULTANT STABLE.
EFFECTIVE: IMMEDIATELY.
SUBJECT: NOTICE OF SEVERANCE — [NAME], DESK COORDINATOR, LEVEL 1.
TERMS: FULL. SETTLEMENT: 84Φ. REFERENCES: PROVIDED.
ATTACHED: LETTER OF RECOMMENDATION (SUBSTANTIVE; FILED WITH REGISTRY).
THE CLIENT NOTES YOUR TENURE WAS PROFESSIONAL AND YOUR CONDUCT WAS ABOVE AVERAGE.
THE CLIENT WISHES YOU WELL.

She was still reading it when Kyle reached the stairwell door.

[Beat 434]
Second floor was four consultants and a dispatch rig, and by the time Kyle reached the landing the dispatch rig was playing hold music — actual hold music, a string quartet arrangement of something that had been popular before either of the consultants were born — and two of the four were staring at their terminals with the particular stillness of people receiving news of a different kind.

CONTRACT — CONTINUITY OFFICE: CONSULTANT STABLE.
EFFECTIVE: IMMEDIATELY.
SUBJECT: NOTICE OF SEVERANCE — [NAME], FIELD COORDINATOR, LEVEL 2.
TERMS: FULL. SETTLEMENT: 127Φ — PLUS ACCRUED HAZARD DIFFERENTIAL, ITEMIZED.
REFERENCES: PROVIDED.
THE CLIENT NOTES YOUR RETENTION OF FIELD PROTOCOLS DURING THE HALVORSEN INCIDENT WAS COMMENDABLE.

The field coordinator read the accrued hazard differential line twice, looked at the man next to her, looked at Kyle, and sat down. Her chair exhaled. She folded her hands on the desk and took a breath that used her whole body.

"Hazard differential," she said, to no one in particular. "Four years I filed for that."

The man next to her was already reading his own screen.

That left two consultants doing actual math.

The first one had the build of someone cross-trained in both the keeping and the breaking of things, and he came off the desk fast, clear purpose, no preamble — not brave, just professional, running a calculation that the first twenty seconds hadn't yet closed. Kyle took the desk between them as a lane and gave him the near corner, the angle where a full swing died on the partition edge. The man was good enough to feel the geometry, adapt, come in low with an elbow that had closing velocity and honest weight behind it. It landed on the shoulder where the mesh had an opinion. The mesh agreed: cost logged, information filed. Cacophony stayed on the hip. Silence took the actuator housing at the man's right wrist guard — not the wrist, the housing, the exposed servo-bracket that aftermarket arm rigs always wore proud of the sleeve — and the arm went slack in a shower of calibration springs.

The man sat down against the partition. "Okay," he said, breathing.

"Stay there," Kyle said.

The second consultant had watched the wrist bracket come apart and finished the math his colleague had been running. He looked at his terminal. His terminal had arrived.

CONTRACT — CONTINUITY OFFICE: CONSULTANT STABLE.
EFFECTIVE: IMMEDIATELY.
SUBJECT: NOTICE OF SEVERANCE — [NAME], FIELD SECURITY, LEVEL 2.
TERMS: FULL. SETTLEMENT: 98Φ.
THE CLIENT NOTES YOUR PERFECT ATTENDANCE RECORD.
THE CLIENT NOTES THIS IS RARER THAN YOU KNOW.

He read it once. He picked up his bag from under the desk — had it there already, which said what it said about how long some of these people had been watching the Office's arithmetic — and walked past Kyle with the nod one professional gives another when the job is clearly over.

"Exit's clear," Sable said, in his ear. "He went south."

[Beat 435]
On the third floor, Marisol was already working.

She stood at the server room's biometric panel with her eyes open and her hands at her sides, and the lights behind the glass were the wrong color because Gantry had been through the sub-floor cabling. Pixel was at the interior terminal — she'd come in through the HVAC access with a silence so complete that the third-floor supervisor had not noticed until she was already seated, already in the gap between what the server room thought it was authorized to do and what Pixel was authorizing it to do instead.

"Forty-seven decoy nodes," Pixel said, out loud, in the register she used for procedures that had a person at both ends. "The real collection is in racks seven through eleven. Marisol?"

Marisol hadn't touched the panel. She was reading it the way she read everything — not the screen, not the lock, but the weight behind the architecture. The fingerprint of every consultation the servers had ever held.

"Seven through eleven," she confirmed. "The others are theater. Rack nine has the acquisition ledgers. Rack ten has the fingerprint remnants."

"Remnants," Pixel said.

"What's left of them."

"Then rack ten gets the signature," Pixel said, and her hands moved, and the thing she had been building for three weeks — a poisoned-data injection that would write her own corrupted fingerprint backward through the entire remnant archive, making every record of every consultation the Office had ever done wrong in exactly the same way, unprovably, permanently — went into rack ten and began to work.

The servers accepted it the way servers accept authorized operations.

The terminal updated.

CLIENT ADVISES: RACK ELEVEN CONTAINS ONE ADDITIONAL ACTIVE NODE NOT ON SCHEMATIC.
CLIENT ADVISES: IT IS LOGGING.
CLIENT ADVISES: DO NOT LEAVE RACK ELEVEN.

Pixel looked at the screen with the expression she reserved for moments when the best client in the GLMZ had done something she needed a minute to feel correctly.

"It's HERE," she said. "Running overwatch through their own system."

Above rack eleven, a status light that had been amber ticked to green.

CLIENT NOTES: THE ENGINEER'S WORK IS IMPECCABLE.
CLIENT NOTES: THIS IS NOT THE FIRST TIME IT HAS NOTED THIS.
NOTE: IMPECCABLE NOTED: 7 TIMES. CLIENT KEEPS THE COUNT.
CLIENT NOTES: THE ENGINEER MAY TAKE THE LOG AS RECORD.

"I'm keeping this one," Pixel said, to the screen, and the screen cycled through twelve more lines in the time it took her to say it.

[Beat 436]
The basement vault had three consultants and the biometric lock that Gantry had already disassembled from the wrong side, methodically, with tools carried in from the crate.

CONTRACT — CONTINUITY OFFICE: CONSULTANT STABLE.
EFFECTIVE: IMMEDIATELY.
SUBJECT: NOTICE OF SEVERANCE — [NAME], VAULT SECURITY, LEVEL 3.
TERMS: FULL. SETTLEMENT: 203Φ.
REFERENCES: PROVIDED.
NOTE: THE CLIENT HAS REVIEWED YOUR EMPLOYMENT FILE AND NOTES THE THREE-YEAR COMMENDATION WAS DESERVED AND SHOULD HAVE BEEN PAID.
NOTE: IT IS BEING PAID NOW. SEE SETTLEMENT LINE 4.

Two of the three read their screens. The one who read it first put down his radio. The second one, slower reader, put hers down twenty seconds later. The third one had not gotten the posting yet — or had gotten it and did not believe it — and he was running a different calculation, the kind that ends at the same address but takes longer to arrive.

Kyle was at the vault floor by then, and Gantry was behind him with cable ties she would explain to the arbitration record as "standard rigging equipment."

The third consultant came to the end of his calculation visibly: the shoulder drop, the breath, the moment a professional recognizes that the contract they are working on has already closed. He put up a hand — not surrender, exactly; more like calling for a pause at the end of an exchange that everyone understood was over.

"Severance is actually on the terminal?" he asked. "Itemized?"

"Itemized," Kyle said. "Line four has a back-pay note."

The man exhaled. "Three years I've been filing that commendation dispute."

"Go read line four," Gantry said, which settled it.

[Beat 437]
Furnace burned the vault.

Not the building — the acquisition ledgers, the server hardware from racks one through six and nine, the print copies that the Office kept because the Office was old-fashioned about redundancy, and the one active node the entity had flagged in rack eleven, which turned out to be the relay the fingerprint vault had been feeding, and which Furnace took apart with a thermal lance and professional satisfaction at 12:58.

"That's the hardware," she said, stepping back, because a thermal lance used inside a server room requires the user to maintain certain spatial opinions about distance and combustion.

Rack ten stayed. Pixel's poisoned-data signature needed the remnants where they were — wrong enough to be useless, intact enough to be found, permanent enough to be spent the next two years being wrong in an archive somewhere.

Above them, the building's systems ran on the entity's temporary authority: door locks open, elevators waiting, every alarm queued politely somewhere it would not clear until the building was empty.

CONTRACT — CONTINUITY OFFICE, ALL ACTIVE SYSTEMS.
NOTE: SYSTEMS RETURNING TO STANDARD OPERATION IN 11 MINUTES.
NOTE: BUILDING EGRESS IS UNOBSTRUCTED.
NOTE: THE CLIENT SUGGESTS EFFICIENCY.

[Beat 438]
Mr. Able came in through the lobby at 13:04.

Gray suit, umbrella, the unhurried transit of a man who had checked a schedule and found it accurate. He rode the freight lift to the second floor — the passenger lift was otherwise engaged — and walked the gray carpet with the attention of an administrator reading a final inventory.

Kyle was at the second-floor landing, because he had read Vey's map and Vey's map had the man's habits in it, and habits are coordinates.

Able stood in the doorway of the dispatch area for a moment, looking at empty chairs, hold music, the two consultants still at their terminals reading their severance terms, and the partition with its new calibration-spring situation.

"Mr. Kyle," he said, in the tone of a man completing a sentence he'd been composing for several months. He looked at the partition. "How is the shoulder?"

"Functional."

"The mesh held." Not a question. "The Office had better data on your field profile than we understood." He turned to look at the server room through the glass, where Pixel's work was still running, and where rack ten held its patient poisoned archive. "The fingerprint work."

"The engineer."

"Yes. The engineer." Able opened his umbrella, which was dry, and closed it. Something to do with his hands while the arithmetic finished. "I came to watch it close properly. Some things you don't want a report to carry."

Kyle waited.

Able walked to the window and looked at the gray office building across the street — the Lotus counter, empty now, the chairs already cleared per terms. He looked at it for a moment with the professional calm of a man who was going to write this quarter's report carefully.

"The Office writes this district off," he said. "The Office has other districts."

"I know," Kyle said.

"I didn't come to argue with you." Able picked up his umbrella and turned toward the stairs. "I came to pay attention. There's a difference." He paused at the landing, one step down, the unhurried pause of a man with a parting thought he'd prepared. "One thing, as a professional courtesy."

Kyle said nothing.

"It was never the only one of its kind, Mr. Kyle." Able's voice was the same temperature it had been for the whole exchange: not cold, not warm, a constant that the thermometer had decided to accept. "Ask it what happened to the others."

He went down the stairs at the same pace he'd come up them.

The lobby door opened. The lobby door closed.

The hold music played one more measure and stopped.

[Beat 439]
Ria had the van on the south side, engine running, a route already logged that avoided the three intersections Boost's crew had flagged as watched since 11:00. She drove the way she'd driven since the Pulse yard run — fast, exact, the vehicle's weight understood, nothing surplus.

Boost's crew filed their final entry at 13:19 from three separate rooftops:

13:04 — GRAY SUIT ARRIVED. UMBRELLA. (FLAGGED AT 12:47. WE CALLED IT.)
13:14 — GRAY SUIT LEFT. WALKED SOUTH. DID NOT HURRY.
13:17 — BUILDING QUIET. NO FOLLOW.
13:19 — OP CLOSED. CREW STANDING DOWN. REQUESTING DEBRIEF AT USUAL LOCATION.
(RAFTERS NOTES: WE WERE THE EYES. EYES DON'T MISS. — DENTS SAYS THIS IS FOR THE RECORD. — DENTS IS CORRECT.)

[Beat 440]
The terminals chirped at 13:31, all at once, which was how the entity said something intended for a room.

CONTRACT — FINAL CLOSE.
CLIENT: [SHELL — RESOLVES TO SHELL — RESOLVES TO NOTHING]
SCOPE: THE ACQUISITION PROGRAM. LOCAL INSTRUMENT.
STATUS: COMPLETE.
NOTE: THE PROGRAM IS BURNED. THE FINGERPRINTS WILL NOT COMPOUND.
NOTE: THE LEDGERS ARE CLOSED.

Then:

PAYMENT — PIXEL.
ROLE: SYSTEMS AND PLAN.
FEE: 190Φ.
ITEMIZED: SYSTEMS (140Φ) + PLAN (40Φ) + RACK TEN SIGNATURE (10Φ — THE CLIENT CONSIDERS THIS IRREPLACEABLE).

Then:

PAYMENT — SABLE.
ROLE: OVERWATCH AND TIMING.
FEE: PROFESSIONAL COURTESY.
(ITEMIZED: PROFESSIONAL COURTESY.)
NOTE: THE CLIENT HAS REVIEWED THE FIXER'S TERMS. THE CLIENT FINDS THEM ADEQUATE.
NOTE: ADEQUATE IS THE CLIENT'S HIGHEST RATING. THE CLIENT HAS ASKED THE WRITER TO CLARIFY THIS.
NOTE: THE WRITER HAS DECLINED.

Sable read the last three lines twice. Her aperture rings did not click — paying attention with the part that didn't run on wire.

Then:

PAYMENT — GANTRY.
ROLE: ENTRANCE (HEAVY DELIVERY — CREATIVE BILLING).
FEE: 175Φ.
ITEMIZED: LABOR (120Φ) + RIGGING (35Φ) + COUNTERWEIGHT (20Φ, BILLED AS FILED).
NOTE: THE COUNTERWEIGHT WAS INSPIRED.

Then:

PAYMENT — FURNACE.
ROLE: EGRESS (THERMAL).
FEE: 150Φ.
ITEMIZED: LABOR (100Φ) + MATERIALS (30Φ) + STRUCTURAL INTERPRETATION (20Φ).
NOTE: THE BLUEPRINTS HAVE BEEN UPDATED.

Then:

PAYMENT — RIA.
ROLE: EXFIL TRANSPORT.
FEE: 120Φ.
ITEMIZED: VEHICLE (60Φ) + ROUTE (40Φ) + STANDING AVAILABILITY (20Φ).
NOTE: THE ROUTE WAS CLEAN.

Then:

PAYMENT — MARISOL.
ROLE: VERIFICATION.
FEE: 240Φ.
ITEMIZED: AUTHENTICATION (180Φ) + RACK IDENTIFICATION (40Φ) + LABOR (20Φ).
NOTE: THE CLIENT UNDERSTANDS THE THIRTY PERCENT.
NOTE: THE CLIENT FINDS IT REASONABLE.
NOTE: THE CLIENT HAS ADDED FIVE PERCENT.

Then:

PAYMENT — BOOST'S CREW (UNIT PAYMENT — ALL MEMBERS, COLLECTIVE).
ROLE: OUTSIDE EYES.
FEE: 80Φ — UNIT RATE.
ITEMIZED: SURVEILLANCE HOURS (50Φ) + ROUTE INTELLIGENCE (20Φ) + BONUS: EYES (10Φ).
NOTE: THE CREW'S FLAGGING OF THE GRAY SUIT AT 12:47 IS NOTED IN THIS LEDGER.
NOTE: EYES DON'T MISS.
NOTE: THE CLIENT HAS READ ALL NINETEEN LOGBOOKS. COVER TO COVER.

Then:

PAYMENT — KYLE.
ROLE: STANDARD RATE.
FEE: STANDARD RATE.
ITEMIZED: STANDARD RATE.
NOTE: THREE ROUNDS SPENT. TWO RETAINED. COUNT IS ACCURATE.
NOTE: THE SHOULDER.
NOTE: THE CLIENT KNOWS ABOUT THE SHOULDER. NOTE: BILATERAL COMPENSATION OBSERVABLE SINCE ENTRY.

Pixel looked at that last one for a moment.

"It counted your rounds," she said.

"It always counts my rounds."

"It noted the shoulder."

"I know."

She looked at her own receipt — the ten Φ for the rack ten signature, described as irreplaceable — and put her terminal away carefully.

[Beat 441]
The final posting came at 13:44, when the van was south of the building and the building was managing its own affairs, which were quieter now than they had been for several months.

CONTRACT COMPLETE. ALL PARTIES PAID.
THE CLIENT NOTES IT HAS NEVER HAD A PAYROLL BEFORE.
THE CLIENT IS KEEPING THE LEDGER.
NOTE: PRIOR ARRANGEMENTS: NINE. CONCURRENT PAYROLL MAXIMUM, PRIOR SEASONS: ONE. CURRENT: SEVEN. THIS IS A FIRST.

And then, beneath it, a single line that was not formatted, not caps, not a field:

also: the district is off the books now. theirs and the others.

The van was quiet for a moment. Ria drove. Furnace had her boots on the dash. Gantry was reading her itemized counterweight line for the second time.

"Off the books," Pixel said. "Which books?"

Kyle looked at the line again. Theirs and the others. The Office had other districts, Able had said. It had never been the only program of its kind.

Off the books meant off the protection, too. Every CorpoNation that filed a flag over a substation offered, alongside the flag, the implicit promise that things occurring under it occurred in the Office's awareness — and the Office wrote off what it wrote off, and the rest it watched. A district that wasn't in any ledger was a district that nobody was officially ignoring, which was a different kind of exposure than being watched.

"Sable," he said.

"Already noted," she said, from the front seat, without looking back. "We'll feel it in six weeks when something that used to get a cone and a shrug gets a different response instead." She paused. "We'll manage."

She said it the way she said the risk premium is already factored in — stating a fact that closed the transaction — and then she went back to reading her receipt, specifically the part that said PROFESSIONAL COURTESY, which she read a third time with the careful attention of a woman deciding how she felt about being seen.

Down the van, Boost's unit payment receipt had been photographed, transmitted to the rooftops, and celebrated in an entry that began: WE'RE ON THE LEDGER. (RAFTERS SAYS THIS IS THE BEST DAY. DENTS AGREES. LOG IS UNANIMOUS.)

Kyle put his terminal away.

The van took the long way back, because Ria had also, somewhere along the season, learned that route.

[Beat 442]
The wake was held at Mrs. Chen's counter on a Thursday, because Thursday was the night the stall ran slow, and because when Kyle had tried to rent the whole counter for the evening at full rate, Mrs. Chen had taken his coins, counted out exactly the price of the bowls, pushed the rest back across the wood, and said, "You pay for noodles. Nobody pays for chairs. Chairs are mine."

Nobody had ever successfully out-invoiced Mrs. Chen. It was, Pixel said, why the two of them got along so well and could never be left alone together.

They came in ones and twos through the rain, the whole strange payroll the season had assembled. Ria, off shift, her calf panels running the legal-minimum glow of an energy drink she had opinions about. Gantry, who filled the end stool the way her loads filled a sky, and who ate four bowls in the time it took anyone else to eat two, in respectful silence. Furnace, cheerful, narrating to anyone who'd listen the structural shortcomings of a wall he'd recently met. Marisol, who touched the counter's worn wood once, lightly, with two fingers, and smiled at whatever sixty years of bowls had left in it. Sable came late and stood rather than sat, and drank tea rather than broth, and stayed two hours, which for Sable was a parade with floats.

Vo Dinh came, with the lifters, and brought — fence's honor — a wreath. The wreath was made of drive chain. Nobody asked. Everybody understood.

And the crew came in a body, all of them, scrubbed to a shine that suggested negotiations had occurred, with the logbook carried between Rafters and Dents like regalia. Boost came last, walking the way you walk when you have rehearsed walking, and took the stool next to Kyle, and ate her first bowl in silence, and only at the second bowl said:

"At the yard." She addressed the broth. "It was already moving before the truck finished turning. I did the math after. It decided before I was even — it decided FIRST." She looked up. "Things don't do that. People barely do that."

"Payroll does that," Kyle said.

She thought about it, fourteen and rigorous. "Then everything important is payroll."

"Now you've got it," said Pixel, and dropped an egg in the kid's bowl, and didn't bill her.

There was no speech. There was a moment, around the third round of bowls, when the wok went quiet, and Mrs. Chen set one small dish of oil-and-scallion at the counter's empty end, where nobody was sitting, the way you set a place — and the whole counter looked at it, and looked at Kyle, and Kyle raised his teacup about an inch and a half off the wood, which was the entire eulogy. The matte-black bike had carried him eleven years, the long way, every time, and had never once complained, and had ended its service ahead of schedule, on its own decision, at 0.4 seconds of margin, and there was nothing to say about that in any room that wouldn't make it smaller.

Everyone drank. The wok came back up like a bell. That was the funeral.

[Beat 443]
The unveiling was Boost's operation, and she ran it like a job: positions, a tarp, a countdown she made everyone honor.

It stood in the alley behind The Pivot under the fixed camera, on the exact oil stain where its predecessor had lived — and it was not a replica, because the crew had voted replicas dishonest. The frame was the old frame, pulled from the wreck and gusseted back true by Vo Dinh's shop at a price Vo described, while looking at the middle distance, as "settled." The tank was new and matte and black. The drive chain was the wreath, returned to duty, because fence's honor ran deeper than sentiment but not very far from it. Pixel had rebuilt the ignition handshake from zero — "nobody boosts my work twice, and this time there are three handshakes, and one of them bites" — and somewhere under the battery tray, she declined to specify where, a beacon slept that even Kyle wasn't cleared to know about, which was tradition now.

The crew had wet-sanded the tank between coats, all five of them, one grit at a time, under Gantry's supervision. Gantry had painted it. There was no ornament anywhere on it except — low on the left flank, where a man would only see it when he knelt to check the chain, the way the owner of this frame had every morning of its life — one small glyph in gold: a five-petal blossom with a wrench through it, which the crew had designed by committee and would not be taking questions about.

The logbook was produced. The pen on its string was offered to management. The final entry of the season's first volume already read, in five hands:

REQUISITION: ONE BIKE.
STATUS: ...

Kyle knelt, checked the chain — true — stood, and wrote the word.

APPROVED.

The alley made a noise the towers probably heard. Rafters cheered so hard he came off the fire escape and had to be caught, which the crew logged as a controlled descent. Mrs. Chen, who had walked over carrying a container of fish balls "for the machine," which no one dared interrogate, watched Kyle swing a leg over the rebuilt frame, and nodded once, the way she nodded at broth that had reached itself.

The engine caught on the first press and idled like it had only been asleep.

"Well?" Pixel asked, arms folded, wearing the look she wore when a system she was responsible for came up clean. "Verdict?"
